# FAA Knowledge — All Questions

**635 questions** across 35 chapters (AFH: 327, PHAK: 308)


---

# AFH — Airplane Flying Handbook


## Chapter 1 — 19 questions

**1.1 [Introduction, p.1-1, difficulty 1]**  
According to the chapter, which comparison does the FAA describe as misleading when discussing learning to fly an aircraft?

- ✅ **Learning to drive an automobile, because aircraft operate in a three-dimensional environment requiring different motor skills**
- Learning to ride a bicycle, because aircraft require no balance skill
- Learning to sail a boat, because both rely entirely on wind
- Learning to operate heavy machinery, because both use foot pedals

  > The chapter states that comparing learning to fly to learning to drive an automobile is misleading because aircraft operate in a three-dimensional environment requiring a depth of knowledge and type of motor skill development more sensitive to that situation, including coordination, timing, control touch, and speed sense.

**1.2 [Introduction, p.1-2, difficulty 2]**  
What does the chapter identify as the primary purpose of primary and intermediate flight training?

- ✅ **To develop knowledge, experience, skills, and safe habits that are transferable to any airplane**
- To learn how to fly a particular make and model of airplane
- To pass the required FAA practical tests for pilot certification
- To accumulate the minimum flight hours required by 14 CFR part 61

  > The chapter explicitly states that the purpose of flight training is not to learn a particular make and model, but to develop transferable skills and safe habits. It also notes that passing practical tests is 'only incidental to this goal' of producing a safe and competent pilot.

**1.3 [Role of the FAA, p.1-3, difficulty 2]**  
Under what condition does a standard airworthiness certificate remain valid?

- ✅ **As long as the aircraft meets its approved type design, is in a condition for safe operation, and maintenance is performed in accordance with 14 CFR parts 21, 43, and 91**
- It must be renewed every 2 years with proof of continued airworthiness
- It remains valid indefinitely regardless of aircraft condition or maintenance
- It is valid only for the original owner and expires upon sale of the aircraft

  > The chapter states a standard airworthiness certificate remains valid as long as the aircraft meets its approved type design, is in a condition for safe operation, and maintenance, preventative maintenance, and alterations are performed in accordance with 14 CFR parts 21, 43, and 91.

**1.4 [Role of the FAA, p.1-4, difficulty 2]**  
If the minimum equipment required for flight is outlined in 14 CFR part 91, section 91.205, where does the chapter say a pilot can find the specific equipment required for a particular airplane to be airworthy?

- ✅ **The Airplane Flight Manual/Pilot's Operating Handbook (AFM/POH)**
- The Type Certificate Data Sheet (TCDS) only
- 14 CFR part 43
- The Airworthiness Directive (AD) database

  > The chapter states that while 14 CFR part 91, section 91.205 outlines the minimum equipment required for flight, the AFM/POH lists the equipment required for the airplane to be airworthy. The equipment list in the AFM/POH is developed during the airplane certification process.

**1.5 [Role of the Pilot Examiner, p.1-6, difficulty 1]**  
Which of the following is a Designated Pilot Examiner (DPE) NOT authorized to do on behalf of the FAA?

- ✅ **Initiate enforcement action against a pilot**
- Conduct practical tests leading to issuance of specific pilot certificates
- Accept applications for pilot certificates and ratings
- Charge a reasonable fee for administering a practical test

  > The chapter explicitly states that a DPE is not an FAA ASI and cannot initiate enforcement action, investigate accidents, or perform surveillance activities on behalf of the FAA. A DPE's authority is generally limited to accepting applications and conducting practical tests.

**1.6 [Role of the Flight Instructor, p.1-6, difficulty 2]**  
What does the chapter describe as the cornerstone of aviation safety, and what responsibility does this role carry?

- ✅ **The flight instructor, who assumes total responsibility for training pilots in all knowledge areas and skills necessary for safe operation in the NAS**
- The DPE, who assumes responsibility for verifying pilot competency through practical tests
- The FSDO inspector, who monitors all training activities and certifies pilots
- The FAA Academy, which designs all training curricula for pilot certification

  > The chapter states 'The flight instructor is the cornerstone of aviation safety' and that the FAA has adopted a training concept placing full responsibility for pilot training on the flight instructor, including airmanship skills, judgment, decision-making, hazard identification, and risk analysis.

**1.7 [Role of the Flight Instructor, p.1-7, difficulty 2]**  
What does the chapter identify as a key reason a flight instructor should meticulously observe safety practices during all flight operations, not just during instruction?

- ✅ **Learners consciously or unconsciously attempt to imitate their instructor's flying habits**
- The FAA requires instructors to maintain a perfect safety record to keep their certification
- Insurance policies for flight schools mandate zero safety violations
- Students are required to evaluate their instructors and report unsafe practices

  > The chapter explains that learners consider their flight instructor to be a paragon of flying proficiency whose flying habits they consciously or unconsciously attempt to imitate, which is why instructors must observe safety practices during all flight operations.

**1.8 [Sources of Flight Training, p.1-8, difficulty 2]**  
Why are graduates of FAA-certificated pilot schools (14 CFR part 141) allowed to meet certification experience requirements with less flight time?

- ✅ **Because the schools operate in a structured environment with an FAA-approved training course outline**
- Because part 141 schools use only more advanced training aircraft
- Because part 141 instructors have higher certification standards than part 61 instructors
- Because part 141 graduates are exempt from taking the FAA practical test

  > The chapter states that training at an FAA-certificated pilot school is structured, and because of this structured environment, graduates are allowed to meet the certification experience requirements of 14 CFR part 61 with less flight time.

**1.9 [Sources of Flight Training, p.1-8, difficulty 2]**  
What authority does an FAA-certificated pilot school with examining authority have that other schools do not?

- ✅ **The authority to recommend its graduates for pilot certificates or ratings without further testing by the FAA**
- The authority to issue medical certificates to its students
- The authority to conduct all FAA knowledge tests on site
- The authority to waive the flight time requirements for any certificate

  > The chapter states that a school with examining authority for a particular course has the authority to recommend its graduates for pilot certificates or ratings without further testing by the FAA.

**1.10 [Airman Certification Standards (ACS) and Practical Test Standards (PTS), p.1-9, difficulty 1]**  
How does the chapter characterize the ACS and PTS documents in terms of their intended use?

- ✅ **They are testing documents, not teaching documents, and are not intended to be used as a training syllabus**
- They are teaching documents that should be used as the primary training syllabus
- They are reference manuals containing detailed descriptions of how to perform maneuvers
- They are advisory documents with no bearing on practical test administration

  > The chapter explicitly states that the ACS and PTS are testing documents rather than teaching documents, and although applicants should be familiar with them during training, they are not intended to be used as a training syllabus. Descriptions of how to perform maneuvers are found in reference and teaching documents such as the handbook itself.

**1.11 [Collision Avoidance, p.1-10, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, where do most midair collision accidents and near midair collision incidents typically occur?

- ✅ **In good VFR weather conditions, during daylight hours, within 5 miles of an airport and/or near navigation aids**
- In IFR conditions, at night, and in remote areas far from airports
- In marginal VFR conditions, during twilight hours, and at cruising altitude above 10,000 feet
- In clear air, at night, and more than 50 miles from any airport

  > The chapter states that most midair collision accidents and reported near midair collision incidents occur in good VFR weather conditions and during the hours of daylight, and that most occur within 5 miles of an airport and/or near navigation aids.

**1.12 [Collision Avoidance, p.1-11, difficulty 2]**  
When scanning for traffic, what visual technique does the chapter recommend for the most effective detection of conflicting aircraft?

- ✅ **A series of short, regularly-spaced eye movements not exceeding 10 degrees, observing each area for at least 1 second**
- Continuous sweeping eye movements across 30-degree segments at a time
- Staring fixedly at one sector of the sky for 10 seconds before moving to the next
- Rapid random eye movements covering the entire visual field in under 5 seconds

  > The chapter states that effective scanning is accomplished with a series of short, regularly-spaced eye movements that bring successive areas of the sky into the central visual field. Each movement should not exceed 10 degrees, and each area should be observed for at least 1 second to enable detection.

**1.13 [Collision Avoidance, p.1-11, difficulty 3]**  
If another aircraft appears to have no relative motion but is increasing in size, what does this indicate and what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **The other aircraft is likely on a collision course, and the pilot should take immediate evasive action**
- The other aircraft is moving away and no action is needed
- The other aircraft is flying parallel and the pilot should maintain course
- The other aircraft is descending below and the pilot should climb to avoid it

  > The chapter states that if another aircraft appears to have no relative motion, it is likely on a collision course. If the other aircraft shows no lateral or vertical motion but is increasing in size, the observing pilot needs to take immediate evasive action.

**1.14 [Runway Incursion Avoidance, p.1-12, difficulty 1]**  
What does the chapter identify as the three major areas contributing to runway incursions?

- ✅ **Communications, airport knowledge, and flight deck procedures for maintaining orientation**
- Weather conditions, aircraft speed, and runway lighting
- ATC staffing, airport size, and time of day
- Pilot fatigue, aircraft type, and taxiway markings

  > The chapter identifies the three major areas contributing to runway incursions as communications, airport knowledge, and flight deck procedures for maintaining orientation.

**1.15 [Stall Awareness, p.1-13, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, what is the direct cause of every stall, and what is the relationship between airspeed and stalls?

- ✅ **An excessive angle of attack is the direct cause; the wing can be stalled at any speed**
- Low airspeed is the direct cause; stalls cannot occur at high speed
- High pitch attitude is the direct cause; stalls cannot occur at level pitch
- Power loss is the direct cause; stalls cannot occur with full power

  > The chapter emphasizes that the direct cause of every stall is an excessive angle of attack (AOA). It further states that low speed is not necessary to produce a stall—the wing can be brought to an excessive AOA at any speed. High pitch attitude is also not an absolute indication of proximity to a stall.

**1.16 [Stall Awareness, p.1-14, difficulty 2]**  
What does the chapter identify as the key skill a pilot needs for stall awareness?

- ✅ **The ability to visualize the wing's angle of attack in any particular circumstance to estimate the margin of safety above stall**
- The ability to memorize the stall speeds for every aircraft configuration
- The ability to feel vibrations in the control yoke before a stall occurs
- The ability to maintain a minimum airspeed buffer above the published stall speed

  > The chapter states that the key to stall awareness is the pilot's ability to visualize the wing's AOA in any particular circumstance and thereby estimate the margin of safety above stall. This is described as a learned skill that should be acquired early in flight training.

**1.17 [Use of Checklists, p.1-15, difficulty 1]**  
What distinction does the chapter make between a checklist and a 'do list'?

- ✅ **A checklist is used to verify that necessary actions have already been accomplished, while a 'do list' implies actions are performed while reading the list**
- A checklist is mandatory while a 'do list' is optional
- A 'do list' is used in flight and a checklist is used only on the ground
- A checklist covers normal procedures while a 'do list' covers emergency procedures

  > The chapter explains that checklists need not be 'do lists.' The proper actions can be accomplished first, and then the checklist used to quickly ensure all necessary tasks have been completed, with emphasis on the 'check' in checklist.

**1.18 [Use of Checklists, p.1-16, difficulty 2]**  
What three-step process does the chapter recommend for the exchange of flight controls between instructor and learner?

- ✅ **The transferor says 'You have the flight controls,' the receiver acknowledges 'I have the flight controls,' and the transferor confirms 'You have the flight controls,' with a visual check**
- The instructor taps the learner's shoulder and says 'Your airplane,' and the learner nods to confirm
- The transferor says 'My airplane,' the receiver releases the controls, and the transferor takes over
- The learner requests control by saying 'Can I fly?' and the instructor hands over the controls without verbal confirmation

  > The chapter describes a three-step process: the transferor says 'You have the flight controls,' the receiver acknowledges 'I have the flight controls,' and the transferor confirms 'You have the flight controls.' A visual check should also be performed to ensure the other person actually has the controls.

**1.19 [FAA WINGS Program, p.1-16, difficulty 2]**  
How does the chapter characterize the relationship between the flight review required under 14 CFR part 61.56(c) and the FAA WINGS Program?

- ✅ **The regulation specifies a minimum requirement, while the WINGS Program offers a path to enhanced proficiency and safety that exceeds the minimum**
- The WINGS Program replaces the need for any flight review under part 61
- The WINGS Program is only available to flight instructors, not private pilots
- The regulation requires WINGS Program participation every 24 calendar months

  > The chapter states that 14 CFR part 61.56(c) specifies a minimum requirement for training and sign-off within 24 calendar months, and that many astute pilots realize this is a minimum. The WINGS Program is presented as a higher degree of commitment for enhanced proficiency, safety, and enjoyment, and many pilots use it to keep their flight review current.


## Chapter 2 — 19 questions

**2.1 [Preflight Assessment of the Aircraft, p.2-1, difficulty 2]**  
During preflight assessment, a pilot reviews the aircraft logbooks and finds the annual inspection expired two weeks ago. The pilot argues the aircraft is still safe because a mechanic recently looked it over. Which statement best reflects the pilot's responsibility?

- ✅ **The PIC is responsible for determining whether the aircraft is in condition for safe flight, and the aircraft does not meet airworthiness requirements without a current annual inspection.**
- The owner/operator is primarily responsible for maintenance, so the PIC has no obligation regarding inspection status.
- A mechanic's informal review satisfies the annual inspection requirement under 14 CFR part 91.
- The aircraft may be flown as long as the pilot believes it is mechanically safe, regardless of inspection status.

  > Per 14 CFR part 91, sections 91.7(a) and (b), the PIC is responsible for determining whether the aircraft is in condition for safe flight, and no person may operate a civil aircraft unless it is in an airworthy condition. An annual inspection within the preceding 12 calendar months is required under section 91.409(a).

**2.2 [Preflight Assessment of the Aircraft, p.2-4, difficulty 1]**  
Which of the following documents must be on board or affixed to the airplane for flight under 14 CFR part 91?

- ✅ **Current Airworthiness Certificate, Registration Certificate, and operating limitations**
- Pilot logbook, medical certificate, and weight and balance data only
- Maintenance logbooks, ELT battery receipt, and radio station license
- Annual inspection endorsement, compass correction card, and insurance policy

  > The chapter lists required documents including the Current Airworthiness Certificate, Current Registration Certificate, and Operating limitations (which may be in the form of an FAA-approved AFM/POH, placards, instrument markings, or any combination thereof). Maintenance logbooks are not normally kept in the airplane.

**2.3 [Visual Preflight Assessment, p.2-5, difficulty 2]**  
During the interior preflight inspection, the pilot notices the VSI reads 300 feet per minute while the airplane is stationary on the ground. What action is appropriate?

- ✅ **Use a small screwdriver to zero the VSI, as this is the only flight instrument a pilot has the prerogative to adjust.**
- Note the error on the compass correction card and continue with the flight.
- Contact an FAA-certificated repairman or mechanic to adjust the VSI before flight.
- Subtract 300 feet per minute from all VSI readings during the flight to correct for the error.

  > The chapter states: 'The mechanical VSI is the only flight instrument that a pilot has the prerogative to adjust. All others need to be adjusted by an FAA-certificated repairman or mechanic.' A small screwdriver can be used to zero the instrument if it is not part of an electronic display.

**2.4 [Visual Preflight Assessment, p.2-4, difficulty 2]**  
During preflight, the pilot discovers the seat rail holes are slightly oval-shaped rather than round. Why is this significant?

- ✅ **Oval-shaped seat rail holes may indicate excessive wear, which could allow the seat lock pins to fail and the seat to slide during flight.**
- Oval holes are normal and expected in older aircraft and require no action.
- Oval holes indicate the seat was installed at the wrong angle and the seat rails must be replaced.
- Oval holes are only a concern if the seat does not move at all during the inspection.

  > The chapter states seats should be inspected to ensure 'that the seats properly latch into the seat rails through the seat lock pins and that seat rail holes are not abnormally worn to an oval shape.' This wear could compromise seat security.

**2.5 [Outer Wing Surfaces and Tail Section, p.2-6, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot inspecting the wing notices a black-oxide film around several rivet heads along the spar line. What does this most likely indicate?

- ✅ **The rivets are working loose in their holes, which may indicate internal structural damage or failure.**
- The wing has been exposed to normal weathering and the film is purely cosmetic.
- The rivets were recently replaced and the film is residual manufacturing lubricant.
- The black-oxide film indicates the spar has been treated with corrosion inhibitor and is performing correctly.

  > The chapter states: 'Inspect around rivet heads looking for cracked paint or a black-oxide film that forms when a rivet works free in its hole.' This is a sign of a loose rivet and potential structural concern.

**2.6 [Outer Wing Surfaces and Tail Section, p.2-7, difficulty 2]**  
When inspecting a composite airplane, the pilot gently taps on the wing surface and notices a change in sound in one area. Why is this technique used?

- ✅ **A change in sound when tapping the structure may identify delamination of the spar to skin or other structural problems.**
- It is used to locate the spar lines since composite airplanes have no visible rivets to identify them.
- It is used to verify that the composite material has been properly cured at the factory.
- It is used to check for water trapped inside the wing tanks before sumping the fuel.

  > The chapter explains that composite airplanes generally have no rivets or screws to aid in identifying spar lines and attach points, but 'delamination of spar to skin or other structural problems may be identified by bubbles, fine hair-line cracks, or changes in sound when gently tapping on the structure with a fingertip.'

**2.7 [Fuel and Oil, p.2-7, difficulty 2]**  
An engine certificated for grade 80/87 AVGAS has 100LL fuel available. The pilot knows 100LL is approved as an alternate. What is the general rule regarding fuel grade substitution?

- ✅ **A lower grade should never be substituted for a required higher grade, but a higher grade may be used if approved as an alternate.**
- Any grade of AVGAS may be substituted freely as long as the engine starts and runs normally.
- Only grade 100/130 may be substituted for 80/87 because both have higher octane ratings.
- A higher grade may never be substituted for a lower grade due to the risk of detonation.

  > The chapter states: 'Aircraft piston engines certificated for grade 80/87 run satisfactorily on 100LL if approved as an alternate. The reverse is not true. Fuel of a lower grade should never be substituted for a required higher grade.' Using a lower grade can cause detonation.

**2.8 [Fuel and Oil, p.2-10, difficulty 2]**  
During preflight, a pilot sumps the fuel tanks and finds a small amount of water in the first sample. What is the appropriate action?

- ✅ **Continue sampling from each sump until no water or contamination appears, and investigate further if contamination is consistent or significant.**
- Discard the contaminated sample and proceed with the flight since a small amount of water is normal.
- Drain the entire fuel system and refuel the aircraft before continuing the preflight.
- Add isopropyl alcohol to the fuel to absorb the water and continue with the flight.

  > The chapter states: 'If water is found in the first fuel sample, continue sampling until no water and contamination appears. Significant and/or consistent water, sediment or contaminations are grounds for further investigation by qualified maintenance personnel.'

**2.9 [Fuel and Oil, p.2-10, difficulty 2]**  
Why is it recommended to fill fuel tanks completely after the last flight of the day?

- ✅ **The more fuel in the tanks, the less room there is for condensation to occur, which is the best preventive measure against water accumulation.**
- It ensures the fuel quantity gauges are always calibrated to full for the next flight.
- It prevents the rubber fuel tanks from shrinking and cracking when exposed to air.
- It reduces the weight of the aircraft on the landing gear struts overnight.

  > The chapter states: 'The best preventive measure is to minimize the opportunity for water to condense in the tanks… The more fuel that is in the tanks, the less room there is for condensation to occur. Keeping fuel tanks filled is also the best way to slow the aging of rubber fuel tanks and tank sealant.'

**2.10 [Engine and Propeller, p.2-12, difficulty 2]**  
During the engine inspection, the pilot notices white stains near the exhaust stacks and cylinder heads. What do these stains most likely indicate?

- ✅ **Exhaust leaks at the cylinder head or cracks in the exhaust stacks**
- Fuel dye from a leaking fuel line, which is a fire hazard
- Coolant leakage from the liquid-cooled engine cylinder heads
- Normal combustion residue that requires no further action

  > The chapter states: 'The exhaust system should be checked for white stains caused by exhaust leaks at the cylinder head or cracks in the exhaust stacks.'

**2.11 [Engine and Propeller, p.2-7, difficulty 1]**  
How can jet fuel inadvertently introduced into an AVGAS-burning reciprocating engine affect the airplane?

- ✅ **The engine may start and run long enough for the airplane to become airborne, only to fail catastrophically after takeoff.**
- The engine will not start at all, making the misfueling immediately obvious.
- The engine will run roughly but safely at reduced power for the duration of the flight.
- The engine will overheat immediately upon startup and shut itself down automatically.

  > The chapter warns: 'A reciprocating engine operating on jet fuel may start, run, and power the airplane long enough for the airplane to become airborne, only to have the engine fail catastrophically after takeoff.'

**2.12 [Risk and Resource Management, p.2-12, difficulty 2]**  
Which of the following best describes the difference between a hazard and risk as defined in the chapter?

- ✅ **A hazard is a condition or circumstance that could lead to an undesired event, while risk is the future impact of a hazard that is not controlled or eliminated.**
- A hazard is the probability of an accident occurring, while risk is the cause of the accident.
- A hazard is always weather-related, while risk is always pilot-related.
- A hazard is a quantitative value assigned to a task, while risk is a qualitative assessment of pilot skill.

  > The chapter defines a hazard as 'a condition, event, object, or circumstance that could lead to or contribute to an unplanned or undesired event' and risk as 'the future impact of a hazard that is not controlled or eliminated.'

**2.13 [Risk and Resource Management, p.2-14, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot becomes fixated on a malfunctioning instrument light during a critical phase of flight. Which SRM concept does this situation best illustrate?

- ✅ **Task management—fixation on a minor issue displaces the ability to process information of greater importance.**
- Situational awareness—the pilot is demonstrating heightened awareness of all cockpit systems.
- Human resource management—the pilot is effectively using available equipment resources.
- Aeronautical decision-making—the pilot is following proper troubleshooting procedures.

  > The chapter states under Task Management: 'distraction and fixation impede the ability to process information. For example, if a pilot becomes distracted and fixates on an instrument light failure, the unnecessary focus displaces capability and prevents appreciation of tasks of greater importance.'

**2.14 [Engine Starting, p.2-16, difficulty 2]**  
After engine start, the pilot notices the ammeter shows a continuous and very high current draw. What does this most likely indicate and what action should be taken?

- ✅ **The starter motor may have remained electrically and mechanically engaged after engine start; the engine should be shut down immediately.**
- The alternator is charging the battery normally; no action is needed.
- The battery has a short circuit; the pilot should turn off the alternator and continue on battery power.
- The magneto switch is faulty; the pilot should cycle the magnetos to reset the system.

  > The chapter states: 'the starter motor may remain electrically and mechanically engaged after engine start. This can be detected by a continuous and very high current draw on the ammeter… The engine should be shut down immediately if this occurs.'

**2.15 [Engine Starting, p.2-16, difficulty 1]**  
What is the maximum recommended continuous starter operation time before a cool-down period is required?

- ✅ **30 seconds, followed by a cool-down period of at least 30 seconds to 1 minute**
- 10 seconds, followed by a cool-down period of 2 minutes
- 60 seconds, followed by a cool-down period of 5 minutes
- 45 seconds, followed by a cool-down period of 45 seconds

  > The chapter states: 'Avoid continuous starter operation for periods longer than 30 seconds without a cool down period of at least 30 seconds to 1 minute (some AFM/POH specify longer cool down routines).'

**2.16 [Taxiing, p.2-19, difficulty 2]**  
While taxiing with a quartering tailwind, what control positions should the pilot use to minimize the tendency of the wind to get under the tail and wing?

- ✅ **Elevator held DOWN and upwind aileron DOWN**
- Elevator held UP and upwind aileron UP
- Elevator held DOWN and upwind aileron UP
- Elevator held UP and upwind aileron DOWN

  > The chapter states: 'When taxiing with a quartering tailwind, the elevator should be held in the DOWN position, and the upwind aileron, DOWN. Since the wind is striking the airplane from behind, these control positions reduce the tendency of the wind to get under the tail and the wing and to nose the airplane over.'

**2.17 [Taxiing, p.2-19, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot routinely receives the same taxi instructions at their home airport and begins to expect them without carefully listening. What human factors risk does this scenario describe?

- ✅ **Expectation bias—the tendency to follow the same procedure repeatedly without evaluating current instructions**
- Confirmation bias—the pilot confirms instructions with ATC before proceeding
- Normalization of deviance—the pilot accepts increasingly dangerous taxi routes as normal
- Task saturation—the pilot is overwhelmed by too many taxi instructions at once

  > The chapter states: 'a pilot given the same taxi instructions by ATC, starts expecting those same instructions and might not realize that those instructions no longer apply… This expectation bias has occurred to many pilots who did not stop and carefully consider and evaluate their taxi instructions.'

**2.18 [Before-Takeoff Check, p.2-21, difficulty 2]**  
During the before-takeoff check, why should the airplane be positioned headed into the wind for the engine run-up?

- ✅ **Air-cooled engines receive less cooling air on the ground, and heading into the wind provides the best degree of engine cooling during ground operations.**
- Heading into the wind prevents the airplane from weathervaning during the magneto check.
- Heading into the wind reduces the engine rpm needed for the magneto check.
- Heading into the wind ensures the altimeter reads field elevation accurately during the run-up.

  > The chapter states: 'To minimize overheating during engine run-up, it is recommended that the airplane be headed as nearly as possible into the wind and, if equipped, engine instruments that indicate cylinder head temperatures should be monitored. Cowl flaps, if available, should be set according to the AFM/POH.'

**2.19 [After-Landing, p.2-22, difficulty 2]**  
After landing, a pilot wants to retract the flaps during the landing rollout to improve braking on a short-field landing. What precaution does the chapter recommend?

- ✅ **The pilot should make a positive identification of the flap control handle before retracting the flaps to avoid mistakenly retracting the landing gear.**
- The pilot should wait until reaching taxi speed before retracting flaps to avoid distraction.
- The pilot should only retract flaps after turning off the runway and stopping completely.
- The pilot should retract flaps only if the aircraft manufacturer specifically recommends doing so during the landing rollout.

  > The chapter states: 'There have been many cases where a pilot has mistakenly manipulated the wrong handle and retracted the landing gear, instead of the flaps… In this situation, the pilot should make a positive identification of the flap control handle before retracting the flaps.'


## Chapter 3 — 18 questions

**3.1 [Effect and Use of Flight Controls, p.3-3, difficulty 2]**  
While maneuvering an airplane, what should the pilot focus on rather than the amount of control surface displacement?

- ✅ **The application of flight control pressures that give the desired result**
- The physical position of the control surface relative to its streamlined position
- The rate at which the control surface returns to neutral after input
- The total range of travel available for the control surface being used

  > The chapter states that resistance to movement increases with airspeed and as controls move from streamlined position, but the pilot should focus on control pressures, not displacement. 'It is not the amount of control surface displacement the pilot needs to consider, but rather the application of flight control pressures that give the desired result.'

**3.2 [Feel of the Airplane, p.3-3, difficulty 2]**  
What is the risk of relying solely on kinesthetic sense without visual information when flying?

- ✅ **It ultimately leads to disorientation and loss of aircraft control**
- It can only detect changes in airspeed and cannot sense changes in direction
- It is only reliable during instrument meteorological conditions with no outside references
- It requires constant engine sound feedback to function properly

  > The chapter explains that kinesthesis can provide critical information about changes in direction and speed, but has limits when relied upon solely without visual information. 'Sole reliance on the kinesthetic sense ultimately leads to disorientation and loss of aircraft control.'

**3.3 [Attitude Flying, p.3-4, difficulty 1]**  
How is an airplane's pitch attitude defined?

- ✅ **The angle formed between the airplane's longitudinal axis and the natural horizon**
- The angle formed between the airplane's lateral axis and the natural horizon
- The angle formed between the airplane's vertical axis and the natural horizon
- The angle formed between the airplane's flight path and the natural horizon

  > The chapter defines pitch attitude as the angle between the longitudinal axis (nose to tail) and the natural horizon. Bank attitude is the angle between the lateral axis (wingtip to wingtip) and the horizon. Yaw is relative to direction of flight, not the natural horizon.

**3.4 [Integrated Flight Instruction, p.3-5, difficulty 1]**  
When using integrated flight instruction, approximately what percentage of the pilot's attention should be devoted to outside visual references and scanning for traffic?

- ✅ **90 percent**
- 50 percent
- 75 percent
- 100 percent

  > The chapter specifies that approximately 90 percent of attention should be outside, with no more than about 10 percent inside the flight deck. The 100 percent distractor is wrong because instruments must be checked to validate performance.

**3.5 [Straight-and-Level Flight, p.3-8, difficulty 1]**  
What is the correct method for maintaining straight flight when minor bank corrections are needed?

- ✅ **The coordinated use of ailerons and rudder**
- Using ailerons independently to level the wings
- Using rudder independently to maintain heading
- Alternating between aileron and rudder inputs as needed

  > The chapter states that while it is possible to maintain straight flight using ailerons or rudder independently, 'the practice of using the ailerons and rudder independently is not correct and makes precise control of the airplane difficult.' The correct method is coordinated use of both.

**3.6 [Trim Control, p.3-10, difficulty 2]**  
In an airplane equipped with multiple trim axes, what is the correct sequence for trimming once a constant airspeed and pitch attitude are established?

- ✅ **Rudder first, then elevator, then aileron**
- Elevator first, then rudder, then aileron
- Aileron first, then rudder, then elevator
- All three axes should be trimmed simultaneously

  > The chapter specifies that the rudder should be trimmed first, then elevator, then aileron. The pilot should hold wings level with aileron pressure while rudder pressure is trimmed out, and finally aileron trim is adjusted to relieve aileron pressure.

**3.7 [Level Turns, p.3-12, difficulty 1]**  
What causes an airplane to begin turning when banked?

- ✅ **The horizontal component of lift**
- The rudder deflecting airflow to one side
- The vertical component of lift acting perpendicular to the earth
- Increased thrust directed toward the banked direction

  > The chapter clearly states that when an airplane is banked, total lift divides into vertical and horizontal components. 'It is the horizontal lift component that begins to turn the airplane and not the rudder.' The rudder's purpose is to coordinate the turn by offsetting adverse yaw.

**3.8 [Descents and Descending Turns, p.3-21, difficulty 1]**  
What is the recommended target descent rate for a partial power (cruise or en route) descent?

- ✅ **500 fpm**
- 1,000 fpm
- 300 fpm
- 1,500 fpm

  > The chapter describes partial power descent as the normal method of losing altitude and states: 'The target descent rate should be 500 fpm.' The 1,000 fpm figure appears in level-off examples, not as a target descent rate.

**3.9 [Level Turns, p.3-14, difficulty 2]**  
At a given angle of bank, what is the effect of higher airspeed on the radius of turn?

- ✅ **The radius of turn becomes larger because the airplane turns at a slower rate**
- The radius of turn becomes smaller because the horizontal lift component is greater
- The radius of turn remains the same because bank angle is the only determining factor
- The radius of turn becomes larger because the airplane turns at a faster rate

  > The chapter explains that at higher airspeed, inertia is greater and more horizontal lift is required, causing the turning rate to become slower. 'At a given angle of bank, a higher airspeed makes the radius of turn larger because the airplane turns at a slower rate.'

**3.10 [Level Turns, p.3-16, difficulty 1]**  
What is the rule of thumb for leading the rollout from a turn to arrive at the desired heading?

- ✅ **Lead by one-half the angle of bank**
- Lead by the full angle of bank
- Lead by one-third the angle of bank
- Lead by 10 degrees regardless of bank angle

  > The chapter provides a specific rule of thumb: 'A rule of thumb is to lead by one-half the angle of bank. For example, if the bank is 30°, lead the rollout by 15°.' The airplane continues turning as long as there is any bank.

**3.11 [Climbs and Climbing Turns, p.3-17, difficulty 1]**  
Which climb airspeed produces the most altitude gained over a given amount of time?

- ✅ **Best rate of climb (VY)**
- Best angle of climb (VX)
- Normal climb (cruise climb) speed
- Minimum sink speed

  > The chapter defines VY as producing 'the most altitude gained over a given amount of time.' VX produces the most altitude over a given horizontal distance. Normal climb is generally higher than VY and provides better cooling and visibility but not the best rate.

**3.12 [Climbs and Climbing Turns, p.3-17, difficulty 2]**  
As altitude increases, what happens to the airspeeds for best angle of climb (VX) and best rate of climb (VY)?

- ✅ **VX increases and VY decreases until they intersect at the absolute ceiling**
- Both VX and VY increase with altitude
- Both VX and VY decrease with altitude
- VX decreases and VY increases with altitude

  > The chapter states that as altitude increases, VX increases and VY decreases. They eventually intersect at the absolute ceiling, where the airplane is incapable of climbing any higher.

**3.13 [Climbs and Climbing Turns, p.3-20, difficulty 2]**  
Using the chapter's guidance, when should a pilot begin the level-off from a climb with a rate of climb of 500 fpm?

- ✅ **50 feet prior to reaching the desired altitude**
- At the desired altitude
- 100 feet prior to reaching the desired altitude
- 500 feet prior to reaching the desired altitude

  > The chapter states that level-off should begin at approximately 10 percent of the rate of climb. For 500 fpm, that is 50 feet. Beginning at the desired altitude is too late, and 100 or 500 feet are incorrect calculations.

**3.14 [Climbs and Climbing Turns, p.3-20, difficulty 2]**  
Why can the same pitch attitude and airspeed not be maintained in a climbing turn as in a straight climb at a constant power setting?

- ✅ **The total lift required increases because some lift is being used to turn the airplane**
- The increased drag from the turn requires a lower pitch attitude to maintain airspeed
- The rudder input reduces the effectiveness of the elevator during the turn
- The bank angle reduces stall speed, allowing a lower airspeed

  > The chapter explains that in a climbing turn, the same pitch attitude and airspeed cannot be maintained 'due to the increase in the total lift required.' Some lift is diverted to turn the airplane, so the airplane climbs at a slightly shallower angle.

**3.15 [Glides, p.3-22, difficulty 2]**  
At what aerodynamic condition does the best glide airspeed occur?

- ✅ **At the highest lift-to-drag ratio (L/DMAX)**
- At the minimum sink speed
- At 1.3 VSO
- At the airspeed for best rate of climb

  > The chapter states that the best glide airspeed occurs at the highest L/D ratio. Minimum sink speed is a different airspeed that maximizes time in flight, not distance. 1.3 VSO relates to minimum safe airspeed descent, not best glide.

**3.16 [Glides, p.3-23, difficulty 3]**  
How does airplane weight affect the glide distance if the pilot uses the proper airspeed for that weight?

- ✅ **Weight does not affect the distance flown, but a heavier airplane flies at a higher airspeed to achieve the same glide ratio**
- A heavier airplane glides less distance because it descends faster
- A heavier airplane glides more distance because it has more momentum
- A heavier airplane has a steeper glide angle regardless of the airspeed used

  > The chapter explains that since the L/D ratio determines glide distance, weight does not affect distance flown if the proper airspeed is used. However, a heavier airplane needs a higher airspeed to obtain the same glide ratio and arrives at the same point in less time.

**3.17 [Glides, p.3-23, difficulty 2]**  
Why should a pilot not attempt to 'stretch' a glide by applying back-elevator pressure?

- ✅ **It likely leads to landing short and may cause loss of control if the airplane stalls**
- It increases parasite drag and reduces the glide ratio
- It temporarily increases lift, allowing the airplane to reach the runway
- It raises the nose, which increases the glide angle and extends the flight path

  > The chapter identifies stretching a glide as a cardinal rule violation: 'attempting to apply elevator back pressure to stretch a glide back to the runway is likely to lead the airplane landing short and may even lead to loss of control if the airplane stalls.' The purpose of pitch control during a glide is to maintain maximum L/D.

**3.18 [Gliding Turns, p.3-23, difficulty 3]**  
Which three elements tend to force the nose down and increase glide speed during gliding turns?

- ✅ **Decrease in lift due to lifting force direction, excessive rudder inputs from reduced control pressures, and inherent nose-down tendency with power off**
- Increase in lift, adverse yaw from aileron deflection, and P-factor from the propeller
- Loss of thrust, increased induced drag, and forward trim forces
- Adverse yaw, engine torque, and spiral instability from the bank

  > The chapter lists three specific elements: (1) decrease in lift due to the direction of the lifting force, (2) excessive rudder inputs as a result of reduced flight control pressures, and (3) the normal stability and inherent characteristics of the airplane to nose-down with power off. These require more back pressure than a straight glide or level turn.


## Chapter 4 — 19 questions

**4.1 [Viewing the Airplane as an Energy System, p.4-1, difficulty 1]**  
The total mechanical energy of an airplane in flight is the sum of which two forms of energy?

- ✅ **Potential energy from altitude and kinetic energy from airspeed**
- Potential energy from airspeed and kinetic energy from altitude
- Thrust energy from the engine and drag energy from the airframe
- Chemical energy from fuel and thermal energy from combustion

  > The chapter defines total mechanical energy as the sum of potential energy (mgh, from altitude) and kinetic energy (½mV², from airspeed). Potential energy is tied to altitude, and kinetic energy is tied to airspeed.

**4.2 [Viewing the Airplane as an Energy System, p.4-1, difficulty 1]**  
The handbook describes a flying airplane as an "open" energy system. What does this mean?

- ✅ **The airplane can gain energy from a source such as fuel and lose energy to the environment such as the surrounding air**
- The airplane's energy state is constantly changing and cannot be controlled by the pilot
- The energy system is open to inspection by maintenance personnel during flight
- The airplane can only gain energy but cannot lose it during normal flight

  > An open energy system means energy can be added (e.g., from fuel via thrust) or removed (e.g., to the surrounding air via drag). The pilot can add or remove energy from the total mechanical energy stored as altitude and airspeed.

**4.3 [A Frame of Reference for Managing Energy State, p.4-1, difficulty 2]**  
When managing the airplane's energy state, what is the pilot's frame of reference for altitude and airspeed?

- ✅ **Indicated altitude (MSL) and indicated airspeed, not height above ground or groundspeed**
- Height above ground level (AGL) and groundspeed, since terrain and wind are the primary hazards
- True altitude and true airspeed, since these reflect the actual energy available
- Density altitude and calibrated airspeed, since these account for non-standard atmospheric conditions

  > The chapter explicitly states that the pilot's frame of reference is airplane-centric, based on indicated altitude (MSL) and indicated airspeed. AGL altitude and groundspeed are affected by external factors like terrain and wind, which the pilot cannot directly alter.

**4.4 [Viewing the Airplane as an Energy System, p.4-2, difficulty 2]**  
When thrust exceeds drag (T – D > 0), what can the pilot do with the surplus energy?

- ✅ **Store the surplus energy as increased altitude or airspeed, or a combination of both**
- The surplus energy is automatically lost to the surrounding air and cannot be used
- The surplus energy can only be stored as altitude, not airspeed
- The pilot must immediately reduce throttle to restore the energy balance

  > When T – D > 0, total mechanical energy increases. The pilot can store the surplus as altitude (climb at constant airspeed), airspeed (accelerate in level flight), or a combination. The elevator determines how the surplus is distributed.

**4.5 [Viewing the Airplane as an Energy System, p.4-2, difficulty 2]**  
During a pure energy exchange between altitude and airspeed (with no other energy inputs), how do altitude and airspeed change relative to each other?

- ✅ **They always change in opposite directions—as one goes up, the other comes down**
- They always change in the same direction—both increase or both decrease together
- Altitude changes first, then airspeed follows after a brief delay
- Only airspeed changes while altitude remains fixed during an energy exchange

  > The chapter states that during energy exchange, altitude and airspeed always change in opposite directions. For example, trading airspeed for altitude means altitude increases while airspeed decreases. The total mechanical energy can remain the same at the end of the exchange.

**4.6 [Primary Energy Role of the Throttle and Elevator, p.4-4, difficulty 2]**  
From an energy management perspective, what is the primary role of the throttle?

- ✅ **It regulates changes in total mechanical energy by increasing or decreasing engine thrust against drag**
- It independently controls altitude while the elevator independently controls airspeed
- It exchanges energy between altitude and airspeed by adjusting pitch attitude
- It controls the distribution of energy between potential and kinetic energy

  > The throttle is the total energy controller. By changing thrust relative to drag, it determines whether total mechanical energy increases, decreases, or remains the same. The elevator, not the throttle, handles energy distribution.

**4.7 [Primary Energy Role of the Throttle and Elevator, p.4-4, difficulty 2]**  
From an energy management perspective, what is the primary role of the elevator?

- ✅ **It acts as an energy exchanger and distribution device, allocating changes in total energy between vertical flight path and airspeed**
- It regulates total mechanical energy by adjusting engine thrust to match drag
- It independently controls airspeed without any effect on altitude
- It increases total drag to help the airplane slow down in all flight conditions

  > The elevator is the energy distribution controller. Its primary job is to allocate changes in total energy between altitude and airspeed by adjusting pitch attitude. The throttle, not the elevator, controls total energy.

**4.8 [Managing Energy is a Balancing Act, p.4-3, difficulty 3]**  
According to the energy balance equation, how do the two sides of the equation relate to each other during flight?

- ✅ **The net energy flow from thrust minus drag always matches the change in energy redistributed over altitude and airspeed, regardless of flight condition**
- The two sides only balance when the airplane is in steady, level flight at constant airspeed
- The left side represents potential energy and the right side represents kinetic energy, and they must be equal
- The equation only applies during climbs and descents, not during level acceleration or deceleration

  > The energy balance equation is inexorably balanced at all times. The left side (net energy flow, a function of T – D) always matches the right side (change in energy stored as altitude and airspeed), whether the airplane is climbing, descending, accelerating, decelerating, or maintaining constant altitude and airspeed.

**4.9 [Additional Role for the Elevator, p.4-5, difficulty 3]**  
On the backside of the power required curve, what happens when the pilot pushes forward on the yoke/stick (elevator down)?

- ✅ **A brief energy exchange descent causes acceleration, which reduces total drag and can create positive excess power**
- The airplane immediately stalls because the angle of attack decreases too rapidly
- Total energy increases immediately without any change in airspeed or altitude
- The airplane climbs because lower angle of attack always produces more lift

  > On the backside of the power curve, pushing forward causes a brief energy exchange descent that accelerates the airplane toward the center of the power curve. This increased airspeed reduces total drag, potentially creating positive excess power (PS > 0) where thrust exceeds drag, enabling a climb or turn.

**4.10 [Three Basic Rules of Energy Control, p.4-10, difficulty 2]**  
According to the energy control rules, what control inputs are needed to transition to a higher altitude at a constant airspeed?

- ✅ **Increase throttle and use up-elevator to stay on speed**
- Decrease throttle and use up-elevator to trade speed for altitude
- Increase throttle and use down-elevator to stay on path
- Keep throttle the same and use up-elevator to exchange speed for altitude

  > Rule #1 applies: moving to a higher energy state requires increased throttle (thrust greater than drag). To climb at constant speed, the pilot uses up-elevator to distribute the energy gain into altitude rather than airspeed. This is a total energy increase with distribution favoring altitude.

**4.11 [Three Basic Rules of Energy Control, p.4-10, difficulty 2]**  
According to the energy control rules, what control inputs are needed to transition to a faster airspeed at a constant altitude?

- ✅ **Increase throttle and use gradual down-elevator to stay on path**
- Decrease throttle and use up-elevator to maintain altitude
- Keep throttle the same and use down-elevator to trade altitude for speed
- Increase throttle and use up-elevator to maintain altitude

  > Rule #1 applies: more total energy is needed. Increased throttle provides the energy, and gradual down-elevator distributes it into airspeed rather than altitude, keeping the airplane on path while it accelerates. Re-trimming is needed as airspeed changes.

**4.12 [Three Basic Rules of Energy Control, p.4-10, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot wants to trade speed for altitude without initially changing the throttle setting. Which energy control rule applies?

- ✅ **Rule #3: No change in total energy—use the elevator to exchange energy between altitude and airspeed**
- Rule #1: Increase total energy—advance throttle and pitch up
- Rule #2: Decrease total energy—reduce throttle and pitch up
- No rule applies; trading speed for altitude always requires a throttle adjustment

  > Rule #3 covers energy exchange with no change in total energy. The pilot does not change throttle initially but uses pitch up to trade kinetic energy (airspeed) for potential energy (altitude). After the exchange, throttle is adjusted to match drag at the new speed.

**4.13 [Managing Energy Errors, p.4-12, difficulty 2]**  
How can a pilot distinguish between a total energy error and an energy distribution error?

- ✅ **In a total energy error, altitude and airspeed deviate in the same direction; in a distribution error, they deviate in opposite directions**
- A total energy error only occurs during climbs, while a distribution error only occurs during descents
- A total energy error affects airspeed only, while a distribution error affects altitude only
- A total energy error is always caused by throttle mismanagement, while a distribution error is always caused by trim failure

  > The chapter explains that in total energy errors, the pilot notices altitude and speed deviate in the same direction ('lower-and-slower' or 'higher-and-faster'). In energy distribution errors, the total energy is correct but distribution is wrong, so altitude and speed deviate in opposite directions ('higher-and-slower' or 'lower-and-faster').

**4.14 [Managing Energy Errors, p.4-12, difficulty 2]**  
An airplane on final approach is below the desired glideslope and slower than the target airspeed. What type of energy error is this, and what is the proper correction?

- ✅ **Total energy error (too little energy)—advance the throttle to regain total energy**
- Energy distribution error—pull back on the yoke/stick to exchange speed for altitude
- Total energy error (too much energy)—reduce throttle and pitch down
- Combination error—reduce throttle while pulling back on the yoke/stick

  > Lower-and-slower means altitude and airspeed deviate in the same direction, indicating a total energy error with too little energy. The correction is to advance the throttle to increase total energy. However, this only works if excess thrust is available (PS > 0).

**4.15 [Managing Energy Errors, p.4-12, difficulty 3]**  
An airplane on final approach is below the desired glideslope but faster than the target airspeed. What type of energy error is this, and what is the proper correction?

- ✅ **Energy distribution error—pull back on the yoke/stick to null the distribution error**
- Total energy error (too little energy)—advance the throttle to increase total energy
- Total energy error (too much energy)—reduce throttle and accept the lower altitude
- Combination error—advance throttle while pushing forward on the yoke/stick

  > Lower-and-faster means altitude and airspeed deviate in opposite directions, indicating an energy distribution error. The total energy is approximately correct but poorly distributed. The correction is to pull back on the yoke/stick to exchange speed for altitude, redistributing the energy correctly.

**4.16 [Managing Energy Errors, p.4-13, difficulty 3]**  
An airplane on final approach has descended below the desired flight path but is flying at the correct airspeed. What control inputs are needed to correct this?

- ✅ **Advance the throttle forward while easing aft on the yoke/stick simultaneously**
- Advance the throttle only, since airspeed is already correct
- Pull back on the yoke/stick only, since the problem is purely an altitude deviation
- Reduce throttle and pitch down to regain the glideslope from below

  > When below the desired path but at the correct speed, there is a combination of total energy and distribution errors. Regaining altitude without changing speed requires decoupling altitude and airspeed, which demands the use of both controls simultaneously—advancing the throttle for more total energy while easing aft on the yoke/stick to direct that energy into altitude.

**4.17 [Visualizing the Airplane's Ability to "Move" Between Energy States, p.4-8, difficulty 3]**  
What does "energy height" (total specific energy) represent in the energy map?

- ✅ **The maximum height an airplane would reach from its current altitude if it were to trade all its speed for altitude**
- The airplane's current altitude above mean sea level at any given moment
- The altitude at which the airplane has zero specific excess power available
- The height above ground level at which the airplane can safely maintain level flight

  > Energy height (ES) is the total specific energy—potential plus kinetic energy divided by weight—expressed in units of height. It represents the maximum altitude the airplane would reach if it traded all its kinetic energy (airspeed) for potential energy (altitude). Points along the same energy height line have the same total energy.

**4.18 [Preventing Irreversible Deceleration and/or Sink Rate, p.4-16, difficulty 3]**  
When confronted with rising terrain and the airplane has slowed to an airspeed where specific excess power (PS) is negative and the stall horn is sounding, what should the pilot do to recover?

- ✅ **Apply full throttle and push forward on the yoke/stick to trade altitude for speed until reaching an airspeed where PS > 0, then climb**
- Pitch up further to gain altitude while accepting additional airspeed loss, since altitude is needed to clear terrain
- Apply full throttle and maintain the current pitch attitude until the airplane begins climbing
- Extend flaps to increase lift and improve climb performance at the current airspeed

  > The recovery requires doing something counterintuitive: applying full throttle and trading altitude for speed by pushing forward on the yoke/stick. This reduces AOA and induced drag, allowing acceleration to an airspeed where PS > 0. Only then can the airplane climb. If the pilot pitches up instead, the airplane goes deeper into the region where PS < 0, making recovery impossible.

**4.19 [Preventing Irreversible Deceleration and/or Sink Rate, p.4-14, difficulty 2]**  
What defines an irreversible deceleration and/or sink rate?

- ✅ **Unrecoverable depletion of mechanical energy from continuous airspeed and/or altitude loss coupled with insufficient excess power**
- Any situation where the stall horn sounds during normal flight operations
- A temporary airspeed deviation that can be corrected by increasing throttle alone
- An engine failure at low altitude that results in a forced landing

  > Irreversible deceleration and/or sink rate occurs when there is continuous loss of airspeed and/or altitude coupled with insufficient excess power. If the pilot fails to recover above a certain critical altitude, the airplane will hit the ground regardless of what the pilot does. This can happen even without stalling—the airplane can be unstalled and still experience unrecoverable sink rates.


## Chapter 5 — 18 questions

**5.1 [Defining an Airplane Upset, p.5-1, difficulty 1]**  
The FAA considers an upset to be an event that unintentionally exceeds certain parameters. Which of the following situations would NOT meet the FAA's upset parameters?

- ✅ **Bank angle of 35°**
- Pitch attitude greater than 25° nose up
- Pitch attitude greater than 10° nose down
- Flying at airspeeds inappropriate for the conditions

  > The FAA defines an upset as an event that unintentionally exceeds parameters including pitch greater than 25° nose up, pitch greater than 10° nose down, bank angle greater than 45°, or inappropriate airspeeds. A 35° bank does not exceed the 45° bank threshold.

**5.2 [Unusual Attitudes Versus Upsets, p.5-2, difficulty 2]**  
Which statement correctly distinguishes an upset from an unusual attitude?

- ✅ **An upset has defined parameters, while an unusual attitude does not necessarily meet those parameters**
- An unusual attitude includes stall events, but an upset does not
- An unusual attitude is always intentional, while an upset is always unintentional
- An upset only occurs in IMC, while an unusual attitude can occur in VMC

  > The chapter states that an upset includes stall events, has defined parameters, and centers on unintentional situations. A 30° bank with 15° nose-up pitch would be an unusual attitude but not meet upset parameters. Both unusual attitudes and upsets can occur in either VMC or IMC.

**5.3 [Environmental Factors, p.5-2, difficulty 1]**  
How does airframe icing contribute to the risk of an upset?

- ✅ **It destroys the smooth flow of air over the airfoil, increasing drag and decreasing lift**
- It increases the critical angle of attack of the wing
- It primarily affects the engine, causing power loss that leads to a stall
- It reduces stall speed by smoothing the airflow over the wing

  > The chapter states that icing can destroy the smooth flow of air over the airfoil and increase drag while decreasing the ability of the airfoil to create lift, which can significantly degrade airplane performance and result in a stall.

**5.4 [Mechanical Factors, p.5-2, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, why should a pilot disengage the autopilot and autothrottles when an in-flight anomaly occurs?

- ✅ **Disengaging allows the pilot to directly control the airplane and possibly eliminate the cause of the problem**
- The autopilot will automatically recover from any upset if left engaged
- Advanced automation always identifies and corrects the cause of the anomaly without pilot input
- The autopilot must be disengaged before the pilot can communicate with ATC

  > The chapter explains that advanced automation may mask the cause of an anomaly, and disengaging the autopilot and autothrottles allows the pilot to directly control the airplane and possibly eliminate the cause of the problem. The pilot should maintain proficiency to manually fly in all conditions.

**5.5 [Human Factors — Spatial Disorientation, p.5-3, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot who is unaware that an upset event is developing and fails to take any corrective action to prevent LOC-I is experiencing which type of spatial disorientation?

- ✅ **Unrecognized spatial disorientation**
- Recognized spatial disorientation
- Incapacitating spatial disorientation
- Reversible spatial disorientation

  > The chapter defines three types of spatial disorientation. In unrecognized spatial disorientation, the pilot is unaware that an upset event is developing or has occurred and fails to make essential decisions or take corrective action. 'Reversible' is not one of the defined types.

**5.6 [Surprise and Startle Response, p.5-3, difficulty 2]**  
What is the key difference between surprise and startle as described in the chapter?

- ✅ **Surprise is an unexpected event that violates expectations, while startle is an uncontrollable automatic muscle reflex**
- Surprise is an uncontrollable muscle reflex, while startle is an unexpected event that violates expectations
- Surprise only occurs in IMC, while startle only occurs in VMC
- Surprise and startle are the same physiological response with different names

  > The chapter explicitly distinguishes the two: surprise is an unexpected event that violates a pilot's expectations and can affect mental processes, while startle is an uncontrollable, automatic muscle reflex with raised heart rate and blood pressure elicited by a sudden, intense event.

**5.7 [UPRT Core Concepts, p.5-5, difficulty 2]**  
How does UPRT differ from aerobatic training?

- ✅ **UPRT focuses on helping pilots overcome sudden onsets of stress to avoid, prevent, and recover from unplanned excursions**
- Aerobatic training focuses on unplanned excursions, while UPRT focuses on intentional maneuvers
- Aerobatic training incorporates startle and surprise, while UPRT does not
- UPRT requires an aerobatic-capable airplane, while aerobatic training does not

  > The chapter states that in aerobatic training, the pilot knows and expects the maneuver, so effects of startle or surprise are missing. The primary goal of UPRT is to help pilots overcome sudden onsets of stress to avoid, prevent, and recover from unplanned excursions that could lead to LOC-I.

**5.8 [Prevention Through Proportional Counter-Response, p.5-6, difficulty 2]**  
What is the typical time scale of proportional counter-response in UPRT prevention?

- ✅ **Seconds or fractions of seconds**
- Minutes or hours
- Several minutes
- It is measured in the number of flight hours remaining

  > The chapter distinguishes prevention through ADM and risk management (minutes or hours) from proportional counter-response, which typically occurs on the order of seconds or fractions of seconds, with the goal of recognizing a developing upset and taking proportionally-appropriate avoidance actions.

**5.9 [Angle of Attack, p.5-9, difficulty 2]**  
In a coordinated, level 60° banked turn with a load factor of 2G, approximately how much higher than the 1G stall speed will the airplane stall?

- ✅ **41 percent higher than the 1G stall speed**
- The same speed as the 1G stall speed
- 20 percent higher than the 1G stall speed
- 100 percent higher than the 1G stall speed

  > The chapter explains that increased load factor directly affects stall speed. In a level 60° banked turn, the load factor is 2G, and the airplane will stall at a speed 41 percent higher than the 1G stall speed because the pilot must increase AOA to generate the lift required to maintain altitude.

**5.10 [Slow Flight, p.5-10, difficulty 2]**  
When flying below L/DMAX (on the backside of the power curve), which statement correctly describes the relationship between pitch and power?

- ✅ **Pitch becomes a more effective control of airspeed, and power is an effective control of the flight path**
- Power becomes the primary control of airspeed, and pitch controls altitude
- Rudder becomes the primary control of airspeed on the backside of the power curve
- Pitch and power have equal and interchangeable effects on both airspeed and altitude

  > The chapter explains that below L/DMAX, small changes in pitch result in disproportionally large changes in induced drag and airspeed, making pitch a more effective control of airspeed, while power becomes an effective control of the flight path.

**5.11 [Slow Flight, p.5-9, difficulty 1]**  
For pilot training and testing purposes, what is a good target airspeed for maneuvering in slow flight?

- ✅ **5 to 10 knots above the 1G stall speed**
- 1 to 2 knots above the 1G stall speed
- 15 to 20 knots above the 1G stall speed
- Exactly at the 1G stall speed

  > The chapter specifies that slow flight includes slowing to, maneuvering at, and recovering from an airspeed where the airplane is still capable of maintaining controlled flight without activating the stall warning, and that 5 to 10 knots above the 1G stall speed is a good target.

**5.12 [Stall Recognition, p.5-13, difficulty 2]**  
Which of the following is identified in the chapter as NOT being a foolproof indicator of an impending stall?

- ✅ **Vision, since the airplane can be stalled in any attitude**
- Hearing, as the sound of airflow changes with decreasing speed
- Kinesthesia, the physical sensation of changes in direction or speed
- Feel, as control pressures change with decreasing speed

  > The chapter lists several sensory cues for stall recognition including feel, vision, hearing, and kinesthesia. However, it specifically notes that vision is not a foolproof indicator because the airplane can be stalled in any attitude, though maintaining pitch awareness is still important.

**5.13 [Angle of Attack Indicators, p.5-13, difficulty 2]**  
Which of the following is identified as a factor that may limit the effectiveness of an AOA indicator?

- ✅ **Wing contamination**
- Excessive use of rudder during turns
- Flying at altitudes above 10,000 feet
- Using trim during slow flight

  > The chapter lists several items that may limit the effectiveness of an AOA indicator, including calibration techniques, probes or vanes not being heated, the type of indicator itself, flap setting, and wing contamination. Rudder use, altitude, and trim are not listed as limiting factors.

**5.14 [Fundamentals of Stall Recovery, p.5-15, difficulty 1]**  
According to the stall recovery template, what is the most important action when recovering from an impending stall or a full stall?

- ✅ **Reduce the angle of attack**
- Add full power immediately
- Roll the wings level before any other action
- Retract speed brakes or spoilers first

  > The chapter emphasizes that the most important action to an impending stall or a full stall is to reduce the AOA. It notes that there have been numerous situations where pilots did not first reduce AOA and instead prioritized power and maintaining altitude, which resulted in a loss of control.

**5.15 [Fundamentals of Stall Recovery, p.5-16, difficulty 2]**  
During stall recovery, why should the pilot reduce the AOA before attempting to roll the wings level?

- ✅ **Both roll stability and roll control will improve considerably after getting the wings flying again**
- Rolling the wings level first will always cause a spin
- The ailerons become inoperative once a stall warning activates
- Reducing AOA first is only necessary in multiengine airplanes

  > The chapter states that it is important not to be tempted to control the bank angle prior to reducing AOA, because both roll stability and roll control will improve considerably after getting the wings flying again. Using ailerons before reducing AOA can exacerbate the stalled condition.

**5.16 [Cross-Control Stall, p.5-20, difficulty 3]**  
In which scenario is a skidding cross-control stall most likely to occur?

- ✅ **During a poorly executed base-to-final approach turn with excessive rudder in the direction of the turn**
- During a steep turn at cruise altitude with coordinated flight controls
- During a power-on stall practice in a clean configuration
- During an accelerated stall in a 45° bank with coordinated controls

  > The chapter describes that a skidding cross-control stall is most likely to occur in the traffic pattern during a poorly planned and executed base-to-final approach turn. The pilot overshoots the centerline and attempts to correct by increasing bank, adding back elevator pressure, and applying excess rudder in the direction of the turn.

**5.17 [Spin Procedures, p.5-25, difficulty 3]**  
According to the generic spin recovery procedure, when should the pilot apply positive, brisk, and straight-forward elevator?

- ✅ **Immediately after applying full opposite rudder, without waiting for rotation to stop**
- Before applying opposite rudder to initiate the recovery
- Only after the rotation has completely stopped
- Simultaneously with neutralizing the ailerons before rudder application

  > The chapter states that forward elevator should be applied immediately after full rudder application, and the pilot should not wait for the rotation to stop before performing this step. Waiting for rotation to stop is listed as a common error in spin recovery.

**5.18 [Spiral Dive, p.5-26, difficulty 3]**  
During spiral dive recovery, why is it important to apply forward elevator pressure before rolling the wings level?

- ✅ **To unload the wing, reduce G-load, and prevent a rolling pullout that could cause structural damage**
- To increase the G-load and tighten the spiral for a faster recovery
- To increase airspeed for better aileron effectiveness during the roll
- To reduce power before rolling the wings level

  > The chapter explains that applying forward elevator before rolling wings level unloads the G-load on the wing, reduces the AOA, slows the turn rate, increases turn radius, and prevents a rolling pullout. The design limit of the airplane is exceeded more easily during a rolling pullout, so failure to reduce G-load first could result in structural damage.


## Chapter 6 — 18 questions

**6.1 [Prior to Takeoff, p.6-1, difficulty 1]**  
What effect does high density altitude have on airplane performance during takeoff?

- ✅ **It reduces engine and propeller performance, increases takeoff roll, and decreases climb performance.**
- It improves climb performance but increases takeoff roll distance.
- It has no measurable effect on takeoff roll but reduces cruise speed.
- It increases propeller efficiency while decreasing engine power output.

  > The chapter explicitly states that high density altitudes reduce engine and propeller performance, increase takeoff rolls, and decrease climb performance, and directs pilots to consult performance charts before takeoff.

**6.2 [Normal Takeoff, p.6-3, difficulty 1]**  
What are the two primary reasons for making a takeoff as nearly into the wind as possible?

- ✅ **A headwind provides airspeed before acceleration begins and decreases the ground speed needed to reach flying speed.**
- A headwind reduces engine torque and improves directional control during the roll.
- A headwind increases propeller efficiency and reduces fuel consumption during climb.
- A headwind allows earlier rotation and reduces wear on the braking system.

  > The chapter identifies two reasons: the airplane depends on airspeed so a headwind provides some of that airspeed before the airplane begins to accelerate, and a headwind decreases the ground speed necessary to achieve flying speed, yielding shorter ground roll distances.

**6.3 [Normal Takeoff, p.6-3, difficulty 2]**  
During the takeoff roll, what does the feel of resistance to control movement indicate?

- ✅ **The degree of controllability attained, not the airplane's speed.**
- The exact airspeed of the airplane at that moment.
- The amount of engine torque being generated.
- The distance remaining on the runway.

  > The chapter is explicit that the feel of resistance is not a measure of the airplane's speed but rather of its controllability. The pilot should sense the control resistance and adjust pressures as needed to control the airplane.

**6.4 [Normal Takeoff, p.6-5, difficulty 2]**  
What is the likely result if the pilot forces the airplane into the air by applying excessive back-elevator pressure before adequate flying speed is attained?

- ✅ **The wing's AOA may become excessive, causing the airplane to settle back to the runway or even to stall.**
- The airplane will accelerate more rapidly due to reduced ground friction.
- The airplane will establish a steeper climb gradient but at a lower airspeed.
- The airplane will remain in ground effect until VY is reached.

  > The chapter warns that forcing the airplane off the ground prematurely may cause the AOA to become excessive, resulting in the airplane settling back or stalling. This is considered an unsafe practice.

**6.5 [Normal Takeoff, p.6-5, difficulty 2]**  
During a takeoff in strong, gusty wind, why should the pilot allow the airplane to stay on the ground longer to attain more speed before rotation?

- ✅ **A takeoff at normal speed may result in a lack of positive control or a stall if the airplane encounters a sudden lull in the gusty wind.**
- The extra speed reduces the effect of torque and P-factor during the initial climb.
- The longer ground roll allows the engine to reach operating temperature before climb.
- The additional speed is needed to overcome the increased parasite drag from the gusty headwind.

  > The chapter states that in strong, gusty wind, an extra margin of speed should be obtained before the airplane leaves the ground, because a takeoff at normal speed may result in loss of control or a stall when encountering a sudden lull in the wind.

**6.6 [Crosswind Takeoff, p.6-6, difficulty 1]**  
At the beginning of a crosswind takeoff roll, how should the pilot apply aileron control?

- ✅ **Apply full aileron pressure into the wind, decreasing as the airplane accelerates and the ailerons become more effective.**
- Apply full aileron pressure away from the wind to prevent the downwind wing from dragging.
- Keep ailerons neutral until lift-off, then apply aileron into the wind.
- Apply half aileron pressure into the wind and hold that amount constant throughout the roll.

  > The chapter specifies that the pilot should apply full aileron pressure into the wind while beginning the takeoff roll, and as the airplane accelerates and ailerons become effective, the pilot will feel an increase in pressure and can relax the input as needed.

**6.7 [Crosswind Takeoff, p.6-7, difficulty 2]**  
What is the danger of side-skipping during a crosswind takeoff?

- ✅ **It imposes severe side stresses on the landing gear and may result in structural failure.**
- It causes the airplane to weathervane excessively into the wind after lift-off.
- It increases induced drag and prevents the airplane from climbing out of ground effect.
- It results in the nose-wheel bearing excessive weight and potential wheel barrowing.

  > The chapter explains that side-skipping develops from a series of small bounces where the crosswind moves the airplane sideways, and this imposes severe side stresses on the landing gear that may result in structural failure.

**6.8 [Crosswind Takeoff, p.6-8, difficulty 2]**  
After becoming airborne in a crosswind, how should the pilot transition from the initial sideslip to the proper climb configuration?

- ✅ **Turn the nose into the wind to establish a crab, bring the wings level, and adjust rudder to maintain runway alignment.**
- Maintain the sideslip throughout the entire climb to keep the flightpath aligned with the runway.
- Level the wings and apply full rudder away from the wind to straighten the nose.
- Lower the nose and accelerate in ground effect before establishing any wind correction.

  > The chapter describes that as the aircraft establishes its climb, the nose should be turned into the wind to offset the crosswind, wings brought to level, and rudder input adjusted to maintain runway alignment, transitioning from a sideslip to a crab.

**6.9 [Ground Effect on Takeoff, p.6-10, difficulty 2]**  
What causes the reduction in induced drag when an airplane is operating in ground effect?

- ✅ **A reduction in upwash, downwash, and wingtip vortices.**
- An increase in static pressure around the wing increasing lift coefficient.
- A decrease in parasite drag due to ground proximity smoothing airflow.
- An increase in airspeed due to the propeller slipstream being compressed against the ground.

  > The chapter states that when the wing is under the influence of ground effect, there is a reduction in upwash, downwash, and wingtip vortices, and as a result of the reduced wingtip vortices, induced drag is reduced.

**6.10 [Ground Effect on Takeoff, p.6-10, difficulty 3]**  
What happens to the airplane's performance as it climbs out of the ground effect area?

- ✅ **The airplane requires an increase in AOA to maintain lift coefficient, experiences an increase in induced drag, and has a pitch-up tendency.**
- The airplane experiences a decrease in induced drag and requires less elevator travel to maintain pitch.
