- The airplane experiences a reduction in static source pressure and a corresponding decrease in indicated airspeed.
- The airplane requires less AOA to maintain lift and experiences a pitch-down tendency.

  > Per the FAA handbook, when climbing out of ground effect: the airplane requires an increase in AOA to maintain lift coefficient; induced drag and thrust required increase; and the airplane experiences a pitch-up tendency and requires less elevator travel because of an increase in downwash at the horizontal tail.

**6.11 [Ground Effect on Takeoff, p.6-11, difficulty 2]**  
Under certain conditions, an airplane may be able to lift off in ground effect below recommended airspeed but then be unable to climb out of ground effect. What factors contribute to this hazardous situation?

- ✅ **High density altitude, high temperature, and/or maximum gross weight.**
- Low density altitude, cold temperature, and minimum gross weight.
- Tailwind, low humidity, and a center of gravity aft of limits.
- Crosswind, low temperature, and a center of gravity forward of limits.

  > The chapter specifically warns that under conditions of high density altitude, high temperature, and/or maximum gross weight, the airplane may be able to lift off but will be unable to climb out of ground effect, and may not be able to clear obstructions.

**6.12 [Short-Field Takeoff and Maximum Performance Climb, p.6-12, difficulty 2]**  
During a short-field takeoff, what airspeed should the pilot maintain after lift-off until all obstacles are cleared?

- ✅ **VX, the best angle-of-climb speed.**
- VY, the best rate-of-climb speed, to gain altitude in the shortest time.
- The normal takeoff speed to ensure maximum acceleration.
- A speed slightly above VY to maintain a positive rate of climb with a margin for gusts.

  > The chapter states that after becoming airborne on a short-field takeoff, the pilot should maintain a wings-level climb at VX until all obstacles have been cleared, or until reaching at least 50 feet above the takeoff surface, then lower the pitch to VY.

**6.13 [Short-Field Takeoff and Maximum Performance Climb, p.6-12, difficulty 2]**  
If an airplane has a natural tendency to lift off well before reaching VX on a short-field takeoff, what does the chapter recommend?

- ✅ **Allow the airplane to lift off in ground effect, then reduce pitch attitude to level until accelerating to VX with the wheels just clear of the surface.**
- Hold the airplane on the ground with forward elevator pressure until VX is attained to ensure the shortest possible ground roll.
- Apply full flaps and reduce power to prevent premature lift-off.
- Abort the takeoff and reconfigure the airplane for a lower gross weight.

  > The chapter states that it may be necessary to allow the airplane to lift off in ground effect and then reduce pitch to level until VX is reached. This is preferable to forcing the airplane to remain on the ground, which puts excessive pressure on the nose-wheel and may result in wheel barrowing.

**6.14 [Soft/Rough-Field Takeoff and Climb, p.6-13, difficulty 2]**  
What is the primary technique for transferring the airplane's weight from the wheels to the wings during a soft-field takeoff?

- ✅ **Establish and maintain a relatively high AOA or nose-high pitch attitude as early as possible during the takeoff roll.**
- Apply full flaps and accelerate rapidly with the nose-wheel on the ground until reaching VY.
- Use minimum power and a nose-low attitude to reduce drag from the soft surface.
- Hold forward elevator pressure to keep the nose-wheel firmly on the ground until lift-off speed is reached.

  > The chapter states that to minimize hazards on soft or rough fields, the pilot should transfer the support of the airplane's weight as rapidly as possible from the wheels to the wings by establishing and maintaining a relatively high AOA or nose-high pitch attitude as early as possible.

**6.15 [Soft/Rough-Field Takeoff and Climb, p.6-13, difficulty 2]**  
After lift-off from a soft field, why is it essential to remain in ground effect until at least VX is reached?

- ✅ **Because while transitioning out of ground effect, the airplane will have a tendency to settle back onto the surface even with full power applied.**
- Because ground effect increases lift coefficient and allows the airplane to climb at a steeper angle.
- Because remaining in ground effect reduces engine wear during high-power operations.
- Because the soft surface causes the landing gear to retract improperly if climbed out too quickly.

  > The chapter warns that immediately after becoming airborne and while it accelerates, the airplane will have a tendency to settle back onto the surface while transitioning out of ground effect, even with full power applied. Therefore, it is essential to remain in ground effect until at least VX is reached.

**6.16 [Rejected Takeoff/Engine Failure, p.6-14, difficulty 1]**  
What should a pilot do prior to takeoff to establish a decision point for a potential rejected takeoff?

- ✅ **Identify a point along the runway at which the airplane should be airborne, and if that point is reached without becoming airborne, discontinue the takeoff.**
- Calculate the total runway length and plan to reject only if less than half the runway remains at rotation speed.
- Note the halfway point of the runway and plan to reject if the airplane has not reached rotation speed by that point.
- Estimate the stopping distance from VY and mark that distance from the departure end of the runway.

  > The chapter states that prior to takeoff, the pilot should identify a point along the runway at which the airplane should be airborne. If that point is reached and the airplane is not airborne, immediate action should be taken to discontinue the takeoff.

**6.17 [Rejected Takeoff/Engine Failure, p.6-14, difficulty 3]**  
In the event of an engine failure on initial climb-out, what is the pilot's first responsibility?

- ✅ **Lower the nose to prevent a stall while moving the rudder to ensure coordinated flight.**
- Immediately turn back toward the takeoff runway to minimize time without power.
- Apply full flaps and pitch for the best glide speed while extending the landing gear.
- Transmit a distress call on the CTAF or tower frequency before making any control inputs.

  > The chapter states that at a climb pitch attitude without power, the airplane is at or near a stalling AOA, and the pilot may still be holding right rudder. The first responsibility is to maintain aircraft control by lowering the nose to prevent a stall while coordinating rudder.

**6.18 [Prior to Takeoff, p.6-1, difficulty 2]**  
If an immediate takeoff behind a large, heavy transport airplane is necessary, what should the pilot do to minimize the risk of encountering wake turbulence?

- ✅ **Avoid the preceding aircraft's flightpath or rotate prior to the point at which the preceding aircraft rotated.**
- Wait at least 3 minutes on the runway before beginning the takeoff roll in all cases.
- Climb at VY immediately after lift-off to pass above the preceding aircraft's wake.
- Request a different runway or delay the takeoff until the wake turbulence has dissipated completely.

  > The chapter states that if an immediate takeoff behind a large heavy aircraft is necessary, the pilot should plan to minimize the chances of flying through the aircraft's wake turbulence by avoiding the other aircraft's flightpath or rotating prior to the point at which the preceding aircraft rotated.


## Chapter 7 — 18 questions

**7.1 [Maneuvering by Reference to Ground Objects, p.7-1, difficulty 2]**  
A beginning pilot fixates on a single ground reference during a ground reference maneuver. What is the primary problem with this technique?

- ✅ **The pilot loses the ability to determine rate, which significantly degrades performance**
- The pilot will be unable to maintain altitude because visual references are too close to the airplane
- The pilot will overcontrol the airplane because the reference appears to move too quickly
- The pilot will confuse the reference with a different landmark on the downwind leg

  > The chapter states that fixating on any one reference causes the pilot to lose the ability to determine rate, which significantly degrades performance. Pilots should scan between several visual references to determine relative motion and drift.

**7.2 [Maneuvering by Reference to Ground Objects, p.7-2, difficulty 2]**  
Before performing any ground reference maneuver, the pilot should clear the area using two 90° clearing turns. What should the pilot look for during these turns?

- ✅ **Left and right, as well as above and below the airplane**
- Only in the direction of the upcoming turn, since other aircraft are unlikely to approach from above
- Only below the airplane, since ground reference maneuvers are flown at low altitudes where the primary hazard is terrain
- Only to the left and right, since altitude separation makes scanning above and below unnecessary

  > The chapter explicitly states pilots should always clear the area with two 90° clearing turns looking to the left and the right, as well as above and below the airplane.

**7.3 [Maneuvering by Reference to Ground Objects, p.7-2, difficulty 2]**  
Ground reference maneuvers should generally be flown at what altitude range AGL, and what obstruction clearance should the pilot maintain?

- ✅ **600 to 1,000 feet AGL, with no less than 500 feet vertically and 2,000 feet horizontally from obstructions**
- 500 to 1,500 feet AGL, with no less than 1,000 feet vertically and 2,000 feet horizontally from obstructions
- 600 to 1,000 feet AGL, with no less than 1,000 feet vertically and 1,500 feet horizontally from obstructions
- 1,000 to 1,500 feet AGL, with no less than 500 feet vertically and 2,000 feet horizontally from obstructions

  > The chapter states ground reference maneuvers are generally flown at altitudes between 600 and 1,000 feet AGL, and the altitude should provide obstruction clearance of no less than 500 feet vertically above the obstruction and 2,000 feet horizontally.

**7.4 [Drift and Ground Track Control, p.7-2, difficulty 2]**  
An airplane has an airspeed of 100 knots and the wind is blowing directly from the side at 20 knots. Approximately what wind correction angle is needed to cancel the drift?

- ✅ **12 degrees into the wind**
- 6 degrees into the wind
- 20 degrees into the wind
- 45 degrees into the wind

  > The chapter gives this exact example: an airplane with an airspeed of 100 knots in an air mass moving at 20 knots directly from the side should turn 12° into the wind to cancel the airplane's drift. If the wind were only 10 knots, the correction would be 6 degrees.

**7.5 [Drift and Ground Track Control, p.7-3, difficulty 2]**  
A crosswind is blowing at 45° to the nose of the airplane (halfway between the side and the nose). Approximately what percentage of the total wind speed will cause sideways drift?

- ✅ **Just over 70 percent of the total wind speed**
- Approximately 50 percent of the total wind speed
- Approximately 45 percent of the total wind speed
- 100 percent of the total wind speed

  > The chapter states that when the wind is halfway between the side and the nose of the airplane (at a 45° angle), it causes a sideways drift up to just over 70 percent of the total speed of the wind.

**7.6 [Constant Radius During Turning Flight, p.7-3, difficulty 2]**  
When performing a constant-radius turn over the ground in wind, how should the pilot adjust bank angle as groundspeed changes?

- ✅ **Increase bank angle when groundspeed increases and decrease bank angle when groundspeed decreases**
- Maintain a constant bank angle throughout the turn and adjust airspeed instead
- Decrease bank angle when groundspeed increases to avoid overshooting the reference
- Increase bank angle when groundspeed decreases to maintain the same turn rate

  > The chapter explains that to maintain a constant-radius turn over the ground, the pilot compensates for changes in groundspeed by varying the bank angle. When groundspeed increases, the pilot banks more steeply; when groundspeed decreases, the pilot uses a shallower bank.

**7.7 [Constant Radius During Turning Flight, p.7-5, difficulty 3]**  
Why should ground reference maneuvers always be entered from a downwind position?

- ✅ **It allows the pilot to establish the steepest bank angle required to maintain a constant radius ground track**
- It allows the pilot to establish the shallowest bank angle, making the maneuver easier to control
- It ensures the airplane has the lowest groundspeed at the entry point, providing more time to assess the maneuver
- It positions the wind on the nose, which helps maintain a constant airspeed throughout the maneuver

  > The chapter states that ground reference maneuvers should always be entered from a downwind position because this allows the pilot to establish the steepest bank angle required to maintain a constant radius ground track. If the bank is too steep, the pilot should exit and re-establish further from the reference.

**7.8 [Rectangular Course, p.7-6, difficulty 2]**  
When flying a rectangular course, how should the turn from the downwind leg onto the base leg be executed, and why?

- ✅ **With a relatively steep bank angle because groundspeed is highest on the downwind leg**
- With a shallow bank angle because the wind will help push the airplane around the corner
- With a medium bank angle because the groundspeed is the same on all legs of the course
- With a steep bank angle only if the wind is directly on the nose during the turn

  > The chapter explains that the entry is downwind with increased groundspeed. The turn from the downwind leg onto the base leg is entered with a relatively steep bank angle. As the airplane turns onto base, the tailwind lessens and becomes a crosswind, so the bank angle is reduced gradually.

**7.9 [Rectangular Course, p.7-7, difficulty 3]**  
On the rectangular course, when the wind could drift the airplane into the course, how should the banking roll rate be adjusted?

- ✅ **The banking roll rate should be slow**
- The banking roll rate should be quick to counteract the drift
- The banking roll rate should be constant regardless of wind direction
- The banking roll rate should be quick only on the upwind leg

  > The chapter states that when the wind is from a direction that could drift the airplane into the course, the banking roll rate should be slow. When the wind is from a direction that could drift the airplane to the outside of the course, the banking roll rate should be quick.

**7.10 [Turns Around a Point, p.7-8, difficulty 2]**  
During the downwind half of a turn around a point, how should the pilot adjust the airplane's heading relative to its position over the ground?

- ✅ **The airplane's heading should be ahead of its position over the ground**
- The airplane's heading should be behind its position over the ground
- The airplane's heading should match its position over the ground exactly
- The airplane's heading should be perpendicular to its position over the ground

  > The chapter states that during the downwind half of the turn, the pilot should progressively adjust the airplane's heading toward the inside of the turn. The airplane's heading should be ahead of its position over the ground during the downwind half and behind its position during the upwind half.

**7.11 [S-Turns, p.7-9, difficulty 2]**  
When performing S-turns across a road, at what angle should the airplane cross the straight-line ground reference?

- ✅ **At a 90° angle**
- At a 45° angle
- At a 180° angle
- At whatever angle the wind dictates

  > The chapter states that with the airplane in the downwind position, the maneuver consists of crossing a straight-line ground reference at a 90° angle and immediately beginning a 180° constant-radius turn.

**7.12 [S-Turns, p.7-10, difficulty 3]**  
During S-turns, the airplane is exactly crosswind at the 90° point with a required 10° wind correction angle (left turn, crosswind from the left). How many degrees of heading change will the first 90° of ground track require?

- ✅ **100 degrees of heading change**
- 90 degrees of heading change
- 80 degrees of heading change
- 10 degrees of heading change

  > The chapter explains that the airplane would be at a heading 10° ahead when directly over the 90° ground reference point. The first 90° track over the ground results in a heading change of 100°, and the last 90° track over the ground results in 80° of heading change.

**7.13 [Eights Along a Road, p.7-11, difficulty 3]**  
When performing eights along a road with the wind perpendicular to the reference, what portion of the groundspeed change occurs during the first 2/3 of the turn from downwind to crosswind?

- ✅ **One-half of the reduction in groundspeed occurs during the first 2/3 of the turn**
- Two-thirds of the reduction in groundspeed occurs during the first 2/3 of the turn
- One-third of the reduction in groundspeed occurs during the first 2/3 of the turn
- All of the reduction in groundspeed occurs during the first 2/3 of the turn

  > The chapter states that when turning from a downwind position to a crosswind position, 1/2 of the reduction in groundspeed occurs during the first 2/3 of the turn from downwind to crosswind. This means the pilot must plan bank angle reduction accordingly.

**7.14 [Eights Across a Road, p.7-12, difficulty 2]**  
What is the primary difference between eights across a road and eights along a road?

- ✅ **At the completion of each loop, the airplane should cross an intersection or specific ground reference point**
- Eights across a road require the wind to be parallel to the road rather than perpendicular
- Eights across a road are performed at a higher altitude than eights along a road
- Eights across a road use two ground reference points instead of one

  > The chapter states that the primary difference is that at the completion of each loop of the figure eight, the airplane should cross an intersection or a specific ground reference point. The loops should be across the road with the wind perpendicular to the loops.

**7.15 [Eights Around Pylons, p.7-13, difficulty 2]**  
In eights around pylons, what determines the initial angle of bank required to maintain a constant turn radius from the pylons?

- ✅ **The distance between the pylons and the wind velocity**
- The altitude of the pylons above sea level
- The airplane's maneuvering speed and the density altitude
- The angle between the road and the wind direction

  > The chapter states that the distance between the pylons and the wind velocity determines the initial angle of bank required to maintain a constant turn radius from the pylons during each turn.

**7.16 [Eights on Pylons, p.7-15, difficulty 3]**  
What is the rule of thumb for estimating pivotal altitude when groundspeed is in knots?

- ✅ **Square the groundspeed, divide by 11.3, and add the MSL altitude of the ground reference**
- Square the groundspeed, divide by 15, and add the MSL altitude of the ground reference
- Square the groundspeed, divide by 11.3, and subtract the MSL altitude of the ground reference
- Multiply the groundspeed by 11.3 and add the MSL altitude of the ground reference

  > The chapter provides the rule of thumb: square the groundspeed, then divide by 15 (if mph) or divide by 11.3 (if knots), and then add the MSL altitude of the ground reference. The question specifies knots, so 11.3 is the correct divisor.

**7.17 [Eights on Pylons, p.7-17, difficulty 3]**  
During eights on pylons, if the visual reference line appears to move ahead of the pylon (pylon appears to move back), what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **Increase altitude to return to the pivotal altitude**
- Decrease altitude to return to the pivotal altitude
- Apply inside rudder pressure to yaw the wing backward
- Increase bank angle to turn faster and catch up with the pylon

  > The chapter states that if the visual reference line appears to move ahead of the pylon (pylon appears to move back), the pilot should increase altitude. This is because the airplane is below pivotal altitude. Applying rudder to force the reference line is explicitly stated as something that should not be attempted.

**7.18 [Eights on Pylons, p.7-17, difficulty 3]**  
How does the bank angle chosen during eights on pylons affect the pivotal altitude?

- ✅ **The bank angle does not alter the pivotal altitude unless the bank is steep enough to affect groundspeed**
- A steeper bank angle always increases the pivotal altitude
- A steeper bank angle always decreases the pivotal altitude
- The pivotal altitude is directly proportional to the tangent of the bank angle

  > The chapter explicitly states that the bank chosen does not alter the pivotal altitude. The pivotal altitude is determined by groundspeed. The chapter also notes that the pivotal altitude does not vary with the angle of bank unless the bank is steep enough to affect the groundspeed.


## Chapter 8 — 18 questions

**8.1 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-1, difficulty 1]**  
Unless the airport displays approved visual markings indicating otherwise, what direction should all turns in the traffic pattern be made?

- ✅ **To the left**
- To the right
- Based on the current wind direction
- Based on the direction of other traffic already in the pattern

  > The chapter states that unless the airport displays approved visual markings indicating that turns should be made to the right, the pilot should make all turns in the pattern to the left.

**8.2 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-1, difficulty 1]**  
What is the standard traffic pattern altitude for most airports?

- ✅ **1,000 feet above the elevation of the airport surface**
- 500 feet above the elevation of the airport surface
- 1,500 feet above mean sea level
- 800 feet above ground level

  > The chapter specifies that the traffic pattern altitude is usually 1,000 feet above the elevation of the airport surface, and that using a common altitude at a given airport is key to minimizing collision risk at non-towered airports.

**8.3 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-1, difficulty 2]**  
When operating in the traffic pattern of an airport with an operating control tower, what is the maximum airspeed typically flown?

- ✅ **200 knots**
- 156 knots
- 230 knots
- 250 knots

  > The chapter states that aircraft speeds are restrained by 14 CFR 91.117, and when operating in the traffic pattern at most airports with an operating control tower, aircraft typically fly at airspeeds no greater than 200 knots (230 mph).

**8.4 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-3, difficulty 2]**  
At what angle and toward what point is the traffic pattern normally entered at a non-towered airport?

- ✅ **45° angle to the downwind leg, headed toward a point abeam the midpoint of the runway**
- 90° angle to the base leg, headed toward the approach end of the runway
- 45° angle to the upwind leg, headed toward the departure end of the runway
- Straight in to the final approach leg, aligned with the runway centerline

  > The chapter specifies that the traffic pattern is normally entered at a 45° angle to the downwind leg, headed toward a point abeam the midpoint of the runway to be used for landing.

**8.5 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-3, difficulty 2]**  
Why should entries into traffic patterns while descending be avoided?

- ✅ **They create specific collision hazards**
- They cause excessive fuel consumption during descent
- They make it difficult to maintain proper spacing from the runway
- They are prohibited by 14 CFR 91.117

  > The chapter explicitly states that entries into traffic patterns while descending create specific collision hazards and should be avoided.

**8.6 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-3, difficulty 2]**  
On the downwind leg, at what point should the pilot reduce power and begin a descent?

- ✅ **After reaching a point abeam the approach end of the landing runway**
- When turning from the entry leg onto the downwind leg
- At the midpoint of the downwind leg
- When abeam the departure end of the runway

  > The chapter states that pattern altitude is maintained until at least abeam the approach end of the landing runway. At this point, the pilot should reduce power and begin a descent.

**8.7 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-3, difficulty 2]**  
When should the pilot turn from the downwind leg onto the base leg?

- ✅ **At a point approximately 45° from the approach end of the runway**
- When abeam the departure end of the runway
- Immediately after completing the before-landing checklist
- When the runway threshold is directly abeam the wingtip

  > The chapter states the pilot should continue the downwind leg past a point abeam the approach end of the runway to a point approximately 45° from the approach end of the runway, and make a medium-bank turn onto the base leg.

**8.8 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-4, difficulty 2]**  
When two or more aircraft are approaching an airport for landing, which aircraft has the right-of-way?

- ✅ **The aircraft at the lower altitude**
- The aircraft at the higher altitude
- The aircraft that is slower
- The aircraft that announced its position first on the radio

  > The chapter states that when two or more aircraft are approaching an airport for the purpose of landing, the aircraft at the lower altitude has the right-of-way. However, pilots should not take advantage of this rule to cut in front of another aircraft on final approach.

**8.9 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-4, difficulty 2]**  
What is the purpose of the upwind leg in the traffic pattern?

- ✅ **It is flown at controlled airports and after go-arounds to transition from final approach to climb altitude**
- It is used to enter the traffic pattern from the opposite side of the airport
- It is flown to depart the traffic pattern after takeoff
- It is used to maintain separation from aircraft on the base leg

  > The chapter states that the upwind leg is flown at controlled airports and after go-arounds. When necessary, it is the part of the traffic pattern in which the pilot transitions from the final approach to the climb altitude to initiate a go-around.

**8.10 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-4, difficulty 2]**  
If remaining in the traffic pattern after takeoff, when should the pilot commence the turn to the crosswind leg?

- ✅ **Beyond the departure end of the runway within 300 feet of the traffic pattern altitude**
- Immediately after reaching the departure end of the runway
- Upon reaching 500 feet AGL regardless of pattern altitude
- After reaching the traffic pattern altitude and flying straight ahead for one mile

  > The chapter states that if remaining in the traffic pattern, the pilot should commence a turn to the crosswind leg beyond the departure end of the runway within 300 feet of the traffic pattern altitude.

**8.11 [Standard Airport Traffic Patterns, p.8-4, difficulty 2]**  
If departing the traffic pattern, how should the pilot exit?

- ✅ **Continue straight out or exit with a 45° turn beyond the departure end of the runway after reaching traffic pattern altitude**
- Turn 90° to the crosswind leg immediately after liftoff
- Turn 45° toward the downwind leg before reaching pattern altitude
- Continue straight out and climb at least 500 feet above pattern altitude before turning

  > The chapter states that if departing the traffic pattern, the pilot should continue straight out or exit with a 45° turn (to the left in a left-hand pattern or to the right in a right-hand pattern) beyond the departure end of the runway after reaching traffic pattern altitude.

**8.12 [Non-Towered Airports, p.8-4, difficulty 2]**  
When entering from the upwind leg side of the airport, one method is to cross over midfield at what altitude above pattern altitude?

- ✅ **At least 500 feet above pattern altitude (normally 1,500 feet AGL)**
- At pattern altitude (1,000 feet AGL)
- At least 1,000 feet above pattern altitude (2,000 feet AGL)
- At least 300 feet above pattern altitude (1,300 feet AGL)

  > The chapter states that one method of entry from the opposite side of the pattern is to announce intentions and cross over midfield at least 500 feet above pattern altitude (normally 1,500 feet AGL). If large or turbine aircraft operate at the airport, it is best to remain 2,000 feet AGL.

**8.13 [Non-Towered Airports, p.8-4, difficulty 3]**  
If large or turbine aircraft operate at a non-towered airport, what altitude should a pilot maintain when crossing over midfield to enter the pattern?

- ✅ **2,000 feet AGL**
- 1,500 feet AGL
- 1,000 feet AGL
- 3,000 feet AGL

  > The chapter states that if large or turbine aircraft operate at the airport, it is best to remain 2,000 feet AGL so as not to conflict with their traffic pattern, which may be flown higher than the standard pattern.

**8.14 [Non-Towered Airports, p.8-5, difficulty 2]**  
Why is the 45° entry to the downwind leg the preferred method for entering a non-towered airport traffic pattern?

- ✅ **If entry is not possible due to conflicting traffic, the pilot can continue turning away from the downwind and return for another attempt while scanning for traffic**
- It provides the shortest path to the runway for landing
- It allows the pilot to descend below pattern altitude before joining the downwind
- It is the only entry method approved by 14 CFR 91.126

  > The chapter explains that the advantage of the 45° entry is that if it is not possible to enter the pattern due to conflicting traffic, the pilot can continue to turn away from the downwind, fly a safe distance away, and return for another attempt—all while scanning for traffic.

**8.15 [Non-Towered Airports, p.8-4, difficulty 3]**  
When should the alternate midfield crosswind entry at pattern altitude not be used?

- ✅ **When the traffic pattern is busy**
- When the wind is calm
- When the airport has a segmented circle
- When arriving from the downwind side of the pattern

  > The chapter describes an alternate method of entering on a midfield crosswind at pattern altitude, but explicitly states this technique should not be used if the pattern is busy.

**8.16 [Safety Considerations, p.8-5, difficulty 2]**  
What is described as the worst-case scenario for traffic pattern visibility between two aircraft?

- ✅ **A low-wing airplane flying above a high-wing airplane**
- A high-wing airplane flying above a low-wing airplane
- Two low-wing airplanes at the same altitude
- Two high-wing airplanes on opposite base legs

  > The chapter explains that high-wing airplanes have restricted visibility above while low-wing airplanes have limited visibility below. The worst-case scenario is a low-wing airplane flying above a high-wing airplane, as neither pilot can see the other.

**8.17 [Safety Considerations, p.8-5, difficulty 2]**  
According to NTSB data cited in the chapter, what is the most probable cause of mid-air collisions?

- ✅ **The pilot failing to see and avoid other aircraft**
- Pilots flying at incorrect traffic pattern altitudes
- Pilots failing to communicate on the CTAF
- Pilots entering the pattern while descending

  > The chapter cites the NTSB finding that the most probable cause of mid-air collisions is the pilot failing to see and avoid other aircraft.

**8.18 [Chapter Summary, p.8-7, difficulty 2]**  
Where do most reported mid-air collisions occur within the traffic pattern?

- ✅ **On the final or short-final approach leg**
- On the downwind leg
- On the base leg
- On the crosswind leg

  > The chapter summary states that most reported mid-air collisions occur during the final or short-final approach leg of the airport traffic pattern.


## Chapter 9 — 18 questions

**9.1 [Use of Flaps, p.9-2, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot extends flaps in increments on downwind, base, and final rather than making one large extension. What is the primary advantage of this technique?

- ✅ **It allows smaller adjustments of pitch and power, supporting a stabilized approach**
- It reduces total drag on the approach, allowing a steeper descent angle
- It prevents the nose-down pitching moment that occurs with any flap extension
- It eliminates the need to re-trim the airplane after each flap change

  > The chapter states that large changes in flap deflection at one point produce large lift changes requiring significant pitch and power changes. Incremental deflection allows smaller adjustments and supports a stabilized approach. Flap extension still requires re-trimming, and deflection beyond 15° produces large drag increases.

**9.2 [Normal Approach and Landing, p.9-2, difficulty 1]**  
On the base leg, the pilot should use a target airspeed of approximately what multiple of VSO?

- ✅ **1.4 VSO**
- 1.3 VSO
- 1.2 VSO
- 1.5 VSO

  > The chapter specifies that after turning onto the base leg, the pilot starts or continues the descent with a target airspeed of approximately 1.4 VSO. The 1.3 VSO speed is referenced for final approach, not base leg.

**9.3 [Stabilized Approach Concept, p.9-4, difficulty 2]**  
During a stabilized approach, how does the aiming point appear to the pilot?

- ✅ **It appears stationary—it does not move under or away from the airplane**
- It appears to move forward away from the airplane as the airplane descends
- It appears to move backward under the airplane as distance is closed
- It appears to move up toward the horizon as the airplane descends

  > The chapter explains that the aiming point is identified by the fact that it does not move. Objects in front of and beyond the aiming point appear to move in opposite directions, but the aiming point itself remains stationary for a constant-angle glide path.

**9.4 [Stabilized Approach Concept, p.9-6, difficulty 2]**  
For a typical general aviation piston aircraft in a traffic pattern, at what altitude should an immediate go-around be initiated if the approach becomes unstabilized?

- ✅ **300 ft AGL**
- 500 ft AGL
- 1,000 ft AGL
- 100 ft AGL

  > The chapter states that typically pilots go-around if unable to establish a stabilized approach by 500 ft above airport elevation in VMC or 1,000 ft in IMC. However, for a typical GA piston aircraft in a traffic pattern, an immediate go-around should be initiated if the approach becomes unstabilized below 300 ft AGL.

**9.5 [Estimating Airplane Movement and Height, p.9-7, difficulty 2]**  
If a pilot focuses too close to the airplane during the round out, what is the likely result?

- ✅ **Over-controlling, rounding out high, and making full-stall, drop-in landings**
- Flying the airplane into the ground nose first due to a too-slow reaction
- A late flare resulting in a hard landing on the main gear
- Excessive floating past the desired touchdown point

  > The chapter explains that focusing too close blurs the reference, causing the pilot's reaction to be too abrupt or too late, with a tendency to over-control, round out high, and make full-stall, drop-in landings. Focusing too far ahead causes the pilot to lose accuracy in judging closeness of the ground, sometimes resulting in flying into the ground nose first.

**9.6 [Round Out (Flare), p.9-8, difficulty 2]**  
During a full-flap approach compared to a no-flap approach, how should the rate of pitch increase during the round out differ?

- ✅ **The pitch should be increased at a faster rate because the nose must travel through a greater pitch change**
- The pitch should be increased at a slower rate because the steeper descent angle provides more time
- The rate should be the same since the round out always starts at the same height regardless of flap setting
- The pitch should be increased at a slower rate because full flaps already provide a nose-high attitude

  > The chapter states that the pitch attitude in a full-flap approach is considerably lower than in a no-flap approach, so the nose needs to travel through a greater pitch change. Since the round out starts at approximately the same height, the pitch attitude should be increased at a faster rate when full flaps are used, while still accounting for the airplane's downward motion.

**9.7 [Go-Arounds (Rejected Landings), p.9-12, difficulty 2]**  
During a go-around, why is it generally recommended to retract flaps (at least partially) before retracting the landing gear?

- ✅ **Full flaps produce more drag than the gear on most airplanes, and the gear should remain down in case of an inadvertent touchdown**
- Retracting flaps first prevents the airplane from ballooning due to increased lift
- The landing gear cannot be retracted until the flaps are in the takeoff position on most airplanes
- Retracting flaps first reduces the workload of trimming the airplane during the go-around

  > The chapter gives two reasons for retracting flaps before gear: first, full flaps produce more drag than the landing gear on most airplanes; second, in case the airplane inadvertently touches down during the go-around, it is desirable to have the landing gear down and locked.

**9.8 [Go-Arounds (Rejected Landings), p.9-12, difficulty 3]**  
If a go-around is initiated close to the ground, what effect does ground effect have on the maneuver?

- ✅ **It temporarily reduces induced drag, creating a false sense of performance that is lost when climbing out of ground effect**
- It increases induced drag, requiring more power to maintain altitude during the go-around
- It provides a permanent boost in performance that helps the airplane climb more rapidly
- It reduces lift, requiring the pilot to pitch up more aggressively to maintain altitude

  > The chapter explains that ground effect reduces induced drag, creating an apparent 'cushion of air' that gives borrowed performance. This performance is repaid when the airplane climbs out of ground effect. An attempt to climb prematurely may result in the airplane not being able to climb or even maintain altitude at full power.

**9.9 [Intentional Slips, p.9-12, difficulty 2]**  
What is the primary difference between a forward slip and a sideslip?

- ✅ **In a forward slip the airplane's nose is yawed away from the flightpath; in a sideslip the longitudinal axis remains parallel to the original flightpath**
- A forward slip is used for crosswind landings; a sideslip is used to lose altitude
- A forward slip requires full flaps; a sideslip is performed with flaps retracted
- A forward slip increases airspeed; a sideslip decreases airspeed

  > The chapter describes a forward slip as one where the nose is yawed opposite to the flightpath while the airplane remains on its original ground track, used to dissipate altitude. A sideslip keeps the longitudinal axis parallel to the original flightpath with the airplane moving sideways toward the low wing, and is frequently used for crosswind landings.

**9.10 [Intentional Slips, p.9-13, difficulty 3]**  
What determines the practical slip limit in most light airplanes?

- ✅ **The point where full rudder is required to prevent a turn despite ailerons being capable of further steepening the bank**
- The point where the airspeed indicator reaches its maximum allowable speed in the slip
- The point where the stall warning horn activates due to the high angle of attack
- The point where the airplane begins to spin if the bank is increased further

  > The chapter states that the steepness of a slip is limited by rudder travel. The practical slip limit is reached when full rudder is required to maintain heading even though ailerons could steepen the bank further. Any additional bank would cause the airplane to turn. If more descent is needed, lowering the nose increases airspeed and rudder effectiveness, permitting a steeper slip.

**9.11 [Crosswind Approach and Landing, p.9-18, difficulty 2]**  
During the crosswind after-landing roll, as the airplane decelerates, how should aileron and rudder inputs be adjusted?

- ✅ **More aileron into the wind is needed as the airplane slows, while rudder effectiveness decreases with speed**
- Less aileron is needed as the airplane slows because the crosswind effect diminishes
- Aileron should be neutralized and only rudder used for directional control below 30 knots
- Aileron should be applied away from the wind to prevent the upwind wing from rising

  > The chapter explains that as the airplane slows, ailerons become less effective while the relative wind becomes more of a crosswind, exerting greater lifting force on the upwind wing. Therefore, more and more aileron must be applied into the wind. When the airplane is coming to a stop, the aileron control should be held fully toward the wind.

**9.12 [Maximum Safe Crosswind Velocities, p.9-19, difficulty 2]**  
What is the FAA's demonstrated crosswind velocity for type certification of an airplane?

- ✅ **A 90° crosswind up to a velocity equal to 0.2 VSO**
- A 90° crosswind up to a velocity equal to 0.3 VSO
- A 45° crosswind up to a velocity equal to 0.2 VSO
- A 90° crosswind up to a velocity equal to the airplane's stall speed in clean configuration

  > The chapter states that before type certification, the FAA requires demonstration that the airplane is satisfactorily controllable in 90° crosswinds up to a velocity equal to 0.2 VSO—two-tenths of the stalling speed with power off in landing configuration. This demonstrated crosswind velocity is included on a placard in airplanes certificated after May 3, 1962.

**9.13 [Turbulent Air Approach and Landing, p.9-20, difficulty 2]**  
When landing in turbulent air with gusty crosswind conditions, why should the pilot use partial flaps rather than full flaps?

- ✅ **With less than full flaps, the airplane is in a higher pitch attitude, requiring less pitch change to establish the landing attitude at a higher airspeed for more positive control**
- Full flaps create excessive drag that could cause the airplane to stall in turbulent conditions
- Partial flaps prevent the nose-down pitching moment that would worsen control in gusts
- Full flaps reduce the stall margin too much for safe operation in turbulence

  > The chapter states that with less than full flaps, the airplane is in a higher pitch attitude, requiring less of a pitch change to establish the landing attitude. This allows touchdown at a higher airspeed, ensuring more positive control in turbulent, gusty conditions.

**9.14 [Short-Field Approach and Landing, p.9-23, difficulty 2]**  
After touchdown on a short-field landing, what technique provides maximum braking effectiveness?

- ✅ **Hold full back pressure on the controls while smoothly applying brakes to the point just short of a skid**
- Apply maximum brake pressure immediately upon touchdown to lock the wheels for shortest stopping distance
- Relax back pressure to put full weight on the nose-wheel, then apply brakes firmly
- Apply brakes in rapid pulses to prevent skidding and maximize friction

  > The chapter states that the pilot increases braking effectiveness by holding the wheel or stick full back while smoothly applying brakes. Back pressure is needed because the airplane tends to lean forward with heavy braking. Best braking results are achieved with the wheels in an 'incipient skid condition'—just short of locking up. If wheels lock, braking effectiveness drops dramatically.

**9.15 [Soft-Field Approach and Landing, p.9-23, difficulty 2]**  
During a soft-field landing, what is the primary reason for using power throughout the level-off and touchdown?

- ✅ **To allow the airspeed to slowly dissipate while the airplane is flown in ground effect, keeping the wings supporting the weight as long as practical**
- To increase the approach speed so the airplane touches down before sinking into the soft surface
- To provide additional elevator authority for the flare since the propeller airflow increases elevator effectiveness
- To prevent the engine from cooling too rapidly during the descent on final approach

  > The chapter explains that a degree of power is used throughout the level-off and touchdown for soft-field landings. This allows the airspeed to slowly dissipate while the airplane is flown 1 to 2 feet off the surface in ground effect. When the wheels touch, the wings continue to support much of the weight, minimizing stresses on the landing gear and preventing sinking into the soft surface.

**9.16 [Power-Off Accuracy Approaches, p.9-24, difficulty 3]**  
On a power-off approach, if the airplane's airspeed is below best glide and the pilot raises the nose, what happens to the descent angle?

- ✅ **The descent angle steepens greatly because airspeed decreases and lift is insufficient**
- The descent angle shallows because the increased pitch attitude increases lift temporarily
- The descent angle remains constant because pitch only affects airspeed, not glide angle
- The descent angle steepens slightly but airspeed increases to compensate

  > The chapter explains that on a power-off approach with airspeed below best glide, pitching up reduces airspeed further and greatly steepens the descent angle. If airspeed is too low, the pilot should lower the nose. The pilot should never try to stretch a glide by raising the pitch attitude, as the airplane settles rapidly due to slow airspeed and insufficient lift.

**9.17 [Faulty Approaches and Landings, p.9-30, difficulty 2]**  
When a pilot recognizes the airplane is well below the proper final approach path, what should the pilot NOT do to correct the situation?

- ✅ **Retract the flaps to reduce drag and extend the glide**
- Apply power immediately to maintain airspeed while raising the pitch attitude
- Execute an immediate go-around if there is doubt about completing the approach safely
- Reestablish the correct approach attitude after intercepting the proper approach path

  > The chapter explicitly warns against retracting flaps to correct for a low final approach, as this causes a sudden decrease in lift and causes the airplane to sink more rapidly. The correct action is to apply power, raise the pitch attitude to stop the descent, and reestablish the approach path—or go around if in doubt.

**9.18 [Hydroplaning, p.9-37, difficulty 3]**  
What is the formula for calculating the minimum dynamic hydroplaning speed (Vp) of a tire?

- ✅ **8.6 times the square root of the tire pressure in PSI**
- 9.0 times the square root of the tire pressure in PSI
- 8.6 times the tire pressure in PSI
- The square root of 8.6 times the tire pressure in PSI

  > The chapter states that the minimum dynamic hydroplaning speed is 8.6 times the square root of the tire pressure in PSI. For example, an airplane with 24 PSI main tire pressure would have a calculated hydroplaning speed of approximately 42 knots. This is the speed for the start of dynamic hydroplaning; once started, it may persist to slower speeds.


## Chapter 10 — 18 questions

**10.1 [Introduction, p.10-1, difficulty 1]**  
What is the primary purpose of performance maneuvers in flight training?

- ✅ **To develop a pilot's proficiency in flight control application, situational awareness, and division of attention**
- To prepare pilots for aerobatic competition and airshow performances
- To satisfy FAA hourly requirements for cross-country flight training
- To practice emergency procedures similar to engine failure scenarios

  > The chapter states that performance maneuvers enhance a pilot's proficiency in flight control application, maneuver planning, situational awareness, and division of attention, and that their design allows for constantly changing flight control pressures, attitudes, airspeeds, and orientations.

**10.2 [Steep Turns, p.10-2, difficulty 2]**  
What is the load factor in a level altitude 60° banked turn?

- ✅ **2.0**
- 1.41
- 1.73
- 3.8

  > The chapter specifies that a level altitude 45° banked turn produces a load factor of 1.41, while a level altitude 60° banked turn produces a load factor of 2.0, meaning the effective weight of the aircraft and occupants doubles.

**10.3 [Steep Turns, p.10-2, difficulty 2]**  
If an airplane stalls in level flight at 50 knots, approximately what is its stalling speed in a level altitude 45° banked turn while maintaining altitude?

- ✅ **60 knots**
- 55 knots
- 71 knots
- 100 knots

  > The chapter provides this exact example: if an airplane stalls in level flight at 50 knots, it will stall at 60 knots in a 45° steep turn. At 60° of bank, it would stall at 70 knots. Stalling speed increases at the square root of the load factor.

**10.4 [Steep Turns, p.10-2, difficulty 2]**  
Why should steep turns be performed at an airspeed that does not exceed the airplane's design maneuvering speed (VA) or operating maneuvering speed (VO)?

- ✅ **At speeds at or below VA or VO, the airplane will stall before exceeding the design load limit**
- Above VA or VO, the airplane will be unable to maintain altitude in a steep turn
- Above VA or VO, the overbanking tendency becomes uncontrollable
- At speeds above VA or VO, the airplane's trim system becomes ineffective

  > The chapter explains that as bank angle increases in level flight, the margin between stalling speed and maneuvering speed decreases. At speeds at or below VA or VO, the airplane will stall before exceeding the design load limit, providing a built-in safety margin against structural overload.

**10.5 [Steep Turns, p.10-2, difficulty 2]**  
When performing a steep turn, what should the pilot do if the nose drops excessively low, resulting in a significant loss of altitude?

- ✅ **Reduce the angle of bank first with coordinated opposite aileron and rudder, then increase pitch with elevator back pressure**
- Apply full elevator back pressure immediately while holding the bank angle constant
- Reduce power to idle and apply forward elevator pressure to lower the nose further
- Roll wings level immediately and then apply full power to climb back to altitude

  > The chapter warns that attempting to recover from an excessively nose-low, steep bank condition by using only the elevator causes a steepening of the bank and puts unnecessary stress on the airplane. The correct procedure is to first reduce the angle of bank with coordinated use of opposite aileron and rudder, then increase pitch attitude with elevator back pressure.

**10.6 [Steep Turns, p.10-3, difficulty 1]**  
Using the rule of thumb from the chapter, if a pilot begins a right steep turn on a heading of 270° using a 60° bank angle, at what heading should the pilot begin the rollout?

- ✅ **240°**
- 210°
- 255°
- 300°

  > The chapter states that a good rule of thumb is to begin the rollout at half the number of degrees of bank prior to reaching the terminating heading. With a 60° bank, the rollout should begin 30° prior to the original heading. Starting from 270°, the rollout should begin at 240°.

**10.7 [Steep Turns, p.10-2, difficulty 2]**  
What causes the overbanking tendency experienced during steep turns?

- ✅ **At steep bank angles, the airplane loses positive or neutral stability about the longitudinal axis and continues rolling in the direction of the bank unless opposite aileron is held**
- The increased load factor shifts the center of gravity laterally during the turn
- P-factor causes one wing to produce more thrust, creating a rolling moment
- The increased G-forces compress the wing structure, changing the lift distribution

  > The chapter explains that in most flight maneuvers, bank angles are shallow enough that the airplane exhibits positive or neutral stability about the longitudinal axis. However, as bank angles steepen, the airplane will continue rolling in the direction of the bank unless deliberate and opposite aileron pressure is held.

**10.8 [Steep Spiral, p.10-3, difficulty 1]**  
What is the primary objective of the steep spiral maneuver?

- ✅ **To rapidly dissipate substantial amounts of altitude while remaining over a selected spot**
- To practice emergency descent procedures at maximum airspeed
- To simulate an engine-out approach to a specific landing zone
- To develop coordination skills during high-speed descending turns

  > The chapter states that the objective of the steep spiral is to provide a flight maneuver for rapidly dissipating substantial amounts of altitude while remaining over a selected spot, and notes it may be useful during an emergency landing.

**10.9 [Steep Spiral, p.10-4, difficulty 2]**  
How should a pilot correct for wind drift to maintain a constant radius during a steep spiral?

- ✅ **Steepen the bank on downwind headings and shallow the bank on upwind headings**
- Maintain a constant bank angle and adjust airspeed to compensate for wind
- Steepen the bank on upwind headings and shallow the bank on downwind headings
- Increase pitch on downwind headings and decrease pitch on upwind headings

  > The chapter explains that maintaining a constant radius around the reference point can only be accomplished by proper correction for wind drift by steepening the bank on downwind headings and shallowing the bank on upwind headings.

**10.10 [Steep Spiral, p.10-4, difficulty 2]**  
Why should the throttle be periodically advanced during a steep spiral maneuver?

- ✅ **To prevent excessive engine cooling, spark plug fouling, or carburetor ice from prolonged idle operation**
- To maintain adequate cylinder head temperatures for optimal fuel efficiency
- To clear the engine of accumulated oil that pools during idle descent
- To practice power-off to power-on transitions as part of the maneuver requirements

  > The chapter warns that operating the engine at idle speed for any prolonged period during the glide may result in excessive engine cooling, spark plug fouling, or carburetor ice. To avoid these issues, the throttle should be periodically advanced and sustained for a few seconds.

**10.11 [Steep Spiral, p.10-3, difficulty 1]**  
At what minimum altitude should the steep spiral maneuver conclude?

- ✅ **1,500 feet AGL**
- 500 feet AGL
- 1,000 feet AGL
- 3,000 feet AGL

  > The chapter specifies that the steep spiral should begin at sufficient altitude such that the maneuver concludes no lower than 1,500 feet above ground level (AGL).

**10.12 [Chandelle, p.10-4, difficulty 2]**  
How are the two phases of a chandelle correctly described?

- ✅ **The first 90° is constant bank with continuously increasing pitch; the second 90° is constant pitch with continuously decreasing bank**
- The first 90° is constant pitch with continuously increasing bank; the second 90° is constant bank with continuously decreasing pitch
- Both 90° segments use constant bank with continuously increasing pitch throughout
- The first 90° is decreasing bank with increasing pitch; the second 90° is constant pitch with constant bank

  > The chapter explicitly describes the chandelle in two phases: the first 90° of turn is constant bank and continuously increasing pitch, and the second 90° is constant pitch and continuously decreasing bank. Maximum pitch-up occurs at the 90° mark and is held for the remainder of the maneuver.

**10.13 [Chandelle, p.10-5, difficulty 2]**  
What is the correct bank angle generally used when initiating a chandelle?

- ✅ **Approximately 30°**
- Approximately 45°
- Approximately 15°
- Approximately 60°

  > The chapter states that once the bank angle is established, which is generally 30°, a climbing turn should be started by smoothly applying elevator back pressure while simultaneously increasing engine power.

**10.14 [Chandelle, p.10-5, difficulty 3]**  
When rolling out from a left chandelle near completion of the maneuver, what rudder pressure is required and why?

- ✅ **Significant right rudder pressure, because left adverse yaw and left-turning tendencies such as P-factor combine during the rollout**
- Significant left rudder pressure, because the decreasing bank angle creates a right yawing moment
- Very little rudder pressure, because the left-turning tendencies cancel the adverse yaw during rollout
- Alternating left and right rudder pressure, because the airspeed is at its slowest point and control effectiveness is minimal

  > The chapter explains that when airspeed is slowest near completion of the chandelle, right rudder pressure is significant, especially when rolling out from a left chandelle due to left adverse yaw and left-turning tendencies such as P-factor. When rolling out from a right chandelle, the yawing moment is to the right, which partially cancels some of the left-turning tendency's effect.

**10.15 [Chandelle, p.10-5, difficulty 2]**  
What should be the airplane's airspeed at the completion of 180° of turn in a chandelle?

- ✅ **Just above the power-on stall speed**
- At the airplane's design maneuvering speed (VA)
- At the entry airspeed used to begin the maneuver
- Just above the power-off stall speed

  > The chapter states that at the completion of 180° of turn, the wings should be level to the horizon, the airspeed should be just above the power-on stall speed, and the airplane's pitch-high attitude should be held momentarily.

**10.16 [Lazy Eight, p.10-6, difficulty 1]**  
What makes the lazy eight unique among standard flight training maneuvers?

- ✅ **It is the only standard flight training maneuver in which flight control pressures are constantly changing**
- It is the only maneuver that requires the pilot to use trim throughout its execution
- It is the only maneuver performed entirely by outside visual references without instrument cross-check
- It is the only maneuver that requires the pilot to change power settings continuously throughout

  > The chapter explicitly states that the lazy eight is the only standard flight training maneuver in which flight control pressures are constantly changing, and that it is designed to develop proper coordination of the flight controls across a wide range of airspeeds and attitudes.

**10.17 [Lazy Eight, p.10-7, difficulty 2]**  
In a lazy eight, at what point should the maximum pitch-up attitude be reached, and what should the approximate bank angle be at that point?

- ✅ **Maximum pitch-up at the 45° reference point with an approximate bank angle of 15°**
- Maximum pitch-up at the 90° reference point with an approximate bank angle of 30°
- Maximum pitch-up at the 45° reference point with an approximate bank angle of 30°
- Maximum pitch-up at the 90° reference point with an approximate bank angle of 15°

  > The chapter describes that the climbing turn should be planned and controlled so that the maximum pitch-up attitude is reached at the 45° point with an approximate bank angle of 15°. At the 90° reference point, the bank angle should reach its maximum of approximately 30°, and the pitch attitude passes through level.

**10.18 [Lazy Eight, p.10-7, difficulty 2]**  
What should the airspeed and pitch attitude be at the 90° reference point of a lazy eight?

- ✅ **Airspeed at its minimum (5–10 knots above stall speed) with pitch attitude passing through level flight**
- Airspeed at its maximum with pitch attitude at its highest point above the horizon
- Airspeed at entry speed with pitch attitude at its lowest point below the horizon
- Airspeed at maneuvering speed with pitch attitude at maximum pitch-up

  > The chapter states that at the lazy eight's 90° reference point, the airspeed should be at its minimum, just about 5 to 10 knots above stall speed, with the airplane's pitch attitude passing through level flight. The bank angle should also have reached its maximum of approximately 30° at this point.


## Chapter 11 — 18 questions

**11.1 [Anatomy of the Eye, p.11-1, difficulty 1]**  
Which photosensitive cells in the eye are primarily responsible for night vision, and where are they concentrated?

- ✅ **Rods, located mainly in the periphery of the retina**
- Cones, concentrated in the fovea
- Both rods and cones equally distributed across the retina
- The iris, which adjusts pupil size in response to dim light

  > The chapter states that rods are located mainly in the periphery of the retina and are used for low light intensity or night vision. Cones, concentrated in the fovea, are used for day or high-intensity light vision.

**11.2 [Types of Vision, p.11-3, difficulty 2]**  
Under scotopic vision conditions, what happens if a pilot looks directly at a dim object for more than a few seconds?

- ✅ **The image of the object fades away completely due to the night blind spot**
- The object becomes sharper as the cones adapt to the darkness
- The object's color becomes distinguishable after several seconds
- The object appears to move, creating the autokinesis illusion

  > The chapter explains that under scotopic conditions, central vision (foveal cones) becomes ineffective. If looking directly at an object for more than a few seconds, the image fades away completely due to the night blind spot. Autokinesis is a separate illusion involving a stationary light appearing to move.

**11.3 [The Night Blind Spot, p.11-3, difficulty 2]**  
What causes the night blind spot, and how much of the visual field does it affect?

- ✅ **Absence of rods in the fovea, affecting the central 5 to 10 degrees of the visual field**
- Absence of cones in the periphery, affecting 30 to 40 degrees of the visual field
- Damage to the optic nerve, affecting the entire peripheral field
- Blood vessels blocking the macula, affecting the central 1 to 2 degrees

  > The night blind spot appears under low ambient illumination due to the absence of rods in the fovea. This affects the central 5 to 10 degrees of the visual field, meaning objects viewed directly may go undetected or fade away.

**11.4 [Vision Under Dim and Bright Illumination, p.11-3, difficulty 1]**  
Approximately how long does it take for the eyes to achieve maximum dark adaptation?

- ✅ **Up to 30 minutes**
- 5 to 10 minutes
- 1 to 2 hours
- Approximately 15 minutes

  > The chapter states that in darkness, vision gradually becomes more sensitive to light and maximum dark adaptation can take up to 30 minutes. Exposure to bright light for 1 second or more can temporarily impair this adaptation.

**11.5 [Factors Affecting Vision, p.11-4, difficulty 2]**  
At what altitude does the chapter state that inflight exposure to low barometric pressure without supplemental oxygen can result in hypoxia impairing visual performance at night?

- ✅ **Above 5,000 feet**
- Above 10,000 feet
- Above 12,500 feet
- Above 8,000 feet

  > The chapter specifies that hypoxia can impair visual performance above 10,000 feet during the day and above 5,000 feet at night. The lower nighttime altitude reflects the eye's greater sensitivity to oxygen deprivation in low-light conditions.

**11.6 [Autokinesis, p.11-4, difficulty 2]**  
What is the visual illusion known as autokinesis?

- ✅ **A stationary light appears to move when stared at for many seconds in the dark**
- Ground lights blend with stars, making it difficult to distinguish the actual horizon
- A runway appears to slope up or down due to a lack of surrounding visual cues
- Bright lights make the runway appear closer than it actually is

  > The chapter describes autokinesis as an illusion where a stationary light appears to move about when stared at for many seconds in the dark. A disoriented pilot could lose control attempting to align the aircraft with the false movements.

**11.7 [Featureless Terrain Illusion, p.11-5, difficulty 2]**  
During a black-hole approach, what is the most likely hazardous outcome if the pilot does not recognize the illusion?

- ✅ **Landing short of the runway**
- Landing long on the runway
- Flaring too high above the runway
- Overshooting the final approach course

  > The chapter states that a black-hole approach occurs when landing from over water or non-lighted terrain where runway lights are the only source of light. Without peripheral visual cues, the runway can seem out of position and, in the worst case, results in landing short of the runway.

**11.8 [Ground Lighting Illusions, p.11-5, difficulty 2]**  
When bright runway and approach lighting systems create the illusion of being closer to the runway than actual, what tendency does this create for the pilot?

- ✅ **Flying a higher-than-normal approach**
- Flying a lower-than-normal approach
- Landing short of the runway threshold
- Overcorrecting with excessive bank on final approach

  > The chapter explains that bright runway and approach lighting systems, especially where few lights illuminate the surrounding terrain, may create the illusion of less distance to the runway. The pilot who does not recognize this illusion will often fly a higher approach.

**11.9 [Pilot Equipment, p.11-5, difficulty 1]**  
According to the chapter, what color flashlight light is recommended for use during flight deck operations at night, and why?

- ✅ **Red light, because it is non-glaring and will not impair night vision**
- White light, because it provides the best detail for instrument reading
- Green light, because it preserves color perception on aeronautical charts
- Blue light, because it reduces eye strain during prolonged use

  > The chapter recommends a dimmable flashlight able to produce white/red light. White light is used for preflight inspection, red light is used for flight deck operations because it is non-glaring and will not impair night vision, and dim white light may be used for chart reading.

**11.10 [Airplane Equipment and Lighting, p.11-6, difficulty 1]**  
How are airplane position lights arranged according to the chapter?

- ✅ **Red on the left wingtip, green on the right wingtip, and white on the tail**
- Green on the left wingtip, red on the right wingtip, and white on the tail
- Red on the left wingtip, white on the right wingtip, and green on the tail
- White on the left wingtip, green on the right wingtip, and red on the tail

  > The chapter states that airplane position lights are arranged similar to those of boats and ships: a red light on the left wingtip, a green light on the right wingtip, and a white light on the tail. This arrangement helps pilots determine the general direction of movement of other aircraft.

**11.11 [Airplane Equipment and Lighting, p.11-6, difficulty 2]**  
Under what authority may a pilot-in-command turn off the aircraft's anti-collision light system at night?

- ✅ **14 CFR part 91, section 91.209(b) allows the PIC to turn them off in the interest of safety**
- The PIC may never turn off anti-collision lights during night flight under any circumstances
- The PIC may turn them off only after receiving ATC approval
- The PIC may turn them off only when operating below 10,000 feet MSL

  > While 14 CFR 91.205(c)(3) requires an approved anti-collision light system for VFR night flight, 14 CFR 91.209(b) gives the pilot-in-command leeway to turn off the anti-collision lights in the interest of safety.

**11.12 [Airport and Navigation Lighting Aids, p.11-7, difficulty 1]**  
What color pattern does a rotating beacon display to identify a lighted civilian land airport?

- ✅ **Alternating white and green lights**
- Alternating white and yellow lights
- Dual peaked white flashes, then green
- Alternating white and red flashes

  > The chapter lists beacon color patterns for different airport types. Civilian land airports use alternating white and green lights. Civilian water airports use alternating white and yellow. Military airports use dual peaked white flashes then green. Red flashes indicate obstructions.

**11.13 [Airport and Navigation Lighting Aids, p.11-7, difficulty 2]**  
What colors are runway threshold lights and runway end lights, respectively?

- ✅ **Threshold lights are aviation green and runway end lights are aviation red**
- Both threshold and runway end lights are aviation white
- Threshold lights are red and runway end lights are green
- Threshold lights are yellow and runway end lights are white

  > The chapter states that at some airports, runway threshold lights are aviation green and runway end lights are aviation red. Runway edge lights are aviation white, though yellow may be substituted for the last 2,000 feet to indicate a caution zone.

**11.14 [Airport and Navigation Lighting Aids, p.11-7, difficulty 2]**  
When using radio-controlled runway lighting, how many microphone clicks are recommended to initially activate all lights to maximum available intensity?

- ✅ **7 clicks**
- 3 clicks
- 5 clicks
- 10 clicks

  > The chapter recommends always initially keying the mike 7 times, which assures all controlled lights are turned on to maximum available intensity. If desired, adjustment can then be made to a lower intensity by keying 5 and/or 3 times.

**11.15 [Preparation and Preflight, p.11-7, difficulty 2]**  
Why does the chapter recommend paying particular attention to the temperature/dew point spread during night flight preparation?

- ✅ **A narrow temperature/dew point spread may indicate the possibility of fog**
- A wide spread ensures better engine performance at night
- A narrow spread guarantees clear skies and good visibility
- A wide spread indicates the need for supplemental oxygen

  > The chapter states that preparation for a night flight includes a thorough review of weather with particular attention to temperature/dew point spread. A narrow spread may indicate the possibility of fog, which is especially hazardous at night.

**11.16 [Takeoff and Climb, p.11-8, difficulty 2]**  
During a night takeoff, why does the chapter emphasize frequent reference to flight instruments?

- ✅ **Limited outside visual references at night make instruments essential for verifying airspeed, attitude, and heading**
- Flight instruments are required to be used for all night departures by regulation
- The airplane's mechanical operation changes at night and instruments compensate for this
- ATC requires instrument reference for all night departures from controlled airports

  > The chapter explains that the most noticeable difference between day and night flying is the limited availability of outside visual references at night. Flight instruments should be used to a greater degree, particularly on night takeoffs and climbs, to ensure proper airspeed, attitude, and heading.

**11.17 [Orientation and Navigation, p.11-9, difficulty 2]**  
What hazard does the chapter identify when flying over large bodies of water at night?

- ✅ **Stars reflected on the water surface can appear as a continuous array of lights, making the horizon difficult to identify**
- Radio navigation signals become unreliable over water at night
- The water absorbs all ambient light, making the surface completely invisible
- Temperature differentials over water cause rapid engine icing at night

  > The chapter identifies that crossing large bodies of water at night is hazardous for single-engine airplanes due to engine failure risk, and also because stars may be reflected on the water surface, appearing as a continuous array of lights and making the horizon difficult to identify.

**11.18 [Night Emergencies, p.11-13, difficulty 3]**  
During a night engine failure emergency, if the landing lights are unusable and outside visual references are not available, what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **Hold the airplane in a level-landing attitude until the ground is contacted**
- Descend in a spiral over the selected landing area to maintain visual contact with the ground
- Use the vertical speed indicator to determine the correct flare height for touchdown
- Turn on all cockpit lights to maximum brightness to illuminate the ground below

  > The chapter states that if the landing lights are unusable and outside visual references are not available during a night emergency landing, the airplane should be held in a level-landing attitude until the ground is contacted. The landing should be completed at the slowest possible airspeed in the normal landing attitude.


## Chapter 12 — 18 questions

**12.1 [Introduction, p.12-1, difficulty 1]**  
According to 14 CFR part 61, which combination of features defines a complex airplane?

- ✅ **Retractable landing gear, flaps, and a controllable pitch propeller**
- An engine developing more than 200 horsepower, flaps, and retractable landing gear
- Retractable landing gear, flaps, and a turbocharged engine
- A controllable pitch propeller, flaps, and an engine developing more than 200 horsepower

  > The chapter distinguishes between a high-performance airplane (more than 200 horsepower) and a complex airplane (retractable landing gear, flaps, and a controllable pitch propeller). The 200 hp threshold defines high-performance, not complex.

**12.2 [Function of Flaps, p.12-1, difficulty 2]**  
How does flap deflection affect the critical (stall) angle of attack?

- ✅ **Flap deflection does not increase the critical AOA and may actually decrease it**
- Flap deflection increases the critical AOA, allowing a higher stall angle
- Flap deflection increases the critical AOA only when combined with slotted flaps
- Flap deflection has no effect on the critical AOA in any case

  > The chapter explicitly states that flap deflection does not increase the critical AOA and in some cases actually decreases it. The distractor claiming 'no effect in any case' is wrong because the chapter notes it can decrease the critical AOA.

**12.3 [Function of Flaps, p.12-2, difficulty 2]**  
What is the aerodynamic effect of deflecting flaps up to 15° versus beyond 15°?

- ✅ **Up to 15° primarily produces lift with minimal drag; beyond 15° produces a large increase in drag**
- Up to 15° produces maximum drag; beyond 15° primarily increases lift
- Drag increases proportionally at all flap deflection angles
- Up to 15° produces no drag; beyond 15° drag increases exponentially

  > The chapter states that flap deflection up to 15° primarily produces lift with minimal drag increases, while deflection beyond 15° produces a large increase in drag. This is why small flap deflections are useful for takeoff and larger deflections for landing.

**12.4 [Flap Effectiveness, p.12-4, difficulty 1]**  
Which type of trailing-edge flap produces the maximum lift coefficient?

- ✅ **Fowler flap**
- Plain flap
- Split flap
- Slotted flap

  > The chapter states that the Fowler flap deflects down and aft to increase wing area, can be multi-slotted, and gives the maximum lift coefficient of the four basic trailing-edge flap types.

**12.5 [Operational Procedures, p.12-4, difficulty 2]**  
With flaps extended in a crosswind during landing, which wing is more affected by the wind and why?

- ✅ **The upwind wing, because the deflected flap presents a larger surface area for the wind to act on**
- The downwind wing, because it is more exposed to the crosswind
- Both wings equally, because flaps are deployed symmetrically
- The downwind wing, because the lowered aileron blocks airflow to the upwind wing

  > The chapter states that with flaps extended in a crosswind, the wing on the upwind side is more affected than the downwind wing because the deflected flap presents a surface area for the wind to act on.

**12.6 [Operational Procedures, p.12-4, difficulty 3]**  
During a go-around with full power applied, why does the pitch-up tendency not completely diminish even as flaps are retracted?

- ✅ **The trim setting used to offset the nose-down pitching moment from flap extension contributes to the pitch-up**
- The horizontal tail loses effectiveness at high power settings
- The constant-speed propeller increases airflow over the tail surfaces unevenly
- The landing gear retraction causes a forward center of gravity shift

  > The chapter explains that trim is used to offset the nose-down pitching moment from flap extension. When full power is applied in a go-around, increased airflow produces additional lift and significant pitch changes, and the pitch-up tendency does not diminish completely with flap retraction because of the trim setting.

**12.7 [Constant-Speed Propeller, p.12-5, difficulty 1]**  
For maximum takeoff power, what position should the propeller control be placed in?

- ✅ **Low pitch/high rpm**
- High pitch/low rpm
- Mid-range pitch and rpm
- High pitch/high rpm

  > The chapter states that for maximum takeoff power, the propeller control is moved all the way forward to the low pitch/high rpm position, and the throttle is moved forward to the maximum allowable manifold pressure. Low blade pitch keeps the blade AOA small and efficient at low speed.

**12.8 [Constant-Speed Propeller Operation, p.12-8, difficulty 2]**  
When decreasing power on an engine equipped with a constant-speed propeller, what sequence should be followed?

- ✅ **Decrease manifold pressure first, then decrease rpm**
- Decrease rpm first, then decrease manifold pressure
- Decrease both simultaneously
- Decrease rpm, increase manifold pressure, then decrease manifold pressure

  > The chapter specifies that a combination of high manifold pressure and low rpm can induce damaging detonation. To avoid this, when decreasing power, the pilot should decrease the manifold pressure first and then decrease the rpm.

**12.9 [Constant-Speed Propeller Operation, p.12-9, difficulty 2]**  
Besides indicating maximum allowable rpm, what else does the red line on the tachometer indicate?

- ✅ **The rpm required to obtain the engine's rated horsepower**
- The maximum manifold pressure allowed for takeoff
- The rpm at which the propeller governor reaches its high pitch stop
- The engine's critical altitude rpm

  > The chapter states that the red line on the tachometer serves a dual purpose: it indicates both maximum allowable rpm and the rpm required to obtain the engine's rated horsepower. This is why a constant-speed propeller airplane can develop maximum rated horsepower even while motionless on the runway.

**12.10 [Constant-Speed Propeller Operation, p.12-8, difficulty 2]**  
Why should the propeller be exercised through a full cycle during engine warm-up before takeoff?

- ✅ **To verify the system is operating correctly and to circulate fresh warm oil through the propeller governor system**
- To test the engine's maximum rpm output before takeoff
- To calibrate the tachometer against the manifold pressure gauge
- To verify the propeller governor can reach its high pitch stop

  > The chapter states this procedure is done for two reasons: to determine whether the system is operating correctly and to circulate fresh warm oil through the propeller governor system. Oil trapped in the propeller cylinder since shutdown may have leaked or congealed, and if not exercised, the engine may over-speed on takeoff.

**12.11 [Governing Range, p.12-8, difficulty 3]**  
What happens when the propeller blades reach their low pitch stop during a decrease in airspeed?

- ✅ **Any further reduction in airspeed causes the engine rpm to decrease, similar to a fixed-pitch propeller**
- The governor automatically increases the blade angle to maintain the selected rpm
- The engine over-speeds until the pilot reduces the throttle
- The propeller feathers automatically to prevent over-speed

  > The chapter explains that once propeller blades reach their pitch-stop limit, the engine rpm increases or decreases with changes in airspeed similar to a fixed-pitch propeller. If airspeed decreases enough that the blades contact the low pitch stops, any further reduction in airspeed causes engine rpm to decrease.

**12.12 [Turbocharging, p.12-10, difficulty 2]**  
What is the purpose of the waste gate in a turbocharger system?

- ✅ **To divert some exhaust gases overboard before they pass through the turbine, preventing excessive manifold pressure**
- To regulate the fuel-to-air ratio entering the engine cylinders
- To control the speed of the compressor by adjusting induction air intake
