- To release excess compressed air from the induction manifold

  > The chapter states that the turbine can produce manifold pressure in excess of the maximum allowable, so a waste gate diverts some exhaust overboard before it passes through the turbine. The waste gate position regulates turbine output and the compressed air available to the engine.

**12.13 [Ground Boosting Versus Altitude Turbocharging, p.12-10, difficulty 2]**  
What happens to manifold pressure when climbing above the airplane's critical altitude?

- ✅ **Manifold pressure decreases as additional altitude is gained**
- Manifold pressure remains constant at the sea-level value
- Manifold pressure increases due to higher compressor speeds
- The waste gate opens fully to maintain manifold pressure

  > The chapter states that altitude turbocharging maintains maximum allowable sea level manifold pressure up to the critical altitude. Above the critical altitude, the turbocharger can no longer maintain that pressure, and manifold pressure decreases as additional altitude is gained.

**12.14 [Operating Characteristics, p.12-10, difficulty 3]**  
When the waste gate is closed, how does manifold pressure respond to an increase in engine rpm?

- ✅ **Manifold pressure increases, which is opposite to a normally aspirated engine**
- Manifold pressure decreases slightly, same as a normally aspirated engine
- Manifold pressure remains constant regardless of rpm changes
- Manifold pressure increases only if the throttle is also advanced

  > The chapter states that when the waste gate is closed, manifold pressure variation with engine rpm is the opposite of a normally aspirated engine. An increase in engine rpm results in an increase in manifold pressure, and a decrease in engine rpm results in a decrease in manifold pressure.

**12.15 [Heat Management, p.12-11, difficulty 2]**  
At any given cruise power, which control is the most influential over exhaust gas temperature or turbine inlet temperature?

- ✅ **The mixture**
- The throttle
- The propeller rpm
- The cowl flaps

  > The chapter states that at any given cruise power, the mixture is the most influential control over the exhaust gas/TIT. The throttle regulates total fuel flow, but the mixture governs the fuel-to-air ratio, which directly controls temperature.

**12.16 [Turbocharger Failure, p.12-11, difficulty 2]**  
A low manifold pressure condition in a turbocharged engine may indicate what potentially hazardous situation?

- ✅ **A serious exhaust leak**
- A minor waste gate malfunction that can be ignored until the next maintenance cycle
- Normal operation at high altitude with the waste gate open
- A clogged fuel injector that requires mixture adjustment

  > The chapter states that although a low manifold pressure condition may be caused by a minor fault, it is quite possible that a serious exhaust leak has occurred, creating a potentially hazardous situation. The recommended procedure is to shut down the engine or use the lowest power setting and land as soon as practicable.

**12.17 [Landing Gear Safety Devices, p.12-14, difficulty 1]**  
What is the function of the landing gear safety switch (squat switch)?

- ✅ **When the strut is compressed by the weight of the airplane, it opens the electrical circuit to prevent gear retraction on the ground**
- It prevents the landing gear from extending while the airplane is in flight
- It locks the landing gear in the down and locked position after touchdown
- It activates the gear warning horn when the gear is in transit

  > The chapter states that a squat switch is mounted on a main gear shock strut. When the strut is compressed by the weight of the airplane, the switch opens the electrical circuit to the motor or mechanism that powers retraction, preventing accidental gear retraction while on the ground.

**12.18 [Approach and Landing, p.12-16, difficulty 2]**  
What is the difference between VLO and VLE?

- ✅ **VLO is the maximum speed at which the gear may be operated through its cycle; VLE is the maximum speed at which the airplane can be flown with the gear extended**
- VLO is the maximum speed for landing; VLE is the maximum speed for takeoff
- VLO applies to the nose gear only; VLE applies to the main gear only
- VLO is the maximum speed with gear down; VLE is the maximum speed with gear up

  > The chapter defines VLE as the maximum speed at which the airplane can be flown with the landing gear extended, and VLO as the maximum speed at which the landing gear may be operated through its cycle. These speeds protect gear components from becoming overstressed.


## Chapter 13 — 18 questions

**13.1 [General, p.13-1, difficulty 1]**  
In a multiengine airplane, loss of thrust from one engine reduces climb performance by approximately what percentage?

- ✅ **80 to 90 percent**
- 50 percent, equal to the power lost
- 25 to 30 percent, since the remaining engine compensates
- 100 percent, since climb is always impossible after an engine failure

  > The chapter explains that while an engine failure means a 50 percent loss of power, the reduction in climb performance is far more dramatic—80 to 90 percent—because climb depends on excess thrust above that required for level flight.

**13.2 [Terms and Definitions, p.13-2, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, which statement about VMC is correct?

- ✅ **VMC only addresses directional control; there is no requirement that the airplane be capable of climbing at this airspeed.**
- VMC guarantees the airplane can maintain altitude with one engine inoperative.
- VMC is the speed at which the airplane can maintain a 50 fpm rate of climb with one engine inoperative.
- VMC is the minimum speed at which the airplane can maintain directional control with both engines operating.

  > The chapter is explicit that VMC is solely about directional control following the sudden critical loss of thrust. There is no climb requirement at VMC under either the current or historical certification definitions.

**13.3 [Operation of Systems — Feathering Propellers, p.13-5, difficulty 2]**  
On most multiengine airplanes, the constant-speed propellers are full-feathering designs. What happens to the propeller blades when oil pressure from the governor is lost?

- ✅ **Counterweights and spring or dome pressure drive the blades toward the feathered position.**
- Oil pressure loss causes the blades to move to the low-pitch, high-rpm position.
- The blades remain in their current position until the pilot manually feathers them.
- Aerodynamic forces alone drive the blades to the flat-pitch position.

  > The chapter explains that most multiengine propellers are oil-pressure-to-decrease-pitch designs. Oil pressure keeps blades at low pitch/high rpm; when oil pressure is lost, counterweights (and then spring or dome pressure) drive blades toward feather.

**13.4 [Operation of Systems — Propeller Synchronization, p.13-7, difficulty 1]**  
Under what conditions should the propeller synchronizer (prop sync) always be turned off?

- ✅ **For takeoff, landing, and any single-engine operation**
- Only during cruise flight at altitude above 10,000 feet
- During engine start and taxi only
- When descending below pattern altitude with both engines operating

  > The chapter clearly states the prop sync should always be off for takeoff, landing, and single-engine operation. It should also be disengaged when selecting a new propeller rpm and re-engaged after the new rpm is set.

**13.5 [Operation of Systems — Fuel Crossfeed, p.13-7, difficulty 2]**  
On most multiengine airplanes, how is fuel crossfeed operation classified?

- ✅ **As an emergency procedure used to extend range and endurance during one-engine-inoperative flight**
- As a normal fuel-balancing technique used routinely during cruise with both engines operating
- As a standard procedure used during takeoff to ensure equal fuel distribution
- As a required procedure for all single-engine approaches and landings

  > The chapter states that on most multiengine airplanes, crossfeed is an emergency procedure for OEI flight. Only a few models permit crossfeed as a normal fuel balancing technique. Pilots should never use crossfeed during takeoff or normal landing operations with both engines operating.

**13.6 [Operation of Systems — Anti-Icing/Deicing Equipment, p.13-10, difficulty 2]**  
Unless otherwise recommended in the AFM/POH, why should the autopilot not be used in icing conditions?

- ✅ **Continuous autopilot use masks trim and handling changes from ice accumulation, and the autopilot may suddenly disconnect at design limits, leaving the pilot with unsatisfactory handling characteristics.**
- The autopilot servos can freeze in icing conditions, causing loss of all flight control.
- The autopilot competes with deicing equipment for electrical power and may cause system failure.
- The autopilot is not certified for operation in any known or suspected icing conditions.

  > The chapter explains that continuous autopilot use masks the trim and handling feedback that would alert a pilot to ice accumulation. When the autopilot reaches its design limits and disconnects, the pilot may be confronted with an airplane that has hazardous handling characteristics.

**13.7 [Performance and Limitations, p.13-10, difficulty 2]**  
The accelerate-go distance, as defined in the AFM/POH, brings the airplane to what altitude above the takeoff elevation?

- ✅ **50 feet**
- 500 feet AGL
- 1,000 feet AGL
- Pattern altitude

  > The chapter defines accelerate-go distance as the horizontal distance required to continue the takeoff and climb to 50 feet, assuming an engine failure at VR or VLOF. The chapter emphasizes this is a meager climb achieved under ideal conditions.

**13.8 [Weight and Balance, p.13-13, difficulty 2]**  
What is the purpose of a zero fuel weight limitation, as published in some multiengine airplane AFM/POHs?

- ✅ **To limit load forces on the wing spars with heavy fuselage loads**
- To ensure the airplane does not exceed maximum landing weight upon arrival
- To prevent the center of gravity from shifting aft of limits during flight
- To limit the total weight of usable fuel that can be loaded on board

  > The chapter defines zero fuel weight as the maximum allowable weight of the airplane and payload assuming no usable fuel. All weight in excess of zero fuel weight should consist of usable fuel. The purpose is to limit load forces on the wing spars when carrying heavy fuselage loads.

**13.9 [Normal and Crosswind Takeoff and Climb, p.13-15, difficulty 2]**  
During takeoff in a multiengine airplane, what is the minimum airspeed at which the airplane should become airborne?

- ✅ **VMC**
- VSSE
- VYSE
- VREF

  > The chapter states that if an engine fails below VMC while airborne, directional control is not possible with the remaining engine at takeoff power. Therefore, the airplane should never be airborne before the airspeed exceeds VMC. If no manufacturer's rotation speed is published, a minimum of VMC plus 5 knots should be used for VR.

**13.10 [Spin Awareness and Stalls, p.13-18, difficulty 1]**  
Which statement correctly describes the spin approval and recovery characteristics of multiengine airplanes?

- ✅ **No multiengine airplane is approved for spins, and their spin recovery characteristics are generally very poor.**
- Multiengine airplanes are approved for spins if operated within normal category weight and CG limits.
- Multiengine airplanes may be spun with both engines operating, but not with one engine inoperative.
- Spins are permitted in multiengine airplanes only when a qualified flight instructor is present.

  > The chapter is unambiguous: no multiengine airplane is approved for spins, and spin recovery characteristics are generally very poor. Very few twins have ever been spin-tested, and none are required to be.

**13.11 [Engine Inoperative Flight Principles — Derivation of VMC, p.13-25, difficulty 3]**  
How does bank angle affect VMC, as determined during aircraft certification?

- ✅ **VMC increases as bank angle decreases, potentially by more than 3 knots for each degree of bank reduction between 5° and wings-level.**
- VMC decreases as bank angle decreases because the airplane is more stable in level flight.
- Bank angle has no measurable effect on VMC during certification testing.
- VMC is lowest at wings level because the rudder is most effective without any bank.

  > The chapter explains that VMC is highly sensitive to bank angle. The manufacturer may use up to 5° of bank toward the operative engine during certification, which lowers VMC. As bank angle decreases toward wings-level, VMC increases significantly—more than 3 knots per degree of bank reduction between 5° and wings-level.

**13.12 [Engine Inoperative Flight Principles — OEI Climb Performance, p.13-29, difficulty 3]**  
To achieve zero sideslip and best single-engine climb performance, approximately what bank angle and slip/skid indicator ball position should be established?

- ✅ **Approximately 2° bank toward the operative engine with the ball deflected one-third to one-half toward the operative engine**
- Wings level with the ball centered, using rudder alone to counteract yaw
- 8 to 10° bank toward the operative engine with no rudder input
- 5° bank toward the inoperative engine with the ball centered

  > The chapter describes three scenarios. Wings-level with centered ball creates moderate sideslip and reduced climb. Excessive bank with no rudder creates large sideslip and greatly reduced climb. The correct combination is approximately 2° bank toward the operative engine with the ball deflected one-third to one-half toward the operative engine, achieving zero sideslip.

**13.13 [Low Altitude Engine Failure Scenarios, p.13-30, difficulty 3]**  
If an engine failure occurs shortly after takeoff and the landing gear selector is still in the DOWN position, what action does the chapter recommend?

- ✅ **Close both throttles, maintain directional control, and land on the remaining runway or overrun**
- Retract the landing gear, feather the failed engine, and continue the climb at VYSE
- Reduce power on the operative engine and attempt a turn back to the airport
- Apply full rudder, maintain heading, and accelerate to VYSE before reconfiguring

  > The chapter states that with the landing gear down, continued takeoff and climb is not recommended. The chances of maintaining directional control while retracting flaps, raising gear, feathering the propeller, and accelerating are minimal. The recommended action is to close both throttles and land straight ahead.

**13.14 [Low Altitude Engine Failure Scenarios — Control, p.13-33, difficulty 2]**  
The memory aid 'dead foot, dead engine' helps identify the failed engine. What is the correct interpretation of this phrase?

- ✅ **The foot not applying rudder pressure is on the same side as the failed engine, because rudder pressure is exerted on the side of the operating engine.**
- The foot that feels no vibration through the rudder pedal indicates the failed engine side.
- The rudder pedal that cannot be moved is on the same side as the failed engine.
- The foot applying the most pressure is on the side of the failed engine.

  > The chapter explains that in maintaining directional control, rudder pressure is exerted on the side with the operating engine. The 'dead foot'—the one not applying pressure—is on the same side as the 'dead engine.' This helps identify the failed engine quickly.

**13.15 [Engine Inoperative Approach and Landing, p.13-35, difficulty 2]**  
During a single-engine approach, why might a single-engine go-around not be possible once the landing gear and flaps are extended on final approach?

- ✅ **Most light-twins lack the performance to climb on one engine with gear and flaps extended, and considerable altitude is lost while retracting them.**
- VMC increases with flaps extended, making directional control impossible during a go-around.
- The operative engine cannot produce enough power with the landing gear doors open.
- The single-engine service ceiling is below pattern altitude, preventing any climb.

  > The chapter states that once on final with gear and flaps extended, the airplane is committed to land. Most light-twins cannot climb on one engine in this configuration. Losses of 500 feet or more are not unusual while retracting gear and flaps, and if alternate gear extension was used, retraction may not be possible at all.

**13.16 [Multiengine Training Considerations, p.13-35, difficulty 2]**  
The FAA recommends that all in-flight simulated engine failures below 3,000 feet AGL be introduced using what method?

- ✅ **A smooth reduction of the throttle**
- Pulling the mixture control to idle cutoff
- Turning off the fuel selector for the affected engine
- Pulling the propeller control fully aft to the feather position

  > The chapter states the FAA recommends smooth throttle reduction for simulated engine failures below 3,000 feet AGL. This keeps the engine running and available for instant use, avoids engine abuse, and avoids the high and unnecessary training risk of simulating failures below VSSE.

**13.17 [Engine Inoperative Flight Principles — VMC Demo, p.13-26, difficulty 2]**  
During a VMC demonstration, what should the pilot do upon first recognizing uncontrollable yaw or experiencing any symptom associated with a stall?

- ✅ **Simultaneously retard the throttle for the operating engine and lower the pitch attitude to regain speed**
- Apply full rudder opposite the yaw and increase power on the operating engine
- Add full power to the idle engine to counteract the asymmetrical thrust
- Roll into a steeper bank toward the operative engine to gain more rudder authority

  > The chapter specifies that the moment the pilot recognizes uncontrollable yaw or any stall symptom, the recovery is to simultaneously retard the operating engine throttle to stop the yaw and lower the pitch attitude to regain speed. Recovery is made to straight flight at VSSE or VYSE.

**13.18 [Short-Field Takeoff and Climb, p.13-17, difficulty 3]**  
When planning a short-field takeoff, if VX is less than 5 knots higher than VMC, what does the chapter recommend?

- ✅ **Give strong consideration to reducing useful load or using another runway so that a short-field technique is not required**
- Proceed with the short-field takeoff but rotate at VMC plus 10 knots for additional margin
- Use partial flaps to increase the margin above VMC during the climb
- Delay rotation until VYSE to ensure adequate directional control is available

  > The chapter warns that VX and VXSE are often perilously close to VMC during short-field takeoffs, leaving scant margin for error. If VX is less than 5 knots higher than VMC, the pilot should strongly consider reducing useful load or using another runway to increase takeoff margins.


## Chapter 14 — 18 questions

**14.1 [Instability, p.14-1, difficulty 2]**  
Why are tailwheel airplanes inherently unstable on the ground during turns?

- ✅ **The CG is aft of the main gear pivot point, so forward momentum acts to continue and tighten the turn**
- The CG is forward of the main gear, causing the airplane to naturally straighten out
- The tailwheel is too small to provide adequate friction against turning forces
- The main gear struts are too close together to resist lateral forces

  > The chapter states that because the CG is aft of the main gear pivot point, forward momentum acts to continue and even tighten a turn once started. This is the fundamental source of ground instability in tailwheel designs.

**14.2 [Instability, p.14-1, difficulty 1]**  
When taxiing a tailwheel airplane, what action is required to stop a turn that has been started?

- ✅ **Apply opposite rudder input**
- Neutralize the rudder pedals and the turn will stop on its own
- Apply brake pressure on the inside wheel
- Reduce throttle to idle and the turn will stop naturally

  > The chapter explicitly states that removal of rudder pressure does not stop a turn that has been started, and it is necessary to apply opposite rudder to bring the aircraft back to straight-line travel.

**14.3 [Angle of Attack, p.14-2, difficulty 2]**  
What characteristic of tailwheel airplanes creates lift while on the ground, even at slow taxi speeds?

- ✅ **The nose-high resting attitude produces a positive angle of attack**
- The propeller slipstream generates lift over the wings at all power settings
- The tailwheel design uses vortex generators to create low-speed lift
- The main gear position forces air over the wing at taxi speeds

  > The chapter explains that due to the built-in AOA from the nose-high attitude, tailwheel aircraft make lift while on the ground anytime there is a relative headwind, even at slow taxi speeds.

**14.4 [Taxiing, p.14-2, difficulty 1]**  
What is the proper procedure for testing brakes when beginning to taxi a tailwheel airplane?

- ✅ **Apply power to start moving slowly forward, then retard throttle and apply both brakes smoothly**
- Apply full power briefly, then test both brakes while holding position
- Test each brake individually while holding the tailwheel straight with rudder
- Accelerate to normal taxi speed, then apply brakes firmly to test stopping power

  > The chapter specifies the procedure: first apply power to start moving slowly forward, then retard the throttle and simultaneously apply pressure smoothly to both brakes. If braking is unsatisfactory, shut down immediately.

**14.5 [Taxiing, p.14-2, difficulty 2]**  
When taxiing a tailwheel airplane in a quartering headwind, what control inputs are generally recommended?

- ✅ **Stick into the wind and elevator full back**
- Stick away from the wind and elevator full forward
- Stick into the wind and elevator neutral
- Stick away from the wind and elevator full back

  > The chapter states that when taxiing in a quartering headwind, the upwind wing can be lifted by gusting winds unless ailerons are positioned to kill lift (stick into the wind). Elevator should usually be held full back to add downward pressure to the tailwheel and improve steering response.

**14.6 [Taxiing, p.14-2, difficulty 3]**  
When taxiing a tailwheel airplane in light tailwind conditions, what elevator position is likely most appropriate for directional control?

- ✅ **Back stick, because propeller wash usually overcomes the light tailwind to produce a net headwind over the tail**
- Full forward stick, because any tailwind requires forward pressure to keep the tail down
- Neutral elevator, because light winds have negligible effect on tailwheel steering
- Full forward stick, because tailwind striking the up-deflected elevator could lift the tail

  > The chapter explains that while full forward stick is essential in strong tailwinds, in light tailwinds the propeller wash is usually strong enough to overcome the tailwind, producing a net headwind over the tail. This means back stick, not forward, does the most to help directional control.

**14.7 [Visibility, p.14-3, difficulty 1]**  
How should a pilot taxi a tailwheel airplane that is completely blind ahead due to the nose-high attitude?

- ✅ **Make a series of slow S-turns to alternately see the area ahead**
- Taxi at higher speed to reduce the time spent in blind spots
- Make sharp turns only to the left, keeping the right side clear for traffic
- Rely on ground personnel for marshalling and do not turn the nose

  > The chapter states that in aircraft completely blind ahead, the pilot should alternately turn the nose from one side to the other (zigzag) or make a series of short S-turns, done slowly, smoothly, positively, and cautiously.

**14.8 [Normal Takeoff Roll, p.14-3, difficulty 2]**  
What causes left yaw when the tail is raised during a tailwheel takeoff roll?

- ✅ **Gyroscopic precession from the propeller**
- Asymmetric thrust from the propeller at high pitch angles
- Spiraling slipstream striking the rudder from the right
- Engine torque causing the left wing to drop

  > The chapter specifically identifies the left yaw during tail-raising as the result of gyroscopic precession created by the propeller. The force is directly related to the rate the propeller axis is tilted when the tail is raised.

**14.9 [Liftoff, p.14-3, difficulty 2]**  
During a takeoff in strong, gusty winds in a tailwheel airplane, what technique is recommended for liftoff?

- ✅ **Hold the airplane on the ground longer to attain more speed, then make a smooth, positive rotation**
- Lift off at normal takeoff speed in a three-point attitude to minimize time on the runway
- Use a soft-field technique, holding the tail low throughout the takeoff roll
- Rotate early and climb at best angle of speed to exit gusty conditions quickly

  > The chapter advises that in strong, gusty winds, taking off at normal speed may result in loss of control or stall when encountering a sudden lull. The pilot should hold the airplane on the ground longer to attain more speed, then make a smooth, positive rotation.

**14.10 [Wheel Landing, p.14-6, difficulty 2]**  
What is the only difference between a three-point landing and a wheel landing in a tailwheel airplane?

- ✅ **The timing of the touchdown**
- The approach airspeed used for the landing
- The flap configuration on final approach
- The power setting used during the roundout

  > The chapter explicitly states that the only difference between three-point and wheel landings is the timing of the touchdown (early and later). There is no difference between the approach angles and airspeeds in the two techniques.

**14.11 [Three-Point Landing, p.14-5, difficulty 2]**  
What should the pilot do with elevator control once the tailwheel makes contact with the runway during a three-point landing?

- ✅ **Ease the elevator control fully back to press the tailwheel on the runway**
- Relax elevator pressure to neutral to allow the tailwheel to settle naturally
- Apply slight forward elevator to transfer weight to the main wheels
- Hold elevator position steady to maintain the current pitch attitude

  > The chapter states that once the tailwheel contacts the runway, the elevator control should be eased fully back to press the tailwheel on the runway. Without this input, lift on the horizontal stabilizer lightens tailwheel pressure and renders it useless for directional control.

**14.12 [Three-Point Landing, p.14-5, difficulty 2]**  
What occurs if the main wheels make runway contact slightly early with the tail still in the air during a three-point landing attempt?

- ✅ **The tail drops, AOA increases, and the plane may become airborne again (a skip)**
- The tailwheel slams down causing possible structural damage to the tail
- The airplane pitches forward onto its nose due to the CG location
- The airplane immediately settles onto all three wheels and stops flying

  > The chapter describes this as the first potential error in a three-point landing. With the CG aft of the main wheels, the tail naturally drops when the main wheels touch, AOA increases, and the plane may become airborne again, causing a skip.

**14.13 [Wheel Landing, p.14-6, difficulty 3]**  
What is the recommended response if a wheel landing results in a bounce due to too high a rate of descent?

- ✅ **Initiate a go-around or convert to a three-point landing if conditions permit**
- Push forward on the elevator to re-establish main wheel contact with the runway
- Apply full back elevator to convert immediately to a three-point attitude
- Apply differential braking to maintain directional control and force the tail down

  > The chapter warns that if the pilot pushes forward after a bounced wheel landing, a potentially dangerous pilot-induced oscillation may develop. It is far better to initiate a go-around or convert to a three-point landing if conditions permit.

**14.14 [Crosswind After-Landing Roll, p.14-7, difficulty 2]**  
How should aileron input be adjusted during the crosswind after-landing roll as the airplane decelerates?

- ✅ **Increase aileron deflection into the wind as speed decreases, reaching full deflection when stopped**
- Decrease aileron deflection as the airplane slows to avoid overcontrolling
- Maintain a constant aileron deflection throughout the landing roll
- Reverse aileron deflection from into the wind to away from the wind as speed decreases

  > The chapter explains that as the airplane slows, ailerons become less effective and the relative wind becomes more of a crosswind, exerting greater lifting force on the upwind wing. Therefore, more aileron should be applied, reaching fully into the wind when coming to a stop.

**14.15 [Crosswind After-Landing Roll, p.14-7, difficulty 2]**  
Why do tailwheel airplanes have a greater weathervaning tendency during the after-landing roll compared to nose-wheel airplanes?

- ✅ **Tailwheel airplanes have a greater surface area behind the main landing gear than forward of it**
- The tailwheel provides less friction on the runway than a nosewheel
- The CG is farther forward in tailwheel airplanes, increasing weathervane tendency
- The higher pitch angle exposes more wing area to crosswinds

  > The chapter explains that an airplane has greater profile area behind the main landing gear than forward of it. With the main wheels as a pivot point, this greater surface area exposed to crosswind behind the pivot causes weathervaning. This tendency is more prevalent in tailwheel airplanes because their surface area behind the main gear is greater than in nose-wheel types.

**14.16 [Soft-Field Landing, p.14-8, difficulty 2]**  
In a soft-field landing in a tailwheel airplane, when should the tailwheel contact the surface relative to the main wheels?

- ✅ **Simultaneously with or just before the main wheels**
- Well after the main wheels to ensure directional control is established
- Only after the main wheels have settled into the soft surface
- After the main wheels touch and the elevator is relaxed to neutral

  > The chapter states that for a soft-field landing, the tailwheel should touchdown simultaneously with or just before the main wheels and be held down with firm back-elevator pressure throughout the roll. This minimizes nose-over tendency and provides aerodynamic braking.

**14.17 [Ground Loop, p.14-8, difficulty 2]**  
What is the initial event that leads to a ground loop in a tailwheel airplane?

- ✅ **A swerve that is allowed to continue for too long**
- A sudden crosswind gust that exceeds rudder authority
- Brake failure on one side during the landing roll
- Touching down with excessive airspeed in a flat attitude

  > The chapter defines a ground loop as starting with a swerve that is allowed to continue for too long. The swerve may result from side-load on landing, a taxi turn started with too much groundspeed, overcorrection, or uneven ground surface.

**14.18 [Ground Loop, p.14-8, difficulty 3]**  
When correcting a swerve to prevent a ground loop, what is critical regarding the removal of rudder and braking inputs?

- ✅ **Remove inputs promptly as corrections become apparent to avoid starting a departure in the opposite direction**
- Hold inputs until the airplane is completely straight and at taxi speed
- Gradually reduce inputs over several seconds to smooth the correction
- Remove braking first but hold full rudder until directional control is assured

  > The chapter emphasizes that as corrections begin to become apparent, rudder and braking inputs need to be removed promptly. Failing to do so risks starting yet another departure in the opposite direction, which could be equally dangerous.


## Chapter 15 — 18 questions

**15.1 [Gas Turbine Engine, p.15-1, difficulty 2]**  
How does the combustion process in a gas turbine engine differ from that in a piston (reciprocating) engine?

- ✅ **In a gas turbine engine, the phases of induction, compression, combustion, expansion, and exhaust occur simultaneously and continuously, with ignition occurring during starting and remaining continuous thereafter.**
- In a gas turbine engine, combustion occurs once per revolution of the main shaft, while in a piston engine it occurs every other revolution.
- In a gas turbine engine, combustion occurs only during the starting cycle and is then maintained by residual heat, while in a piston engine ignition occurs each cycle.
- Both engines have identical combustion cycles, but the gas turbine operates at higher compression ratios to achieve continuous power.

  > The chapter states that unlike reciprocating engines, where each event is a separate distinct occurrence in each cylinder, gas turbine engines conduct all phases of power simultaneously and continuously. Ignition occurs during the starting cycle and is continuous thereafter.

**15.2 [Turboprop Engines, p.15-2, difficulty 1]**  
At what speed and altitude range are turboprop engines most efficient?

- ✅ **Between 250 and 400 mph and between 18,000 and 30,000 feet.**
- Between 150 and 250 mph and between 10,000 and 18,000 feet.
- Between 400 and 500 mph and between 30,000 and 40,000 feet.
- Between 250 and 400 mph and between 10,000 and 18,000 feet.

  > The chapter explicitly states the efficient speed and altitude ranges for turboprop engines, noting that the minimum specific fuel consumption is normally available in the altitude range of 25,000 feet up to the tropopause.

**15.3 [Turboprop Engines, p.15-2, difficulty 2]**  
What is equivalent shaft horsepower (eshp) in a turboprop engine?

- ✅ **Shaft horsepower plus jet thrust produced by exhaust leaving the engine.**
- Shaft horsepower minus the power required to drive the engine's compressor section.
- The total horsepower measured at the propeller shaft before reduction gearing.
- Shaft horsepower multiplied by the propeller's efficiency percentage.

  > The chapter explains that shaft horsepower is determined by rpm and torque applied to the propeller shaft. Since turboprops are gas turbine engines, some jet thrust is also produced, and this thrust is added to shaft horsepower to determine eshp. Jet thrust usually accounts for less than 10 percent of total engine power.

**15.4 [Turboprop Engines, p.15-2, difficulty 2]**  
In cold weather conditions, which engine operating limit is most likely to be exceeded first when advancing the power lever on a turboprop engine?

- ✅ **Torque limits can be exceeded while temperature limits are still within acceptable range.**
- Temperature limits may be exceeded without exceeding torque limits.
- Fuel flow limits will be reached before any other operating limit.
- RPM limits will be exceeded before torque or temperature limits.

  > The chapter explains that in cold weather, dense air allows the engine to produce more torque, so torque limits can be exceeded while temperature limits remain acceptable. In hot weather, the opposite occurs—temperature limits may be exceeded without exceeding torque limits. Reaching one operating limit normally occurs before the throttle reaches full forward.

**15.5 [Turboprop Engine Types - Fixed-Shaft, p.15-3, difficulty 2]**  
In a fixed-shaft constant-speed turboprop engine, how are power changes accomplished during flight?

- ✅ **By increasing fuel flow and propeller blade angle while engine speed remains constant.**
- By varying engine rpm within a wide range from 70 to 100 percent.
- By adjusting the condition lever to change the fuel metering rate.
- By increasing engine rpm and decreasing propeller blade angle simultaneously.

  > The chapter explains that in the fixed-shaft constant-speed engine, the engine operates at a constant speed maintained by the propeller governor. Power changes are made by increasing fuel flow, which increases temperature and energy to the turbine, producing more torque. The increased torque forces the propeller blade angle to increase to maintain constant speed.

**15.6 [Turboprop Engine Types - Fixed-Shaft, p.15-5, difficulty 3]**  
What is the purpose of the negative torque sensing (NTS) system on a fixed-shaft turboprop engine?

- ✅ **To limit the torque the engine can extract from the propeller during windmilling by automatically moving the blades toward their feathered position.**
- To automatically feather the propeller when the engine loses oil pressure during flight.
- To prevent the propeller from overspeeding during a sudden power reduction.
- To provide reverse thrust capability during landing rollout if the condition lever fails.

  > The chapter describes NTS as an emergency backup system that activates when the engine suddenly loses power. It limits the torque the failed engine can extract from the windmilling propeller, moving blades toward feather to reduce drag. It is not a substitute for the feathering device controlled by the condition lever.

**15.7 [Split-Shaft/Free Turbine Engine, p.15-5, difficulty 2]**  
What is a key operational advantage of the split-shaft/free turbine engine design over the fixed-shaft design?

- ✅ **The propeller can be feathered in flight or on the ground with the basic engine still running.**
- The engine operates at a higher maximum rpm, producing significantly more thrust.
- The propeller is directly connected to the compressor for faster power response.
- The engine has fewer moving parts and is lighter than a fixed-shaft engine.

  > The chapter states that in a free power-turbine engine, the propeller is driven by a separate turbine and is not on the same shaft as the engine turbine and compressor. This design allows the propeller to be feathered while the basic engine continues to run, and allows the pilot to select a desired propeller governing rpm independent of basic engine rpm.

**15.8 [Split-Shaft/Free Turbine Engine, p.15-6, difficulty 2]**  
On a split-shaft/free turbine engine, what is the function of the condition lever?

- ✅ **It acts as an on/off valve for fuel delivery to the engine, with HIGH IDLE and LOW IDLE positions for ground operations, but has no metering function.**
- It meters fuel to the engine like a mixture control on a piston engine, allowing the pilot to lean the mixture.
- It sets the desired propeller governing rpm through the primary governor.
- It directly controls propeller blade angle when operating in the beta range.

  > The chapter explains that the condition lever on a free turbine engine controls fuel flow but is really just an on/off valve. It has HIGH IDLE and LOW IDLE positions for ground operations but no metering function. Leaning is not required in turbine engines; this function is performed automatically by a dedicated fuel control unit. The propeller lever controls propeller rpm, and the power lever controls beta range blade angle.

**15.9 [Reverse Thrust and Beta Range Operations, p.15-9, difficulty 2]**  
What characterizes the beta range of propeller operation in a turboprop engine?

- ✅ **The power lever directly controls propeller blade angle, with positions ranging from flight idle to maximum reverse.**
- The propeller governor maintains constant rpm while the power lever controls fuel flow to the engine.
- The propeller is in its maximum pitch angle to minimize drag during engine-out conditions.
- The propeller blades are positioned for maximum forward thrust efficiency at high airspeeds.

  > The chapter explains that when the power lever is positioned aft of flight idle, it directly controls propeller blade angle rather than engine fuel flow. This is the beta range, consisting of positions from flight idle to maximum reverse. In contrast, forward of flight idle is the alpha or governing mode, where the propeller governor selects pitch to maintain constant speed.

**15.10 [Reverse Thrust and Beta Range Operations, p.15-9, difficulty 1]**  
When a turboprop propeller is placed in reverse pitch, what occurs?

- ✅ **The engine continues to turn in the same direction, but the propeller blade angle is positioned to the other side of flat pitch, pushing air away from the airplane.**
- The engine reverses its rotation direction, causing the propeller to push air forward.
- The propeller is placed in flat pitch to create maximum drag without delivering engine power.
- The propeller blades are feathered to stop rotation and maximize drag on the airplane.

  > The chapter clarifies that reverse pitch does not mean reverse rotation. The engine delivers power in the same direction, but the propeller blade angle is positioned to the other side of flat pitch, causing air to be pushed away from the airplane rather than drawn over it, resulting in braking action.

**15.11 [Turboprop Airplane Electrical Systems, p.15-10, difficulty 1]**  
How does a starter/generator function in a turboprop airplane's electrical system?

- ✅ **It uses electrical power to produce mechanical torque to start the engine, then uses the engine's mechanical torque to produce electrical power after the engine is running.**
- It uses electrical power to start the engine and is then physically disconnected from the engine by a clutch.
- It uses hydraulic pressure to start the engine and then switches to producing electrical power from the engine.
- It uses battery power to start the engine and is then replaced in operation by a separate, dedicated generator.

  > The chapter describes starter/generators as DC generators that double as starter motors. They use electrical power to produce mechanical torque for engine starting, then reverse roles to use the engine's mechanical torque to produce electrical power once the engine is running.

**15.12 [Turboprop Airplane Electrical Systems, p.15-11, difficulty 2]**  
What is the function of a current limiter in a turboprop airplane's electrical power distribution system?

- ✅ **It protects buses from short circuits by opening the circuit to isolate the affected power source and separate the affected bus from the system when excessive current is supplied.**
- It reduces the current supplied to essential buses during normal operations to prevent equipment overload.
- It limits the maximum current output of the battery during engine start to prevent battery damage.
- It automatically transfers power from the main buses to the essential buses when generator output decreases.

  > The chapter describes current limiters as a type of fuse that protects power distribution buses from short circuits and malfunctions. When excessive current is supplied by any power source, the current limiter opens the circuit, isolating that power source and separating the affected bus from the system.

**15.13 [Operational Considerations, p.15-11, difficulty 2]**  
What should a transitioning pilot understand about power response when flying an airplane equipped with a split-shaft/free turbine engine?

- ✅ **Power output lags for several seconds when the power lever is moved from flight idle to a high power setting, and the last 30 percent of engine rpm represents the majority of engine thrust.**
- Power response is instantaneous, similar to a piston engine with a constant-speed propeller.
- Power output temporarily decreases when the power lever is advanced rapidly, then recovers.
- The engine responds immediately, but the propeller takes several seconds to adjust its blade angle.

  > The chapter explains that in a split-shaft/free turbine engine, power output lags for several seconds when the power lever is moved from flight idle to high power. This may surprise pilots transitioning from piston or fixed-shaft turboprop engines. The pilot should anticipate power changes and understand that the last 30 percent of engine rpm produces the majority of thrust.

**15.14 [Operational Considerations, p.15-12, difficulty 2]**  
What is a 'hot start' in a turbine engine, and what may be the first indication that one is occurring?

- ✅ **A condition where the engine tends to exceed maximum starting temperature limits, which may be preceded by unusually high initial fuel flow.**
- A start where engine temperature rises gradually but exceeds limits after the engine stabilizes at idle.
- A start where the engine accelerates too quickly due to excessive battery voltage supplied to the starter.
- A start where the engine reaches normal temperature but exhaust gas temperature exceeds cruise limits after takeoff.

  > The chapter defines a hot start as an engine tendency to exceed maximum starting temperature limits. After fuel is introduced during the start sequence, light-off and heat rise occur very quickly, and temperatures may approach maximum in 2 or 3 seconds. Unusually high initial fuel flow may be the first indication of a hot start, and serious engine damage occurs if it is allowed to continue.

**15.15 [Operational Considerations, p.15-12, difficulty 2]**  
What characterizes a 'hung start' or 'false start' in a turbine engine?

- ✅ **The engine accelerates more slowly than normal and may stabilize at an rpm too low for the engine to continue running without help from the starter.**
- The engine starts normally but then loses power shortly after the starter disengages.
- The engine temperature rises rapidly while the rpm remains fixed at the initial starting value.
- The engine starts but produces significantly less than rated power due to a malfunctioning fuel control unit.

  > The chapter describes a hung start as a condition where the engine is accelerating more slowly than normal. The engine may stabilize at an rpm that is not high enough for it to continue running without starter assistance. This is usually caused by low battery power or the starter not turning the engine fast enough.

**15.16 [Operational Considerations, p.15-12, difficulty 2]**  
How is takeoff power typically set in a turboprop airplane?

- ✅ **Takeoff power may be limited by either torque or engine temperature, and the power lever position is normally somewhat aft of full forward.**
- The power lever is advanced full forward to the stops to ensure maximum available power is delivered.
- Takeoff power is set by advancing the power lever until fuel flow reaches its maximum indicated value.
- The condition lever is advanced to set takeoff rpm while the power lever modulates fuel flow.

  > The chapter explicitly states that takeoffs in turboprop airplanes are not made by automatically pushing the power lever full forward. Depending on conditions, takeoff power may be limited by torque or engine temperature. The power lever position on takeoff is normally somewhat aft of full forward because reaching an operating limit typically occurs before the throttle reaches the full forward position.

**15.17 [Operational Considerations, p.15-13, difficulty 2]**  
Why might it be preferable to maintain power through the flare and touchdown when landing some turboprop airplanes?

- ✅ **Large propellers spinning rapidly in low pitch create considerable drag, and idling the engines too soon can result in a hard, premature touchdown.**
- The engines must be at operating temperature to ensure proper beta range operation after landing.
- Maintaining power prevents the constant-speed propeller from automatically feathering during the flare.
- The propeller needs engine power to maintain the correct blade angle for touchdown.

  > The chapter explains that large propellers in low pitch create considerable drag. If the engines are idled too soon during landing, this drag can cause a hard, premature touchdown. In some turboprop airplanes, it may be preferable to maintain power throughout the flare and touchdown, then use beta range operation once on the ground to reduce landing roll.

**15.18 [Training Considerations, p.15-13, difficulty 1]**  
What topics should thorough ground training cover for a pilot transitioning to turboprop airplanes?

- ✅ **High-altitude flight environment, weather, flight planning and navigation, physiological aspects of high-altitude flight, oxygen and pressurization system operation, and high-altitude emergencies.**
- Only aircraft systems and emergency procedures specific to the turboprop engine type being flown.
- Basic aerodynamics, weight and balance calculations, and standard traffic pattern procedures.
- Type-specific procedures for beta range operation, reverse thrust, and engine starting only.

  > The chapter emphasizes that the medium and high altitudes at which turboprops operate provide an entirely different environment. Ground training should cover all aspects of high/medium altitude flight, including the flight environment, weather, flight planning and navigation, physiological aspects, oxygen and pressurization system operation, and high-altitude emergencies.


## Chapter 16 — 18 questions

**16.1 [Ground Safety, p.16-1, difficulty 1]**  
What makes jet engines particularly hazardous to ground personnel even when operating at idle?

- ✅ **There is no visible propeller to indicate the engine is running, and intake suction at idle is sufficient to pull a nearby person into the fan.**
- Jet engines at idle produce unpredictable thrust surges that can blow a person down.
- The engine cowling reaches temperatures high enough to cause severe burns on contact.
- Jet fuel vapor around an idling engine is toxic and can cause immediate disorientation.

  > The chapter states there is no propeller to visually indicate whether a jet engine is running, and even at idle, enough air is being sucked into the intake to pull a nearby person into the fan. The exhaust is also hot and moving fast enough to blow a person down.

**16.2 [Jet Engine Basics, p.16-1, difficulty 2]**  
In a turbofan engine, what is the primary role of the bypass air that diverts around the engine core?

- ✅ **It produces some of the engine's thrust, particularly at lower speeds and altitudes.**
- It cools the turbine section to prevent temperature exceedances during high-power operations.
- It provides combustion air for the engine core during high-altitude operations.
- It reduces engine noise by insulating the exhaust gases from the surrounding air.

  > The chapter explains that the large first stage of a turbofan diverts some air around the engine core, and this cooler bypass air produces some of the thrust. The turbofan design increases thrust particularly at lower speeds and altitudes, improving acceleration and initial climb performance.

**16.3 [Thrust to Thrust Lever Relationship, p.16-2, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot needs to make a significant thrust increase from a low RPM setting in a jet engine. What is the proper technique?

- ✅ **Make a smooth and significant thrust lever position change, because inching the lever forward at low RPM will have little effect.**
- Advance the thrust lever in small increments to avoid exceeding temperature limits.
- Rapidly push the thrust lever to the full forward position to achieve maximum acceleration.
- Wait for the engine to stabilize at intermediate RPM before advancing the thrust lever further.

  > The chapter states that at low RPM, inching the thrust lever forward has little effect. The pilot needs to make a smooth and significant thrust lever position change. At high engine speeds, thrust output changes much more per increment of throttle movement, so only small movements are needed.

**16.4 [Variation of Thrust with RPM, p.16-3, difficulty 2]**  
How does thrust output change as a jet engine's RPM increases from 90 to 100 percent?

- ✅ **Thrust may increase by as much as the total thrust available at 70 percent RPM.**
- Thrust increases linearly, with each 10 percent RPM increase producing roughly equal thrust gains.
- Thrust increases only slightly because the engine is already near its maximum efficiency.
- Thrust remains relatively constant until reaching 100 percent, then increases sharply.

  > The chapter explains that jets operate most efficiently in the 85 to 100 percent RPM range, and the thrust curve is highly nonlinear. An increase from 90 to 100 percent RPM may produce as much additional thrust as the total available at 70 percent.

**16.5 [Slow Acceleration of the Jet Engine, p.16-3, difficulty 1]**  
Why should pilots of jet airplanes anticipate the need for adding power from low power settings?

- ✅ **Jet engine acceleration from idle to full power can take up to 10 seconds, significantly longer than a piston engine.**
- The fuel control system requires manual stabilization before accepting higher power settings.
- Jet engines must pass through a critical temperature range during acceleration that limits spool-up rate.
- The thrust levers must be held at an intermediate position for several seconds before advancing further.

  > The chapter notes that while piston engine acceleration from idle to full power is relatively rapid, jet engine acceleration is usually much slower and can take up to 10 seconds. This necessitates anticipating power needs and maintaining a stabilized approach with stable power settings.

**16.6 [Absence of Propeller Slipstream, p.16-4, difficulty 2]**  
In a jet airplane, how does the absence of propeller slipstream affect the relationship between adding power and stall speed?

- ✅ **The stall speed is not decreased by adding power, and lift is not increased instantly.**
- The stall speed decreases slightly because jet exhaust increases airflow over the tail surfaces.
- The stall speed decreases significantly because jet thrust directly reduces the wing's angle of attack.
- The stall speed increases because jet engine exhaust disrupts the boundary layer on the wing.

  > The chapter explains that a propeller produces thrust by accelerating air over the wing, allowing instant lift changes without airspeed changes and lowering power-on stall speed. Without a propeller, a jet airplane gains no such benefits: lift is not increased instantly by adding power, and stall speed is not decreased.

**16.7 [Absence of Propeller Drag, p.16-4, difficulty 2]**  
When a jet pilot reduces thrust to idle in level flight, approximately how much distance is required to lose 10 knots of airspeed?

- ✅ **About 1 mile.**
- About 1/4 mile.
- About 1/2 mile.
- About 2 miles.

  > The chapter states that reducing power to idle on a jet engine produces no drag effect comparable to a propeller, and the jet engine still produces forward thrust at idle. Combined with the clean airframe, slowing down is one of the initial problems for transitioning pilots. In level flight at idle power, it takes about 1 mile to lose 10 knots of airspeed.

**16.8 [Speed Margins, p.16-4, difficulty 2]**  
Why does a jet airplane need both an airspeed indicator and a Mach indicator to observe all speed limitations?

- ✅ **At low, warm altitudes the aircraft is limited by indicated airspeed, while at high, cold altitudes the lower speed of sound means the aircraft is limited by Mach number.**
- The airspeed indicator is accurate only below 10,000 feet, and the Mach indicator must be used above that altitude.
- VMO applies only to the climb phase, while MMO applies only to the descent phase.
- The Mach indicator serves as a backup for the airspeed indicator in case of pitot-static system failure.

  > The chapter explains that the speed of sound varies with temperature. At low, warm altitudes, the speed of sound is high, so the aircraft is limited by indicated airspeed (VMO). At high, cold altitudes, the speed of sound is lower, so the aircraft becomes limited by Mach number (MMO). Both indicators are needed to observe both limits.

**16.9 [Mach Buffet, p.16-6, difficulty 3]**  
What defines the region of a jet airplane's flight envelope known as "coffin corner"?

- ✅ **The altitude where flying any faster would exceed MMO causing high-speed buffet, and flying any slower would require an AOA leading to low-speed buffet.**
- The airspeed at which Mach buffet and stall buffet occur simultaneously at sea level.
- The Mach number at which the critical Mach number equals the stall speed at any altitude.
- The altitude where the airplane's thrust available equals thrust required at MMO.

  > The chapter describes coffin corner as the altitude where an airplane flying at MMO would experience buffeting with any increase in AOA. At this altitude, flying faster exceeds MMO leading to high-speed Mach buffet, and flying slower requires an AOA that leads to low-speed Mach buffet. This altitude determines the airplane's absolute or aerodynamic ceiling.

**16.10 [Low-Speed Flight, p.16-6, difficulty 3]**  
What characteristic of flight near VMD in a jet airplane creates a potential for speed divergence?

- ✅ **A lack of speed stability, where a decrease in speed leads to increased drag, causing a further decrease in speed.**
- A pronounced nose-down pitching moment that provides clear warning of the approaching stall.
- A significant increase in buffet that provides clear aerodynamic warning of the approaching stall.
- A dramatic loss of lift similar to a straight-wing airplane stall at the same speed.

  > The chapter explains that in a jet airplane, flight near VMD does not produce noticeable changes in flying qualities other than a lack of speed stability. A decrease in speed leads to an increase in drag, which leads to a further decrease in speed, creating the potential for speed divergence. A pilot may find a serious sink rate developing while pitch attitude appears normal.

**16.11 [Stalls, p.16-7, difficulty 2]**  
Why does a swept wing tend to stall at the wingtips first, and what design features do manufacturers use to counteract this tendency?

- ✅ **The boundary layer tends to flow spanwise toward the tips; manufacturers use wing twist, airfoil section changes, and vortex generators to discourage tip stall.**
- The wingtips operate at a lower angle of attack due to sweep; winglets are added to increase tip lift.
- The tips have thinner airfoils that stall earlier; leading-edge cuffs are used to delay tip stall.
- The wing root is heated by engine bleed air preventing root stall; fence panels redirect airflow to the tips.

  > The chapter explains that when a straight-wing airfoil is swept, the boundary layer tends to flow spanwise toward the tips, causing tip stall first. This is problematic because tip stall allows the center of lift to move forward. Manufacturers counteract this with twist, changes in airfoil section, vortex generators, or a combination of these modifications to help the pilot retain roll control during a stall.

**16.12 [Drag Devices, p.16-9, difficulty 2]**  
What is the primary design purpose difference between spoilers and speed brakes?

- ✅ **The primary purpose of spoilers is to spoil lift, while the primary purpose of speed brakes is to produce drag.**
- Spoilers are used only on the ground, while speed brakes are used only in flight.
- Spoilers are mounted on the fuselage, while speed brakes are mounted on the wings.
- Spoilers increase lift for short-field landings, while speed brakes decrease lift for rapid descents.

  > The chapter clearly distinguishes the two: the primary purpose of spoilers is to spoil lift (though they also increase drag), while the primary purpose of speed brakes is to produce drag. When spoilers are deployed after landing, most of the wing's lift is destroyed, transferring weight to the landing gear for more effective braking.

**16.13 [Thrust Reversers, p.16-11, difficulty 2]**  
How does the effectiveness of jet engine reverse thrust vary with speed during the landing roll?

- ✅ **Reverse thrust is more effective at high speed than at low speed, so it should be used as soon as prudent after touchdown.**
- Reverse thrust is equally effective at all speeds throughout the landing roll.
- Reverse thrust is more effective at low speed, so it should be delayed until the airplane has slowed significantly.
- Reverse thrust is most effective at touchdown speed but becomes ineffective once the nose-wheel is on the ground.

  > The chapter states that reverse thrust is more effective at high speed than at low speed, and for maximum efficiency the pilot should use it as soon as prudent after touchdown. This is similar to aerodynamic drag and unlike wheel brakes, which are most effective at low speed.

**16.14 [Rejected Takeoff, p.16-16, difficulty 3]**  
During a rejected takeoff, what is the correct sequence of pilot actions for maximum deceleration?

- ✅ **Apply maximum braking immediately while simultaneously retarding the throttles, followed by spoiler extension and thrust reverser deployment.**
- Retard throttles, deploy spoilers, deploy thrust reversers, then apply braking as in a normal landing.
- Retard throttles, apply braking, deploy thrust reversers, then deploy spoilers.
- Deploy spoilers, apply maximum braking, retard throttles, then deploy thrust reversers.

  > The chapter warns that the initial tendency of flight crews is to use normal after-landing braking (retarding throttles and deploying spoilers first, then braking), which delays the primary deceleration force. Instead, pilots should apply maximum braking immediately while simultaneously retarding the throttles, with spoiler extension and thrust reverser deployment following in short sequence.

**16.15 [Rotation and Lift-Off, p.16-16, difficulty 2]**  
How does the target takeoff pitch attitude in a jet airplane vary with changes in aircraft weight?

- ✅ **The takeoff pitch attitude remains constant regardless of weight.**
- It increases with higher weights to generate additional lift.
- It decreases with higher weights to maintain the same climb gradient.
- It varies based on the center of gravity location rather than weight.

  > The chapter states that each airplane has its own specific takeoff pitch attitude that remains constant regardless of weight. The takeoff pitch attitude in a jet is normally between 10 and 15 degrees nose up, and the rotation should be made smoothly at a rate of approximately 2.5 to 3 degrees per second.

**16.16 [Descent Planning, p.16-18, difficulty 2]**  
For a straight-in VFR approach without factoring wind, how can a pilot estimate the top of descent point?

- ✅ **Multiply the planned descent in thousands of feet by 3, and add distance needed for speed reductions in level flight.**
- Multiply the planned descent in thousands of feet by 2, and subtract distance for tailwind.
- Divide the planned descent in feet by 1,000, then multiply by 5 for the distance in nautical miles.
- Multiply the cruise altitude in feet by 0.003 to get the distance in nautical miles.

  > The chapter provides a rule of thumb: multiply the planned descent in thousands of feet by 3 and add distance for speed reductions. For example, descending from 35,000 feet: 35 times 3 equals 105, plus about 15 miles for speed reduction, gives approximately 120 miles. Variables include wind (adjust 1 mile per 10 knots), field elevation, terrain, ATC vectors, and type of approach.

**16.17 [Stabilized Approach, p.16-22, difficulty 1]**  
According to the chapter, what should the pilot do if the approach is not stabilized by 500 feet AGL in a jet airplane?

- ✅ **Initiate a go-around.**
- Increase thrust and adjust the glidepath to re-stabilize before reaching the threshold.
- Extend full flaps and landing gear immediately to increase drag and slow to approach speed.
- Continue the approach but plan for a firm landing within the touchdown zone.

  > The chapter states that every approach should be evaluated at 500 feet, which in a typical jet airplane is approximately 1 minute from touchdown. If the approach is not stabilized at that height, a go-around should be initiated. The stabilized approach requires the airplane in landing configuration by 1,000 feet, on profile, with airspeed within 0 to 10 knots of target, and an appropriate descent rate.

**16.18 [The Flare, p.16-23, difficulty 2]**  
How should a jet airplane be landed compared to a light piston airplane during the flare?

- ✅ **A jet airplane should be flown onto the runway rather than held off, as holding off increases landing distance and may cause a tail strike.**
- A jet airplane should be held off the runway as long as possible to achieve the smoothest touchdown.
- A jet airplane should maintain a nose-high attitude throughout the flare to maximize aerodynamic braking.
- A jet airplane should use a burst of thrust just before touchdown to cushion the landing.

  > The chapter explains that unlike light airplanes, a jet should be flown onto the runway rather than held off. A jet is aerodynamically clean even in landing configuration, and its engines still produce residual thrust at idle. Holding off during the flare greatly increases landing distance and may lead to a tail strike. A firm, deliberate landing is normal and desirable.


## Chapter 17 — 18 questions

**17.1 [Light-Sport Aircraft Background, p.17-2, difficulty 1]**  
Which of the following is a defining characteristic of a light-sport aircraft (LSA) under 14 CFR part 1, section 1.1?

- ✅ **A maximum speed in level flight at maximum continuous power of 120 knots CAS**
- A maximum takeoff weight of 1,500 pounds for all landplanes
- A maximum stall speed of 55 knots
- A pressurized cabin with a maximum of two occupants

  > The chapter lists the LSA defining standards from 14 CFR part 1, section 1.1, including a maximum speed of 120 knots CAS in level flight at maximum continuous power and a maximum stall speed of 45 knots. The max takeoff weight is 1,320 lb for landplanes, and LSAs must be non-pressurized.

**17.2 [Light-Sport Aircraft Background, p.17-2, difficulty 2]**  
S-LSA and E-LSA aircraft are designed and manufactured to which set of standards instead of 14 CFR part 23?

- ✅ **ASTM Committee F-37 Industry Consensus Standards**
- 14 CFR part 23 certification requirements, identical to standard airworthiness aircraft
- FAA Type Certificate Data Sheets specific to light-sport aircraft
- European Aviation Safety Agency (EASA) CS-LSA standards

  > The chapter states that S-LSAs and E-LSAs are not type certificated by the FAA and are not required to meet 14 CFR part 23. Instead, they are designed and manufactured in accordance with ASTM Committee F-37 Industry Consensus Standards, which the FAA accepted in 2005.

**17.3 [Light-Sport Airplane Synopsis, p.17-4, difficulty 2]**  
A private pilot holding a valid U.S. driver's license wishes to fly an LSA as PIC. Under what condition is the driver's license NOT sufficient for medical eligibility?

- ✅ **If the pilot's most recent FAA medical certificate was denied, revoked, suspended, or withdrawn**
- If the pilot has never applied for an FAA medical certificate
- If the pilot's most recent FAA medical certificate was issued more than 24 months ago
- If the pilot holds only a student pilot certificate

  > The chapter specifies that pilots holding higher level certificates may fly LSAs using a valid U.S. driver's license as evidence of medical eligibility. However, if the pilot's most recent medical certificate was denied, revoked, suspended, or withdrawn, the driver's license is not sufficient and the pilot must hold a valid FAA medical certificate.

**17.4 [Sport Pilot Certificate, p.17-3, difficulty 2]**  
A commercial pilot rated in airplane multiengine land and rotorcraft gyroplane wants to act as PIC of a light-sport airplane. What additional requirement must be met?

- ✅ **Supervision from an authorized instructor, since the pilot lacks an airplane single-engine class rating**
- No additional requirements; the commercial certificate allows PIC in any LSA
- Obtaining a sport pilot certificate with an airplane category rating
- Completing a flight review in a light-sport airplane within the preceding 24 calendar months

  > The chapter gives this exact example: a commercial pilot rated in airplane multiengine land and rotorcraft gyroplane is qualified to fly a light-sport gyroplane as PIC, but is not qualified to act as PIC of a light-sport airplane (sport airplanes are single-engine) without supervision from an authorized instructor, because the pilot does not hold the appropriate single-engine class rating.

**17.5 [Light-Sport Airplane Synopsis, p.17-3, difficulty 2]**  
Under what conditions may an LSA be operated at night under VFR?

- ✅ **The aircraft must be equipped per 14 CFR 91.205(c), night operations must be allowed by the airplane's operating limitations, and the pilot may not be operating under sport pilot privileges**
- Any pilot with a sport pilot certificate may fly at night if the aircraft is equipped per 14 CFR 91.205(c)
- The aircraft may fly at night if equipped with a ballistic parachute system, regardless of pilot certificate
- Only private pilots with a current flight review may operate LSAs at night

  > Per the FAA handbook, LSAs may be operated VFR at night if the aircraft is equipped per 14 CFR 91.205(c) and if night operations are allowed by the airplane's operating limitations. However, sport pilots may not fly at night per 14 CFR 61.315(c)(5).

**17.6 [Transition Training Considerations – Flight Instructors, p.17-4, difficulty 1]**  
What is a limitation of a flight instructor-S (Sport Pilot flight instructor) compared to a flight instructor-A?

- ✅ **A flight instructor-S may only provide instruction in LSAs and cannot instruct in non-LSA airplanes**
- A flight instructor-S may only provide ground instruction, not flight instruction
- A flight instructor-S may instruct in any airplane but cannot endorse students for solo flight
- A flight instructor-S may instruct in non-LSA airplanes but only if the student holds a private pilot certificate

  > The chapter states that 14 CFR part 61, section 61.413 limits a flight instructor-S to instruction in LSAs—a flight instructor-S cannot give instruction in a non-LSA airplane such as a Cessna 150. A flight instructor-A, by contrast, may give instruction in both LSA and non-LSA airplanes.

**17.7 [LSA Maintenance, p.17-5, difficulty 2]**  
How does the FAA handle Airworthiness Directives (ADs) for S-LSA and E-LSA aircraft?

- ✅ **The FAA does not issue ADs for S-LSAs or E-LSAs, but will issue ADs for any FAA-certificated components installed on them**
- The FAA issues ADs for S-LSAs and E-LSAs in the same manner as for standard airworthiness aircraft
- ADs are issued by the LSA manufacturer and enforced by the FAA for all S-LSA and E-LSA aircraft
- No ADs apply to S-LSAs or E-LSAs under any circumstances, including installed FAA-certificated components

  > The chapter clearly states the FAA does not issue ADs for S-LSAs or E-LSAs. However, if an FAA-certificated component is installed on an LSA, the FAA issues any pertaining ADs for that specific component. S-LSA owners should also comply with manufacturer safety directives.

**17.8 [LSA Maintenance, p.17-5, difficulty 2]**  
Which of the following individuals is NOT listed as someone who may maintain and inspect an S-LSA?

- ✅ **A certificated flight instructor (CFI)**
- An LSA Repairman with a Maintenance rating
- An FAA-certificated Airframe and Powerplant Mechanic (A&P)
- An owner performing limited maintenance as permitted on their S-LSA

  > The chapter lists four categories of those who may maintain and inspect S-LSAs: (1) an LSA Repairman with a Maintenance rating, (2) an FAA-certificated A&P, (3) as specified by the aircraft manufacturer, or (4) owners performing limited maintenance on their S-LSA. A CFI is not among those listed.

**17.9 [Airframe and Systems – Engines, p.17-6, difficulty 2]**  
Why do most light-sport airplanes with high-rpm engines require a gear-box?

- ✅ **To reduce the propeller rpm to the proper range while allowing the engine to operate at higher rpm**
- To increase the propeller rpm above the engine rpm for better climb performance
- To provide reverse thrust capability for shorter landings on unprepared surfaces
- To convert the engine's rotary motion into a reciprocating motion for the propeller

  > The chapter explains that most light-sport airplane engines are water-cooled, 4-cycle, and carbureted with much higher operating rpm. These engines require a gear-box to reduce propeller rpm to the proper range. Because of the higher engine operating rpm, vibration and noise signatures differ from standard type certificated designs.

**17.10 [Airframe and Systems – Instrumentation, p.17-7, difficulty 2]**  
What is a potential hazard identified with EFIS systems in LSAs if the pilot is not properly trained?

- ✅ **A 'heads down' syndrome causing loss of situational awareness**
- The EFIS automatically disconnects the autopilot during turns greater than 30 degrees of bank
- The EFIS creates excessive heat in the cockpit that can lead to pilot fatigue
- The EFIS interferes with VHF radio communications when displaying weather data

  > The chapter warns that EFIS systems can cause a 'heads down' syndrome and loss of situational awareness if the pilot is not trained to quickly and properly configure, access, program, and interpret the information provided. Transition training should include instruction in the use of the specific EFIS installed.

**17.11 [Weather Considerations, p.17-8, difficulty 1]**  
Why does the chapter advise that even greater distances from convective weather should be considered the norm for LSAs?

- ✅ **Because an LSA's lighter weight makes it more susceptible to the effects of winds associated with convection**
- Because LSAs lack the electrical systems needed to detect convective weather
- Because LSA engines are more likely to ingest water and quit in humid air near thunderstorms
- Because LSAs cannot climb fast enough to get above convective cloud tops

  > The chapter explains that managing weather factors becomes more significant as airplane weight decreases. Due to an LSA's lighter weight, even greater distances from convective weather should be considered the norm. Operations in the vicinity of convection should not be attempted in lightweight airplanes.

**17.12 [Flight Environment – Inside the Airplane, p.17-10, difficulty 1]**  
When performing a full range of motion check of flight controls on an LSA, what should a pilot verify regarding aileron movement?

- ✅ **The direction of control surface movement, noting that differential aileron geometry may show very little 'down' aileron compared to 'up' aileron**
- That both ailerons move equal distances up and down simultaneously
- That the ailerons remain fixed during ground checks and only move in flight
- That the ailerons move in the same direction as the elevator during the check

  > The chapter states that on some LSAs, aileron control geometry moves ailerons in a highly differential manner to minimize adverse yaw, so a pilot may see very little 'down' aileron compared to the 'up' aileron. Pilots should always verify the direction of control surface movement during the full range of motion check.

**17.13 [Flight Environment – Inside the Airplane, p.17-10, difficulty 2]**  
During preflight of an LSA with electric elevator trim, what should the pilot verify regarding the trim system?

- ✅ **Actuate the trim in both nose-up and nose-down directions, verify the trim disconnect functions if equipped, and set takeoff trim position**
- Verify that the trim is in the full nose-down position to ensure proper takeoff rotation
- Disconnect the trim system entirely before flight to prevent trim runaway
- Check that the trim operates only in the nose-up direction for go-around capability

  > The chapter specifies that preflight should include actuating the trim switch in both nose-up and nose-down directions, verifying that the trim disconnect (if equipped) is properly functioning, and then properly setting the takeoff trim position. Pilots should understand how to respond to a trim-runaway condition.

**17.14 [Flight Environment – Outside the Airplane, p.17-11, difficulty 2]**  
When preflighting a ground-adjustable composite propeller on an LSA, what specific check should be performed?

- ✅ **Snugly twist the blade at the root to verify there is no rotation of the blade at the hub**
- Measure the blade angle with a protractor to confirm factory pitch setting
- Tap the blade with a metal hammer to listen for a hollow sound indicating delamination
- Apply lubricant to the hub to ensure smooth blade rotation during flight

  > The chapter states that on ground adjustable propellers, the pilot should ensure that the blades are tight against the hub by snugly twisting the blade at the root to verify that there is no rotation of the blade at the hub. This is an additional check specific to composite, ground-adjustable propellers.

**17.15 [Flight Environment – Taxi, p.17-12, difficulty 2]**  
When taxiing an LSA equipped with a full-castoring nose wheel, what technique is required for steering?

- ✅ **Differential braking, which requires practice to maintain centerline while minimizing brake and tire wear**
- Rudder pedal deflection alone, which mechanically steers the nose wheel through cables
- Differential thrust using the throttle to create yawing moments for directional control
- Asymmetric braking on the main wheels combined with full rudder deflection in the direction of turn

  > The chapter explains that to taxi a full-castoring nose-wheel equipped airplane, the use of differential brakes is required. This type of nose-wheel can require practice to keep the airplane on the centerline while minimizing brake application or damage to the tires. If taxi speed is too slow, brake application can cause the aircraft to pivot to a stop.

**17.16 [Flight Environment – Cruise, p.17-13, difficulty 2]**  
A transitioning pilot notices cruise rpm of 4,800 on an LSA and is concerned the engine is turning too fast. What explains this reading?

- ✅ **The engine has a gear-reduction drive, so the propeller is turning much slower than the engine**
- The tachometer is miscalibrated and needs to be replaced with a mechanical gauge
- The high rpm indicates the mixture is too lean and the engine is overspeeding
- The propeller governor has failed and the propeller is in fine pitch, requiring immediate power reduction

  > The chapter addresses this directly, noting that the first time a transitioning pilot sees a cruise rpm setting of 4,800 rpm, they may sense the engine is turning too fast. However, the engine has a gear-reduction drive and the propeller is turning much slower. If equipped with a standard aircraft engine, rpm would be in a comparable range to what the pilot is used to.

**17.17 [Flight Environment – Approach and Landing, p.17-15, difficulty 3]**  
According to the Key Points section, why is it important to maintain airspeed during a crosswind approach in a high-drag LSA rather than allowing a rapid power reduction?

- ✅ **A rapid reduction in airspeed prior to touchdown may result in loss of rudder and/or aileron control, pushing the aircraft off runway heading**
- A rapid power reduction will cause the LSA to float excessively, resulting in a long landing
- A rapid power reduction will overspeed the flaps and cause structural damage to the wing
- A rapid power reduction will cause the engine to quit and cannot be restarted during the approach

  > The Key Points section explains that LSAs with high drag, low mass, and low inertia tend to decelerate rapidly when power is reduced. During a crosswind landing, a rapid reduction in airspeed prior to touchdown may result in loss of rudder and/or aileron control, which may push the aircraft off the runway heading. The chapter advises maintaining airspeed and, when power is reduced, lowering the nose to maintain airspeed.

**17.18 [Key Points, p.17-15, difficulty 3]**  
In the event of an engine failure in an LSA at low altitude, why is excessive pitch attitude during climb particularly dangerous?

- ✅ **The higher the pitch attitude when failure occurs, the quicker the aircraft loses airspeed, making a stall more likely with less altitude available for recovery**
