- The higher pitch attitude makes it harder for ATC to see the aircraft on radar for rescue coordination
- The higher pitch attitude increases fuel flow to the engine, making restart impossible due to flooding
- The higher pitch attitude causes the ballistic parachute to deploy inadvertently due to G-forces

  > The Key Points section states that in an engine failure, the pilot should quickly transition to the required nose-down attitude to maintain airspeed. The higher the pitch attitude when failure occurs, the quicker the aircraft will lose airspeed and the more likely it is to stall. Stalls at low altitudes are especially dangerous because the closer to the ground the stall occurs, the less time there is to recover. Excessive pitch attitude during low-altitude climbs is discouraged.


## Chapter 18 — 18 questions

**18.1 [Types of Emergency Landings, p.18-1, difficulty 1]**  
Why is a precautionary landing generally less hazardous than a forced landing?

- ✅ **The pilot has more time for terrain selection and planning, and can use power to compensate for errors in judgment or technique**
- The airplane is always at a lower altitude, making the approach easier to manage
- ATC is more likely to provide radar vectors to a nearby suitable airport
- The airplane's stalling speed is reduced, allowing a slower touchdown speed

  > The chapter states that a precautionary landing is less hazardous because the pilot has more time for terrain selection and approach planning, and can use power to compensate for errors. A forced landing, by contrast, is an immediate landing necessitated by the inability to continue flight.

**18.2 [Psychological Hazards, p.18-2, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot experiences an engine failure shortly after takeoff and attempts a 180° turn back to the runway despite insufficient altitude. Which psychological hazard does this exemplify?

- ✅ **Desire to save the airplane**
- Reluctance to accept the emergency situation
- Undue concern about getting hurt
- Failure to maintain airplane control while attempting to correct the malfunction

  > The chapter lists 'making a 180° turn back to the runway when available altitude is insufficient' as a typical consequence of the 'desire to save the airplane.' This hazard occurs when a pilot ignores basic rules of airmanship to avoid airplane damage, sometimes influenced by financial stake in the airplane or the belief that an undamaged airplane implies no bodily harm.

**18.3 [Basic Safety Concepts, p.18-3, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, how does groundspeed affect the total destructive energy during an emergency landing?

- ✅ **Doubling the groundspeed quadruples the total destructive energy**
- Doubling the groundspeed doubles the total destructive energy
- Doubling the groundspeed triples the total destructive energy
- Groundspeed has no effect on destructive energy; only stopping distance matters

  > The chapter identifies speed as the most critical factor in deceleration severity. Doubling groundspeed quadruples total destructive energy, which is why the touchdown should be made at the lowest possible controllable airspeed using all available aerodynamic devices.

**18.4 [Attitude and Sink Rate Control, p.18-4, difficulty 2]**  
What danger does a flat touchdown at a high sink rate present during an emergency landing on a hard surface?

- ✅ **It can be injurious to occupants without destroying the cabin structure, especially during gear-up landings in low-wing airplanes**
- It guarantees the cabin structure will collapse, resulting in serious injury regardless of airplane type
- It is only a concern for high-wing airplanes, not low-wing airplanes
- It has no effect on occupant safety as long as the landing gear is extended

  > The chapter warns that a flat touchdown at a high sink rate (well in excess of 500 fpm) on a hard surface can be injurious without destroying the cabin structure. Low-wing airplanes with rigid bottom construction may not provide adequate cushioning by structural deformation during gear-up landings.

**18.5 [Airplane Configuration, p.18-4, difficulty 2]**  
Under what condition might a gear-up landing result in less airplane damage than a gear-down landing?

- ✅ **When a normal touchdown is assured and ample stopping distance is available, on level but soft terrain or across a plowed field**
- When landing on a smooth, hard-surface runway with crash rescue facilities standing by
- When landing in dense trees where the gear could snag and cause the airplane to flip
- When landing on water, since the extended landing gear would help cushion the impact and protect the cabin from damage

  > The chapter states that when a normal touchdown is assured and ample stopping distance is available, a gear-up landing on level but soft terrain or across a plowed field may result in less damage than gear-down. In rugged terrain or trees, however, extended gear has a protective effect on the cabin area.

**18.6 [Terrain Types — Trees (Forest), p.18-6, difficulty 2]**  
When executing a forced landing in trees, what airspeed and attitude should the pilot maintain at the moment of contact?

- ✅ **Minimum indicated airspeed but not below stall speed, in a nose-high landing attitude**
- Best glide speed in a level attitude to maximize control authority through the canopy
- Slightly above stall speed in a nose-low attitude to ensure the nose contacts first
- Maneuvering speed in a steep bank to distribute the impact across both wings equally

  > The chapter recommends making contact at minimum indicated airspeed (but not below stall speed) and 'hanging' the airplane in the tree branches in a nose-high attitude. This involves the underside of the fuselage and both wings for even cushioning and prevents penetration of the windshield.

**18.7 [Water (Ditching) and Snow, p.18-6, difficulty 2]**  
Why should a pilot use no more than intermediate flaps on a low-wing airplane when ditching?

- ✅ **The water resistance of fully extended flaps may result in asymmetrical flap failure and slowing of the airplane**
- Full flaps cause the airplane to float on the water surface, preventing a controlled touchdown
- Full flaps reduce the approach angle too much, making depth perception impossible
- Full flaps increase the stalling speed, making it harder to touch down at minimum speed

  > The chapter specifically warns that on low-wing airplanes, the water resistance of fully extended flaps may result in asymmetrical flap failure and slowing of the airplane. Intermediate flaps are recommended, and the retractable gear should be kept up unless the AFM/POH advises otherwise.

**18.8 [Engine Failure After Takeoff (Single-Engine), p.18-7, difficulty 3]**  
Using a standard rate turn (3° per second) to attempt a turnback after engine failure on takeoff, what is the primary problem with this rate of turn?

- ✅ **It consumes too much time, requires too much distance, and generates an unacceptable solution**
- It takes only 30 seconds, which is too fast and may cause an accelerated stall
- It takes 2 minutes, resulting in excessive altitude loss but keeps the airplane close to the runway centerline
- It takes 1 minute, but the primary problem is that the turn radius is too small to properly align with the runway

  > The chapter's example shows a standard rate turn takes 1 minute for 180°, plus additional time for alignment. At a typical glide descent rate, this places the airplane over 1,000 feet below the runway. The text concludes that a standard rate or shallow turn consumes too much time, requires too much distance, and generates an unacceptable solution.

**18.9 [Emergency Descents, p.18-8, difficulty 1]**  
What bank angle should be established when initiating an emergency descent, and what is the purpose?

- ✅ **Approximately 30 to 45° to maintain positive load factors (G forces) on the airplane**
- Wings level to minimize structural stress and maintain the highest possible descent rate
- 10 to 20° to provide a gentle turn while maintaining passenger comfort
- 60° or greater to maximize the descent rate and quickly lose altitude

  > The chapter specifies that when initiating an emergency descent, a bank of approximately 30 to 45° should be established to maintain positive load factors on the airplane. The turn also serves to check for other air traffic below and to look for a possible emergency landing area.

**18.10 [In-Flight Fire — Engine Fire, p.18-9, difficulty 2]**  
When responding to an in-flight engine compartment fire, why should the ignition switch be left ON after shutting off the fuel supply?

- ✅ **To use up the fuel that remains in the fuel lines and components between the fuel selector/shutoff valve and the engine**
- To keep the engine running so the airplane can maintain altitude and reach an airport
- To generate airflow through the engine compartment to blow out the fire
- To keep the electrical system active for radio communications with ATC

  > The chapter states that after placing the mixture in idle cutoff and the fuel selector to OFF, the ignition switch should be left ON to use up remaining fuel in the lines and components. This procedure may starve the engine compartment of fuel and cause the fire to die naturally. If the flames are snuffed out, no restart should be attempted.

**18.11 [In-Flight Fire — Electrical Fires, p.18-10, difficulty 3]**  
If electrical power is essential and the pilot must identify a faulty circuit after an electrical fire, what is the proper procedure?

- ✅ **Turn the master OFF, turn all individual switches OFF, turn the master back ON, then select switches one at a time with a short time lapse after each to check for odor, smoke, or sparks**
- Turn the master switch ON and immediately turn on all electrical switches simultaneously to identify which circuit trips its breaker
- Leave the master switch OFF and test each circuit by touching its wire to the battery terminal
- Turn on the master and avionics first, then systematically turn off each switch until the odor disappears

  > The chapter outlines a four-step procedure: master OFF, all individual switches OFF, master back ON, then select switches one at a time with a short delay to check for signs of the fire. However, the chapter warns this procedure has the effect of recreating the original problem, and the most prudent course of action is to land as soon as possible.

**18.12 [Flight Control Malfunction/Failure — Asymmetric (Split) Flap, p.18-11, difficulty 2]**  
What is the typical indication that a pilot has encountered an asymmetric (split) flap situation?

- ✅ **A pronounced roll toward the wing with the least flap deflection when flaps are extended or retracted**
- A sudden pitch-up tendency and a significant increase in airspeed
- A violent yaw toward the wing with the most flap deflection
- Complete loss of aileron authority on the side with the deployed flap

  > The chapter states that a split flap situation is indicated by a pronounced roll toward the wing with the least flap deflection. The roll is countered with opposite aileron, and the yaw from the additional drag of the extended flap requires substantial opposite rudder, resulting in a cross-control condition.

**18.13 [Flight Control Malfunction/Failure — Loss of Elevator Control, p.18-11, difficulty 3]**  
If the 'up' elevator cable fails but the 'down' cable remains intact, how can the pilot retain pitch control for landing?

- ✅ **Apply considerable nose-up trim, push the yoke forward to attain and maintain attitude, and release forward pressure to flare**
- Apply considerable nose-down trim and pull the yoke aft to maintain attitude, then increase back pressure to flare
- Reduce power to idle and use rudder inputs to control pitch through yaw-roll coupling
- Extend full flaps and use throttle changes as the sole means of pitch control

  > The chapter describes the procedure for loss of up-elevator control: apply nose-up trim, push the control yoke forward to attain and maintain desired attitude, increase forward pressure to lower the nose and relax forward pressure to raise the nose, and release forward pressure to flare for landing. The procedure for loss of down-elevator control is the opposite.

**18.14 [Landing Gear Malfunction, p.18-12, difficulty 2]**  
If only one main gear leg fails to extend, what factor should influence the pilot's decision to land on the available gear versus retracting all gear?

- ✅ **If the runway is narrow and/or ditches and obstacles line the runway edge, landing with all three gear retracted may be the safest course of action**
- Landing on the available gear legs is always safer because it reduces the risk of post-crash fire
- Landing with all gear retracted is always safer because it prevents the airplane from veering off the runway
- The decision should be based solely on the amount of fuel remaining, since fuel weight affects wing loading

  > The chapter explains that landing on only one main gear usually causes the airplane to veer strongly toward the faulty gear leg after touchdown. If the runway is narrow or has obstacles along the edges where maximum directional control is needed, landing with all three gear retracted may be the safest option.

**18.15 [System Malfunctions — Pitot-Static System, p.18-14, difficulty 3]**  
If the static reference system is severely restricted but not entirely blocked, what will the instruments indicate during a descent?

- ✅ **The altimeter indicates higher than actual, the airspeed indicator indicates higher than actual, and the VSI shows a descent rate lower than actual**
- The altimeter indicates lower than actual, the airspeed indicator indicates lower than actual, and the VSI shows a climb
- The altimeter indicates higher than actual, the airspeed indicator indicates lower than actual, and the VSI shows a descent rate higher than actual
- All three instruments indicate normally during descent; the malfunction only appears during climb

  > The chapter explains that with a restricted static system, the reference pressure lags behind actual outside air pressure during descent. The altimeter reads higher than actual, the airspeed indicator reads higher than actual (due to lower static reference pressure), and the VSI shows a descent rate much lower than actual. To the pilot, the airplane appears too high, too fast, and descending too slowly.

**18.16 [Door Opening In-Flight, p.18-17, difficulty 1]**  
According to the chapter, what is the primary cause of accidents related to an open cabin door in flight?

- ✅ **The pilot's distraction or failure to maintain control of the airplane**
- Structural failure of the door hinges causing the door to separate and strike the tail
- Loss of cabin pressure resulting in hypoxia and pilot incapacitation
- Disruption of airflow over the wing causing an uncontrollable roll

  > The chapter emphasizes that accidents are almost never caused by an open door itself. Rather, an open door accident is caused by the pilot's distraction or failure to maintain control. A cabin door that opens in flight seldom compromises the airplane's ability to fly, and the pilot should concentrate on flying the airplane.

**18.17 [Inadvertent VFR Flight Into IMC — Maintaining Airplane Control, p.18-18, difficulty 2]**  
For a VFR pilot who has inadvertently entered IMC, what is the most important initial concern for maintaining airplane control?

- ✅ **Keep the wings level, since good bank control makes pitch control much easier**
- Reduce power immediately to minimize airspeed buildup in the clouds
- Initiate a standard-rate turn to reverse course and exit the IMC conditions
- Descend immediately to get below the cloud layer and regain visual reference

  > The chapter stresses that the pilot should not panic and that the only concern at this point is to keep the wings level. An uncontrolled turn or bank leads to difficulty achieving any desired flight condition, but good bank control has the effect of making pitch control much easier. The pilot should trim for hands-off level flight and use fingertip control.

**18.18 [Emergency Response Systems — Ballistic Parachutes, p.18-22, difficulty 1]**  
Under which of the following conditions would deployment of a ballistic parachute system be appropriate?

- ✅ **A catastrophic loss of controllability due to a collision or mechanical failure, actual loss of control, or pilot incapacitation**
- Any time the pilot is unsure of the airplane's position or is uncertain about the weather ahead
- Only when the airplane is above 3,000 feet AGL to ensure the parachute fully deploys
- Any time the engine fails, as the parachute eliminates the need for an emergency landing

  > The chapter states that deployment results in the loss of the airframe, but deploying within an acceptable flight regime prevents injuries and saves lives. Qualifying conditions include a catastrophic loss of controllability due to a collision or mechanical failure, actual loss of control, or pilot incapacitation. Pilots should brief passengers on deployment conditions and procedures.


---

# PHAK — Pilot's Handbook of Aeronautical Knowledge


## Chapter 1 — 18 questions

**1.1 [History of Flight, p.1-2, difficulty 1]**  
Who is credited with discovering the basic principles on which the modern science of aeronautics is founded?

- ✅ **Sir George Cayley**
- Leonardo da Vinci, who sketched ornithopter wings
- Robert Hooke, who concluded human flight required artificial propulsion
- Otto Lilienthal, who proved human flight in heavier-than-air aircraft was practical

  > While da Vinci, Hooke, and Lilienthal all contributed to early aviation thought, the chapter specifically identifies Sir George Cayley as the 'Father of Aerial Navigation' who discovered the basic principles of modern aeronautics, built the first successful flying model, and tested the first full-size man-carrying airplane.

**1.2 [History of the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA), p.1-4, difficulty 2]**  
The Air Commerce Act of 1926 charged which official with licensing pilots, certificating aircraft, and establishing airways?

- ✅ **The Secretary of Commerce**
- The Postmaster General, who already managed airmail routes
- The Administrator of the Federal Aviation Agency
- The Secretary of Transportation

  > The Air Commerce Act of 1926 predated both the FAA and the Department of Transportation. It charged the Secretary of Commerce with aviation oversight responsibilities. The Postmaster General managed airmail but not certification, and the FAA and DOT did not yet exist.

**1.3 [History of the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA), p.1-6, difficulty 2]**  
What primary circumstances led Congress to pass the Federal Aviation Act of 1958?

- ✅ **A series of mid-air collisions highlighting the need for more regulation**
- The PATCO strike that disrupted air traffic control services
- The Airline Deregulation Act that removed government economic controls
- The creation of the Department of Transportation

  > The chapter states that by mid-century, a series of mid-air collisions underlined the need for more regulation as aircraft increased in numbers and speed. The PATCO strike occurred in 1981, deregulation in 1978, and the DOT was established in 1966—all after the 1958 Act.

**1.4 [The Role of the FAA, p.1-7, difficulty 1]**  
Under which title of the Code of Federal Regulations are all FAA civil aviation regulations found?

- ✅ **Title 14, "Aeronautics and Space"**
- Title 49, "Transportation"
- Title 1, "General Provisions"
- Title 91, "Air Traffic and General Rules"

  > The CFR is divided into 50 titles representing broad areas of federal regulation. FAA regulations are under Title 14. Title 49 covers transportation broadly but is not where FAA regulations reside. Title 91 is not a CFR title; 14 CFR Part 91 is a specific regulation within Title 14.

**1.5 [The Role of the FAA, p.1-8, difficulty 2]**  
Which FAA field office serves as the primary point of interaction between the Flight Standards Service and the aviation community?

- ✅ **Flight Standards District Office (FSDO)**
- The Mike Monroney Aeronautical Center (MMAC)
- The William J. Hughes Technical Center (WJHTC)
- The Regional FAASTeam Office

  > The MMAC is a training and logistics facility, the WJHTC is a research and development facility, and the FAASTeam Office focuses on safety programs. The FSDO is specifically identified as the focus of interaction between Flight Standards Service and the public, handling certification, surveillance, and enforcement.

**1.6 [Obtaining Assistance from the FAA, p.1-9, difficulty 1]**  
What is the primary purpose of the Aeronautical Information Manual (AIM)?

- ✅ **To provide basic flight information and ATC procedures for flying in the National Airspace System**
- To establish certification requirements for pilots and flight instructors
- To provide aircraft maintenance and inspection procedures
- To list aircraft specifications and operating limitations by serial number

  > The AIM is the official guide to basic flight information and ATC procedures for the NAS. It is not a regulatory document for certification (that is 14 CFR Part 61), nor a maintenance manual (Part 43), nor an aircraft-specific document (that is the POH).

**1.7 [Obtaining Assistance from the FAA, p.1-11, difficulty 2]**  
In the Advisory Circular number 61-65E, what does the letter "E" signify?

- ✅ **It is the fifth revision of that Advisory Circular**
- The AC was published in the year ending in E
- The AC applies only to aircraft in the "E" weight category
- The AC is mandatory and enforceable as an emergency rule

  > An AC number has three parts: the subject area (corresponding to a 14 CFR part), a sequential number, and a revision letter. The first version has no letter; each subsequent revision adds the next letter. 'E' means the fifth revision. ACs are advisory, not regulatory.

**1.8 [Pilot and Aeronautical Information, p.1-13, difficulty 2]**  
Which type of NOTAM typically contains regulatory information such as changes to instrument approach procedures and temporary flight restrictions?

- ✅ **FDC NOTAMs**
- NOTAM (D) information
- Pointer NOTAMs
- Military NOTAMs

  > FDC NOTAMs are issued by the National Flight Data Center and contain regulatory information including interim IFR procedures, temporary flight restrictions, and airspace changes. NOTAM (D) covers distant dissemination items like runway closures and lighting issues. Pointer NOTAMs direct attention to other NOTAMs, and Military NOTAMs pertain to military facilities.

**1.9 [Aircraft Classifications and Ultralight Vehicles, p.1-15, difficulty 3]**  
When used with respect to the certification of aircraft (not airmen), what does the term "category" mean under 14 CFR 1.1?

- ✅ **A grouping of aircraft based upon intended use or operating limitations, such as normal, utility, or acrobatic**
- A broad classification of aircraft such as airplane, rotorcraft, or glider
- A classification within a category having similar operating characteristics, such as single-engine or multiengine
- A specific make and basic model of aircraft, such as a Cessna 172

  > The term 'category' has different meanings depending on context. For aircraft certification, it refers to intended use or operating limitations (e.g., transport, normal, utility). For airmen certification, it means a broad classification (e.g., airplane, rotorcraft). 'Class' refers to similar operating characteristics, and 'type' refers to a specific make and model.

**1.10 [Pilot Certifications, p.1-16, difficulty 1]**  
What may a sport pilot use as evidence of medical eligibility instead of obtaining an FAA medical certificate?

- ✅ **A current and valid driver's license**
- A statement from any physician certifying no medical defects exist
- A third-class medical certificate issued under 14 CFR part 67
- A self-certification statement submitted through MedXPress

  > The sport pilot category does not require a medical examination; a current and valid driver's license serves as proof of medical competence. A third-class medical certificate is required for private, recreational, and student pilots in airplanes. Glider and balloon pilots do not need a medical certificate but must self-certify that they have no known medical condition that would prevent safe operation.

**1.11 [Pilot Certifications, p.1-17, difficulty 2]**  
Without additional training and a logbook endorsement, what is the maximum cross-country range a recreational pilot may fly from the departure airport?

- ✅ **50 NM**
- 25 NM
- 100 NM
- There is no cross-country limit for recreational pilots

  > The recreational pilot certificate carries a limitation restricting cross-country flight to 50 NM from the departure airport. This restriction can be lifted with additional training per 14 CFR 61.101(c). The 25 NM figure is not associated with recreational pilot limitations, and 100 NM is not the specified limit.

**1.12 [Pilot Certifications, p.1-17, difficulty 2]**  
Under 14 CFR part 61, what is the minimum total piloting time required for a Private Pilot Certificate?

- ✅ **40 hours, including 20 hours with an instructor and 10 hours solo**
- 35 hours, which is the part 141 school minimum
- 30 hours, including 15 hours of dual instruction and 3 hours solo
- 60 to 75 hours, which is the national average

  > Part 61 requires at least 40 hours of piloting time including 20 hours with an instructor and 10 hours solo. The 35-hour minimum applies to part 141 schools. The 30-hour figure matches recreational pilot requirements, and 60–75 hours is the national average, not the regulatory minimum.

**1.13 [Pilot Certifications, p.1-18, difficulty 2]**  
Which three features must an aircraft have to be considered a "complex aircraft" for commercial pilot certification purposes?

- ✅ **Retractable landing gear, movable flaps, and a controllable-pitch propeller**
- Retractable landing gear, a turbocharger, and movable flaps
- A constant-speed propeller, pressurization system, and retractable gear
- Movable flaps, tricycle landing gear, and a constant-speed propeller

  > The chapter specifically defines a complex aircraft as one with retractable landing gear, movable flaps, and a controllable-pitch propeller. Turbochargers, pressurization, and tricycle gear are not part of the definition. Tricycle gear is not retractable by definition in this context.

**1.14 [Pilot Certifications, p.1-18, difficulty 2]**  
What is the minimum pilot experience required to be eligible for an Airline Transport Pilot (ATP) certificate?

- ✅ **1,500 hours of flight time**
- 1,000 hours of flight time
- 500 hours of flight time
- 250 hours of flight time

  > The ATP certificate requires 1,500 hours of flight time, the highest minimum among all pilot certificates. A restricted-privileges ATP is available at reduced minimums for those with specific academic and flight training experience, but the standard minimum is 1,500 hours.

**1.15 [Selecting a Flight School, p.1-19, difficulty 2]**  
How do the minimum flight hour requirements for a Private Pilot Certificate compare between part 141 and part 61 schools?

- ✅ **35 hours under part 141 versus 40 hours under part 61**
- 40 hours under part 141 versus 35 hours under part 61
- Both require 40 hours regardless of the regulation part
- 30 hours under part 141 versus 50 hours under part 61

  > Part 141 schools follow an FAA-approved structured curriculum allowing fewer minimum hours (35), while part 61 offers flexibility but requires more minimum hours (40). The chapter notes the national average is 60–75 hours, so this difference may be insignificant in practice.

**1.16 [The Student Pilot, p.1-20, difficulty 2]**  
Under the process described in the chapter, which of the following individuals is authorized to process a Student Pilot Certificate application?

- ✅ **An FAA inspector, DPE, CFI, or Airman Certification Representative**
- Only an FAA inspector at a FSDO can process the application
- Only a Designated Pilot Examiner may process the application
- Only the student's assigned CFI may process the application

  > The chapter describes multiple authorized individuals who can process the application: an FAA inspector or technician, an FAA-Designated Pilot Examiner, a CFI, or an Airman Certification Representative. The application is then submitted to AFS-760 via IACRA.

**1.17 [Becoming a Pilot, p.1-22, difficulty 2]**  
When does the FAA recommend a student pilot take the knowledge test for maximum benefit?

- ✅ **After the student pilot has completed a solo cross-country flight**
- Before beginning any flight training, to establish a baseline of knowledge
- Immediately after the first solo flight but before any cross-country training
- Only after all dual instruction is complete and just before the practical test

  > The FAA recommends taking the knowledge test after completing a solo cross-country flight because the operational knowledge gained from that experience helps the applicant on the test. Taking it too early or too late reduces its value. The CFI is the best judge of when the applicant is ready.

**1.18 [Role of the Designated Pilot Examiner, p.1-24, difficulty 3]**  
Under which specific regulation are Designated Pilot Examiners appointed, and what is a key characteristic of their role?

- ✅ **14 CFR part 183, section 183.23; they are private individuals, not FAA employees, who may charge a reasonable fee**
- 14 CFR part 61, section 61.58; they are FAA employees paid a government salary for conducting tests
- 14 CFR part 141, section 141.36; they must be employed by an FAA-approved flight school
- 14 CFR part 91, section 91.3; they serve as volunteers without compensation

  > DPEs are appointed under 14 CFR part 183, section 183.23. They are private individuals, not FAA employees, who meet qualification requirements in FAA Order 8710.3. They may charge a reasonable fee for their services. The majority of private and commercial practical tests are administered by DPEs.


## Chapter 2 — 18 questions

**2.1 [Introduction, p.2-2, difficulty 1]**  
According to the chapter, approximately what percentage of all aviation accidents are related to human factors?

- ✅ **80 percent**
- 50 percent
- 65 percent
- 95 percent

  > The chapter states that approximately 80 percent of all aviation accidents are related to human factors, and the vast majority of these occur during landing (24.1%) and takeoff (23.4%).

**2.2 [Risk Management, p.2-4, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot is planning to fly an unfamiliar aircraft for the first time and decides to wait for a day with good weather rather than flying in low IFR conditions. Which fundamental principle of risk management does this best illustrate?

- ✅ **Accept risk when benefits outweigh dangers (costs)**
- Accept no unnecessary risk
- Make risk decisions at the appropriate level
- Integrate risk management into planning at all levels

  > The chapter's third principle states to accept risk when benefits outweigh dangers. The example given is nearly identical: 'A day with good weather, for example, is a much better time to fly an unfamiliar airplane for the first time than a day with low IFR conditions.'

**2.3 [Hazardous Attitudes and Antidotes, p.2-4, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot notices a small nick in the propeller leading edge before a flight. An experienced pilot assesses it as low risk because the nick is blunt, in the strongest portion of the propeller, and unlikely to propagate a crack. An inexperienced pilot sees the same nick as high risk and cancels the flight. What does this scenario best illustrate about hazard and risk?

- ✅ **Risk is an assessment that varies by pilot because different pilots see hazards differently**
- Hazard and risk are identical concepts that depend on the aircraft type
- Risk is an objective measurement that does not change between pilots
- The hazard only exists if the pilot identifies it as a problem

  > The chapter defines hazard as a real or perceived condition, and risk as an assessment of that hazard. It explicitly states 'different pilots see hazards differently,' using this exact propeller nick example to show how experience changes risk assessment.

**2.4 [Hazardous Attitudes and Antidotes, p.2-5, difficulty 1]**  
A pilot consistently feels that accidents happen to other people but never to him, and this belief leads him to take chances and increase risk. Which hazardous attitude does this describe, and what is the correct antidote?

- ✅ **Invulnerability — "It could happen to me."**
- Macho — "Taking chances is foolish."
- Impulsivity — "Not so fast. Think first."
- Resignation — "I'm not helpless. I can make a difference."

  > The chapter describes invulnerability as the attitude that 'accidents happen to others, but never to them.' The antidote to memorize is 'It could happen to me.'

**2.5 [Assessing Risk, p.2-6, difficulty 2]**  
Using the risk assessment matrix, a non-instrument-rated pilot assigns a likelihood of 'occasional' for encountering IMC and a severity of 'catastrophic.' What risk level results, and what action is appropriate?

- ✅ **High risk — the pilot must either not fly or mitigate, eliminate, or control the risk**
- Serious risk — the pilot may continue with extra caution
- Medium risk — the pilot should proceed but monitor conditions
- Low risk — no special action is required

  > The chapter's risk matrix shows that 'occasional' likelihood combined with 'catastrophic' severity falls in the high risk area. The text states: 'the risk is high and the pilot must either not fly or fly only after finding ways to mitigate, eliminate, or control the risk.'

**2.6 [Mitigating Risk, p.2-8, difficulty 1]**  
A pilot uses the IMSAFE checklist before a flight. Under which letter does the checklist address psychological pressure from the job, money, health, or family problems?

- ✅ **Stress**
- Emotion
- Illness
- Fatigue

  > The IMSAFE checklist's 'S' stands for Stress. The chapter defines it as: 'Am I under psychological pressure from the job? Do I have money, health, or family problems? Stress causes concentration and performance problems.'

**2.7 [The PAVE Checklist, p.2-10, difficulty 2]**  
The chapter identifies management of which PAVE risk category as the single most important key to risk management, because it can cause a pilot to ignore all other risk factors?

- ✅ **External pressures**
- Pilot in command
- Aircraft
- EnVironment

  > The chapter states: 'Management of external pressure is the single most important key to risk management because it is the one risk factor category that can cause a pilot to ignore all the other risk factors.'

**2.8 [Human Factors, p.2-12, difficulty 3]**  
According to the FAA research study described in the chapter, which of the following was NOT identified as a trait found in pilots prone to having accidents?

- ✅ **Strong reliance on checklists and established procedures**
- Disdain toward rules
- High correlation between flying accidents and driving safety violations
- Impulsive rather than methodical information gathering and action selection

  > The chapter lists five traits of accident-prone pilots: disdain toward rules, correlation between flying and driving violations, thrill and adventure seeking, impulsivity, and disregard for outside sources of information. Reliance on checklists is the opposite of the identified traits.

**2.9 [Single-Pilot Resource Management (SRM), p.2-13, difficulty 2]**  
The 5 Ps check recommends reviewing the Plan, the Plane, the Pilot, the Passengers, and the Programming at key decision points. According to the chapter, what is the easiest point to cancel a flight due to bad weather?

- ✅ **During preflight in the flight planning room, before walking out to the aircraft**
- Just prior to takeoff, after all passengers are loaded
- At the midpoint of the flight when ATIS weather is available
- Just prior to descent into the terminal area

  > The chapter states: 'the easiest point to cancel a flight due to bad weather is before the pilot and passengers walk out the door and load the aircraft. So the first decision point is preflight in the flight planning room.'

**2.10 [Perceive, Process, Perform (3P) Model, p.2-16, difficulty 2]**  
In the 3P model, a pilot uses the CARE checklist during the 'Process' step. What does the 'A' in CARE stand for, and what question does it address?

- ✅ **Alternatives — what other options are available**
- Aircraft — is the aircraft suitable for the flight
- Attitude — what hazardous attitudes might affect judgment
- Assessment — what is the overall risk level

  > The CARE checklist stands for Consequences, Alternatives, Reality, External factors. The chapter explains it is used to process hazards by evaluating their impact on flight safety, with 'Alternatives' addressing what other options exist.

**2.11 [Perceive, Process, Perform (3P) Model, p.2-18, difficulty 2]**  
In the 3P model's TEAM checklist, a pilot decides to hire a CFI to accompany her on a cross-country flight to mitigate risk from her lack of experience. Which TEAM action does this represent?

- ✅ **Mitigate**
- Transfer
- Eliminate
- Accept

  > The chapter shows that hiring a CFI to accompany the pilot is listed under 'Mitigate' in the TEAM checklist example. Transfer would mean having another pilot act as PIC; Eliminate would mean canceling the trip entirely.

**2.12 [The DECIDE Model, p.2-20, difficulty 2]**  
In the DECIDE model, what is the first step a pilot should take when faced with a changing situation, such as an engine failure?

- ✅ **Detect — recognize that a change occurred or an expected change did not occur**
- Estimate — determine the need to react to the situation
- Choose — select a course of action to reach the desired outcome
- Identify — formulate solutions that lead to the objective

  > The DECIDE model begins with Detect: 'Problem detection is the first step in the decision-making process. It begins with recognizing a change occurred or an expected change did not occur.'

**2.13 [Decision-Making in a Dynamic Environment, p.2-21, difficulty 3]**  
When an expert pilot faces an emergency with no time for analytical decision-making, what type of decision-making does the chapter describe them using?

- ✅ **Automatic or naturalistic decision-making, taking the first workable option they can find**
- Analytical decision-making, comparing the pros and cons of all available approaches
- The DECIDE model, working through all six steps systematically
- The 5 Ps check, reviewing all five variables before acting

  > The chapter describes automatic/naturalistic decision-making: 'Rather than comparing the pros and cons of different approaches, they quickly imagine how one or a few possible courses of action in such situations will play out. Experts take the first workable option they can find.'

**2.14 [Operational Pitfalls, p.2-22, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot on an IFR approach is tempted to descend below minimums, believing there is a built-in margin of error in every approach procedure. Which operational pitfall does this describe?

- ✅ **Duck-under syndrome**
- Scud running
- Get-there-itis
- Getting behind the aircraft

  > The chapter defines duck-under syndrome as: 'A pilot may be tempted to make it into an airport by descending below minimums during an approach. There may be a belief that there is a built-in margin of error in every approach procedure.'

**2.15 [Stress Management, p.2-21, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, how does performance typically change as stress levels increase?

- ✅ **Performance increases with stress, peaks, then falls off rapidly as stress exceeds the ability to cope**
- Performance decreases steadily as soon as any stress is present
- Performance remains constant until stress reaches a critical threshold, then stops entirely
- Performance increases linearly with stress and never decreases

  > The chapter states: 'Performance generally increases with the onset of stress, peaks, and then begins to fall off rapidly as stress levels exceed a person's ability to cope.'

**2.16 [Situational Awareness, p.2-24, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot is being vectored by ATC but is simply making turns without understanding why. According to the chapter, what problem does this create?

- ✅ **The pilot has added an additional burden to risk management in the event of an emergency**
- The pilot is demonstrating good CRM by trusting ATC completely
- The pilot is reducing workload by not analyzing the vectoring
- The pilot is maintaining situational awareness by following instructions

  > The chapter states: 'A pilot who is simply making turns without understanding why has added an additional burden to his or her management in the event of an emergency.' Understanding why vectors are given is part of maintaining situational awareness.

**2.17 [Workload Management, p.2-24, difficulty 2]**  
When a pilot becomes task saturated due to high workload, what is the primary effect on decision-making?

- ✅ **There is no awareness of input from various sources, so decisions may be made on incomplete information**
- The pilot automatically defaults to using checklists more frequently
- The pilot becomes more efficient by focusing on multiple tasks simultaneously
- The pilot instinctively slows down and prioritizes tasks without conscious effort

  > The chapter states: 'When a pilot becomes task saturated, there is no awareness of input from various sources, so decisions may be made on incomplete information and the possibility of error increases.'

**2.18 [Managing Aircraft Automation, p.2-27, difficulty 3]**  
A study described in the chapter compared pilots flying EFIS-equipped aircraft with pilots flying analog-equipped aircraft. What key finding emerged about the EFIS pilots when required to fly maneuvers manually without a flight director?

- ✅ **They exhibited erosion of flying skills, with greater deviations especially during abnormal and emergency maneuvers**
- They performed identically to the analog group in all maneuvers
- They performed better than the analog group due to greater overall situational awareness
- They performed better in normal maneuvers but worse only in IFR approaches

  > The chapter reports: 'When pilots who had flown EFIS for several years were required to fly various maneuvers manually, the aircraft parameters and flight control inputs clearly showed some erosion of flying skills.' The differences became more significant during abnormal and emergency maneuvers.


## Chapter 3 — 18 questions

**3.1 [Introduction, p.3-1, difficulty 1]**  
According to 14 CFR part 1, how is an airplane defined?

- ✅ **An engine-driven, fixed-wing aircraft supported in flight by the dynamic reaction of air against its wings**
- Any device that is used or intended to be used for flight
- An aircraft that contains a GPS navigation system with a moving map display and an autopilot
- A weight-shift control aircraft that is powered by an engine and supported by a parafoil

  > The chapter states that 14 CFR part 1 defines an airplane as an engine-driven, fixed-wing aircraft that is supported in flight by the dynamic reaction of air against its wings.

**3.2 [Aircraft Design, Certification, and Airworthiness, p.3-2, difficulty 2]**  
What is required for an aircraft's airworthiness certificate to remain valid?

- ✅ **The required maintenance and inspections must be kept up to date**
- The aircraft must pass an annual conformity inspection by the FAA
- The Type Certificate Data Sheet (TCDS) must be updated annually
- The aircraft must be flown at least once every 90 days

  > The text states the airworthiness certificate remains valid as long as the required maintenance and inspections are kept up to date for the aircraft.

**3.3 [Aircraft Design, Certification, and Airworthiness, p.3-2, difficulty 2]**  
Which type of airworthiness certificate is issued for a normal, utility, or acrobatic category aircraft?

- ✅ **Standard airworthiness certificate**
- Special airworthiness certificate
- Provisional airworthiness certificate
- Light Sport Aircraft airworthiness certificate

  > Standard airworthiness certificates are white and issued for normal, utility, acrobatic, commuter, or transport category aircraft.

**3.4 [A Note About Light Sport Aircraft, p.3-2, difficulty 2]**  
How do Light Sport Aircraft (LSA) differ from standard aircraft in terms of design criteria?

- ✅ **They are designed to a consensus of standards agreed upon in the aviation industry**
- They are designed to FAA airworthiness standards under 14 CFR part 23
- They must have individually type certificated engines and propellers
- They are certified under a combination of 14 CFR parts 23, 25, 27, and 29

  > The chapter notes that LSA are not designed according to FAA airworthiness standards, but rather to a consensus of standards agreed upon in the aviation industry, which the FAA has accepted.

**3.5 [Lift and Basic Aerodynamics, p.3-3, difficulty 2]**  
What effect does moving the center of gravity (CG) rearward have on an aircraft?

- ✅ **The aircraft becomes more dynamically unstable**
- The aircraft becomes more stable and easier to flare
- The aircraft's stall speed will significantly increase
- The center of lift moves forward, increasing drag

  > The text states that as the CG moves rearward (towards the tail), the aircraft becomes more and more dynamically unstable.

**3.6 [Wings, p.3-4, difficulty 1]**  
What are the principal structural parts of a wing?

- ✅ **Spars, ribs, and stringers**
- Longerons, ribs, and bulkheads
- Struts, formers, and skin
- Ailerons, flaps, and trim tabs

  > The chapter states that the principal structural parts of the wing are spars, ribs, and stringers, which are reinforced by trusses, I-beams, tubing, or other devices.

**3.7 [Wings, p.3-4, difficulty 2]**  
What type of wing structure is used when a high-wing airplane has external braces or struts transmitting flight and landing loads to the main fuselage?

- ✅ **Semi-cantilever**
- Monocoque
- Full cantilever
- Truss

  > The text explains that since wing struts are usually attached halfway out on the wing, this type of wing structure is called semi-cantilever.

**3.8 [Empennage, p.3-6, difficulty 3]**  
How does an antiservo tab function on a stabilator?

- ✅ **It moves in the same direction as the trailing edge to make the stabilator less sensitive**
- It moves in the opposite direction of the trailing edge to decrease control pressure
- It acts as a trim tab only, moving opposite to the control wheel input
- It provides aerodynamic balance to reduce the physical force needed to move the controls

  > The chapter states that the antiservo tab moves in the same direction as the trailing edge of the stabilator and helps make the stabilator less sensitive.

**3.9 [The Powerplant, p.3-8, difficulty 2]**  
How is the pitch of a propeller defined?

- ✅ **The distance a propeller would travel in one revolution if turning in a solid**
- The angle between the propeller blade and the relative wind
- The rotational speed of the propeller measured in revolutions per minute
- The amount of thrust produced by the propeller at a given power setting

  > The text defines pitch as the distance a propeller would travel in one revolution if it were turning in a solid.

**3.10 [Types of Aircraft Construction, p.3-9, difficulty 3]**  
Why is semimonocoque construction generally preferred over pure monocoque construction in modern aircraft?

- ✅ **Monocoque construction is not highly tolerant to deformation of the surface, which can cause it to collapse easily**
- Monocoque construction lacks a streamlined shape and requires external bracing
- Monocoque construction is highly tolerant to surface deformation
- Semimonocoque uses stressed skin to support almost all loads, eliminating the need for a substructure

  > The text explains that while monocoque construction is very strong, it is not highly tolerant to deformation of the surface and can collapse easily if dented. Semimonocoque uses a substructure to take some of the bending stress.

**3.11 [Composite Construction, p.3-10, difficulty 2]**  
What is a primary disadvantage of carbon fiber compared to fiberglass in aircraft construction?

- ✅ **It is relatively poor in impact resistance and tends to shatter under sharp impact**
- It is significantly heavier and has lower bending stiffness
- It has poor tensile and compressive strength
- It is difficult to form into complex curved or streamlined structures

  > The chapter notes that carbon fiber is stronger, stiffer, and lighter than fiberglass, but it is relatively poor in impact resistance; the fibers are brittle and tend to shatter under sharp impact.

**3.12 [Disadvantages of Composites, p.3-10, difficulty 3]**  
What is a major concern regarding low energy impacts on composite structures?

- ✅ **They may not leave any visible sign of impact but can cause extensive hidden delaminations**
- They always result in visible surface crushing that is easy to detect
- They cause the structure to corrode rapidly from the inside out
- They immediately compromise the lightning strike protection mesh

  > The text states that a low energy impact on a composite structure may not leave any visible sign of the impact on the surface, but underneath there may be extensive delaminations that are hidden from view.

**3.13 [Disadvantages of Composites, p.3-11, difficulty 2]**  
At what approximate temperature do many epoxy resins begin to weaken, prompting the use of white paint on composite aircraft?

- ✅ **150 °F**
- 100 °F
- 220 °F
- 300 °F

  > The chapter states that many epoxies begin to weaken over 150 °F, and white paint on composites is often used to minimize this heat absorption issue.

**3.14 [Disadvantages of Composites, p.3-11, difficulty 2]**  
How should paint be removed from composite aircraft structures?

- ✅ **By using mechanical methods such as gentle grit blasting or sanding**
- By using standard chemical paint strippers
- By applying a specialized solvent and wiping it off with a rag
- By heating the surface with a heat gun and scraping the paint

  > The text specifies that chemical paint strippers are very harmful to composites and must not be used on them. If paint needs to be removed, only mechanical methods are allowed.

**3.15 [Lightning Strike Protection, p.3-12, difficulty 3]**  
How is lightning strike protection typically added to the outside layer of a carbon fiber composite skin?

- ✅ **By bonding copper mesh to the skin surface**
- By applying a thick coat of conductive epoxy paint
- By using aluminum tubing inside the composite layers
- By bonding aluminum mesh to the skin surface

  > The text states that additional electrical conductivity is added with fine metal meshes bonded to the skin surfaces, with aluminum used on fiberglass and copper on carbon fiber.

**3.16 [Instrumentation: Moving into the Future, p.3-13, difficulty 2]**  
Which category of instruments displays immediate attitude and power changes, permitting adjustments in precise increments?

- ✅ **Control instruments**
- Performance instruments
- Navigation instruments
- Primary flight display instruments

  > The chapter states that the control instruments display immediate attitude and power changes and are calibrated to permit adjustments in precise increments.

**3.17 [Instrumentation: Moving into the Future, p.3-12, difficulty 1]**  
Which of the following is considered a performance instrument?

- ✅ **Altimeter**
- Attitude indicator
- Manifold pressure gauge
- VOR indicator

  > The text lists the altimeter, airspeed indicator, VSI, heading indicator, and turn-and-slip indicator as performance instruments. The attitude indicator and manifold pressure gauge are control instruments, and the VOR is a navigation instrument.

**3.18 [Global Positioning System (GPS), p.3-13, difficulty 1]**  
How many satellites must a GPS receiver be locked onto to calculate a three-dimensional position (latitude, longitude, and altitude)?

- ✅ **Four or more**
- At least two
- At least three
- Six or more

  > The chapter explains that with three satellites, a 2D position is calculated. With four or more satellites in view, the receiver can determine the user's three-dimensional position.


## Chapter 4 — 18 questions

**4.1 [Structure of the Atmosphere, p.4-1, difficulty 1]**  
Why is most of the atmosphere's oxygen concentrated below 35,000 feet?

- ✅ **Oxygen is heavier than other atmospheric gases, so it settles toward the Earth's surface.**
- Oxygen is generated primarily at the Earth's surface by plant life and has not had time to rise higher.
- Oxygen molecules are too large to remain suspended above 35,000 feet.
- Oxygen is lighter than nitrogen and rises to fill the upper atmosphere first.

  > The chapter explains that the atmosphere is a mixture of gases, and the heavier elements, such as oxygen, settle to the surface of the Earth, while the lighter elements are lifted up to the region of higher altitude.

**4.2 [Air is a Fluid, p.4-3, difficulty 1]**  
When air flows over a wing, what two properties working together create drag?

- ✅ **Friction (air adhering to the surface) and viscosity (air sticking to itself)**
- Pressure and temperature differences between the upper and lower wing surfaces
- Gravity and the weight of the air flowing over the wing
- Compressibility and density of the air at the leading edge

  > The chapter describes how the boundary layer of air adheres to the wing by friction, and then further resistance is caused by viscosity—the tendency of air to stick to itself. When these two forces act together to resist airflow over a wing, it is called drag.

**4.3 [Atmospheric Pressure, p.4-3, difficulty 2]**  
Under standard atmospheric conditions, approximately what percentage of the atmosphere's weight is below 18,000 feet?

- ✅ **50 percent**
- 25 percent
- 75 percent
- 90 percent

  > The chapter states that the thickness of the atmosphere is limited, so the higher the altitude, the less air there is above. At 18,000 feet, the weight of the atmosphere is one-half what it is at sea level.

**4.4 [Standard Atmosphere, p.4-3, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot is flying at 10,000 feet on a standard day. What temperature should the pilot expect according to the standard atmosphere model?

- ✅ **23.3 °F**
- 44.7 °F
- 37.6 °F
- 30.5 °F

  > The standard atmosphere table in Figure 4-3 lists the temperature at 10,000 feet as 23.3 °F. This is consistent with the standard temperature lapse rate of approximately 3.5 °F per thousand feet from the sea level value of 59 °F.

**4.5 [Pressure Altitude, p.4-4, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot wants to determine pressure altitude using the altimeter. What setting should be entered in the Kollsman window?

- ✅ **29.92 "Hg**
- The current field elevation corrected for nonstandard temperature
- The current altimeter setting reported by ATIS
- 30.00 "Hg to correct for standard sea level pressure

  > The chapter states that pressure altitude can be determined by setting the barometric scale of the altimeter to 29.92 "Hg and reading the indicated altitude. Pressure altitude is the height above the standard datum plane where atmospheric pressure is 29.92 "Hg.

**4.6 [Density Altitude, p.4-4, difficulty 2]**  
Which scenario would result in the highest density altitude?

- ✅ **High elevation, high temperature, high humidity, and low atmospheric pressure**
- Low elevation, high temperature, low humidity, and high atmospheric pressure
- High elevation, low temperature, low humidity, and high atmospheric pressure
- Low elevation, low temperature, high humidity, and low atmospheric pressure

  > The chapter states that high density altitude refers to thin air, and the conditions that result in a high density altitude are high elevations, low atmospheric pressures, high temperatures, high humidity, or some combination of these factors.

**4.7 [Density Altitude, p.4-4, difficulty 2]**  
As air density decreases, how are engine power, thrust, and lift affected?

- ✅ **All three decrease because the engine takes in less air, the propeller is less efficient, and thin air exerts less force on the airfoils.**
- Engine power and thrust decrease, but lift increases because less drag is produced in thin air
- Only lift decreases; engine power and thrust remain unchanged because the engine compensates for density changes
- Engine power increases due to reduced air resistance, but thrust and lift decrease

  > The chapter explicitly lists the three effects of decreased air density: power is reduced because the engine takes in less air, thrust is reduced because a propeller is less efficient in thin air, and lift is reduced because thin air exerts less force on the airfoils.

**4.8 [Effect of Pressure on Density, p.4-4, difficulty 3]**  
The chapter states that air density is directly proportional to pressure and inversely proportional to temperature. In the real atmosphere, both pressure and temperature decrease with altitude. Why does density still decrease with altitude?

- ✅ **The rapid drop in pressure as altitude increases usually has a dominating effect over the temperature decrease.**
- The temperature decrease actually increases density, which amplifies the pressure effect, causing density to increase with altitude
- Both effects cancel each other out, so density remains roughly constant with altitude
- The temperature decrease has a dominating effect because cold air is always denser regardless of pressure changes

  > The chapter explains that in the atmosphere, both temperature and pressure decrease with altitude and have conflicting effects upon density. However, a fairly rapid drop in pressure as altitude increases usually has a dominating effect, so pilots can expect density to decrease with altitude.

**4.9 [Effect of Humidity (Moisture) on Density, p.4-5, difficulty 2]**  
Why does high humidity decrease air density and increase density altitude?

- ✅ **Water vapor is lighter than dry air, so replacing dry air molecules with water vapor molecules reduces the overall mass of the air.**
- Water vapor is heavier than dry air, which compresses the air column and reduces its volume
- Humidity increases air temperature, which in turn increases density altitude independently of the water vapor itself
- Water vapor displaces oxygen molecules, reducing the amount of combustible air available to the engine

  > The chapter states that water vapor is lighter than air; consequently, moist air is lighter than dry air. As the water content of the air increases, the air becomes less dense, increasing density altitude and decreasing performance.

**4.10 [Newton's Basic Laws of Motion, p.4-6, difficulty 1]**  
A propeller pushes air backward. Which principle explains why the airplane moves forward, and which of Newton's laws directly applies?

- ✅ **The air pushes back on the propeller with equal and opposite force, as described by Newton's Third Law.**
- The propeller creates a pressure differential that pulls the airplane forward, as described by Bernoulli's Principle
- The airplane's mass resists changes in motion until thrust overcomes inertia, as described by Newton's First Law
- The acceleration of the airplane is proportional to its mass and the force applied, as described by Newton's Second Law

  > The chapter uses the propeller as a direct example of Newton's Third Law: 'For every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction.' The propeller moves and pushes back the air; consequently, the air pushes the propeller (and thus the airplane) in the opposite direction—forward.

**4.11 [Bernoulli's Principle of Differential Pressure, p.4-6, difficulty 2]**  
In a venturi tube, what happens to air pressure at the constriction (throat) compared to the inlet?

- ✅ **Pressure decreases because the velocity increases at the constriction.**
- Pressure increases because the air is compressed at the narrow throat
- Pressure remains constant because the same mass of air enters and exits the tube
- Pressure decreases because the air cools as it passes through the constriction

  > The chapter explains that in a venturi tube, the mass of air entering must equal the mass exiting. At the constriction, speed must increase to allow the same amount of air to pass. Bernoulli's Principle states that as velocity increases, pressure decreases. So at the constriction, pressure drops.

**4.12 [Airfoil Design, p.4-6, difficulty 2]**  
What is the chord line of an airfoil?

- ✅ **A straight line connecting the leading edge to the trailing edge**
- A line equidistant at all points from the upper and lower surfaces of the airfoil
- A line drawn from the leading edge to the point of maximum camber on the upper surface
- A line representing the direction of the oncoming airflow relative to the airfoil

  > The chapter defines the chord line as a straight line drawn through the profile connecting the extremities of the leading and trailing edges. The line equidistant from upper and lower surfaces is the mean camber line, which is a different reference.

**4.13 [Airfoil Design, p.4-7, difficulty 3]**  
Why don't modern high-speed jet aircraft use a concave (scooped-out lower surface) airfoil as a fixed design, despite it being the most efficient at producing lift?

- ✅ **A concave airfoil sacrifices too much speed while producing lift, making it unsuitable for high-speed flight.**
- A concave airfoil produces too much drag at high angles of attack, making stalls uncontrollable
- A concave airfoil cannot generate enough lift at the high angles of attack required for high-speed flight
- A concave airfoil is structurally too weak to withstand the forces encountered at high speeds

  > The chapter states that the most efficient airfoil for producing the greatest lift is one with a concave lower surface, but as a fixed design, this type of airfoil sacrifices too much speed while producing lift and is not suitable for high-speed flight. Modern jets instead use leading and trailing edge flaps to temporarily change the airfoil shape into a concave form for slow flight.

**4.14 [Low Pressure Above, p.4-7, difficulty 2]**  
If a teardrop-shaped airfoil is cut in half lengthwise and inclined so airflow strikes it at an angle, what causes the reduced pressure on the upper surface?

- ✅ **Air moving over the upper surface is forced to move faster than air along the bottom, and the increased velocity reduces the pressure per Bernoulli's Principle.**
- The angled airfoil deflects air downward from the upper surface, creating a vacuum above the wing
- The lower surface blocks airflow, compressing air below and forcing air above to expand and cool
- Friction between the air and the upper surface heats the air, causing it to rise and reduce pressure

  > The chapter describes how cutting a teardrop airfoil in half produces a basic wing section. When inclined at an angle, air moving over the upper surface is forced to move faster than air along the bottom. Applying Bernoulli's Principle, this increased velocity reduces the pressure above the airfoil, contributing to lift.

**4.15 [High Pressure Below, p.4-8, difficulty 2]**  
At a point near the leading edge of the lower surface of an airfoil, the airflow is virtually stopped. What is this point called, and what is the pressure effect beneath the airfoil?

- ✅ **It is called the stagnation point, and the slowed airflow creates a positive upward pressure beneath the airfoil.**
- It is called the boundary layer, and the slowed airflow creates a negative pressure that reduces lift
- It is called the center of pressure, and the slowed airflow creates equal pressure on both surfaces
- It is called the downwash point, and the slowed airflow creates drag that opposes thrust

  > The chapter identifies the stagnation point near the leading edge where airflow is virtually stopped. As the airflow slows beneath the airfoil, Bernoulli's principle dictates that pressure must increase, creating a positive upward pressure that contributes to total lift.

**4.16 [Pressure Distribution, p.4-8, difficulty 3]**  
How does the center of pressure (CP) move as the angle of attack of an airfoil changes from low to high?

- ✅ **The CP moves forward as angle of attack increases and moves aft as angle of attack decreases.**
- The CP moves aft as angle of attack increases and forward as angle of attack decreases
- The CP remains fixed at the aerodynamic center regardless of changes in angle of attack
- The CP moves in a circular pattern around the chord line as angle of attack changes

  > The chapter states that at high angles of attack, the CP moves forward, while at low angles of attack, the CP moves aft. This CP travel is important in wing structure design and affects the airplane's aerodynamic balance and controllability.

**4.17 [Airfoil Behavior, p.4-9, difficulty 3]**  
Symmetrical airfoils and flat plates (like a paper airplane) can produce lift even though their upper and lower surfaces are identical. What does this demonstrate about the production of lift?

- ✅ **Lift can be produced by flow turning—the deflection of air caused by the airfoil's angle relative to the oncoming airstream—not just by pressure differences from unequal surface curvature.**
- Symmetrical airfoils produce lift only at supersonic speeds where compressibility effects create pressure differences
- Symmetrical airfoils cannot actually produce lift; they require asymmetric flaps to function
- Symmetrical airfoils produce lift because the stagnation point shifts to the lower surface at all angles of attack

  > The chapter notes that many lifting airfoils, including symmetrical airfoils and flat plates, do not have an upper surface longer than the bottom. The only difference is the relationship of the airfoil with the oncoming airstream (angle). Flow turning—deflection of the air—is partly or fully responsible for creating lift in these cases.

**4.18 [A Third Dimension, p.4-9, difficulty 2]**  
What causes a tip vortex to form at the end of a wing, and what is its effect on the airfoil's performance?

- ✅ **High-pressure air from beneath the wing flows around the tip to the low-pressure area on top, creating a rotating vortex that produces downwash and reduces lift for the affected portion of the airfoil.**
- Air stagnating at the leading edge spills over the tip, creating a vortex that increases lift at the wingtip
- Friction between the wingtip and the air creates a vortex that increases drag but has no effect on lift
- The boundary layer separates at the trailing edge and curls around the tip, creating turbulence that increases lift at the wingtip

  > The chapter explains that the high-pressure area on the bottom of an airfoil pushes around the tip to the low-pressure area on top, creating a rotating flow called a tip vortex. The vortex flows behind the airfoil creating a downwash that results in an overall reduction in lift for the affected portion of the airfoil.


## Chapter 5 — 18 questions

**5.1 [Forces Acting on the Aircraft, p.5-2, difficulty 2]**  
In straight-and-level, unaccelerated flight, which statement correctly describes the relationship between the four forces acting on an aircraft?

- ✅ **The sum of all upward components of forces equals the sum of all downward components, and the sum of all forward components equals the sum of all backward components**
- All four forces—thrust, drag, lift, and weight—are equal in magnitude
- Thrust is always greater than drag, while lift is always greater than weight
- Weight is always the greatest force, with lift being slightly less than weight

  > The FAA handbook clarifies that the simplified 'lift equals weight and thrust equals drag' description can be misleading because it assumes all forces act purely vertically or horizontally. In reality, forces such as thrust may not be perfectly aligned with the flight path. The correct principle is that the sum of all upward force components equals the sum of all downward force components, and the sum of all forward force components equals the sum of all backward force components.

**5.2 [Lift, p.5-4, difficulty 2]**  
If an aircraft's airspeed doubles while the angle of attack and all other factors remain constant, what happens to the lift produced?

- ✅ **Lift increases by a factor of four**
- Lift doubles
- Lift increases by a factor of three
- Lift remains the same because AOA hasn't changed

  > The lift equation (L = CL · ρ · V² · S / 2) shows that lift is proportional to the square of velocity. The chapter explicitly states that an airplane traveling at 200 knots has four times the lift as the same airplane traveling at 100 knots, if AOA and other factors remain constant.

**5.3 [Lift/Drag Ratio, p.5-5, difficulty 2]**  
At the angle of attack where the lift-to-drag ratio (L/D) is at its maximum, what condition exists?

- ✅ **The total drag is at its minimum**
- The coefficient of lift is at its maximum
- The coefficient of drag is at its minimum
- The aircraft is flying at its maximum speed

  > The chapter states that the maximum L/D ratio (L/DMAX) occurs at one specific CL and AOA. If the aircraft is operated in steady flight at L/DMAX, the total drag is at a minimum. CL-MAX occurs at the critical (stalling) AOA, which is a different point. The coefficient of drag being at its minimum is not the same as total drag being at its minimum.

**5.4 [Drag, p.5-8, difficulty 2]**  
How does induced drag vary with changes in airspeed?

- ✅ **Induced drag varies inversely with the square of the airspeed**
- Induced drag increases as the square of the airspeed
- Induced drag varies directly and proportionally with airspeed
- Induced drag remains constant regardless of airspeed

  > The chapter states that as AOA increases (which happens at lower airspeeds to maintain lift), induced drag increases proportionally. Specifically, 'the amount of induced drag varies inversely with the square of the airspeed.' This is the opposite of parasite drag, which increases as the square of airspeed.

**5.5 [Wingtip Vortices, p.5-9, difficulty 1]**  
Under what conditions are wingtip vortices the strongest from a generating aircraft?

- ✅ **When the generating aircraft is heavy, clean, and slow**
- When the generating aircraft is light, dirty, and fast
- When the generating aircraft is at high altitude in thin air
- When the generating aircraft has full flaps extended at cruise speed

  > The chapter explicitly states that wingtip vortices are greatest when the generating aircraft is 'heavy, clean, and slow.' This condition is most commonly encountered during approaches or departures because the aircraft's AOA is at its highest to produce the lift necessary to land or take off.

**5.6 [Ground Effect, p.5-12, difficulty 2]**  
What happens when an aircraft leaves ground effect after takeoff with a deficiency of airspeed?

- ✅ **The aircraft experiences an increase in induced drag, requires an increase in AOA to maintain the same CL, and may have marginal initial climb performance**
- The aircraft experiences a decrease in induced drag and improved climb performance
- The aircraft experiences a nose-down pitch change and increased stability
- The aircraft requires less thrust and a lower AOA to maintain flight

  > The chapter explains that an aircraft leaving ground effect will require an increase in AOA to maintain the same CL, experience an increase in induced drag and thrust required, and experience a decrease in stability and a nose-up change in moment. With a deficiency of speed, the greater induced drag may result in marginal initial climb performance, and in extreme conditions the aircraft may settle back to the runway.

**5.7 [Axes of an Aircraft, p.5-13, difficulty 1]**  
Which motion of an aircraft occurs about its lateral axis?

- ✅ **Pitch**
- Roll
- Yaw
- Sideslip

  > The chapter defines the three axes and their associated motions: motion about the longitudinal axis is 'roll,' motion about the lateral axis is 'pitch,' and motion about the vertical axis is 'yaw.' The lateral axis passes through the CG parallel to a line from wingtip to wingtip.

**5.8 [Aircraft Design Characteristics, p.5-14, difficulty 2]**  
An aircraft that, after being disturbed from equilibrium, initially tends to remain in its new displaced condition rather than returning or continuing away, is exhibiting which type of static stability?

- ✅ **Neutral static stability**
- Positive static stability
- Negative static stability
- Dynamic stability

  > The chapter defines three types of static stability. Positive static stability is the initial tendency to return to equilibrium. Negative static stability is the initial tendency to continue moving away from equilibrium. Neutral static stability is the initial tendency to remain in the new condition after being disturbed. Dynamic stability refers to the aircraft's response over time, not the initial tendency.

**5.9 [Longitudinal Stability (Pitching), p.5-16, difficulty 3]**  
Why do aircraft designers typically place the center of gravity (CG) forward of the center of lift (CL)?

- ✅ **To create a nose-heavy tendency that is counterbalanced by a downward force on the horizontal stabilizer, providing longitudinal stability**
- To align the thrust line with the flight path and reduce drag in cruise
- To eliminate the need for a horizontal stabilizer entirely
- To increase maneuverability by making the aircraft less stable in pitch

  > The chapter explains that when the CG is forward of the CL, the aircraft is 'nose heavy' and requires a downward force on the horizontal stabilizer to balance it. This arrangement provides longitudinal stability: if the aircraft pitches nose up or down, the changing moments from the wing and tail work to restore the original attitude. Designers fix the aft limit of the CG forward of the CP to retain flight equilibrium.

**5.10 [Lateral Stability (Rolling), p.5-18, difficulty 2]**  
How does dihedral contribute to lateral stability when an aircraft is disturbed by a gust causing a roll?

- ✅ **The resulting sideslip increases the AOA on the wing being lowered, generating more lift on that wing and rolling the aircraft back toward level**
- The sideslip causes both wings to generate equal lift, stabilizing the aircraft
- The fuselage weight acts as a pendulum below the wings to return the aircraft to level
- The sweepback of the wing creates more drag on the low wing, pulling it back up

  > The chapter explains that when a gust causes a roll, a sideslip results. The relative wind from the side causes the wing slipping into the wind (the lowered wing) to experience an increased AOA and more lift, while the wing on the other side experiences decreased AOA and less lift. This rolling moment tends to raise the lowered wing, restoring the aircraft to level flight. The pendulum effect described in option B is the keel effect, and option C describes sweepback, not dihedral.

**5.11 [Aerodynamic Forces in Flight Maneuvers, p.5-22, difficulty 2]**  
In a coordinated, banked turn, what force actually causes the aircraft to deviate from a straight flight path?

- ✅ **The horizontal component of lift**
- The rudder pushing the tail in the desired direction of turn
- Centrifugal force pulling the aircraft toward the center of the turn
- The vertical component of lift acting opposite to weight

  > The chapter explains that when an aircraft banks, lift acts inward toward the center of the turn as well as upward. The horizontal component of lift (centripetal force) is what pulls the aircraft from a straight flight path to make it turn. The rudder is used only to keep the nose and tail tracking along the same path, not to turn the aircraft. Centrifugal force is the equal and opposite reaction, acting outward, not inward.

**5.12 [Stalls, p.5-25, difficulty 2]**  
Which statement about stalls is correct according to the chapter?

- ✅ **A given aircraft always stalls at the same AOA regardless of airspeed, weight, load factor, or density altitude**
- An aircraft stalls at a fixed airspeed that does not change with weight or bank angle
- Stalls can only occur at low airspeeds below the published stall speed
- The critical AOA changes depending on the aircraft's weight and density altitude

  > The chapter explicitly states that each aircraft has a particular AOA where airflow separates and the stall occurs, and this critical AOA does not change with weight, bank angle, temperature, density altitude, or CG. The stalling speed, however, does vary with these factors. An aircraft can be stalled at any airspeed if the critical AOA is exceeded.

**5.13 [Basic Propeller Principles, p.5-30, difficulty 3]**  
Why are propeller blades twisted along their length, with greater blade angle near the hub and lesser blade angle near the tip?

- ✅ **To maintain a relatively constant AOA along the entire blade length despite differences in rotational velocity from hub to tip**
- To reduce noise generated by the propeller tips at high RPM
- To increase the geometric pitch at the hub for better climb performance
- To balance the propeller dynamically for smoother operation at cruise RPM

  > The chapter explains that the outer parts of propeller blades travel faster than portions near the hub. If blades had the same geometric pitch throughout, portions near the hub could have negative AOAs while the tips would be stalled at cruise speed. Twisting the blades varies the blade angle in proportion to the speed differences, keeping the AOA relatively constant and thrust more equalized along the blade length.

**5.14 [Torque and P-Factor, p.5-32, difficulty 3]**  
At a high angle of attack, why does asymmetric loading (P-factor) cause a left yawing tendency in aircraft with clockwise-rotating propellers (as viewed from the cockpit)?

- ✅ **The downward-moving blade on the right side has a higher resultant velocity and produces more thrust than the upward-moving blade**
- The upward-moving blade stalls first, reducing thrust on the left side of the propeller disc
- Gyroscopic precession deflects the propeller force 90° ahead in the direction of rotation
- The corkscrew slipstream strikes the left side of the vertical fin, pushing the nose left

  > The chapter explains that at high AOA, the downward-moving (right) blade has a greater resultant velocity because the aircraft's forward velocity adds to the blade's rotational velocity. Since the propeller blade is an airfoil, increased velocity means increased lift (thrust). This moves the center of thrust to the right, causing a yawing moment toward the left. Option B describes gyroscopic precession, option C describes the corkscrew effect—both are separate elements of left-turning tendency.

**5.15 [Load Factors, p.5-34, difficulty 2]**  
In a coordinated, constant-altitude turn, what is the load factor at a 60° bank angle?

- ✅ **2 Gs**
- 1.41 Gs
- 1.73 Gs
- 3 Gs

  > The chapter states that the load factor for any aircraft in a coordinated level turn at 60° bank is 2 Gs. The load factor increases rapidly after 45°–50° of bank. At 80° bank, the load factor would be 5.76 Gs. A 90° banked, constant-altitude turn is not mathematically possible.

**5.16 [Load Factors and Stalling Speeds, p.5-34, difficulty 3]**  
If an aircraft has a normal unaccelerated stalling speed of 50 knots, at approximately what airspeed would it stall if subjected to a load factor of 4 Gs?

- ✅ **100 knots**
- 75 knots
- 150 knots
- 200 knots
