  > The chapter states that stalling speed increases in proportion to the square root of the load factor. The square root of 4 is 2, so the stalling speed doubles from 50 to 100 knots. The chapter uses this exact example: 'an aircraft with a normal unaccelerated stalling speed of 50 knots can be stalled at 100 knots by inducing a load factor of 4 Gs.'

**5.17 [Vg Diagram, p.5-37, difficulty 3]**  
What is the significance of the maneuvering speed (VA) as depicted on the Vg diagram?

- ✅ **Below this speed, the aircraft will stall before it can produce a damaging positive flight load**
- It is the maximum speed at which the aircraft can be flown in smooth air
- It is the speed at which the aircraft reaches its maximum coefficient of lift
- It is the minimum speed at which the aircraft can sustain level flight

  > The chapter explains that the maneuvering speed is the intersection of the positive limit load factor and the line of maximum positive lift capability on the Vg diagram. Below this speed, the aircraft cannot produce enough lift to exceed the limit load factor—it will stall first. The chapter states, 'an aircraft operating below this point cannot produce a damaging positive flight load.' VA is not the never-exceed speed (VNE), nor is it CL-MAX or minimum level flight speed.

**5.18 [Radius of Turn, p.5-40, difficulty 2]**  
If an aircraft doubles its airspeed while maintaining the same bank angle, what happens to the radius of turn?

- ✅ **The radius quadruples**
- The radius doubles
- The radius remains the same because bank angle is unchanged
- The radius is halved

  > The chapter's radius of turn formula (R = V² / 11.26 × tangent of bank angle) shows that radius is proportional to velocity squared. The chapter provides a concrete example: at 120 knots with 30° bank, the radius is 2,215 feet; at 240 knots with the same 30° bank, the radius is 8,861 feet—four times greater. The chapter emphasizes that 'airspeed is the most influential factor in determining how much distance is required to turn.'


## Chapter 6 — 20 questions

**6.1 [Flight Control Systems, p.6-2, difficulty 1]**  
What distinguishes secondary flight controls from primary flight controls?

- ✅ **Primary controls are required for safe flight; secondary controls improve performance or relieve control forces**
- Primary controls are hydraulically actuated; secondary controls are mechanically actuated
- Primary controls affect all three axes; secondary controls affect only one axis at a time
- Primary controls are used during takeoff and landing; secondary controls are used only during cruise

  > The chapter states that ailerons, elevator, and rudder constitute the primary control system and are required to control an aircraft safely during flight, while wing flaps, leading edge devices, spoilers, and trim systems constitute the secondary control system and improve performance characteristics or relieve the pilot of excessive control forces.

**6.2 [Introduction, p.6-2, difficulty 2]**  
What distinguishes a fly-by-wire flight control system from a traditional mechanical system?

- ✅ **It replaces the physical connection between pilot controls and control surfaces with an electrical interface**
- It uses hydraulic pressure to boost control forces while maintaining mechanical linkages
- It uses fiber optic cables to mechanically actuate the control surfaces directly
- It uses a neural network to autonomously fly the aircraft without pilot input

  > The chapter describes fly-by-wire as replacing the physical connection between pilot controls and the flight control surfaces with an electrical interface. Hydromechanical systems, by contrast, still use a mechanical circuit combined with a hydraulic circuit.

**6.3 [Primary Flight Controls, p.6-3, difficulty 1]**  
How do flight controls typically feel and respond at low airspeeds compared to high airspeeds?

- ✅ **Soft and sluggish with slow response at low airspeeds; firm and rapid response at high airspeeds**
- Firm and rapid response at low airspeeds; soft and sluggish with slow response at high airspeeds
- Identical feel and response at all airspeeds due to control system design limits
- Soft at low airspeeds but with rapid response; firm at high airspeeds but with slow response

  > The chapter explains that at low airspeeds, controls usually feel soft and sluggish and the aircraft responds slowly, while at higher airspeeds, controls become increasingly firm and aircraft response is more rapid.

**6.4 [Adverse Yaw, p.6-3, difficulty 2]**  
When rolling right, why does adverse yaw tend to pull the nose to the left?

- ✅ **The down-going aileron on the left wing creates more lift and more drag, slowing that wing and yawing the nose left**
- The up-going aileron on the right wing creates more drag, slowing the right wing and yawing the nose left
- The rudder automatically deflects left due to the aileron-rudder interconnect springs
- The right wing stalls first due to increased angle of attack, causing the nose to yaw left

  > The chapter explains that the downward-deflected aileron produces more lift and therefore more drag, slowing that wing. This results in the aircraft yawing toward the wing that experienced an increase in lift and drag, which is opposite the direction of bank.

**6.5 [Differential Ailerons, p.6-4, difficulty 2]**  
How do differential ailerons reduce adverse yaw?

- ✅ **The up-going aileron is raised more than the down-going aileron is lowered, creating more drag on the descending wing**
- Both ailerons deflect equally but in opposite directions to balance drag on both wings
- The down-going aileron is lowered more than the up-going aileron is raised, increasing drag on the rising wing
- The ailerons are mechanically linked to the rudder to automatically coordinate turns

  > The chapter states that with differential ailerons, one aileron is raised a greater distance than the other is lowered, producing increased drag on the descending wing. While adverse yaw is reduced, it is not eliminated completely.

**6.6 [Frise-Type Ailerons, p.6-4, difficulty 2]**  
How does a frise-type aileron help counteract adverse yaw?

- ✅ **The raised aileron's leading edge projects into the airflow, creating drag on the descending wing to equalize drag**
- A slot is formed that directs high-energy air over the lowered aileron, reducing its drag entirely
- The ailerons are mechanically coupled to the rudder pedals to automatically coordinate turns
- Both ailerons are lowered simultaneously to function as flaps and increase lift equally

  > The chapter explains that with a frise-type aileron, the aileron being raised pivots on an offset hinge, projecting its leading edge into the airflow to create drag. This helps equalize the drag created by the lowered aileron on the opposite wing and reduces adverse yaw, though it does not eliminate it entirely.

**6.7 [T-Tail, p.6-6, difficulty 2]**  
Why does a T-tail aircraft require greater elevator deflection to raise the nose at slow speeds compared to a conventional-tail aircraft?

- ✅ **The T-tail is above the propeller downwash that assists conventional-tail aircraft in raising the nose**
- The T-tail horizontal surface is smaller and produces less aerodynamic force at all speeds
- The T-tail is continuously affected by wing wake at every airspeed and AOA
- The T-tail's antiservo tab opposes elevator movement only at slow speeds

  > The chapter explains that conventional-tail aircraft benefit from propeller downwash pushing down on the tail to assist in raising the nose. The T-tail, being above this downwash, does not receive this assistance, requiring greater elevator movement and force at slow speeds.

**6.8 [T-Tail, p.6-6, difficulty 3]**  
Which combination of conditions makes a T-tail aircraft most susceptible to a deep stall?

- ✅ **High AOA, low airspeed, and aft CG**
- Low AOA, high airspeed, and forward CG
- High AOA, high airspeed, and forward CG
- Low AOA, low airspeed, and aft CG

  > The chapter states that when flying at a very high AOA with a low airspeed and an aft CG, the T-tail aircraft may be more susceptible to a deep stall, where the wake of the wing impinges on the tail surface and renders it almost ineffective.

**6.9 [T-Tail, p.6-6, difficulty 3]**  
What is the purpose of an elevator down spring?

- ✅ **It mechanically drives the elevator to a nose-down position if the trim tab becomes ineffective, preventing a stall**
- It increases elevator sensitivity at high airspeeds by opposing the pilot's control inputs
- It automatically trims the elevator for level flight during cruise without pilot input
- It prevents the elevator from moving past its design limits during high-speed flight

  > The chapter explains that an elevator down spring produces a mechanical load on the elevator, causing it to move toward the nose-down position. When the trim tab becomes ineffective at low airspeeds, the down spring drives the elevator nose-down, lowering the nose, building speed, and preventing a stall.

**6.10 [Stabilator, p.6-7, difficulty 2]**  
What is the purpose of an antiservo tab on a stabilator?

- ✅ **It deflects in the same direction as the stabilator to increase the force required to move it, preventing overcontrolling**
- It deflects opposite to the stabilator to reduce control forces and make the aircraft easier to maneuver
- It trims the stabilator by moving opposite to the control surface, just like an elevator trim tab
- It provides additional lift to the horizontal tail surface at high angles of attack

  > The chapter states that because stabilators are extremely sensitive to control inputs, antiservo tabs are incorporated on the trailing edge to decrease sensitivity. They deflect in the same direction as the stabilator, increasing the force required to move it and making it less prone to pilot-induced overcontrolling.

**6.11 [Canard, p.6-7, difficulty 2]**  
How does a canard design differ from a conventional aft-tail configuration in providing longitudinal stability?

- ✅ **The canard creates lift to hold the nose up, while a conventional tail exerts a downward force on the tail**
- The canard exerts a downward force like a conventional tail but is mounted forward of the wing
- The canard uses a servo tab instead of aerodynamic force for pitch stability
- The canard relies on thrust vectoring rather than an aerodynamic surface for stability

  > The chapter explains that the canard is an airfoil that actually creates lift and holds the nose up, as opposed to the aft-tail design which exerts a downward force on the tail to prevent the nose from rotating downward. Theoretically, the canard is more efficient because both surfaces contribute to lifting the aircraft's weight.

**6.12 [Rudder, p.6-8, difficulty 1]**  
How does rudder effectiveness change with airspeed, and what does this mean for required control inputs?

- ✅ **Effectiveness increases with speed, requiring large deflections at low speed and small deflections at high speed**
- Effectiveness decreases with speed, requiring small deflections at low speed and large deflections at high speed
- Effectiveness remains constant regardless of speed, requiring the same deflection at all speeds
- Effectiveness increases with speed, requiring small deflections at low speed and large deflections at high speed

  > The chapter states that rudder effectiveness increases with speed. Therefore, large deflections at low speeds and small deflections at high speeds may be required to provide the desired reaction. In propeller-driven aircraft, slipstream over the rudder also increases its effectiveness.

**6.13 [V-Tail, p.6-8, difficulty 2]**  
On a V-tail aircraft, how do the ruddervators respond to elevator versus rudder inputs?

- ✅ **The control wheel moves both surfaces simultaneously; rudder pedals move them differentially**
- The control wheel moves the surfaces differentially; rudder pedals move them simultaneously
- Both controls move the surfaces simultaneously but in opposite directions from each other
- The control wheel moves only the left surface; rudder pedals move only the right surface

  > The chapter explains that ruddervators are connected through a special linkage allowing the control wheel to move both surfaces simultaneously for pitch control, while rudder pedal displacement moves the surfaces differentially for directional control. A control mixing mechanism handles combined inputs.

**6.14 [Flaps, p.6-9, difficulty 2]**  
How do slotted flaps achieve a greater increase in maximum lift coefficient compared to plain or split flaps?

- ✅ **High-energy air from the lower surface is ducted to the flap's upper surface, delaying airflow separation**
- The flap slides backward on tracks, increasing wing area as well as camber
- The flap deflects from the lower surface only, creating turbulent air that increases lift
- The flap increases camber without changing the center of pressure on the airfoil

  > The chapter explains that when slotted flaps are lowered, a duct forms between the flap well and the flap's leading edge, ducting high-energy air from the lower surface to the flap's upper surface. This accelerates the boundary layer and delays airflow separation, providing a higher CL-MAX than plain or split flaps.

**6.15 [Flaps, p.6-9, difficulty 2]**  
How do Fowler flaps differ from plain, split, and slotted flaps?

- ✅ **They slide backward on tracks to increase wing area as well as camber**
- They deflect from the lower surface only to create maximum drag with minimal lift increase
- They use a slot to direct high-energy air over the upper surface without changing camber
- They rotate down on a simple hinge to increase camber only, with no area change

  > The chapter explains that Fowler flaps not only change the camber of the wing but also increase the wing area. Instead of rotating down on a hinge, they slide backward on tracks. In the first portion of extension, drag increases very little while lift increases greatly due to the increase in both area and camber.

**6.16 [Leading Edge Devices, p.6-9, difficulty 2]**  
What do fixed slots accomplish, and how do they differ from leading edge flaps?

- ✅ **Fixed slots direct airflow to the upper surface to delay stall without increasing camber; leading edge flaps increase camber**
- Fixed slots increase camber and wing area; leading edge flaps only delay stall without changing camber
- Fixed slots move on tracks at high AOA; leading edge flaps are fixed aerodynamic devices
- Fixed slots reduce drag at cruise speed; leading edge flaps increase drag at cruise speed

  > The chapter states that fixed slots direct airflow to the upper wing surface and delay airflow separation at higher angles of attack without increasing wing camber. Leading edge flaps, by contrast, are used to increase both CL-MAX and the camber of the wings.

**6.17 [Spoilers, p.6-10, difficulty 2]**  
When spoilers are used for roll control, why is adverse yaw eliminated?

- ✅ **The spoiler creates drag on the same wing that loses lift, so the yaw is in the same direction as the roll**
- The spoiler creates additional lift on the opposite wing, balancing the drag on both sides
- The spoilers are mechanically linked to the rudder for automatic turn coordination
- The spoilers reduce drag on both wings equally, preventing any differential yaw

  > The chapter explains that to turn right, the spoiler on the right wing is raised, destroying lift and creating more drag on the right. The right wing drops and the aircraft banks and yaws to the right. Since the drag and lift loss occur on the same side, the yaw is in the same direction as the roll, eliminating adverse yaw.

**6.18 [Trim Tabs, p.6-10, difficulty 2]**  
When a pilot applies full nose-down trim using the elevator trim tab, what happens to the tab and the elevator?

- ✅ **The trim tab moves up, forcing the elevator trailing edge down, moving the tail up and nose down**
- The trim tab moves down, forcing the elevator trailing edge up, moving the tail down and nose up
- The trim tab moves down, forcing the elevator trailing edge down, moving the tail up and nose down
- The trim tab moves up, forcing the elevator trailing edge up, moving the tail down and nose up

  > The chapter explains that placing the trim control in the full nose-down position moves the trim tab to its full up position. The airflow over the horizontal tail surface forces the trailing edge of the elevator down, causing the tail to move up and the nose to move down. The trim tab always moves opposite to the elevator.

**6.19 [Balance Tabs, p.6-11, difficulty 3]**  
How does a balance tab differ from a trim tab in its operation?

- ✅ **A balance tab automatically moves opposite the primary control surface to reduce control forces; a trim tab is manually positioned by the pilot**
- A balance tab moves in the same direction as the control surface to increase sensitivity; a trim tab moves opposite
- A balance tab is only adjustable on the ground; a trim tab is adjustable in flight
- A balance tab is used on large aircraft only; a trim tab is used on small aircraft only

  > The chapter explains that the essential difference between balance tabs and trim tabs is that the balance tab is coupled to the control surface rod so that when the primary control surface is moved in any direction, the tab automatically moves in the opposite direction. The airflow striking the tab counterbalances air pressure against the primary control surface, enabling easier movement.

**6.20 [Autopilot, p.6-12, difficulty 1]**  
What does a single-axis autopilot control, and which control surface does it actuate?

- ✅ **It controls the longitudinal axis (roll) and actuates the ailerons**
- It controls the lateral axis (pitch) and actuates the elevator
- It controls the vertical axis (yaw) and actuates the rudder
- It controls all three axes but only actuates the ailerons

  > The chapter states that a single-axis autopilot controls the aircraft about the longitudinal axis and a servo actuates the ailerons. A three-axis autopilot, by contrast, controls all three axes using separate servos for ailerons, elevator, and rudder.


## Chapter 7 — 18 questions

**7.1 [Reciprocating Engines, p.7-2, difficulty 1]**  
What is the main difference between a spark ignition engine and a compression ignition engine?

- ✅ **The process used to ignite the fuel-air mixture in the cylinder**
- The type of fuel that can be used in the engine
- The number of strokes in the operating cycle
- The cylinder arrangement with respect to the crankshaft

  > The text states that the main mechanical components of both engine types are essentially the same, and the main difference is the ignition process. Spark ignition uses a spark plug to ignite a pre-mixed fuel-air mixture, while compression ignition compresses air first to raise its temperature for automatic ignition when fuel is injected.

**7.2 [Propeller, p.7-4, difficulty 2]**  
Why is a propeller blade twisted so that the blade angle changes from hub to tip?

- ✅ **To produce uniform lift throughout the length of the blade**
- To reduce aerodynamic drag at the propeller tip
- To increase the blade angle at the tip for greater thrust
- To prevent the propeller hub from stalling at high rpm

  > The tip of the blade travels faster than the hub because it covers a greater distance in the same time. The twist adjusts the angle of incidence from hub to tip to correspond with the different speeds, producing uniform lift throughout the blade length.

**7.3 [Fixed-Pitch Propeller, p.7-5, difficulty 2]**  
On an aircraft with a fixed-pitch propeller, if the tachometer shows 2,300 rpm at 5,000 feet, how does the engine power output compare to 2,300 rpm at sea level?

- ✅ **It produces less horsepower at 5,000 feet than at sea level**
- It produces more horsepower due to cooler air at altitude
- It produces the same horsepower because rpm is unchanged
- It produces less horsepower only if the mixture is not leaned

  > The text explains that power output depends on air density. Air density decreases as altitude increases, so 2,300 rpm at 5,000 feet produces less horsepower than 2,300 rpm at sea level. The throttle must be opened further at altitude to maintain the same rpm.

**7.4 [Adjustable-Pitch Propeller, p.7-6, difficulty 2]**  
On an aircraft with a constant-speed propeller, what does the manifold pressure gauge measure?

- ✅ **The absolute pressure of the fuel-air mixture inside the intake manifold**
- The rotational speed of the propeller in rpm
- The pressure difference between the fuel pump and carburetor
- The density altitude at the current flight altitude

  > The manifold pressure gauge measures the absolute pressure of the fuel-air mixture inside the intake manifold (MAP). On a constant-speed propeller aircraft, power output is controlled by the throttle and indicated by the manifold pressure gauge, while rpm is regulated by the propeller control.

**7.5 [Adjustable-Pitch Propeller, p.7-7, difficulty 3]**  
When decreasing power settings on an aircraft with a constant-speed propeller, what is the proper order of adjustments to avoid engine overstress?

- ✅ **Reduce manifold pressure first, then reduce rpm**
- Reduce rpm first, then reduce manifold pressure
- Reduce both manifold pressure and rpm simultaneously
- The order does not matter when decreasing power

  > If rpm is reduced before manifold pressure, the manifold pressure automatically increases, possibly exceeding the manufacturer's tolerances. When increasing power, the order is reversed—increase rpm first, then manifold pressure.

**7.6 [Carburetor Icing, p.7-9, difficulty 2]**  
Under what temperature and humidity conditions is carburetor ice most likely to occur?

- ✅ **Below 70 °F (21 °C) with relative humidity above 80 percent**
- Only below 32 °F (0 °C) with relative humidity above 50 percent
- Above 80 °F (27 °C) with relative humidity below 30 percent
- Only at temperatures below freezing regardless of humidity

  > The text states carburetor ice is most likely when temperatures are below 70 °F (21 °C) and relative humidity is above 80 percent. However, due to the sudden cooling in the carburetor, icing can occur at outside air temperatures as high as 100 °F (38 °C) with humidity as low as 50 percent.

**7.7 [Carburetor Heat, p.7-10, difficulty 2]**  
Why does the use of carburetor heat cause a decrease in engine power?

- ✅ **The heated air is less dense than the outside air that was entering the engine**
- The heat leans the fuel-air mixture, reducing power
- The heat restricts airflow through the venturi
- The heat increases friction on the throttle valve

  > Carburetor heat can decrease engine power by up to 15 percent because the heated air is less dense than the outside air. This less dense air also enriches the mixture. For this reason, carburetor heat should not be used when full power is required, such as during takeoff.

**7.8 [Carburetor Icing, p.7-10, difficulty 3]**  
In an aircraft with a constant-speed propeller, what is usually the first indication of carburetor icing?

- ✅ **A decrease in manifold pressure with no reduction in rpm**
- A decrease in engine rpm followed by engine roughness
- An increase in manifold pressure and engine roughness
- Engine roughness followed by a rise in oil temperature

  > With a constant-speed propeller, the propeller pitch is automatically adjusted to compensate for the loss of power, maintaining a constant rpm. The first indication is a decrease in manifold pressure. In contrast, a fixed-pitch propeller aircraft shows a decrease in rpm as the first sign.

**7.9 [Fuel Injection Systems, p.7-12, difficulty 2]**  
Which of the following is a disadvantage of a fuel injection system compared to a carburetor system?

- ✅ **Difficulty in starting a hot engine**
- Higher susceptibility to carburetor icing
- Slower throttle response
- Less precise control of the fuel-air mixture

  > The text lists several disadvantages of fuel injection, including difficulty starting a hot engine, vapor locks during ground operations on hot days, and problems restarting an engine that quits due to fuel starvation. Fuel injection is actually less susceptible to icing, has faster throttle response, and provides more precise mixture control.

**7.10 [Turbosuperchargers, p.7-13, difficulty 3]**  
What is a key advantage of a turbocharger over a gear-driven supercharger?

- ✅ **The turbocharger is powered by exhaust gases that would otherwise be lost**
- The turbocharger can boost manifold pressure above 30 "Hg while a supercharger cannot
- The turbocharger operates at lower rotational speeds than a supercharger
- The turbocharger does not require engine oil for lubrication

  > The major disadvantage of a gear-driven supercharger is that it uses a large amount of the engine's power output. A turbocharger avoids this by using exhaust gases to drive the compressor, recovering energy that would otherwise be lost. Both systems can boost manifold pressure above 30 "Hg.

**7.11 [Ignition System, p.7-15, difficulty 2]**  
Why can a reciprocating engine fire and turn over even with the battery and master switches OFF?

- ✅ **The magneto generates electrical current completely independent of the aircraft's electrical system**
- The battery retains enough residual charge to fire the spark plugs
- The alternator continues to produce electricity whenever the engine rotates
- The starter motor provides backup electrical power to the magnetos

  > A magneto uses a permanent magnet to generate electrical current and requires no outside source of electrical power. This is why the engine can fire if the ignition switch is left ON and the propeller is moved, even with all electrical switches OFF. This is a serious safety hazard during ground handling.

**7.12 [Oil Systems, p.7-16, difficulty 2]**  
What is the primary difference between a wet-sump and a dry-sump oil system?

- ✅ **In a dry-sump system, the oil is contained in a separate tank external to the engine**
- A wet-sump system does not require an oil pump to circulate oil
- A dry-sump system does not require an oil cooler for temperature regulation
- A wet-sump system is only used on turbine engines while a dry-sump is used on reciprocating engines

  > In a wet-sump system, the oil is located in a sump that is an integral part of the engine. In a dry-sump system, the oil is contained in a separate tank and circulated through the engine by pumps. Dry-sump systems allow for a greater volume of oil, making them suitable for very large reciprocating engines.

**7.13 [Combustion, p.7-19, difficulty 3]**  
Which of the following is a common operational cause of detonation in a reciprocating engine?

- ✅ **Using a lower fuel grade than that specified by the aircraft manufacturer**
- Using a higher fuel grade than that specified by the manufacturer
- Operating at low power settings with an excessively rich mixture
- Operating with cowl flaps fully open during cruise flight

  > Detonation is an uncontrolled, explosive ignition of the fuel-air mixture. Using a lower-than-specified fuel grade is a common cause. The text specifically notes that if the proper grade is not available, the next higher grade should be used as a substitute—never a lower grade.

**7.14 [Turboprop, p.7-21, difficulty 2]**  
Why is reduction gearing necessary in turboprop engines?

- ✅ **Optimum propeller performance is achieved at much slower speeds than the engine's operating rpm**
- To reduce fuel consumption during cruise flight
- To decrease engine noise during takeoff and landing
- To allow the propeller to reverse thrust for ground operations

  > Turboprop engines operate at very high rpm, but propellers are most efficient at slower rotational speeds. Reduction gearing bridges this difference, allowing both the engine and propeller to operate at their optimal speeds.

**7.15 [Compressor Stalls, p.7-23, difficulty 3]**  
What causes a compressor stall in a turbine engine?

- ✅ **An imbalance between inlet air velocity and compressor rotational speed causing the compressor blades' AOA to exceed the critical AOA**
- Excessive fuel flow causing the combustion chamber to overheat
- Insufficient oil pressure to the turbine bearings
- Ice buildup on the compressor inlet guide vanes

  > Compressor blades are airfoils subject to aerodynamic principles. A compressor stall occurs when the compressor blades' angle of attack exceeds the critical AOA due to an imbalance between inlet velocity and compressor rotational speed, disrupting smooth airflow.

**7.16 [Fuel Grades, p.7-27, difficulty 1]**  
What color is 100LL AVGAS, and how is it identified on fuel handling equipment?

- ✅ **Blue, identified by white letters on a red background**
- Green, identified by white letters on a red background
- Red, identified by white letters on a black background
- Colorless or straw, identified by white letters on a black background

  > 100LL AVGAS is blue. All AVGAS grades are identified on fuel handling equipment using white letters on a red background. In contrast, turbine fuels (JET A, JET A-1, JET B) are identified by white letters on a black background.

**7.17 [Electrical System, p.7-31, difficulty 2]**  
On an ammeter with a center zero point, what does a full-scale negative (minus) deflection indicate?

- ✅ **A malfunction of the alternator or generator**
- A malfunction of the voltage regulator
- The battery is fully charged and no longer drawing current
- An electrical short in the bus bar

  > A full-scale minus deflection on an ammeter indicates that more current is being drawn from the battery than is being replaced, which signals a malfunction of the alternator or generator. A full-scale positive deflection would indicate a malfunction of the voltage regulator.

**7.18 [Pressurized Aircraft, p.7-36, difficulty 3]**  
What distinguishes an explosive decompression from a rapid decompression?

- ✅ **In an explosive decompression, cabin pressure changes faster than the lungs can decompress**
- An explosive decompression is caused by system malfunction while a rapid decompression is caused by structural damage
- An explosive decompression occurs only above 35,000 feet while a rapid decompression occurs below
- An explosive decompression involves fog formation while a rapid decompression does not

  > The distinction is based on the speed of pressure change relative to lung decompression. Explosive decompression occurs faster than the lungs can decompress (less than 0.5 seconds), potentially causing lung damage. In rapid decompression, the lungs decompress faster than the cabin, which is less dangerous physiologically.


## Chapter 8 — 18 questions

**8.1 [Static Pressure Chamber and Lines, p.8-3, difficulty 2]**  
When an aircraft's alternate static source is activated, how are the pitot-static instrument indications affected?

- ✅ **The altimeter reads higher than actual, the ASI reads higher than actual, and the VSI momentarily shows a climb.**
- The altimeter reads lower than actual, the ASI reads lower than actual, and the VSI momentarily shows a descent.
- All three instruments read lower than actual because the flight deck pressure is higher than outside pressure.
- The altimeter and ASI are unaffected, but the VSI shows a momentary descent.

  > Due to the venturi effect of air flowing around the fuselage, the air pressure inside the flight deck is lower than the exterior pressure. When the alternate static source is used, this lower pressure causes the altimeter to indicate higher than actual, the ASI to indicate faster than actual, and the VSI to momentarily show a climb.

**8.2 [Effect of Nonstandard Pressure and Temperature, p.8-4, difficulty 1]**  
An aircraft is flown from an area of high pressure to an area of low pressure without updating the altimeter setting. How does the actual altitude compare to the indicated altitude?

- ✅ **The actual altitude is lower than indicated.**
- The actual altitude is higher than indicated.
- The actual altitude is the same as indicated because the altimeter automatically adjusts.
- The altimeter would indicate a descent even though the aircraft is maintaining altitude.

  > When flying from high to low pressure without adjusting the altimeter, the altimeter continues to display the same indication, but the aircraft is actually lower than it indicates. The memory aid is: 'GOING FROM A HIGH TO A LOW, LOOK OUT BELOW.'

**8.3 [Effect of Nonstandard Pressure and Temperature, p.8-4, difficulty 2]**  
While maintaining a constant indicated altitude, an aircraft flies from an area of standard temperature into a colder air mass. What happens to the aircraft's true altitude?

- ✅ **True altitude decreases, placing the aircraft lower than indicated.**
- True altitude increases, placing the aircraft higher than indicated.
- True altitude remains the same because temperature does not affect the altimeter.
- The altimeter would indicate a climb even though the aircraft is level.

  > Cold air is denser than warm air, so when operating in temperatures colder than standard, the aircraft is lower than the altimeter indicates. The memory aid is: 'FROM HOT TO COLD, LOOK OUT BELOW.' This is especially dangerous in mountainous terrain.

**8.4 [Types of Altitude, p.8-7, difficulty 2]**  
At an airport with a field elevation of 5,048 feet MSL, the temperature is 30°C instead of the standard 5°C. How does this affect the density altitude compared to the pressure altitude?

- ✅ **The density altitude is higher than the pressure altitude, and the aircraft performs as if at a higher elevation.**
- The density altitude is lower than the pressure altitude, and the aircraft performs better.
- The density altitude equals the pressure altitude because field elevation determines both.
- The density altitude cannot be determined without knowing the current altimeter setting.

  > Density altitude is pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature. When temperature is above standard, density altitude is higher than pressure altitude. In the example given, the density altitude increases to 7,855 feet, meaning the aircraft performs as though the field elevation were 7,855 feet at standard temperature.

**8.5 [Vertical Speed Indicator (VSI), p.8-7, difficulty 2]**  
How does the VSI produce a rate of climb or descent indication using only static pressure?

- ✅ **The diaphragm receives unrestricted static pressure while the instrument case receives static pressure through a calibrated leak, creating a pressure differential during altitude changes.**
- The diaphragm receives pitot pressure while the case receives static pressure, and the difference indicates climb or descent rate.
- The VSI uses both pitot and static pressure through restricted orifices to measure the rate of altitude change.
- The VSI measures the difference between current static pressure and a fixed reference pressure sealed inside the diaphragm at manufacture.

  > Although the VSI operates solely from static pressure, it is a differential pressure instrument. The inside of the diaphragm connects directly to the static line, while the case receives static pressure through a restricted orifice (calibrated leak). When the aircraft climbs or descends, the diaphragm pressure changes immediately while the case pressure lags, creating the differential that drives the indication.

**8.6 [Airspeed Indicator (ASI), p.8-9, difficulty 1]**  
How is true airspeed (TAS) defined, and how does it relate to calibrated airspeed (CAS) as altitude changes?

- ✅ **TAS is CAS corrected for altitude and nonstandard temperature, and for a given CAS, TAS increases as altitude increases.**
- TAS is IAS corrected for installation and instrument error, and it decreases as altitude increases.
- TAS is CAS corrected for wind, and it equals groundspeed in zero wind conditions.
- TAS is IAS corrected for density altitude only, and it equals CAS at all altitudes on a standard day.

  > TAS is CAS corrected for altitude and nonstandard temperature. Because air density decreases with altitude, an aircraft must fly faster at higher altitudes to produce the same pressure difference between pitot and static pressure. Therefore, for a given CAS, TAS increases as altitude increases.

**8.7 [Airspeed Indicator Markings, p.8-9, difficulty 1]**  
What does the red line on a standard airspeed indicator represent?

- ✅ **VNE, the never-exceed speed, above which operation may result in damage or structural failure.**
- VNO, the maximum structural cruising speed, which should not be exceeded except in smooth air.
- VNE, the maximum speed at which full or abrupt control deflection can be applied without structural damage.
- VFE, the maximum speed with flaps extended.

  > The red line on the ASI marks VNE (never-exceed speed). Operating above this speed is prohibited since it may result in damage or structural failure. VNO is the upper limit of the green arc, and VFE is the upper limit of the white arc. The speed for full control deflection is VA, which is not marked on the ASI.

**8.8 [Blockage of the Pitot-Static System, p.8-10, difficulty 2]**  
If the pitot tube opening becomes blocked but the drain hole remains clear, what indication does the airspeed indicator show?

- ✅ **The ASI reads zero because the pressure in the pitot system drops to ambient pressure.**
- The ASI continues to operate normally because the drain hole compensates for the blockage.
- The ASI freezes at the airspeed indicated at the time of the blockage.
- The ASI acts as an altimeter, showing an increase in airspeed when the aircraft climbs.

  > When the pitot tube is blocked but the drain hole remains clear, ram air can no longer enter the system. Air already in the system vents through the drain hole, and the remaining pressure drops to ambient. The ASI senses no difference between ram and static pressure, so it reads zero. The ASI acting as an altimeter occurs only when both the pitot opening and drain hole are blocked simultaneously.

**8.9 [Blockage of the Pitot-Static System, p.8-11, difficulty 3]**  
If the static port becomes blocked but the pitot tube remains clear, what are the instrument indications when the aircraft climbs above the altitude where the blockage occurred?

- ✅ **The ASI indicates lower than actual airspeed, the altimeter freezes at the blockage altitude, and the VSI reads zero.**
- The ASI indicates higher than actual airspeed, the altimeter continues to climb, and the VSI shows a descent.
- All three instruments continue to operate normally because the pitot tube is still clear.
- The ASI reads zero, the altimeter freezes, and the VSI shows a continuous climb.

  > With a blocked static system, the trapped static pressure is higher than normal for the new altitude, causing the ASI to read lower than actual when climbing. The altimeter freezes at the blockage altitude because it cannot sense pressure changes. The VSI continuously reads zero because there is no pressure differential between the diaphragm and the case.

**8.10 [Gyroscopic Principles, p.8-15, difficulty 2]**  
When a deflective force is applied to a spinning gyroscope, at what point does the resulting precession occur?

- ✅ **90° later in the direction of rotation from where the force was applied.**
- At the exact point where the force is applied, opposite to the direction of rotation.
- 180° opposite the point where the force is applied, in the direction of rotation.
- 90° before the point of force application, opposite the direction of rotation.

  > Precession is the tilting or turning of a gyro in response to a deflective force. The reaction occurs not at the point of application but 90° later in the direction of rotation. This principle allows gyros to determine rate of turn by sensing pressure created by a change in direction.

**8.11 [Sources of Power, p.8-16, difficulty 2]**  
What happens to gyroscopic instruments powered by the vacuum system when the vacuum pressure drops below the normal operating range?

- ✅ **The attitude and heading indicators may become unstable and provide inaccurate information.**
- The instruments continue to operate normally on electrical backup power automatically.
- Only the turn coordinator is affected because it relies solely on the vacuum system.
- The instruments immediately tumble and must be reset using the caging knob.

  > The attitude and heading indicators rely on the vacuum system to spin their gyros. When vacuum pressure drops below normal, these gyros slow down and become unstable and inaccurate. The turn coordinator is typically electrically powered, so it is not affected by a vacuum failure. Pilots should cross-check instruments routinely to detect this condition.

**8.12 [Turn Indicators, p.8-16, difficulty 2]**  
What is the primary difference between a turn coordinator and a turn-and-slip indicator?

- ✅ **The turn coordinator's gyro is canted, allowing it to initially show roll rate, while the turn-and-slip indicator shows only the rate of turn.**
- The turn coordinator displays angle of bank, while the turn-and-slip indicator displays only rate of turn.
- The turn coordinator uses an electric gyro, while the turn-and-slip indicator uses a vacuum-driven gyro.
- The turn coordinator displays pitch attitude, while the turn-and-slip indicator does not.

  > The turn-and-slip indicator's gyro rotates in the vertical plane and shows only the rate of turn in degrees per second. The turn coordinator's gyro is mounted at an angle (canted), so it can initially show roll rate and then indicate rate of turn once the roll stabilizes. Neither instrument displays a specific angle of bank.

**8.13 [Heading Indicator, p.8-20, difficulty 2]**  
Aside from precession caused by bearing friction, what is the primary source of drift in a free-gyro heading indicator, and approximately how much error can it introduce per hour?

- ✅ **The Earth's rotation causes up to 15° of error per hour.**
- Magnetic variation causes up to 15° of error per hour.
- Compass deviation causes up to 10° of error per hour.
- The Coriolis effect causes up to 20° of error per hour.

  > The heading indicator is a free gyro oriented in space. The Earth rotates at 15° per hour, so the heading indicator may drift up to 15° per hour even without bearing friction. This is why pilots must periodically realign the heading indicator with the magnetic compass, approximately every 15 minutes.

**8.14 [Compass Systems, p.8-24, difficulty 1]**  
Which statement correctly describes magnetic variation?

- ✅ **Variation is the angular difference between true and magnetic directions, and it does not change with the heading of the aircraft.**
- Variation is the angular difference between magnetic and compass directions, and it changes with aircraft heading.
- Variation is the angular difference between true and magnetic directions, and it changes with the heading of the aircraft.
- Variation is the angular difference between magnetic and compass directions, and it is the same anywhere in the world.

  > Variation is the angular difference between true directions (measured from geographic poles) and magnetic directions (measured from magnetic poles). It is a geographic property fixed along isogonic lines and does not change with aircraft heading. Deviation, not variation, depends on aircraft heading.

**8.15 [Compass Systems, p.8-24, difficulty 2]**  
How does compass deviation differ from variation?

- ✅ **Deviation depends on aircraft heading and is caused by magnetic fields within the aircraft, while variation is fixed for a given geographic location.**
- Deviation is the same for all headings and is caused by the Earth's magnetic field, while variation changes with heading.
- Deviation depends on geographic location and is caused by the Earth's magnetic field, while variation depends on aircraft heading.
- Deviation is caused by solar activity and varies with time of day, while variation is fixed by the aircraft's electrical system.

  > Deviation is caused by magnetic fields within the aircraft (electrical current, magnetized parts, etc.) and depends on the aircraft's heading. Unlike variation, it is not affected by geographic location. An AMT can minimize deviation by 'swinging the compass,' and remaining errors are recorded on a compass correction card.

**8.16 [Compass Systems, p.8-26, difficulty 2]**  
When turning toward a northerly heading, when should the turn be stopped to compensate for magnetic compass turning error?

- ✅ **Stop the turn prior to the desired heading, using the rule of 15 degrees plus half the latitude.**
- Stop the turn after passing the desired heading by 15 degrees plus half the latitude.
- Stop the turn directly on the desired heading because the compass is accurate on northerly headings.
- Stop the turn after passing the desired heading by 15 degrees minus half the latitude.

  > Due to magnetic dip, the compass card leads during a northerly turn, causing a false indication that arrives at the desired heading early. The turn should be stopped prior to the desired heading. The rule of thumb is to stop 15 degrees plus half the latitude before the desired heading. For southerly turns, the compass lags, so the turn should be stopped after passing the desired heading.

**8.17 [Compass Systems, p.8-26, difficulty 2]**  
On which headings does magnetic compass acceleration error occur, and what does the mnemonic 'ANDS' represent?

- ✅ **It occurs on easterly or westerly headings; Acceleration indicates North, Deceleration indicates South.**
- It occurs on northerly or southerly headings; Acceleration indicates North, Deceleration indicates South.
- It occurs on all headings; Acceleration indicates North, Deceleration indicates South.
- It occurs on easterly or westerly headings; Acceleration indicates South, Deceleration indicates North.

  > Acceleration error occurs on easterly and westerly headings due to magnetic dip and the pendulous mounting of the compass. When accelerating, the compass indicates a turn toward north; when decelerating, it indicates a turn toward south. The mnemonic ANDS (Acceleration-North/Deceleration-South) helps recall this error.

**8.18 [Angle of Attack Indicators, p.8-22, difficulty 2]**  
According to the chapter, for a given configuration, the critical angle of attack does not change with which of the following factors?

- ✅ **Weight, bank angle, temperature, density altitude, and center of gravity.**
- Weight, bank angle, and airspeed only.
- Temperature, density altitude, and center of gravity only.
- Weight, bank angle, temperature, density altitude, center of gravity, and configuration.

  > For a given configuration, an airplane always stalls at the same critical AOA regardless of weight, bank angle, temperature, density altitude, or center of gravity. This is why AOA is a better parameter than speed for avoiding a stall—an airplane can stall at any speed, but always at the same AOA. Configuration is held as a condition, not listed as a factor that does not change the critical AOA.


## Chapter 9 — 18 questions

**9.1 [Airplane Flight Manuals (AFM), p.9-2, difficulty 2]**  
What distinguishes an Aircraft Owner/Information Manual from an FAA-approved AFM/POH?

- ✅ **The owner/information manual is not FAA-approved, is not specific to an individual aircraft, and cannot be substituted for the AFM/POH**
- The owner/information manual is FAA-approved but lacks weight and balance data for the specific aircraft
- The owner/information manual is specific to an individual aircraft by serial number but is not kept current by the manufacturer
- The owner/information manual contains the same information as the AFM/POH but is only valid for the first 90 days after aircraft purchase

  > The chapter states that the aircraft owner/information manual is developed by the manufacturer, is not approved by the FAA, is not specific to an individual aircraft, is not kept current, and cannot be substituted for the AFM/POH.

**9.2 [Airplane Flight Manuals (AFM), p.9-2, difficulty 1]**  
If a flight manual does not indicate a specific aircraft registration and serial number on the title page, how may that manual be used?

- ✅ **It is limited to general study purposes only**
- It may be used for flight operations for up to 90 days until a proper manual is obtained
- It may be used for flight operations as long as the pilot carries a separate weight and balance document
- It may be used for VFR flight only, since IFR operations require the original manual

  > The chapter explicitly states that manufacturers are required to include the serial number and registration on the title page to identify the aircraft to which the manual belongs, and if a manual does not indicate a specific aircraft registration and serial number, it is limited to general study purposes only.

**9.3 [Limitations (Section 2), p.9-3, difficulty 2]**  
On a multi-engine airplane's airspeed indicator, what does a blue radial line indicate?

- ✅ **Single-engine best rate of climb speed at maximum weight at sea level (VYSE)**
- Single-engine minimum controllable airspeed (VMC)
- Maximum structural cruising speed (VNO)
- Maximum flap extended speed (VFE)

  > The chapter states that small multi-engine airplanes have a red radial line to indicate VMC and a blue radial line to indicate VYSE, the single-engine best rate of climb speed at maximum weight at sea level.

**9.4 [Limitations (Section 2), p.9-2, difficulty 2]**  
What airspeed is represented by the lower end of the green arc on an airspeed indicator?

- ✅ **Stalling speed at maximum weight with the landing gear and flaps retracted (VS1)**
- Stalling speed with the landing gear and flaps in the landing configuration (VS0)
- Maximum structural cruising speed (VNO)
- The airspeed limit beyond which structural damage could occur (VNE)

  > The chapter states that a green arc depicts the normal operating speed range, with the upper end at VNO and the lower end at stalling speed at maximum weight with the landing gear and flaps retracted (VS1).

**9.5 [Limitations (Section 2), p.9-2, difficulty 2]**  
What does the white arc on an airplane's airspeed indicator depict?

- ✅ **The flap operating range, from VS0 at the lower end to VFE at the upper end**
- The caution speed range for smooth air only, from VNO to VNE
- The normal operating range, from VS1 to VNO
- The single-engine operating range, from VMC to VYSE

  > The chapter states that for airplanes, the flap operating range is depicted by the white arc, with the upper end at the maximum flap extended speed (VFE) and the lower end at the stalling speed in the landing configuration (VS0).

**9.6 [Emergency Procedures (Section 3), p.9-4, difficulty 2]**  
What is the recommended procedure regarding immediate action items during an emergency situation?

- ✅ **Memorize the immediate action items, complete them, and then refer to the appropriate checklist**
- Always locate and read the entire checklist before taking any action
- Memorize and perform only the abbreviated checklist items; the amplified checklist is for post-flight review
- Rely solely on the amplified checklist since it contains more detailed information than the abbreviated version

  > The chapter states that manufacturers may first show an emergency checklist in abbreviated form reflecting the sequence of action, followed by amplified checklists. To be prepared for emergency situations, pilots should memorize the immediate action items and, after completion, refer to the appropriate checklist.

**9.7 [Supplements (Section 9), p.9-5, difficulty 1]**  
What type of information is found in the Supplements section of an AFM/POH?

- ✅ **Information necessary to operate the aircraft when equipped with optional systems and equipment not provided with the standard aircraft**
- Information about mandatory manufacturer recalls and service bulletins
- Information about the standard aircraft systems such as electrical and fuel systems
- Information about weight and balance computations for various loading configurations

  > The chapter states that the Supplements section contains information necessary to safely and efficiently operate the aircraft when equipped with optional systems and equipment not provided with the standard aircraft, such as autopilots, navigation systems, and air-conditioning systems.

**9.8 [Certificate of Aircraft Registration, p.9-6, difficulty 2]**  
Under which condition is the Certificate of Aircraft Registration invalid for operations?

- ✅ **When the ownership of the aircraft is transferred**
- When the aircraft is flown outside of its home state for more than 30 days
- When the aircraft undergoes a major repair or alteration
- When the aircraft's annual inspection expires

  > The chapter lists several conditions under which the Certificate of Aircraft Registration cannot be used, including when the aircraft is registered under the laws of a foreign country, when registration is canceled upon written request, when the aircraft is totally destroyed or scrapped, when ownership is transferred, and when the certificate holder loses U.S. citizenship.

**9.9 [Airworthiness Certificate, p.9-8, difficulty 2]**  
Under what condition does a Standard Airworthiness Certificate remain in effect?

- ✅ **As long as the aircraft receives the required maintenance and is properly registered in the United States**
- For a period of 12 calendar months, at which point it must be renewed by an FAA inspector
- Indefinitely, regardless of maintenance status, as long as the aircraft is not sold to a foreign purchaser
- For the life of the aircraft, provided the owner submits an annual airworthiness report to the FAA

  > The chapter states that a Standard Airworthiness Certificate remains in effect if the aircraft receives the required maintenance and is properly registered in the United States. The Terms and Conditions on the certificate specify it is effective as long as maintenance, preventative maintenance, and alterations are performed in accordance with 14 CFR parts 21, 43, and 91.

**9.10 [Aircraft Inspections, p.9-8, difficulty 2]**  
Which aircraft must receive a 100-hour inspection in addition to an annual inspection?

- ✅ **Aircraft under 12,500 pounds used to carry passengers for hire, and aircraft used for flight instruction for hire when provided by the person giving the instruction**
- All aircraft regardless of weight or use, if operated under 14 CFR part 91
- Only turbojet and turbopropeller-powered multi-engine airplanes
- All aircraft over 12,500 pounds used for any purpose

  > The chapter states that all aircraft under 12,500 pounds (with certain exceptions) used to carry passengers for hire must receive a 100-hour inspection, and additionally, aircraft used for flight instruction for hire, when provided by the person giving the flight instruction, must also have received a 100-hour inspection.

**9.11 [Aircraft Inspections, p.9-9, difficulty 3]**  
By how much may the 100-hour inspection limitation be exceeded, and what is the purpose for which this excess time may be used?

- ✅ **By no more than 10 hours, for the purpose of traveling to a location where the required inspection can be performed**
- By no more than 10 hours, for any purpose, as long as the inspection is completed before further flight
- By no more than 25 hours, for the purpose of returning the aircraft to its home base for inspection
- By no more than 5 hours, for the purpose of completing a revenue flight already in progress

  > The chapter states the 100-hour limitation may be exceeded by no more than 10 hours for the purpose of traveling to a location at which the required inspection can be performed, and any excess time used for this purpose must be included in computing the next 100 hours of time in service.

**9.12 [Aircraft Inspections, p.9-8, difficulty 2]**  
Who may perform an annual inspection on a reciprocating-engine-powered aircraft?

- ✅ **A certificated A&P mechanic holding an inspection authorization (IA), the manufacturer of the aircraft, or a certificated and appropriately rated repair station**
- Any FAA-certificated A&P mechanic, regardless of whether they hold an inspection authorization
- Only an FAA inspector or designated airworthiness representative
- The aircraft owner if they hold at least a private pilot certificate and have completed a maintenance course

  > The chapter specifies that the annual inspection shall be performed by a certificated A&P mechanic who holds an inspection authorization (IA), by the manufacturer of the aircraft, or by a certificated and appropriately rated repair station.

**9.13 [Emergency Locator Transmitter, p.9-9, difficulty 2]**  
Under what conditions must ELT batteries be replaced or recharged?

- ✅ **When the transmitter has been in use for more than 1 cumulative hour, or when 50 percent of the battery useful life (or charge, for rechargeable batteries) has expired**
- Every 12 calendar months regardless of use, or after any hard landing
- When the transmitter has been in use for more than 1 cumulative hour, or every 24 calendar months regardless of battery age
- Every 24 calendar months, or whenever the annual inspection is due, whichever occurs first

  > The chapter lists two conditions for battery replacement or recharge: when the transmitter has been in use for more than 1 cumulative hour, and when 50 percent of the battery useful life has expired (or, for rechargeable batteries, 50 percent of the useful life of the charge has expired). An expiration date must be marked on the outside of the transmitter.

**9.14 [Minimum Equipment Lists (MEL) and Operations With Inoperative Equipment, p.9-9, difficulty 3]**  
When using the deferral provision of 14 CFR part 91, section 91.213(d) for inoperative equipment, what must the pilot do if the inoperative item is not required by type design, 14 CFR, or ADs, and the aircraft can be safely operated without it?

- ✅ **Deactivate or remove the item and place an INOPERATIVE placard near the appropriate switch, control, or indicator**
- Notify the local FSDO within 24 hours and obtain verbal authorization to defer the repair
- Remove the item from the aircraft's equipment list and file an amended weight and balance document
- Log the inoperative item in the maintenance record and obtain a special flight permit before the next flight

  > The chapter states that under 91.213(d), if the inoperative item is not required and the aircraft can be safely operated without it, the deferral may be made. The inoperative item shall be deactivated or removed and an INOPERATIVE placard placed near the appropriate switch, control, or indicator. If deactivation or removal involves maintenance, it must be accomplished by certificated maintenance personnel and recorded per 14 CFR part 43.

**9.15 [Minimum Equipment Lists (MEL) and Operations With Inoperative Equipment, p.9-10, difficulty 3]**  
Once an operator requests an MEL and a Letter of Authorization (LOA) is issued by the FAA, what is the requirement regarding use of the MEL?

- ✅ **Use of the MEL becomes mandatory for that aircraft, and all maintenance deferrals must be accomplished in accordance with the MEL terms and conditions**
- The operator may choose between using the MEL or the 91.213(d) deferral provision on a per-flight basis
- The MEL is optional, but once used for a specific deferral, it must be used for all subsequent deferrals on that aircraft
- The MEL replaces the AFM/POH as the primary document for determining aircraft airworthiness

  > The chapter states that once an operator requests an MEL and a LOA is issued by the FAA, the use of the MEL becomes mandatory for that aircraft. All maintenance deferrals must be accomplished in accordance with the terms and conditions of the MEL and the operator-generated procedures document.

**9.16 [Preventive Maintenance, p.9-12, difficulty 2]**  
Which pilots are excluded from performing preventive maintenance on an aircraft they own or operate?

- ✅ **Student pilots, sport pilots, and recreational pilots**
- Private pilots and commercial pilots who do not hold an instrument rating
- Flight instructors and ATP certificate holders who are not aircraft owners
- All pilots except those who hold a current flight review endorsement within the preceding 12 calendar months

  > The chapter states that certificated pilots, excluding student pilots, sport pilots, and recreational pilots, may perform preventive maintenance on any aircraft owned or operated by them, provided the aircraft is not used in air carrier service and does not qualify under parts 121, 129, or 135. Sport pilots may perform preventive maintenance only on LSA-category aircraft they own or operate.

**9.17 [Airworthiness Directives (ADs), p.9-12, difficulty 2]**  
What are the two categories into which Airworthiness Directives are divided?

- ✅ **Those of an emergency nature requiring immediate compliance prior to further flight, and those of a less urgent nature requiring compliance within a specified period of time**
- Those applicable to the airframe and those applicable to the engine or propeller
- Those that require one-time compliance and those that require recurring compliance
- Those issued by the manufacturer and those issued by the FAA Administrator

  > The chapter states that ADs are divided into two categories: those of an emergency nature requiring immediate compliance prior to further flight, and those of a less urgent nature requiring compliance within a specified period of time.

**9.18 [Special Flight Permits, p.9-12, difficulty 2]**  
For which of the following purposes may a special flight permit be issued?

- ✅ **To allow an aircraft to be flown to a base where repairs, alterations, or maintenance can be performed**
- To allow an aircraft with an expired annual inspection to be operated for compensation or hire on a one-time basis
- To allow an aircraft to be operated indefinitely with inoperative equipment not listed in the MEL
- To allow an unregistered aircraft to be operated within the United States for up to 90 days

  > The chapter states that a special flight permit is issued to allow the aircraft to be flown to a base where repairs, alterations, or maintenance can be performed, for delivering or exporting the aircraft, for evacuating an aircraft from an area of impending danger, and for operation of an overweight aircraft for flight beyond its normal range.


## Chapter 10 — 18 questions

**10.1 [Effects of Weight, p.10-2, difficulty 1]**  
Which of the following is NOT listed as a performance deficiency of an overloaded aircraft?

- ✅ **Increased maneuverability**
- Higher stalling speed
- Longer takeoff run
- Reduced rate and angle of climb

  > The chapter lists 'Reduced maneuverability' as a deficiency of an overloaded aircraft, not increased maneuverability. Higher stalling speed, longer takeoff run, and reduced rate and angle of climb are all explicitly listed as performance deficiencies.

**10.2 [Weight Changes, p.10-2, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot reduces the fuel load to lower the aircraft's weight during flight planning. What consequence must be taken into consideration?

- ✅ **The range of the airplane will decrease**
- The CG will shift forward proportionally
- The aircraft's structural integrity will be compromised
- The stalling speed will increase

  > The chapter states that reducing fuel to lower weight results in a decrease in range that must be considered. The CG does not necessarily shift forward proportionally, structural integrity is not compromised by reducing weight, and stalling speed would decrease, not increase, with lower weight.

**10.3 [Balance, Stability, and Center of Gravity, p.10-2, difficulty 1]**  
The center of gravity (CG) of an aircraft is best described as:

- ✅ **The point at which the aircraft would balance if it were suspended at that point**
- The fixed point at which the aircraft's lift is concentrated
- The geometric center of the wing's mean aerodynamic chord
- The location of the reference datum established by the manufacturer

  > The chapter defines CG as the point at which the aircraft would balance if suspended at that point. It is not fixed (it shifts with weight distribution), is not the MAC center, and is not the datum.

**10.4 [Effects of Adverse Balance — Stability, p.10-3, difficulty 2]**  
Why is the forward CG limit often established based on an aircraft's landing characteristics?

- ✅ **Exceeding the forward CG limit during landing may result in excessive loads on the nosewheel and higher stalling speeds**
- A forward CG improves landing performance by increasing approach speed
- The forward CG limit determines the maximum landing weight of the aircraft
- A forward CG during landing reduces elevator effectiveness during cruise flight

  > The chapter states the forward CG limit is determined by landing characteristics, and exceeding it may cause excessive nosewheel loads, nose-over tendency in tailwheel aircraft, decreased performance, higher stalling speeds, and higher control forces. The distractors describe incorrect relationships between forward CG and landing.

**10.5 [Effects of Adverse Balance — Control, p.10-3, difficulty 2]**  
Which undesirable characteristic may result from a CG located aft of the allowable range?

- ✅ **Violent stall characteristics and very light control forces**
- Excessive nose-down tendency during takeoff
- Increased structural loads on the nosewheel during landing
- Difficulty raising the nose for landing flare

  > The chapter lists extreme control difficulty, violent stall characteristics, and very light control forces as effects of an aft CG. The distractors describe forward CG effects: nose-heavy takeoff difficulty, nosewheel loads, and inability to flare are all associated with a forward CG, not aft.

**10.6 [Balance, Stability, and Center of Gravity, p.10-3, difficulty 2]**  
In an airplane, lateral unbalance can occur if:

- ✅ **Fuel is mismanaged by supplying the engine(s) unevenly from tanks on one side of the airplane**
- Baggage is loaded too far aft in the baggage compartment
- Heavy passengers are placed in the rear seats
- Fuel is burned from wing tanks located very near the CG

  > The chapter specifically identifies uneven fuel management from tanks on one side as the cause of lateral unbalance. Loading baggage aft or heavy passengers in rear seats affects longitudinal balance, not lateral. Fuel tanks near the CG have little effect on CG when burned.

**10.7 [Management of Weight and Balance Control, p.10-4, difficulty 2]**  
Under 14 CFR part 91, what is required of the pilot in command regarding weight and balance?

- ✅ **The PIC must comply with the operating limits, including weight and balance, in the approved AFM**
- The PIC must conduct weight and balance calculations prior to each flight as specifically required by Part 91
- The PIC must have the aircraft weighed every 36 calendar months
- The PIC must obtain a weight and balance sign-off from a mechanic before each flight

  > The chapter states that while Part 91 does not specifically require weight and balance calculations before each flight, section 91.9 requires the PIC to comply with operating limits in the approved AFM, which include weight and balance. The 36-month weighing requirement applies to Part 125 and Part 135 operations, not Part 91.

**10.8 [Terms and Definitions, p.10-4, difficulty 1]**  
In weight and balance terminology, the 'arm' is defined as:

- ✅ **The horizontal distance in inches from the reference datum line to the CG of an item**
- The weight of an item multiplied by its distance from the datum
- The maximum weight the floor can sustain per square inch or foot
- The distance between the forward and aft CG limits

  > The chapter defines arm as the horizontal distance from the datum to the CG of an item. The first distractor defines moment, the second defines floor load limit, and the third defines CG range.

**10.9 [Principles of Weight and Balance Computations, p.10-6, difficulty 1]**  
How is the moment of an item calculated?

- ✅ **By multiplying the weight of the item by its arm**
- By dividing the weight by the arm
- By adding the arm to the total weight
- By subtracting the datum distance from the station number

  > The chapter states that moment is the product of weight multiplied by arm. Dividing weight by arm, adding arm to weight, or subtracting distances do not produce a moment.

**10.10 [Terms and Definitions, p.10-5, difficulty 1]**  
What is the standard weight of aviation gasoline per U.S. gallon?

- ✅ **6 lb/US gal**
- 6.8 lb/US gal
- 7.5 lb/US gal
- 8.35 lb/US gal

  > The chapter lists standard weights: gasoline at 6 lb/gal, Jet A at 6.8 lb/gal, oil at 7.5 lb/gal, and water at 8.35 lb/gal. The distractors correspond to other substances.

**10.11 [Terms and Definitions, p.10-5, difficulty 2]**  
Useful load is defined as the weight of:

- ✅ **The pilot, copilot, passengers, baggage, usable fuel, and drainable oil**
- The airframe, engines, and all permanently installed operating equipment
- The maximum weight exclusive of usable fuel
- Only the expendable part of the aircraft's load, including usable fuel

  > The chapter defines useful load as the weight of the pilot, copilot, passengers, baggage, usable fuel, and drainable oil. The first distractor describes standard empty weight, the second describes maximum zero fuel weight, and the third incorrectly limits useful load to only expendable items (fuel and oil), excluding the pilot, passengers, and baggage.

**10.12 [Terms and Definitions, p.10-4, difficulty 2]**  
Under GAMA standardization, basic empty weight consists of:

- ✅ **The standard empty weight plus the weight of optional and special equipment that have been installed**
- The airframe weight plus engine weight and usable fuel only
- The maximum takeoff weight minus the useful load
- The aircraft weight with full fuel but no passengers or baggage

  > The chapter defines basic empty weight (GAMA) as standard empty weight plus optional and special equipment. Standard empty weight includes the airframe, engines, fixed ballast, hydraulic fluid, unusable fuel, and full engine oil. The distractors incorrectly include usable fuel or describe unrelated calculations.

**10.13 [Determining Loaded Weight and CG — Computational Method, p.10-7, difficulty 2]**  
When using the computational method, how is the CG location of a loaded aircraft determined?

- ✅ **By dividing the total moment by the total weight**
- By multiplying the total weight by the total moment
- By subtracting the empty weight CG from the loaded weight
- By dividing the total weight by the total moment

  > The chapter's computational method states: list weights and moments, find totals, then divide total moment by total weight to find CG. Multiplying weight by moment or dividing weight by moment do not yield CG location.

**10.14 [Weight and Balance Restrictions, p.10-7, difficulty 2]**  
Which set of environmental conditions may require a reduction in aircraft weight before flight is attempted, even if the aircraft is certified for maximum gross takeoff weight?

- ✅ **High elevation, high temperature, and high humidity**
- Low elevation, low temperature, and low humidity
- Sea level pressure, standard temperature, and clear skies
- Cold temperatures, dry air, and calm winds

  > The chapter identifies high elevations, high temperatures, and high humidity (high density altitudes) as conditions that may require weight reduction. The distractors all describe favorable density altitude conditions that would not typically require weight reduction.

**10.15 [Shifting, Adding, and Removing Weight — Weight Shifting, p.10-10, difficulty 2]**  
When weight is shifted from one location to another within an aircraft, what happens to the total weight and total moments?

- ✅ **The total weight remains unchanged, but the total moments change in proportion to the distance the weight is moved**
- Both the total weight and total moments remain unchanged
- The total weight changes proportionally to the distance the weight is moved
- The total moments remain unchanged, but the total weight changes

  > The chapter states that when weight is shifted, total weight is unchanged, but total moments change in proportion to the direction and distance the weight is moved. The distractors incorrectly claim moments stay the same, weight changes, or both remain unchanged.

**10.16 [Shifting, Adding, and Removing Weight — Weight Addition or Removal, p.10-11, difficulty 3]**  
When weight is added to an aircraft at a known station, how is the change in CG (∆CG) calculated?

- ✅ **Divide the added weight by the new total weight, then multiply by the distance between the added weight and the old CG**
- Divide the added weight by the distance between the weight and the old CG
- Multiply the added weight by the new total weight, then divide by the distance
- Subtract the old CG from the added weight's station and divide by the total weight

  > The chapter presents the formula: Added weight / New total weight = ∆CG / Distance between weight and old CG. Rearranging: ∆CG = (Added weight / New total weight) × Distance. The distractors do not match this proportional relationship.

**10.17 [Effects of Adverse Balance — Control, p.10-3, difficulty 3]**  
A restricted forward CG limit is specified in order to assure that:

- ✅ **Sufficient elevator/control deflection is available at minimum airspeed**
- The aircraft can rotate earlier during the takeoff roll
- The stall speed is minimized for approach
- The structural load on the tailwheel is reduced

  > The chapter states the forward CG limit is restricted to assure sufficient elevator/control deflection at minimum airspeed. A forward CG makes rotation harder, not earlier; it increases stall speed rather than minimizing it; and it affects nosewheel loads, not tailwheel loads.

**10.18 [Terms and Definitions, p.10-5, difficulty 2]**  
Maximum zero fuel weight (GAMA) is defined as:

- ✅ **The maximum weight exclusive of usable fuel**
- The maximum allowable weight for takeoff
- The greatest weight an aircraft is normally allowed to have at landing
- The weight of the aircraft with no fuel on board, including unusable fuel

  > The chapter defines maximum zero fuel weight as the maximum weight exclusive of usable fuel. The distractors define maximum takeoff weight, maximum landing weight, or describe a state rather than a maximum limit.


## Chapter 11 — 18 questions

**11.1 [Importance of Performance Data, p.11-2, difficulty 1]**  
Why must a pilot understand the basis on which performance data in the AFM/POH is presented before using it for flight planning?

- ✅ **Manufacturers' data is not standardized; it may be presented in tables or graphs and based on pressure altitude, density altitude, or standard atmospheric conditions**
- The FAA requires all AFM/POH data to be presented in identical formats so pilots can use any aircraft's charts interchangeably
- Performance data is always based on standard day conditions at sea level, so pilots only need to adjust for field elevation
- Manufacturers provide data only for maximum gross weight, requiring the pilot to calculate all reductions manually

  > The chapter states that manufacturers' information and data furnished in the AFM/POH is not standardized. Some provide data in tabular form, others use graphs, and the data may be presented on the basis of standard atmospheric conditions, pressure altitude, or density altitude. The pilot must recognize these variations and make necessary adjustments.

**11.2 [Structure of the Atmosphere, p.11-2, difficulty 1]**  
What are the standard sea level temperature and pressure values defined by the International Standard Atmosphere (ISA)?

- ✅ **15 °C and 29.92 "Hg**
- 0 °C and 29.92 "Hg
- 15 °C and 30.00 "Hg
- 59 °C and 1013.2 millibars

  > The chapter defines the standard atmosphere at sea level as having a surface temperature of 59 °F or 15 °C and a surface pressure of 29.92 "Hg or 1013.2 millibars. 59 °C would be far too hot and is not a standard value; 59 °F is the correct Fahrenheit equivalent.

**11.3 [Structure of the Atmosphere, p.11-2, difficulty 2]**  
As air becomes less dense, which three aircraft performance factors are directly reduced?

- ✅ **Power, thrust, and lift**
- Range, endurance, and fuel economy
- Indicated airspeed, true airspeed, and calibrated airspeed
- Propeller RPM, manifold pressure, and oil pressure

  > The chapter explicitly lists the three factors reduced by less dense air: power (engine takes in less air), thrust (propeller is less efficient in thin air), and lift (thin air exerts less force on the airfoils). Range and endurance are secondary effects, not the direct reductions listed.

**11.4 [Pressure Altitude, p.11-3, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot needs to determine pressure altitude before computing density altitude. Which method can be used to find pressure altitude?

- ✅ **Set the barometric scale of the altimeter to 29.92 "Hg and read the indicated altitude**
- Set the altimeter to the current local altimeter setting and read the indicated altitude
- Set the altimeter to field elevation and subtract the difference from 29.92
- Set the altimeter to 30.00 "Hg and subtract 80 feet from the indicated altitude

  > The chapter lists three methods for determining pressure altitude, the first being setting the barometric scale of the altimeter to 29.92 "Hg and reading the indicated altitude. Setting the altimeter to the current local altimeter setting gives indicated altitude, not pressure altitude.

**11.5 [Density Altitude, p.11-3, difficulty 1]**  
How is density altitude defined?

- ✅ **Pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature**
- The altitude indicated when the altimeter is set to the local altimeter setting
- The height above the standard datum plane where pressure is 29.92 "Hg
- The altitude read directly from the altimeter when set to the current field elevation

  > The chapter defines density altitude as pressure altitude corrected for nonstandard temperature. The second distractor describes pressure altitude, not density altitude. Density altitude is the altitude in the standard atmosphere corresponding to a particular value of air density.

**11.6 [Effects of Humidity (Moisture) on Density, p.11-5, difficulty 2]**  
Why does high humidity reduce aircraft performance?

- ✅ **Water vapor is lighter than dry air, so moist air is less dense, increasing density altitude**
- Water vapor increases atmospheric pressure, which raises the density altitude
- Water vapor adds mass to the air column, increasing parasite drag on the aircraft
- Humidity causes the engine to run excessively rich, reducing available power output

  > The chapter explains that water vapor is lighter than air; consequently, moist air is lighter than dry air. As the water content of the air increases, the air becomes less dense, increasing density altitude and decreasing performance. The mechanism is not about increased pressure, added mass, or mixture issues.

**11.7 [Straight-and-Level Flight, p.11-5, difficulty 2]**  
If an aircraft in steady, level flight at 100 knots is accelerated to 200 knots, what happens to the parasite drag and the power required to overcome it?

- ✅ **Parasite drag becomes four times as great, and the power required is eight times the original value**
- Parasite drag doubles, and the power required doubles
- Parasite drag becomes four times as great, and the power required is four times the original value
