- Parasite drag becomes eight times as great, and the power required is four times the original value

  > The chapter states that when speed doubles from 100 to 200 knots, parasite drag becomes four times as great (drag proportional to velocity squared), but the power required to overcome that drag is eight times the original value (power proportional to velocity cubed). The distinction between drag and power is critical.

**11.8 [Angle of Climb (AOC), p.11-7, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot is taking off from a short airfield surrounded by high obstacles such as trees and power lines. Which climb speed should be used to clear the obstacles while traveling the least horizontal distance?

- ✅ **VX, because it provides maximum angle of climb for the greatest altitude gain over a given horizontal distance**
- VY, because it provides the greatest altitude gain over a given period of time
- VNE, because maximum speed generates the most lift for clearing obstacles
- VA, because maneuvering speed provides the best structural margin during the climb

  > The chapter states that maximum Angle of Climb (AOC), obtained at VX, may provide climb performance to ensure an aircraft will clear obstacles. A good use of maximum AOC is when taking off from a short airfield surrounded by high obstacles, with the objective of gaining sufficient altitude while traveling the least horizontal distance.

**11.9 [Rate of Climb (ROC), p.11-8, difficulty 2]**  
Maximum rate of climb (ROC) depends primarily on which factor?

- ✅ **Excess power**
- Excess thrust
- Maximum lift coefficient
- Minimum parasite drag

  > The chapter distinguishes between AOC (which depends on excess thrust) and ROC (which depends on excess power). It states that ROC performance depends upon excess power, since climbing is work and power is the rate of performing work. A pilot can increase climb rate by using any power not used to maintain level flight.

**11.10 [Climb Performance Factors, p.11-8, difficulty 2]**  
What defines the service ceiling of an aircraft?

- ✅ **The altitude at which the aircraft is unable to climb at a rate greater than 100 feet per minute**
- The altitude at which there is no excess power and only one speed allows steady, level flight
- The maximum altitude at which the aircraft can maintain straight and level flight at maximum power
- The altitude at which the aircraft's engine produces its maximum rated horsepower

  > The chapter defines the service ceiling as the altitude at which the aircraft is unable to climb at a rate greater than 100 fpm. The first distractor describes the absolute ceiling, where there is no excess power and zero ROC. The service ceiling is a practical operational limit, not the theoretical maximum.

**11.11 [Range Performance, p.11-9, difficulty 2]**  
What is the fundamental difference between range and endurance?

- ✅ **Range involves consideration of flying distance, while endurance involves consideration of flying time**
- Range is measured in hours and endurance is measured in nautical miles
- Range and endurance are both maximized at the same airspeed and power setting
- Range applies only to cruise flight, while endurance applies only to climbs and descents

  > The chapter explicitly distinguishes the two: range must be clearly distinguished from endurance. Range involves consideration of flying distance, while endurance involves consideration of flying time. Specific range is nautical miles per pound of fuel, while specific endurance is flight hours per pound of fuel.

**11.12 [Range Performance, p.11-10, difficulty 3]**  
At what flight condition is maximum range obtained for a given aircraft configuration, and how is this condition affected by changes in weight or altitude?

- ✅ **At maximum lift/drag ratio (L/DMAX), which occurs at a particular AOA and lift coefficient unaffected by weight or altitude**
- At minimum power required, which shifts to a lower airspeed as weight increases
- At maximum continuous power, which varies with altitude but not weight
- At the speed for maximum endurance, which is unaffected by weight or altitude changes

  > The chapter states that the maximum range condition is obtained at L/DMAX, and it is important to note that for a given aircraft configuration, L/DMAX occurs at a particular AOA and lift coefficient and is unaffected by weight or altitude. A variation in weight alters the airspeed and power required to obtain L/DMAX, but not the L/DMAX itself.

**11.13 [Region of Reversed Command, p.11-12, difficulty 3]**  
A pilot is performing a short-field landing approach at a low airspeed and high pitch attitude. If an unacceptably high sink rate develops, what should the pilot understand about operating in the region of reversed command?

- ✅ **It may be possible to reduce or stop the descent by applying power, but lowering the nose without power would result in a rapid sink rate and loss of altitude**
- Lowering the nose will immediately restore normal command and stop the sink rate without power
- Reducing power further will decrease the sink rate because less power means less drag in this regime
- The aircraft will stall regardless of power application, so the pilot must lower the nose immediately

  > The chapter describes the region of reversed command using the short-field landing example. It states it may be possible to reduce or stop the descent by applying power, but without power, lowering the nose to regain flying speed would result in a rapid sink rate and corresponding loss of altitude. In this regime, lower airspeed requires more power, not less.

**11.14 [Runway Surface and Gradient, p.11-13, difficulty 2]**  
How does an upsloping runway affect takeoff and landing performance respectively?

- ✅ **It impedes acceleration resulting in a longer takeoff ground run, but typically reduces the landing roll**
- It aids acceleration on takeoff and also reduces the landing roll
- It impedes acceleration on takeoff and also increases the landing distance
- It aids acceleration on takeoff but increases the landing distance

  > The chapter states that an upsloping runway impedes acceleration and results in a longer ground run during takeoff. However, landing on an upsloping runway typically reduces the landing roll. A downsloping runway has the opposite effects: aiding takeoff acceleration but increasing landing distances.

**11.15 [Water on the Runway and Dynamic Hydroplaning, p.11-14, difficulty 2]**  
Using the hydroplaning speed formula, at what minimum speed would an aircraft with main gear tire pressure of 64 psi begin to hydroplane on a wet runway?

- ✅ **72 knots**
- 54 knots
- 64 knots
- 90 knots

  > The chapter provides the formula: minimum hydroplaning speed = 9 × √(tire pressure in psi). For 64 psi: √64 = 8, and 9 × 8 = 72 knots. The 54-knot distractor is the example from the book using 36 psi. Simply using the tire pressure number (64) or multiplying by 10 instead of 9 gives incorrect results.

**11.16 [Takeoff Performance, p.11-15, difficulty 2]**  
A headwind that is 10 percent of the takeoff airspeed reduces the takeoff distance by approximately what percentage?

- ✅ **19 percent**
- 10 percent
- 21 percent
- 50 percent

  > The chapter states that a headwind that is 10 percent of the takeoff airspeed reduces the takeoff distance approximately 19 percent. The 21 percent distractor is the increase caused by a tailwind of 10 percent. A 50 percent headwind would reduce distance by 75 percent, not 10 percent.

**11.17 [Landing Performance, p.11-17, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot touches down approximately 10 percent faster than the recommended landing speed. Approximately how much does this increase the landing distance?

- ✅ **At least 21 percent**
- Approximately 10 percent
- Approximately 5 percent
- Approximately 50 percent

  > The chapter states that a ten percent excess landing speed causes at least a 21 percent increase in landing distance. The excess speed places a greater working load on the brakes because of the additional kinetic energy to be dissipated, and the increased lift reduces the normal force on the braking surfaces. The relationship follows the square of the velocity.

**11.18 [Performance Speeds, p.11-18, difficulty 2]**  
What is the significance of VA (design maneuvering airspeed)?

- ✅ **It is the maximum speed at which full deflection of the control surfaces can be imposed without causing structural damage**
- It is the maximum speed at which the landing gear can be safely extended or retracted
- It is the maximum structural cruising speed for normal operations
- It is the speed that should never be exceeded under any circumstances

  > The chapter defines VA as the calibrated design maneuvering airspeed—the maximum speed at which the limit load can be imposed (either by gusts or full deflection of the control surfaces) without causing structural damage. The chapter also notes that operating at or below VA does not provide structural protection against multiple full control inputs in one axis or full control inputs in more than one axis simultaneously.


## Chapter 12 — 18 questions

**12.1 [Composition of the Atmosphere, p.12-2, difficulty 1]**  
What are the two most abundant gases in the Earth's atmosphere, and approximately what percentage of the atmosphere do they comprise together?

- ✅ **Nitrogen (78%) and oxygen (21%), comprising about 99% of the atmosphere.**
- Oxygen (78%) and nitrogen (21%), comprising about 99% of the atmosphere.
- Nitrogen (78%) and argon (21%), comprising about 99% of the atmosphere.
- Oxygen (78%) and carbon dioxide (21%), comprising about 99% of the atmosphere.

  > The chapter states that in any given volume of air, nitrogen accounts for 78 percent of the gases, while oxygen makes up 21 percent. Argon, carbon dioxide, and other trace gases make up the remaining one percent.

**12.2 [Atmosphere, p.12-3, difficulty 2]**  
Why is the tropopause important to pilots and meteorologists?

- ✅ **It traps moisture and associated weather in the troposphere and is commonly associated with the jet stream and clear air turbulence.**
- It is the boundary where temperature begins to increase with altitude, creating stable air.
- It is the highest layer of the atmosphere where aircraft can operate without pressurization.
- It separates the mesosphere from the thermosphere and prevents weather from rising above it.

  > The text notes that the tropopause is a boundary at the top of the troposphere that traps moisture and associated weather below it. Its location is important because it is commonly associated with the location of the jet stream and clear air turbulence.

**12.3 [Coriolis Force, p.12-3, difficulty 2]**  
How does the Coriolis force affect the movement of air in the Northern Hemisphere?

- ✅ **It deflects moving air to the right, causing it to follow a curved path instead of a straight line.**
- It deflects moving air to the left, creating a counterclockwise circulation around high-pressure systems.
- It causes air to flow directly from areas of high pressure to areas of low pressure in a straight line.
- It only affects air movement at the equator, causing air to rise directly upward.

  > The chapter explains that the Coriolis force deflects air to the right in the Northern Hemisphere, causing it to follow a curved path instead of a straight line. The amount of deflection is greatest at the poles and diminishes to zero at the equator.

**12.4 [Measurement of Atmosphere Pressure, p.12-5, difficulty 1]**  
Under standard atmospheric conditions at sea level, what is the standard sea level pressure and temperature?

- ✅ **29.92 "Hg and 15 °C**
- 29.92 "Hg and 0 °C
- 1013.2 "Hg and 59 °C
- 14.7 "Hg and 15 °C

  > The International Standard Atmosphere (ISA) has established standard sea level pressure as 29.92 "Hg and a standard temperature of 59 °F (15 °C). These conditions are the basis for certain flight instruments and aircraft performance data.

**12.5 [Measurement of Atmosphere Pressure, p.12-5, difficulty 2]**  
If a weather station located at 5,000 feet above sea level measures a station pressure of 24.92 "Hg, what pressure will it report to provide a standard sea level pressure reading?

- ✅ **29.92 "Hg**
- 24.92 "Hg
- 25.92 "Hg
- 19.92 "Hg

  > To provide a common reference, stations convert barometric pressure to sea level pressure by adding approximately 1 "Hg for every 1,000 feet of elevation. A station at 5,000 feet with 24.92 "Hg adds 5 "Hg, reporting 29.92 "Hg.

**12.6 [Wind Patterns, p.12-7, difficulty 1]**  
What type of weather is typically associated with low-pressure systems, and why?

- ✅ **Bad weather, because air flows into the low to replace rising air, bringing increasing cloudiness and precipitation.**
- Good weather, because the air is dry and descending, preventing cloud formation.
- Bad weather, because cold, dense air sinks and traps moisture near the surface.
- Good weather, because the clockwise circulation disperses clouds and precipitation.

  > High-pressure systems are areas of dry, descending air associated with good weather. Conversely, air flows into a low-pressure area to replace rising air, usually bringing increasing cloudiness and precipitation, hence bad weather is commonly associated with low pressure.

**12.7 [Convective Currents, p.12-7, difficulty 2]**  
During the day, how does the wind circulate near a large body of water adjacent to land, and what is this called?

- ✅ **Land heats faster than water, causing warm air over land to rise and be replaced by cooler air from over the water, creating a sea breeze.**
- Water heats faster than land, causing warm air over water to rise and be replaced by cooler air from land, creating a sea breeze.
- Land cools faster than water, causing warm air over water to rise and be replaced by cooler air from land, creating a land breeze.
- Water cools faster than land, causing warm air over land to rise and be replaced by cooler air from water, creating a land breeze.

  > During the day, land heats faster than water, making the air over land warmer and less dense. It rises and is replaced by cooler, denser air flowing in from over the water, causing an onshore wind called a sea breeze. At night, the reverse occurs, creating a land breeze.

**12.8 [Low-Level Wind Shear, p.12-11, difficulty 3]**  
If an aircraft inadvertently takes off into a microburst, what sequence of wind changes will the aircraft experience?

- ✅ **An increasing headwind, followed by severe downdrafts, followed by an increasing tailwind.**
- An increasing tailwind, followed by an updraft, followed by a decreasing headwind.
- Severe downdrafts, followed by an increasing headwind, followed by an increasing tailwind.
- An increasing tailwind, followed by severe downdrafts, followed by an increasing headwind.

  > The text describes a microburst encounter during takeoff as starting with a performance-increasing headwind, followed by performance-decreasing downdrafts, and finally a rapidly increasing tailwind, which can result in terrain impact.

**12.9 [Wind and Pressure Representation on Surface Weather Maps, p.12-12, difficulty 1]**  
On a surface weather chart, what do closely spaced isobars indicate about the weather?

- ✅ **A steep pressure gradient and strong winds.**
- A shallow pressure gradient and light winds.
- A steep pressure gradient and calm winds.
- A shallow pressure gradient and strong winds.

  > Isobars are lines of equal pressure. Closely spaced isobars indicate a steep pressure gradient, which results in strong winds. Shallow gradients are represented by isobars spaced far apart and indicate light winds.

**12.10 [Atmospheric Stability, p.12-13, difficulty 3]**  
What is the standard lapse rate for unsaturated (dry) air, and how does it compare to the moist adiabatic lapse rate?

- ✅ **The dry adiabatic lapse rate is 3 °C (5.4 °F) per 1,000 feet, which is faster than the moist adiabatic lapse rate.**
- The dry adiabatic lapse rate is 2 °C (3.5 °F) per 1,000 feet, which is slower than the moist adiabatic lapse rate.
- The dry adiabatic lapse rate is 1.1 °C to 2.8 °C per 1,000 feet, which is faster than the moist adiabatic lapse rate.
- The dry adiabatic lapse rate is 3 °C (5.4 °F) per 1,000 feet, which is slower than the moist adiabatic lapse rate.

  > The dry adiabatic lapse rate (unsaturated air) is 3 °C (5.4 °F) per 1,000 feet. The moist adiabatic lapse rate varies from 1.1 °C to 2.8 °C (2 °F to 5 °F) per 1,000 feet, meaning dry air cools faster than moist air as it rises.

**12.11 [Temperature/Dew Point Relationship, p.12-14, difficulty 3]**  
Given a surface temperature of 75 °F and a dew point of 65 °F, approximately what is the height of the cloud base AGL?

- ✅ **2,272 feet AGL**
- 3,180 feet AGL
- 4,400 feet AGL
- 10,000 feet AGL

  > The formula to find the cloud base is (Temperature - Dew Point) / 4.4 * 1,000. (75 - 65) = 10. Then, 10 / 4.4 = 2.27. Finally, 2.27 * 1,000 = 2,272 feet AGL.

**12.12 [Fog, p.12-15, difficulty 2]**  
Which type of fog requires wind for formation, can persist for days, and commonly occurs in coastal areas where sea breezes blow air over cooler landmasses?

- ✅ **Advection fog**
- Radiation fog
- Upslope fog
- Steam fog

  > Advection fog occurs when warm, moist air moves over a cold surface. Unlike radiation fog, wind is required (up to 15 knots) to form it. It is common in coastal areas and can persist for days, unlike radiation fog which burns off with the morning sun.

**12.13 [Clouds, p.12-16, difficulty 2]**  
What type of cloud is formed by heating of the air near the Earth's surface, appears individually or in groups, and is known as an air mass thunderstorm?

- ✅ **Cumulonimbus**
- Altocumulus
- Cirrocumulus
- Nimbostratus

  > The cumulonimbus cloud is perhaps the most dangerous cloud type to pilots. It appears individually or in groups and is known as either an air mass or orographic thunderstorm. Heating of the air near the Earth's surface creates an air mass thunderstorm.

**12.14 [Precipitation, p.12-17, difficulty 2]**  
If rain falls through a temperature inversion and passes through underlying cold air, what form of precipitation will reach the ground, and what does it indicate?

- ✅ **Ice pellets, indicating that freezing rain exists at a higher altitude.**
- Snow, indicating that clear icing exists at a higher altitude.
- Hail, indicating that severe updrafts are present in the clouds above.
- Virga, indicating that the air below the inversion is very dry.

  > If rain falls through a temperature inversion, it may freeze as it passes through the underlying cold air and fall to the ground in the form of ice pellets. Ice pellets are an indication of a temperature inversion and that freezing rain exists at a higher altitude.

**12.15 [Air Masses, p.12-18, difficulty 2]**  
How does an air mass change when it passes over a colder surface, and what effect does this have on visibility?

- ✅ **It creates a stable air mass with poor surface visibility because smoke, dust, and other particles are trapped near the surface.**
- It creates an unstable air mass with good surface visibility due to the formation of convective currents.
- It creates a stable air mass with good surface visibility because the cold air forces particles to the ground.
- It creates an unstable air mass with poor surface visibility due to continuous cloud cover and precipitation.

  > When an air mass passes over a colder surface, it does not form convective currents but instead creates a stable air mass with poor surface visibility. The poor visibility is because smoke, dust, and other particles cannot rise out of the air mass and are trapped near the surface.

**12.16 [Warm Front, p.12-18, difficulty 2]**  
What is a typical characteristic of a warm front's movement and slope compared to a cold front?

- ✅ **Warm fronts move slowly (10 to 25 mph) and slide over the top of the cooler air with a gradual slope.**
- Warm fronts move rapidly (25 to 30 mph) and slide under the cooler air with a steep slope.
- Warm fronts move slowly (10 to 25 mph) and slide under the cooler air with a steep slope.
- Warm fronts move rapidly (25 to 30 mph) and slide over the top of the cooler air with a gradual slope.

  > A warm front occurs when warm air advances and replaces colder air. Warm fronts move slowly, typically 10 to 25 mph. The slope of the advancing front slides over the top of the cooler air and gradually pushes it out of the area.

**12.17 [Occluded Front, p.12-21, difficulty 3]**  
What is an occluded front, and what weather sequence does it typically bring?

- ✅ **It occurs when a fast-moving cold front catches a slow-moving warm front, bringing warm front weather immediately followed by cold front weather.**
- It occurs when two air masses are equal in force, bringing a mixture of warm and cold front weather that influences the area for days.
- It occurs when a warm front overtakes a cold front, bringing clear skies and gusty winds.
- It occurs when a cold front dissipates before reaching a warm front, resulting in prolonged fog and low ceilings.

  > An occluded front occurs when a fast-moving cold front catches up with a slow-moving warm front. As the occluded front approaches, warm front weather prevails but is immediately followed by cold front weather.

**12.18 [Thunderstorms, p.12-22, difficulty 2]**  
During which stage of a thunderstorm's life cycle do drops of moisture become too heavy for the cloud to support and begin falling, creating a downward motion of air?

- ✅ **The mature stage**
- The cumulus stage
- The dissipating stage
- The squall line stage

  > Within approximately 15 minutes, the thunderstorm reaches the mature stage, which is the most violent time period. Drops of moisture become too heavy for the cloud to support and begin falling, creating a downward motion of the air.


## Chapter 13 — 18 questions

**13.1 [Aviation Routine Weather Report (METAR), p.13-6, difficulty 1]**  
In a METAR report, the first three digits of the wind group indicate the wind direction in reference to what?

- ✅ **True north**
- Magnetic north
- Grid north
- The local runway heading

  > The METAR wind group reports direction in reference to true north. The first three digits indicate the direction the true wind is blowing from in tens of degrees, and a 'Z' is not appended to the wind group—the Zulu time designation appears in the date/time group.

**13.2 [Upper Air Observations, p.13-2, difficulty 2]**  
What is the approximate rate of ascent of a radiosonde balloon used for upper air observations?

- ✅ **1,000 feet per minute**
- 500 feet per minute
- 2,000 feet per minute
- 1,500 feet per minute

  > The radiosonde is suspended below a hydrogen- or helium-filled balloon that rises at approximately 1,000 fpm. It can ascend to altitudes as high as 115,000 feet and drift as far as 125 miles before the balloon expands and pops.

**13.3 [Radar Observations, p.13-3, difficulty 2]**  
How does NEXRAD Doppler radar operation in clear air mode differ from precipitation mode?

- ✅ **Clear air mode is more sensitive and updates approximately every 10 minutes, while precipitation mode updates every 4 to 6 minutes**
- Clear air mode updates every 4 to 6 minutes, while precipitation mode updates every 10 minutes
- Clear air mode is less sensitive but updates more frequently than precipitation mode
- Both modes have the same sensitivity but different update rates

  > In clear air mode, a slow antenna rotation allows the radar to sample the atmosphere longer, making it the most sensitive mode with updates about every 10 minutes. In precipitation mode, stronger return signals allow a faster antenna rotation, updating every 4 to 6 minutes.

**13.4 [Flight Service Station (FSS), p.13-4, difficulty 1]**  
Which service outlet is described as the primary source for preflight weather information?

- ✅ **Flight Service Station (FSS)**
- Hazardous Inflight Weather Advisory Service (HIWAS)
- Telephone Information Briefing Service (TIBS)
- Transcribed Weather Broadcast (TWEB)

  > The FSS is the primary source for preflight weather information and can be reached 24 hours a day at 1-800-WX-BRIEF. TIBS is a preliminary briefing tool, HIWAS is an inflight broadcast, and TWEB is primarily an Alaska service.

**13.5 [Hazardous Inflight Weather Advisory Service (HIWAS), p.13-4, difficulty 1]**  
Over what type of navigational aid does HIWAS broadcast hazardous weather information?

- ✅ **Selected VORs**
- Selected NDBs
- Selected ILS localizers
- Selected GPS waypoints

  > HIWAS is an automated continuous broadcast of hazardous weather information over selected VOR NAVAIDs. VORs with HIWAS capability are depicted on aeronautical charts with an 'H' in the upper right corner of the identification box.

**13.6 [Outlook Briefing, p.13-5, difficulty 1]**  
Under what circumstance should a pilot request an outlook briefing?

- ✅ **When the planned departure is 6 hours or more away**
- When the flight is delayed less than 2 hours from the original departure time
- When the pilot needs to update a previously obtained standard briefing
- When the planned departure is within 2 hours and current conditions are needed

  > An outlook briefing is requested when a planned departure is 6 hours or more away. It provides initial forecast information limited in scope. A prudent pilot should request a follow-up standard briefing near the time of departure. An abbreviated briefing is used to update a previous briefing.

**13.7 [Standard Briefing, p.13-5, difficulty 2]**  
When a standard weather briefing includes the statement 'VFR flight not recommended,' what is the pilot's responsibility?

- ✅ **The pilot must make the final go/no-go decision, but should carefully weigh the advisory**
- The pilot is prohibited from conducting the flight under VFR
- The pilot must file an IFR flight plan before departing
- The pilot must request an abbreviated briefing before departing

  > The 'VFR flight not recommended' statement is an advisory, not a prohibition. The briefer states this when weather is below VFR minimums or doubtful for VFR flight. It remains the pilot's decision whether to continue, but the advisory should be weighed carefully.

**13.8 [Pilot Weather Reports (PIREPs), p.13-8, difficulty 2]**  
Under what conditions are ATC facilities required to solicit PIREPs from pilots in the area?

- ✅ **When the ceiling is below 5,000 feet or visibility is at or below five miles**
- When the ceiling is below 3,000 feet or visibility is at or below three miles
- When the ceiling is below 1,000 feet or visibility is at or below one mile
- When the ceiling is below 10,000 feet or visibility is at or below ten miles

  > ATC facilities are required to solicit PIREPs when the ceiling is below 5,000 feet or visibility is at or below five miles. PIREPs provide real-time information about turbulence, icing, and cloud heights that cannot be gathered from any other source.

**13.9 [Terminal Aerodrome Forecasts (TAF), p.13-9, difficulty 2]**  
In a TAF, which cloud types are specifically identified in the forecast sky condition group?

- ✅ **Only cumulonimbus (CB) clouds**
- Cumulonimbus (CB) and towering cumulus (TCU) clouds
- All cloud types including stratus, cumulus, and cirrus
- Only towering cumulus (TCU) clouds

  > Unlike METARs, which can report both CB and TCU clouds, TAFs only specifically forecast cumulonimbus (CB) clouds in the sky condition group. This is because CB clouds indicate thunderstorms and have the most significant operational impact.

**13.10 [Area Forecasts (FA), p.13-10, difficulty 2]**  
How often are area forecasts (FA) issued, and what is their valid period?

- ✅ **Issued three times a day, valid for 18 hours**
- Issued four times a day, valid for 24 hours
- Issued twice a day, valid for 12 hours
- Issued six times a day, valid for 6 hours

  > Area forecasts are issued three times a day and are valid for 18 hours. They provide a picture of clouds, general weather conditions, and VMC expected over a large area encompassing several states, and are especially useful for smaller airports without terminal forecasts.

**13.11 [AIRMET, p.13-11, difficulty 2]**  
Which AIRMET designator is used to denote turbulence, strong surface winds, and low-level wind shear?

- ✅ **Tango**
- Sierra
- Zulu
- Romeo

  > AIRMET Tango denotes turbulence, strong surface winds, and low-level wind shear. Sierra denotes IFR and mountain obscuration, and Zulu denotes icing and freezing levels. Only three AIRMET designators exist (Sierra, Tango, Zulu), so Romeo is not a valid AIRMET designator.

**13.12 [SIGMET, p.13-12, difficulty 2]**  
What is the standard valid period for a SIGMET, and how does it differ for hurricanes?

- ✅ **4 hours, but 6 hours if related to a hurricane**
- 6 hours, but 4 hours if related to a hurricane
- 2 hours, but 4 hours if related to a hurricane
- 4 hours for all SIGMETs regardless of weather type

  > SIGMETs are unscheduled forecasts valid for 4 hours. However, if the SIGMET relates to a hurricane, it is valid for 6 hours. SIGMETs concern non-convective weather hazardous to all aircraft, including severe icing, severe turbulence, dust storms, and volcanic ash.

**13.13 [Convective Significant Meteorological Information (WST), p.13-12, difficulty 3]**  
Which of the following conditions would trigger the issuance of a Convective SIGMET?

- ✅ **Severe thunderstorms with surface winds greater than 50 knots, hail at the surface greater than or equal to ¾ inch in diameter, or tornadoes**
- Severe icing not associated with thunderstorms
- Moderate turbulence and sustained surface winds of 30 knots or greater
- Any thunderstorm with moderate precipitation regardless of coverage area

  > Convective SIGMETs are issued for severe thunderstorms with surface winds greater than 50 knots, hail at the surface greater than or equal to ¾ inch, or tornadoes. They are also issued for embedded thunderstorms, lines of thunderstorms, or thunderstorms with heavy or greater precipitation affecting 40 percent or more of a 3,000 square mile or greater region. Severe icing and moderate turbulence are covered by SIGMETs and AIRMETs, respectively.

**13.14 [Winds and Temperature Aloft Forecast (FB), p.13-13, difficulty 3]**  
In a Winds and Temperature Aloft Forecast (FB), how should a pilot decode a four-digit wind group that appears as '7319'?

- ✅ **Subtract 50 from the direction and add 100 to the speed, yielding 230° at 119 knots**
- Add 50 to the direction and subtract 100 from the speed, yielding 123° at 81 knots
- The wind is from 730° at 19 knots, which is invalid
- The wind is from 73° at 19 knots, indicating light and variable winds

  > When wind speed is forecast to be greater than 99 knots but less than 199 knots, the computer adds 50 to the direction and subtracts 100 from the speed. To decode, the pilot must reverse this: subtract 50 from 73 to get 230°, and add 100 to 19 to get 119 knots.

**13.15 [Weather Depiction Chart, p.13-15, difficulty 2]**  
On a weather depiction chart, how are areas of IFR conditions depicted?

- ✅ **Hatched areas outlined by a smooth line**
- Nonhatched areas outlined by a smooth line
- Areas not outlined at all
- Hatched areas outlined by a jagged line

  > IFR conditions (ceilings less than 1,000 feet and visibility less than three miles) are shown by a hatched area outlined by a smooth line. MVFR regions are shown by a nonhatched area outlined by a smooth line. VFR areas are not outlined.

**13.16 [ATC Radar Weather Displays, p.13-17, difficulty 2]**  
What limitation of ATC radar should pilots be aware of regarding turbulence near thunderstorms?

- ✅ **ATC radar cannot detect turbulence, and severity can be much greater than precipitation intensity indicates**
- ATC radar can detect turbulence but only within 20 miles of a thunderstorm
- ATC radar detects turbulence but cannot display it to pilots
- ATC radar can detect turbulence but only in clear air conditions

  > ATC radar is not able to detect turbulence. While turbulence generally correlates with precipitation intensity, the severity of turbulence near thunderstorms can be much greater than the precipitation intensity might indicate. Pilots should use caution within 20 miles of thunderstorms.

**13.17 [What Can Pilots Do?, p.13-19, difficulty 3]**  
When using in-cockpit NEXRAD weather displays, how much older could the actual weather conditions be compared to the age indicated on the display?

- ✅ **Up to 15 to 20 minutes older than the age indicated on the display**
- Up to 5 minutes older than the age indicated on the display
- Up to 30 to 40 minutes older than the age indicated on the display
- The age indicated on the display always matches the actual weather conditions

  > The NEXRAD age indicator shows the age of the mosaic image, not the actual weather conditions. Due to processing and transmission delays, the actual weather could be up to 15 to 20 minutes older than the age indicated on the display. Pilots should never use NEXRAD imagery for storm penetration or navigation through a line of storms.

**13.18 [Flight Information Service- Broadcast (FIS-B), p.13-23, difficulty 2]**  
What is the regulatory status of weather products provided through FIS-B?

- ✅ **They are for information only and do not meet the safety and regulatory requirements of official weather products**
- They are official weather products that meet all regulatory requirements for flight planning
- They can be used as primary weather products but only for VFR flight
- They are official weather products but only when received within 5 minutes of transmission

  > FIS-B weather products are for information only and do not meet the safety and regulatory requirements of official weather products. They should not be used as primary weather products for operational and safety requirements. Some rendered data can be up to 20 or 30 minutes old.


## Chapter 14 — 18 questions

**14.1 [Runway Markings and Signs, p.14-5, difficulty 2]**  
A displaced threshold reduces the length of runway available for landings. What use is permitted on the portion of runway behind a displaced threshold?

- ✅ **Takeoffs in either direction, or landings from the opposite direction**
- Takeoffs in the direction of the displaced threshold only
- Taxiing only, since the surface is not structurally rated for landing or takeoff
- Landings from either direction

  > The chapter states that the portion of runway behind a displaced threshold is available for takeoffs in either direction or landings from the opposite direction. It is not limited to taxiing, and landings are only permitted from the opposite direction, not from the direction of the displacement.

**14.2 [Types of Airports, p.14-3, difficulty 2]**  
When arriving from the upwind side of a nontowered airport traffic pattern, what is one recommended method of entry?

- ✅ **Cross over midfield at least 500 feet above pattern altitude, then when clear of the pattern, descend to pattern altitude and turn right to enter on a 45° to the downwind leg at midfield**
- Descend directly to pattern altitude and enter on a base leg
- Cross over midfield at pattern altitude and immediately turn downwind
- Enter on a 45° to the upwind leg at pattern altitude

  > The chapter describes crossing over midfield at least 500 feet above pattern altitude, then when approximately 2 miles clear of the pattern, descending to pattern altitude and turning right to enter at 45° to the downwind leg at midfield. Entering at pattern altitude directly or entering on the upwind leg is not the recommended procedure.

**14.3 [Runway Holding Position Marking, p.14-8, difficulty 2]**  
When exiting a runway, how does a pilot interpret the runway holding position markings, and is ATC clearance required to cross them?

- ✅ **The aircraft approaches the dashed lines first; the entire aircraft must cross both the dashed and solid lines to be clear of the runway, and no ATC clearance is needed**
- The aircraft approaches the solid lines first, and must stop until ATC provides clearance to cross
- The aircraft approaches the dashed lines first, and must stop until ATC provides clearance to cross
- The aircraft approaches the solid lines first, and no clearance is needed since the aircraft is exiting

  > When exiting the runway, the aircraft approaches the dashed lines first. The entire aircraft must cross both the dashed and solid lines to be clear of the runway. No ATC clearance is needed to cross when exiting. When approaching the runway (the opposite direction), the solid lines are encountered first and ATC clearance is required.

**14.4 [Land and Hold Short Operations (LAHSO), p.14-11, difficulty 2]**  
Which of the following statements about LAHSO restrictions is correct?

- ✅ **LAHSO are not authorized for student pilots who are performing a solo flight**
- LAHSO are authorized at night for properly rated non-student pilots
- LAHSO are authorized on wet runways if the pilot has received ATC approval
- Student pilots may participate in LAHSO only when a CFI is on board

  > The chapter explicitly states that LAHSO are not authorized for student pilots on solo flights, are generally not authorized at night, and are not authorized on wet runways. The text does not provide an exception for student pilots with a CFI on board.

**14.5 [Visual Approach Slope Indicator (VASI), p.14-16, difficulty 1]**  
A VASI installation provides obstruction clearance within what spatial limits?

- ✅ **Within 10° of the extended runway centerline and up to 4 NM from the runway threshold**
- Within 10° of the extended runway centerline and up to 10 NM from the runway threshold
- Within 15° of the extended runway centerline and up to 4 NM from the runway threshold
- Within 5° of the extended runway centerline and up to 3 NM from the runway threshold

  > The chapter specifies that the VASI provides obstruction clearance within 10° of the extended runway centerline and up to 4 nautical miles from the runway threshold. The other options combine incorrect distance or angular values.

**14.6 [Airport Beacon, p.14-16, difficulty 1]**  
A pilot at night observes an airport beacon flashing white and yellow. What type of airport does this indicate?

- ✅ **A water airport**
- A civilian land airport
- A heliport
- A military airport

  > The chapter lists beacon color combinations: flashing white and green indicates a civilian land airport, white and yellow indicates a water airport, white/yellow/green indicates a heliport, and two quick white flashes alternating with green identifies a military airport.

**14.7 [Automated Terminal Information Service (ATIS), p.14-5, difficulty 1]**  
When contacting the tower controller after listening to ATIS, what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **State the ATIS code letter received so the controller can verify the pilot has the current information**
- Request the controller to read back the current weather conditions for confirmation
- State only 'ATIS received' without specifying the code letter
- Wait for the controller to ask if the pilot has the current ATIS before stating the code

  > The chapter states that upon contacting the tower controller, the pilot should state the ATIS code received (e.g., 'information Bravo'). This allows the controller to verify the pilot has the current weather and airport information without relaying it individually. Simply saying 'ATIS received' without the code does not allow verification.

**14.8 [Vortex Behavior, p.14-28, difficulty 2]**  
Which wind condition presents the worst case scenario for wake turbulence on final approach?

- ✅ **A light quartering tailwind**
- A direct headwind
- A strong crosswind from the right
- A light quartering headwind

  > The chapter identifies a light quartering tailwind as the worst case scenario because the wake vortices could be present along a significant portion of the final approach and extended centerline, not just in the touchdown zone. A headwind would push vortices away, and a crosswind moves vortices laterally but does not concentrate them along the approach path as effectively.

**14.9 [Holding Position Signs and Markings for an Instrument Landing System (ILS) Critical Area, p.14-12, difficulty 2]**  
What do the ILS critical area holding position markings look like on the pavement?

- ✅ **A horizontal yellow ladder extending across the width of the taxiway**
- Four yellow lines, two solid and two dashed, extending across the taxiway
- A single dashed yellow line extending across the taxiway
- White characters on a red background painted on the left side of the taxiway centerline

  > The chapter describes the ILS critical area holding position markings as appearing on the pavement as a horizontal yellow ladder extending across the width of the taxiway. The four-line solid/dashed pattern is the runway holding position marking, the single dashed line is for taxiway/taxiway intersections, and white-on-red characters describe a surface painted holding position sign.

**14.10 [ATC Instructions—Hold Short, p.14-32, difficulty 2]**  
When ATC issues a 'hold short' instruction, what is the pilot required to read back?

- ✅ **The entire clearance and 'hold short' instruction, including the runway identifier and aircraft call sign**
- Only the runway identifier, acknowledged with 'roger'
- The entire clearance, but the call sign may be omitted if the frequency is busy
- Only 'hold short' and the runway identifier

  > ATC is required to obtain a read-back of all runway 'hold short' instructions. The pilot must read back the entire clearance and 'hold short' instruction, including the runway identifier and aircraft call sign. Partial read-backs or omitting the call sign do not satisfy this requirement.

**14.11 [Engineered Materials Arresting Systems (EMAS), p.14-36, difficulty 3]**  
If a pilot determines the aircraft will exit the runway end and enter an EMAS bed during an overrun, what actions should the pilot take?

- ✅ **Continue maximum deceleration efforts, maintain runway centerline, and do not attempt to taxi once stopped**
- Apply nosewheel steering to steer the aircraft out of the EMAS bed to one side
- Reduce braking to allow the EMAS bed to absorb the aircraft's momentum more gently
- Shut down engines and apply the parking brake before entering the EMAS bed

  > The chapter provides specific guidance: continue deceleration following rejected takeoff or maximum braking procedures, maintain runway centerline to maximize the EMAS bed's stopping capability, maintain deceleration efforts since the bed is passive, and once stopped, do not attempt to taxi or move the aircraft. Steering out of the bed or reducing braking would reduce effectiveness.

**14.12 [Nontowered Airport, p.14-2, difficulty 1]**  
At an airport with no tower, FSS, or UNICOM, what frequency and procedure should a pilot use for self-announcing?

- ✅ **Self-announce on MULTICOM frequency 122.9**
- Self-announce on UNICOM frequency 122.8
- Use the tower frequency even though the tower is not in operation
- Self-announce on the emergency frequency 121.5

  > Figure 14-1 specifies that at an airport with no tower, FSS, or UNICOM, pilots should self-announce on MULTICOM frequency 122.9. UNICOM frequencies are used when a UNICOM station exists, the tower frequency is used when a tower exists but is not in operation, and 121.5 is for emergencies only.

**14.13 [Traffic Patterns, p.14-20, difficulty 2]**  
On a segmented circle, what do the traffic pattern indicators indicate?

- ✅ **The direction of turns when there is a variation from the normal left traffic pattern**
- The direction of the wind
- The alignment of the landing strips
- The recommended traffic pattern altitude

  > The chapter states that traffic pattern indicators are arranged in pairs in conjunction with landing strip indicators and are used to indicate the direction of turns when there is a variation from the normal left traffic pattern. Wind direction is shown by wind direction indicators, landing strip alignment by landing strip indicators, and pattern altitude is not indicated by the segmented circle.

**14.14 [Lost Communication Procedures, p.14-23, difficulty 2]**  
If both the transmitter and receiver are inoperative and a pilot needs to land at a towered airport, what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **Remain outside of Class D airspace until the flow of traffic is determined, then enter the pattern and watch for light signals**
- Squawk 7700 and wait for a steady green light before entering Class D airspace
- Enter Class D airspace immediately and orbit the tower until light signals are received
- Remain outside Class D and land at the nearest nontowered airport since landing at a towered airport is not possible

  > The chapter states that when both receiver and transmitter are inoperative, the pilot should remain outside Class D airspace until the flow of traffic is determined, then enter the pattern and watch for light signals. The text does not say landing at a towered airport is impossible; it provides a specific procedure. Squawking 7700 is for emergencies, and orbiting the tower without determining traffic flow first is not the recommended procedure.

**14.15 [Taxiway Markings and Signs, p.14-11, difficulty 1]**  
How are taxiway direction signs identified?

- ✅ **Yellow background with black characters, with arrows indicating the direction of turn**
- Black background with yellow inscription and no arrows
- Red background with white inscription
- Yellow background with black characters and no arrows

  > The chapter describes taxiway direction signs as having a yellow background with black characters, with arrows indicating the direction of turn. A black background with yellow inscription and no arrows describes a location sign. A red background with white inscription describes a mandatory instruction sign. Direction signs always include arrows.

**14.16 [Runway Distance Remaining Signs, p.14-8, difficulty 1]**  
What do runway distance remaining signs indicate?

- ✅ **The distance of the remaining runway in thousands of feet**
- The distance of the remaining runway in hundreds of feet
- The distance from the current position to the next taxiway intersection
- The distance remaining to the nearest runway threshold

  > The chapter states that runway distance remaining signs have a black background with a white number indicating the distance, in thousands of feet, of landing runway remaining. They do not indicate hundreds of feet, taxiway distances, or distance to a threshold.

**14.17 [Wind Direction Indicators, p.14-20, difficulty 2]**  
Why should a pilot also check the wind sock even when a tetrahedron is available at an airport?

- ✅ **A tetrahedron can be manually set to align with the runway in use, so the wind sock provides actual wind information**
- The wind sock is more visible at night than the tetrahedron
- The wind sock is calibrated to FAA standards while the tetrahedron is not
- The tetrahedron only indicates wind direction and not velocity, while the wind sock indicates both

  > The chapter explains that wind tees and tetrahedrons can swing freely and align with the wind, but they can also be manually set to align with the runway in use. For this reason, a pilot should also look at the wind sock for wind information if one is available. The other options give reasons not stated in the text.

**14.18 [ATC Instructions—Line Up and Wait (LUAW), p.14-34, difficulty 2]**  
After receiving a 'line up and wait' instruction, if a takeoff clearance has not been received within a certain time, what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **Contact ATC immediately if no takeoff clearance is received within 90 seconds**
- Contact ATC if no takeoff clearance is received within 60 seconds
- Cancel the LUAW clearance and taxi clear of the runway after 2 minutes
- Begin the takeoff roll if no clearance is received within 120 seconds

  > The chapter specifies that if a takeoff clearance is not received within 90 seconds after receiving a 'line up and wait' instruction, the pilot should contact ATC immediately. The pilot should never begin takeoff without an explicit clearance, and taxiing off the runway without ATC instruction is not authorized.


## Chapter 15 — 18 questions

**15.1 [Introduction, p.15-1, difficulty 1]**  
According to the chapter, what factors dictate the categories and types of airspace?

- ✅ **The complexity or density of aircraft movements, nature of operations, level of safety required, and national and public interest**
- The number of control towers and radar facilities within a given region
- The volume of IFR versus VFR traffic in the area
- The geographic location and population density below the airspace

  > The chapter explicitly states that the categories and types of airspace are dictated by these four factors, not by facility counts, traffic ratios, or geography.

**15.2 [Class A Airspace, p.15-2, difficulty 1]**  
A pilot is planning a VFR cross-country flight at FL 190. What must the pilot do to operate legally at this altitude?

- ✅ **File and fly IFR with an ATC clearance, since all operations in Class A must be conducted under IFR**
- Fly VFR as long as basic VFR weather minimums are met
- Fly VFR during daylight hours but switch to IFR at night
- Obtain a VFR clearance from ATC prior to entering the airspace

  > Class A airspace begins at 18,000 feet MSL and extends to FL 600. Unless otherwise authorized, all operations in Class A must be conducted under IFR. VFR flight is not permitted.

**15.3 [Class B Airspace, p.15-2, difficulty 2]**  
What service does ATC provide to all aircraft that are cleared to operate within Class B airspace?

- ✅ **Separation services within the airspace**
- Traffic advisories only; pilots maintain their own separation
- Sequencing to the runway but pilots maintain their own separation
- Weather avoidance and navigation assistance only

  > The chapter states that ATC clearance is required for all aircraft to operate in Class B, and all aircraft that are so cleared receive separation services within the airspace. This is a key distinction from Class C and D where only communications are required.

**15.4 [Class C Airspace, p.15-2, difficulty 2]**  
A VFR pilot is approaching a Class C airspace area. What must the pilot establish before entering the airspace?

- ✅ **Two-way radio communications with ATC prior to entering the airspace**
- An ATC clearance prior to entering the airspace
- Radar contact with ATC prior to entering the airspace
- Visual contact with the control tower prior to entering

  > Class C requires two-way radio communications to be established prior to entry and maintained while in the airspace. Unlike Class B, an ATC clearance is not required—only communications. The chapter distinguishes between the clearance requirement of Class B and the communications requirement of Class C and D.

**15.5 [Class D Airspace, p.15-2, difficulty 1]**  
What is the typical vertical extent of Class D airspace?

- ✅ **From the surface to 2,500 feet above the airport elevation**
- From the surface to 4,000 feet above the airport elevation
- From 700 feet AGL to 10,000 feet MSL
- From the surface to 10,000 feet MSL

  > Class D airspace generally extends from the surface to 2,500 feet above the airport elevation. The 4,000-foot figure applies to Class C, while 10,000 feet MSL is associated with Class B.

**15.6 [Class E Airspace, p.15-3, difficulty 2]**  
In areas where aeronautical charts do not depict a Class E airspace base, at what altitude does Class E airspace begin?

- ✅ **14,500 feet MSL**
- 1,200 feet AGL
- 700 feet AGL
- 18,000 feet MSL

  > The chapter states that where charts do not depict a Class E base, Class E begins at 14,500 feet MSL. In most depicted areas the base is 1,200 feet AGL, and in many others it is 700 feet AGL or the surface, but the default undepicted base is 14,500 feet MSL.

**15.7 [Uncontrolled Airspace, p.15-3, difficulty 1]**  
What is true regarding ATC's authority and weather minimums in Class G airspace?

- ✅ **ATC has no authority or responsibility to control air traffic, but VFR minimums still apply**
- ATC has authority to control IFR traffic but not VFR traffic
- ATC provides traffic advisories upon request but has no separation authority
- ATC has no authority, and no weather minimums apply

  > Class G is uncontrolled airspace where ATC has no authority or responsibility to control air traffic. However, pilots must still comply with VFR minimums that apply to Class G, which vary by altitude and time of day.

**15.8 [Prohibited Areas, p.15-3, difficulty 1]**  
For what purpose are prohibited areas established, and how are they charted?

- ✅ **For security or reasons associated with the national welfare; charted with a "P" followed by a number**
- To separate military training from IFR traffic; charted with an "M" followed by a number
- To warn pilots of hazardous activities over international waters; charted with a "W" followed by a number
- To confine artillery firing activities; charted with an "R" followed by a number

  > Prohibited areas are established for security or national welfare reasons, and flight within them is prohibited. They are charted as a "P" followed by a number (e.g., P-40). The other charting symbols apply to different special use airspace types.

**15.9 [Restricted Areas, p.15-3, difficulty 2]**  
How does ATC handle an IFR aircraft transiting joint-use restricted airspace when the area is active and has NOT been released to the FAA?

- ✅ **ATC issues a clearance that ensures the aircraft avoids the restricted airspace**
- ATC allows the aircraft to operate in the restricted airspace without issuing specific clearance
- ATC denies the IFR clearance entirely until the restricted area becomes inactive
- ATC issues a clearance for the aircraft to transit at the pilot's own risk

  > The chapter outlines two scenarios for joint-use restricted areas. If the area is not active and has been released to the FAA, ATC allows operation without specific clearance. If the area is active and has not been released, ATC issues a clearance that ensures the aircraft avoids the restricted airspace.

**15.10 [Warning Areas, p.15-4, difficulty 2]**  
What key factor distinguishes a warning area from a restricted area?

- ✅ **The United States government does not have sole jurisdiction over the airspace in a warning area**
- Warning areas prohibit all flight, while restricted areas allow limited flight
- Warning areas are located only over domestic land, while restricted areas extend over water
- Warning areas extend from the surface to 10,000 feet MSL, while restricted areas have no altitude limit

  > The chapter explicitly states that warning areas are similar in nature to restricted areas; however, the United States government does not have sole jurisdiction over the airspace. Warning areas extend from 3 NM outward from the coast and may be over domestic or international waters.

**15.11 [Military Operation Areas (MOAs), p.15-4, difficulty 2]**  
When an MOA is active, what happens to nonparticipating IFR traffic transiting the area?

- ✅ **It may be cleared through the MOA if IFR separation can be provided; otherwise, ATC reroutes or restricts it**
- It must always be rerouted around the MOA regardless of separation capability
- It is automatically cleared through the MOA without any ATC restrictions
- It must cancel IFR and maintain VFR while transiting the MOA

  > The chapter states that when an MOA is being used, nonparticipating IFR traffic may be cleared through if IFR separation can be provided by ATC. Otherwise, ATC reroutes or restricts the nonparticipating IFR traffic.

**15.12 [Alert Areas, p.15-4, difficulty 2]**  
In an alert area, who is responsible for collision avoidance?

- ✅ **Pilots of participating aircraft and pilots transiting the area are equally responsible**
- ATC provides separation services for all aircraft within the alert area
- Only pilots of participating aircraft are responsible for seeing and avoiding
- Pilots transiting the area have right-of-way over participating aircraft

  > Alert areas are advisory in nature. The chapter states that pilots of participating aircraft as well as pilots transiting the area shall be equally responsible for collision avoidance. ATC does not provide separation services in alert areas.

**15.13 [Controlled Firing Areas (CFAs), p.15-4, difficulty 2]**  
Why are Controlled Firing Areas not charted on aeronautical charts?

- ✅ **Because activities are suspended when an aircraft approaches, so they do not cause a nonparticipating aircraft to change its flight path**
- Because they are only active at night when chart use is minimal
- Because they are temporary and their locations change daily
- Because they are located only over restricted military ranges and do not affect civil aircraft

  > The key difference between CFAs and other special use airspace is that activities must be suspended when a spotter aircraft, radar, or ground lookout indicates an aircraft might be approaching. Since this ensures no nonparticipating aircraft needs to alter course, there is no need to chart CFAs.

**15.14 [Military Training Routes (MTRs), p.15-6, difficulty 2]**  
A military training route is designated as "VR1208." What does the four-number character designation indicate about this route?

- ✅ **No segment of the route is above 1,500 feet AGL**
- One or more segments of the route are above 1,500 feet AGL
- The route is conducted at or above 10,000 feet MSL
- The route is for IFR operations only

  > MTRs with no segment above 1,500 feet AGL are identified by four number characters (e.g., IR1206, VR1207). MTRs that include one or more segments above 1,500 feet AGL are identified by three number characters (e.g., IR206, VR207). The VR prefix indicates VFR, while IR indicates IFR.

**15.15 [Temporary Flight Restrictions (TFR), p.15-6, difficulty 2]**  
How is a Temporary Flight Restriction (TFR) officially designated, and what is the pilot's responsibility?

- ✅ **An FDC NOTAM is issued, and the pilot must check NOTAMs as part of flight planning to be aware of TFRs**
- A TFR is published in the Chart Supplement, and pilots must check it before flight
- A TFR is announced by ATC broadcast on guard frequency, and pilots must monitor 121.5
- A TFR is designated by FAA website posting only, and pilots have no specific regulatory responsibility

  > TFRs are designated by a Flight Data Center (FDC) NOTAM beginning with the phrase "FLIGHT RESTRICTIONS." The chapter emphasizes that it is a pilot's responsibility to be aware of TFRs in their proposed area of flight, and one way to check is the FAA website.

**15.16 [Terminal Radar Service Areas (TRSAs), p.15-7, difficulty 2]**  
What is the policy regarding pilot participation in Terminal Radar Service Area (TRSA) services?

- ✅ **Participation is voluntary, but VFR pilots are encouraged to contact the radar approach control and take advantage of the service**
- Participation is mandatory for all aircraft operating within the TRSA
- Participation is mandatory for IFR aircraft but voluntary for VFR aircraft
- Participation is mandatory for VFR aircraft but voluntary for IFR aircraft

  > The chapter clearly states that participation in TRSA services is voluntary. However, VFR pilots are encouraged to contact the radar approach control and take advantage of TRSA service. The primary airport within a TRSA becomes Class D airspace, which does have its own communications requirements.

**15.17 [Basic VFR Weather Minimums, p.15-8, difficulty 2]**  
What are the basic VFR weather minimums for operating in Class B airspace?

- ✅ **3 statute miles flight visibility and clear of clouds**
- 3 statute miles flight visibility and 1,000 feet above, 500 feet below, 2,000 feet horizontal from clouds
- 1 statute mile flight visibility and clear of clouds
- 5 statute miles flight visibility and 1,000 feet above, 1,000 feet below, 1 statute mile horizontal from clouds

  > Class B airspace has the most lenient cloud clearance requirement of any controlled airspace: only "clear of clouds" with 3 statute miles visibility. The 1,000/500/2,000 requirement applies to Class C, D, and E (below 10,000 MSL). The 5-mile/1,000/1,000/1 requirement applies at or above 10,000 feet MSL in Class E and G.

**15.18 [Operating Rules and Pilot/Equipment Requirements, p.15-10, difficulty 3]**  
If an aircraft's radio fails in flight under VFR while in Class D airspace, under what conditions may the pilot land at the primary airport?

- ✅ **If weather is at or above basic VFR minimums, visual contact with the tower is maintained, and a clearance to land is received**
- The pilot must immediately exit the Class D airspace and land at a non-towered airport
- The pilot must declare an emergency and wait for ATC to dispatch emergency vehicles
- The pilot may land at their discretion without any additional requirements since VFR applies

  > The chapter provides specific radio failure procedures for Class D. Under VFR, the PIC may operate and land if three conditions are met: weather at or above basic VFR minimums, visual contact with the tower maintained, and a clearance to land received. The same procedure applies in Class C and Class E surface areas with operational towers.


## Chapter 16 — 18 questions

**16.1 [VFR Terminal Area Charts, p.16-2, difficulty 1]**  
A VFR pilot is planning a flight that will transit near Class B airspace and wants a chart that provides a more detailed display of topographical information for that congested area. Which chart should the pilot use in addition to a sectional chart?

- ✅ **VFR Terminal Area Chart**
- World Aeronautical Chart
- Chart Supplement U.S.
- A second sectional chart from an adjacent area

  > The chapter states that VFR Terminal Area Charts are helpful when flying in or near Class B airspace and provide a more detailed display of topographical information at a scale of 1:250,000. World Aeronautical Charts have less detail (1:1,000,000). Chart Supplement U.S. is a text-based directory, not a chart.

**16.2 [Time Zones, p.16-5, difficulty 1]**  
A pilot in the Pacific Standard Time zone needs to convert local time to UTC (Zulu time) for filing a VFR flight plan. If the planned departure time is 0900 Pacific Standard Time, what is the equivalent UTC time?

- ✅ **1700Z**
- 1500Z
- 1600Z
- 1800Z

  > The chapter states that to convert Pacific Standard Time to UTC, add 8 hours. 0900 + 8 hours = 1700Z. 1500Z would result from adding 6 hours (the Central Standard Time conversion factor). 1600Z would be the conversion from Mountain Standard Time (adding 7 hours). 1800Z would result from adding 9 hours instead of 8.

**16.3 [Variation, p.16-7, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot is flying in an area where the magnetic variation is 9° E. If the true course measured on the chart is 270°, what magnetic course should the pilot fly?

- ✅ **261°**
- 279°
- 270°
- 081°

  > The chapter states 'east is least (subtract) and west is best (add).' With 9° E variation, subtract 9° from the true course: 270° − 9° = 261°. Answer 279° incorrectly adds the variation. Answer 270° applies no variation correction. Answer 081° is the reciprocal of 261°.

**16.4 [Effect of Wind, p.16-9, difficulty 2]**  
An aircraft is flying eastward at a true airspeed of 120 knots. The wind is from 090° at 20 knots (blowing westward). What is the aircraft's groundspeed?

- ✅ **100 knots**
- 140 knots
- 120 knots
- 80 knots

  > The chapter explains that when the air mass is moving westward at 20 knots opposite the aircraft's direction, the airspeed remains 120 knots but groundspeed becomes 120 minus 20, or 100 knots. Answer 140 treats the headwind as a tailwind. Answer 120 incorrectly assumes wind does not affect groundspeed. Answer 80 doubles the wind correction.

**16.5 [Fuel Consumption, p.16-12, difficulty 1]**  
A pilot is planning a 400 NM cross-country flight with a forecast groundspeed of 100 knots. The aircraft consumes 5 gallons of fuel per hour. How much fuel will be used during the flight (not including reserve)?

- ✅ **20 gallons**
- 15 gallons
- 25 gallons
- 40 gallons

  > The chapter uses this exact example: time = distance ÷ groundspeed = 400 ÷ 100 = 4 hours. Fuel = time × rate = 4 × 5 = 20 gallons. Answer 15 uses 3 hours. Answer 25 uses 5 hours. Answer 40 uses a fuel rate of 10 GPH instead of 5.

**16.6 [Pilotage, p.16-12, difficulty 2]**  
When selecting checkpoints for pilotage navigation, which practice does the FAA recommend?

- ✅ **Select features that make useful boundaries or brackets on each side of the course, and never place complete reliance on any single checkpoint.**
- Select only one prominent checkpoint every 50 miles to minimize cockpit workload.
- Choose towns represented by a small circle on the chart since they are easy to identify from the air.
- Select only roads as checkpoints since they are the most prominent features visible from the air.

  > The chapter states to select features that make useful boundaries or brackets on each side of the course and to 'Never place complete reliance on any single checkpoint.' Towns shown as small circles 'may turn out to be only a half-dozen houses.' New roads may not be shown on the chart until the next edition is issued.

**16.7 [Wind Triangle or Vector Analysis, p.16-15, difficulty 2]**  
In constructing a wind triangle, if the wind is blowing from the right of the true course, how should the wind correction angle (WCA) be applied to determine the true heading?

- ✅ **Add the WCA to the true course.**
- Subtract the WCA from the true course.
- Add the WCA to the magnetic heading.
- Subtract the WCA from the compass heading.

  > The chapter states: 'If the wind blows from the right of TC, the angle is added; if from the left, it is subtracted.' Subtracting is for wind from the left. The WCA is applied to TC to obtain TH, not to MH or CH, which are later steps in the heading sequence.

**16.8 [Very High Frequency (VHF) Omnidirectional Range (VOR), p.16-23, difficulty 2]**  
A VOR receiver is tuned and the station's Morse code identification is verified, but the NAV warning flag remains visible on the indicator. What is the most likely cause?

- ✅ **Signal strength is inadequate because the aircraft is too far from the VOR or too low to receive line-of-sight signals.**
- The VOR station is out of service for maintenance.
- The OBS is set to an incorrect radial.
- The aircraft's VOR receiver has never been calibrated.

  > The chapter states the NAV flag appears when 'signal strength is inadequate to operate the navigational equipment' due to being too far or too low. If the station were out of service, the coded identification would be removed—not received. An incorrect OBS setting deflects the CDI needle but does not trigger the NAV flag.

**16.9 [Course Deviation Indicator (CDI), p.16-26, difficulty 3]**  
A pilot flying toward a VOR station notices the CDI needle is moving in the opposite direction from what is expected—when the aircraft drifts right, the needle moves right instead of left. Which situation explains this reverse sensing?

- ✅ **The pilot is flying toward the station with a 'FROM' indication displayed on the indicator.**
- The pilot is flying toward the station with a 'TO' indication.
- The VOR station is operating without its coded identification.
- The course deviation needle is stuck due to a mechanical failure.

  > The chapter explains that flying toward a station with a 'FROM' indication (or away with a 'TO' indication) causes reverse sensing—the needle indicates opposite to the direction it should. Flying with a 'TO' indication toward the station is correct procedure. A station without coded identification is out of service. Reverse sensing is a normal characteristic, not a mechanical failure.

**16.10 [Distance Measuring Equipment (DME), p.16-27, difficulty 2]**  
An aircraft is flying directly over a VORTAC at an altitude of 6,076 feet AGL. What distance will the DME display?

- ✅ **Approximately 1.0 NM**
- 0 NM
- 6,076 feet
- Approximately 2.0 NM

  > DME measures slant range distance, which includes the altitude component. The chapter gives this exact example: station passage directly over a VORTAC at 6,076 feet AGL shows approximately 1.0 NM. Answer 0 NM ignores the slant range effect. Answer 6,076 feet is in the wrong unit (DME displays NM). Answer 2.0 NM would correspond to roughly 12,000 feet AGL.

**16.11 [Automatic Direction Finder (ADF), p.16-29, difficulty 2]**  
What advantage does an NDB have over a VOR for navigation?

- ✅ **NDB signals are not affected by line-of-sight limitations and follow the curvature of the Earth, so they can be received regardless of altitude if within range.**
- NDB signals provide more accurate course guidance than VOR.
- NDB signals are not affected by electrical disturbances such as lightning.
- NDB stations have greater range than all VOR stations regardless of altitude.

  > The chapter states NDBs use low/medium frequencies not affected by line-of-sight, with signals following the Earth's curvature. VOR accuracy is described as 'excellent, generally within plus or minus 1°,' superior to ADF. The text states LF signals are 'very susceptible to electrical disturbances, such as lightning.' VOR range increases significantly with altitude, and high-altitude VORs can reach 130 miles.

**16.12 [Global Positioning System, p.16-31, difficulty 3]**  
For a GPS receiver to perform RAIM (Receiver Autonomous Integrity Monitoring) to detect an integrity anomaly, what is the minimum satellite requirement?

- ✅ **Five satellites in view, or four satellites with barometric altimeter input (baro-aiding)**
- Four satellites in view, or three with baro-aiding
- Six satellites in view, or five with baro-aiding
- Three satellites in view with a barometric altimeter

  > The chapter states RAIM needs a minimum of five satellites or four with baro-aiding to detect an anomaly. Four satellites alone provide only a 3D position fix, not RAIM. Six satellites (or five with baro-aiding) are needed to isolate and remove a corrupt satellite signal, which is a higher level than detection. Three satellites are insufficient even for a basic position fix.

**16.13 [Lost Procedures, p.16-34, difficulty 1]**  
If a pilot becomes lost during a VFR cross-country flight and cannot see a town or city, what should be one of the first actions taken?

- ✅ **Climb, being mindful of traffic and weather conditions, to increase radio and navigation reception range and radar coverage.**
- Descend immediately to maintain visual contact with the ground.
- Immediately turn 180° to reverse course back to the last known position.
- Squawk 7700 on the transponder immediately.

  > The chapter states the first thing to do is climb, which increases radio and navigation reception range and radar coverage. Descending reduces reception. A 180° turn is not recommended as a first step. The text reserves squawking 7700 for when 'the situation becomes threatening,' after first attempting to communicate and determine position.

**16.14 [Flight Diversion, p.16-35, difficulty 2]**  
During a flight, a situation arises that requires an immediate diversion to an alternate airport. What should the pilot prioritize?

- ✅ **Divert promptly toward the alternate destination, then compute heading, groundspeed, and fuel once established on course.**
- Complete all course plotting, measuring, and fuel calculations before turning toward the alternate.
- Remain on the original heading and contact ATC for vectors before diverting.
- Climb to a higher altitude before diverting to improve navigation reception.

  > The chapter states: 'in an emergency, divert promptly toward your alternate destination. Attempting to complete all plotting, measuring, and computations involved before diverting to the alternate destination may only aggravate an actual emergency.' Once established on course, the pilot should note the time and calculate heading, GS, and fuel consumption.

**16.15 [Filing a VFR Flight Plan, p.16-21, difficulty 1]**  
Which statement about VFR flight plans is correct?

- ✅ **Filing a VFR flight plan is not required by regulations, but it is good operating practice because the information can be used for search and rescue in the event of an emergency.**
- Filing a VFR flight plan is required by 14 CFR part 91 for all cross-country flights.
- A VFR flight plan must be filed at least 30 minutes before the proposed departure time.
- Once activated, a VFR flight plan remains active indefinitely until the pilot closes it.

  > The chapter explicitly states filing is not required by regulation but is good practice for search and rescue purposes. It also states the flight plan is held by FSS for 1 hour after the proposed departure time and then canceled unless the actual departure time is received—so it does not remain active indefinitely. No minimum filing time is stated.

**16.16 [Dead Reckoning, p.16-13, difficulty 1]**  
Dead reckoning is best described as which type of navigation?

- ✅ **Navigation solely by means of computations based on time, airspeed, distance, and direction.**
- Navigation by reference to visible landmarks or checkpoints.
- Navigation by use of radio aids such as VOR and GPS.
- Navigation using a combination of pilotage and radio navigation.

  > The chapter defines dead reckoning as 'navigation solely by means of computations based on time, airspeed, distance, and direction.' Navigation by visible landmarks is pilotage. Navigation by radio aids is radio navigation. The chapter lists all three as distinct methods: pilotage, dead reckoning, and radio navigation.

**16.17 [Effect of Wind, p.16-9, difficulty 3]**  
A pilot has determined a true heading of 088°. The magnetic variation in the flight area is 10° E, and the compass deviation card indicates a correction of −2° for the nearest heading. What is the compass heading?

- ✅ **076°**
- 096°
- 080°
- 100°

  > The chapter gives the formula: TH ± V = MH ± D = CH. With 10° E variation, subtract (east is least): 088 − 10 = 078 (MH). Apply deviation of −2°: 078 − 2 = 076 (CH). Answer 096° adds variation instead of subtracting (088 + 10 = 098, − 2 = 096). Answer 080° applies deviation in the wrong direction (078 + 2 = 080). Answer 100° adds both variation and deviation (088 + 10 + 2 = 100).

**16.18 [Course Intercept, p.16-27, difficulty 2]**  
When intercepting a VOR radial, what limits apply to the angle of intercept?

- ✅ **The angle of intercept must be greater than the degrees from course but should not exceed 90°.**
- The angle of intercept should be exactly 45° in all situations.
- The angle of intercept should not exceed 30° to prevent overshooting.
- The angle of intercept must equal the degrees from course for proper interception.

  > The chapter states the angle of intercept 'must be greater than the degrees from course, but should not exceed 90°.' A fixed 45° ignores the factors of distance from station, degrees from course, and groundspeed. A 30° limit is too restrictive. Equaling the degrees from course would not produce an intercept—interception requires an angle greater than the offset.


## Chapter 17 — 18 questions

**17.1 [Obtaining a Medical Certificate, p.17-2, difficulty 1]**  
A 35-year-old pilot obtains a third-class medical certificate. How long is it valid for exercising private pilot privileges?

- ✅ **5 years**
- 2 years
- 1 year
- 6 months

  > A third-class medical certificate is valid for 5 years for individuals who have not reached age 40, and 2 years for those 40 or older. One year is the validity period for a second-class medical, and 6 months applies to a first-class medical for airmen 40 or older.

**17.2 [Obtaining a Medical Certificate, p.17-3, difficulty 2]**  
Which statement is true regarding the fifteen medical conditions that 14 CFR part 67 lists as specifically disqualifying?

- ✅ **With very few exceptions, all disqualifying medical conditions may be considered for a Special Issuance Authorization.**
- These fifteen conditions permanently bar an individual from holding any medical certificate.
- Only medical conditions not listed in the regulations are eligible for special issuance consideration.
- A special issuance is only available after the condition has been completely cured for at least five years.

  > The chapter states that with very few exceptions, all disqualifying medical conditions may be considered for special issuance if the airman can present satisfactory medical documentation that the condition is stable. The fifteen listed conditions are not an absolute bar, and special issuance does not require a cure—only demonstrated stability.

**17.3 [Hypoxia, p.17-3, difficulty 2]**  
At altitude, the percentage of oxygen in the atmosphere remains constant, yet pilots can still develop hypoxic hypoxia. What is the primary reason?

- ✅ **The partial pressure of oxygen decreases proportionately as atmospheric pressure decreases, reducing the number of oxygen molecules available at sufficient pressure.**
- The percentage of oxygen in the atmosphere decreases significantly as altitude increases.
- Oxygen molecules become chemically unstable at reduced atmospheric pressure.
- The lungs lose their ability to transfer oxygen into the bloodstream above 10,000 feet.

  > The chapter explains that although the percentage of oxygen in the atmosphere is constant, its partial pressure decreases proportionately as atmospheric pressure decreases. This means fewer oxygen molecules are available at the pressure required to pass through the membranes in the respiratory system, leading to hypoxic hypoxia.

**17.4 [Hypoxia, p.17-3, difficulty 2]**  
Carbon monoxide poisoning is classified as which type of hypoxia, and what is the mechanism?

- ✅ **Hypemic hypoxia, because CO attaches to hemoglobin and prevents it from carrying oxygen to the cells.**
- Hypoxic hypoxia, because CO reduces the amount of oxygen available in the lungs.
- Histotoxic hypoxia, because CO prevents the cells from effectively using the oxygen delivered to them.
- Stagnant hypoxia, because CO restricts blood flow to the extremities and brain.

  > Hypemic hypoxia occurs when the blood cannot transport sufficient oxygen to the cells. CO poisoning is the most common form because CO attaches to hemoglobin about 200 times more easily than oxygen, preventing hemoglobin from carrying oxygen. Histotoxic hypoxia is caused by alcohol and drugs preventing cells from using oxygen, not by CO.

**17.5 [Hyperventilation, p.17-4, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot at altitude is using supplemental oxygen and has verified the equipment is functioning properly, but still experiences tingling sensations, lightheadedness, and muscle spasms. What is the likely condition and appropriate treatment?

- ✅ **Hyperventilation; breathe normally, talk aloud, or breathe into a paper bag to restore proper CO2 levels.**
- Hypoxia; descend immediately and increase the oxygen flow rate.
- Carbon monoxide poisoning; turn off the heater and open all fresh air vents.
- Motion sickness; open fresh air vents and focus on objects outside the aircraft.

  > Because the oxygen equipment has been checked and is working properly, hypoxia is unlikely. The symptoms described match hyperventilation, which results from abnormal loss of carbon dioxide. Treatment involves restoring proper CO2 levels by breathing normally, talking aloud, or breathing into a paper bag.

**17.6 [Middle Ear and Sinus Problems, p.17-5, difficulty 2]**
