During descent, a pilot experiences increasing ear pain and reduced hearing that is difficult to relieve. What causes this condition, and what should the pilot do?

- ✅ **The partial vacuum in the middle ear constricts the Eustachian tube walls; the pilot should pinch the nostrils shut, close the mouth, and blow gently to force air through the Eustachian tube.**
- Air pressure in the middle ear exceeds external pressure, bulging the eardrum outward; the pilot should yawn repeatedly to release the excess pressure.
- The eardrum has ruptured due to pressure differential; the pilot should climb back to altitude immediately to equalize pressure.
- The Eustachian tube is permanently blocked by wax; the pilot should swallow repeatedly and continue descent.

  > During descent, the middle ear cavity is at lower pressure than the external ear canal, causing the eardrum to bulge inward. The partial vacuum tends to constrict the walls of the Eustachian tube, making equalization difficult. The remedy is the Valsalva-type maneuver: pinch nostrils, close the mouth and lips, and blow slowly and gently to force air through the Eustachian tube into the middle ear.

**17.7 [Middle Ear and Sinus Problems, p.17-5, difficulty 1]**  
A sinus block is most likely to occur during which phase of flight, and what is the primary contributing factor?

- ✅ **During descent, when congestion from an upper respiratory infection plugs the sinus openings.**
- During climb, when expanding air cannot escape rapidly enough through clear sinus passages.
- During level cruise at high altitude, when cabin pressure changes gradually over time.
- During taxi, when abrupt heading changes cause fluid to shift in the sinus cavities.

  > The chapter states that a sinus block occurs most frequently during descent. Congestion from an upper respiratory infection or nasal allergic condition can slow equalization, and as the pressure difference increases, congestion may plug the opening entirely. Slow descent rates can reduce the associated pain.

**17.8 [Vestibular Illusions, p.17-7, difficulty 2]**  
What causes the vestibular illusion known as 'the leans,' and how does the pilot typically respond?

- ✅ **A gradual, prolonged turn below the detection threshold of the semicircular canals goes unnoticed; leveling the wings creates an illusion of banking in the opposite direction, causing the pilot to lean toward the original turn.**
- A rapid head movement during a prolonged turn causes fluid in the ear canals to create a tumbling sensation, causing the pilot to grip the controls tightly.
- A prolonged coordinated constant-rate turn goes unnoticed; when the pilot perceives a descent and pulls back on the controls to correct, the spiral tightens and altitude loss increases.
- An abrupt upward acceleration creates the illusion of climbing, causing the pilot to push the nose down.

  > The leans is caused by a gradual and prolonged turn (approximately 2 degrees per second or less) that goes unnoticed because it is below the detection threshold of the semicircular canals. When the pilot levels the wings, the sensation of turning in the opposite direction occurs, and the pilot leans toward the original turn to correct. The second distractor describes a graveyard spiral, which involves a prolonged descending turn where the pilot pulls back to correct the perceived descent, tightening the spiral. The third describes an elevator illusion.

**17.9 [Vestibular Illusions, p.17-7, difficulty 3]**  
In a graveyard spiral, what sequence of events leads the pilot to tighten the spiral and lose increasing altitude?

- ✅ **After a prolonged turn, the pilot senses no turning; upon leveling, the pilot feels a turn in the opposite direction and returns to the original bank, then pulls back thinking the aircraft is in a level descent, tightening the spiral.**
- The pilot feels a nose-up attitude from acceleration and pushes forward, steepening the descent angle.
- The pilot sees a false horizon from sloping clouds and banks to align with it, entering a descending spiral.
- The pilot experiences a tumbling sensation from head movement and rolls the aircraft inverted while pulling back.

  > In a graveyard spiral, the pilot in a prolonged coordinated constant-rate turn experiences the illusion of not turning. During recovery to level flight, the pilot senses turning in the opposite direction and may return the aircraft to its original turn. The aircraft loses altitude in the turn, and the pilot—feeling no sensation of turning—perceives a level descent, pulling back on the controls. This tightens the spiral and increases the loss of altitude. The first distractor describes somatogravic illusion, the second describes false horizon, and the third describes coriolis illusion.

**17.10 [Vestibular Illusions, p.17-7, difficulty 2]**  
During a nighttime takeoff over featureless terrain, a pilot experiences rapid acceleration and perceives the aircraft is pitching up. What illusion is this, and what is the primary danger?

- ✅ **Somatogravic illusion; the pilot may push the aircraft into a nose-low or dive attitude.**
- Inversion illusion; the pilot may push the aircraft into a nose-low attitude, intensifying the illusion.
- Elevator illusion; the pilot may push the aircraft into a nose-low attitude due to perceived climb.
- Coriolis illusion; the pilot may maneuver the aircraft into a dangerous attitude while correcting perceived roll.

  > A rapid acceleration stimulates the otolith organs in the same way as tilting the head backward, creating the somatogravic illusion of being in a nose-up attitude. The disoriented pilot may push the aircraft into a nose-low or dive attitude. Inversion illusion results from an abrupt change from climb to straight-and-level, elevator illusion from upward vertical acceleration in an updraft, and coriolis illusion from head movement during a prolonged turn.

**17.11 [Optical Illusions, p.17-10, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot on final approach observes that the runway appears narrower than usual. If the pilot does not recognize this illusion, what is the likely outcome?

- ✅ **The pilot will fly a lower approach, with the risk of striking objects or landing short.**
- The pilot will fly a higher approach, risking a hard landing or overshooting the runway.
- The pilot will steepen the approach abruptly, thinking the aircraft is pitching up.
- The pilot will fly a lower approach because the terrain appears featureless and higher than it is.

  > A narrower-than-usual runway creates an illusion that the aircraft is at a higher altitude than it actually is. The pilot who does not recognize this will fly a lower approach, risking striking objects along the approach path or landing short. A wider-than-usual runway has the opposite effect. Featureless terrain illusion is a separate phenomenon related to black hole approaches.

**17.12 [Carbon Monoxide (CO) Poisoning, p.17-12, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot detects a strong exhaust odor in the cabin during flight. What should the pilot assume and what immediate actions should be taken?

- ✅ **Assume CO is present; turn off the heater, open fresh air vents and windows, and use supplemental oxygen if available.**
- Assume the odor is from normal engine operation; continue flight and monitor for headache or dizziness.
- Open the heater vents fully to dilute the exhaust fumes with warm air from the engine compartment.
- Descend immediately to a lower altitude and declare an emergency before taking any other action.

  > The chapter states that if a strong odor of exhaust gases is detected, the pilot should assume CO is present. Immediate corrective actions include turning off the heater (since heater vents may provide CO a passageway into the cabin), opening fresh air vents and windows, and using supplemental oxygen if available. CO may also be present in dangerous amounts even without an exhaust odor.

**17.13 [Alcohol, p.17-15, difficulty 1]**  
Under 14 CFR part 91, what are the regulatory requirements regarding blood alcohol level and time between drinking and flying?

- ✅ **Blood alcohol level must be less than .04 percent, and at least 8 hours must pass between drinking alcohol and piloting an aircraft.**
- Blood alcohol level must be less than .08 percent, and at least 12 hours must pass between drinking and flying.
- Blood alcohol level must be less than .04 percent, and at least 12 hours must pass between drinking and flying.
- Blood alcohol level must be .00 percent, and at least 4 hours must pass between drinking and flying.

  > 14 CFR part 91 requires that blood alcohol level be less than .04 percent and that 8 hours pass between drinking alcohol and piloting an aircraft. A pilot with a blood alcohol level of .04 percent or greater after 8 hours cannot fly until it falls below that amount. The chapter recommends being more conservative than the regulations.

**17.14 [Altitude-Induced Decompression Sickness (DCS), p.17-19, difficulty 2]**  
A pilot plans to fly at 6,000 feet MSL the day after completing a scuba dive that required controlled ascent (decompression stop diving). What is the minimum recommended waiting time before flying?

- ✅ **At least 24 hours**
- At least 12 hours
- At least 4 hours
- At least 48 hours

  > The recommended waiting time before going to flight altitudes up to 8,000 feet is at least 12 hours after diving that does not require controlled ascent (nondecompression stop diving), and at least 24 hours after diving that does require controlled ascent (decompression stop diving). For flight altitudes above 8,000 feet, the waiting time should be at least 24 hours after any scuba dive.

**17.15 [Vision in Flight, p.17-20, difficulty 2]**  
What is the key functional difference between rods and cones as it relates to night vision?

- ✅ **Rods are concentrated away from the fovea, are very sensitive at low-light levels, but cannot discern color; cones are concentrated at the fovea and provide color and detail vision but are ineffective in dim light.**
- Cones are concentrated in the periphery and provide night vision; rods are at the fovea and provide color vision.
- Both rods and cones provide equal night vision capability, but rods also detect color in low light.
- Rods are responsible for color vision in daylight; cones are responsible for black-and-white vision at night.

  > Cones are concentrated at the fovea and are responsible for all color vision and fine detail, but they function only in good illumination. Rods are more numerous away from the fovea, are very sensitive at low-light levels, and are the primary receptors for night vision, but they cannot discern color. This is why a night blind spot exists in the center of the visual field.

**17.16 [Vision in Flight, p.17-23, difficulty 1]**  
How long does it typically take for the rods to fully adapt to darkness after exposure to bright light?

- ✅ **Approximately 30 minutes**
- Approximately 5 minutes
- Approximately 10 minutes
- Approximately 1 hour

  > While cones adapt rapidly to changes in light intensities, the rods take much longer. The rods can take approximately 30 minutes to fully adapt to darkness. A bright light can completely destroy night adaptation, requiring the process to restart.

**17.17 [Vision in Flight, p.17-22, difficulty 2]**  
Why must pilots use off-center viewing—looking 5° to 10° to the side of an object—to see it clearly at night?

- ✅ **The fovea contains almost all cones and virtually no rods, creating a night blind spot in the center of the visual field when light is dim.**
- The optic nerve blocks the central field of view at night, requiring peripheral viewing to compensate.
- The lens cannot focus light properly in low-light conditions, so peripheral vision is sharper.
- Rods in the center of the retina become overstimulated by dim light, causing temporary blindness.

  > The fovea centralis, where most 'looking' occurs, contains almost all cones and virtually no rods. Since cones are ineffective in low light, the center of the visual field has a night blind spot. By looking 5° to 10° off center, the pilot exposes the rods—which are more numerous away from the fovea—to the image, allowing the object to be seen.

**17.18 [Enhanced Night Vision Systems, p.17-29, difficulty 3]**  
What operational advantage does an approved Enhanced Flight Vision System (EFVS) have over a Synthetic Vision System (SVS) when conducting straight-in instrument approaches?

- ✅ **EFVS provides operational credit, allowing pilots to descend below the published DH or MDA when displayed on a HUD; SVS is certified only as an aid to situation awareness with no such credit.**
- SVS provides real-time sensor imagery of the external scene, while EFVS uses only a stored terrain database for navigation.
- EFVS requires a terrain and obstacle database to function, while SVS uses a forward-looking infrared sensor.
- Both EFVS and SVS provide identical operational credit for descending below DH/MDA on instrument approaches.

  > In 2004, 14 CFR 91.175 was amended to allow operators conducting straight-in instrument approaches to descend below the published DH or MDA when using an approved EFVS shown on the pilot's HUD. This 'operational credit' exists only for EFVS, not for SVS. SVS uses a stored terrain database and is certified as an aid to situation awareness only. EFVS uses a real-time imaging sensor such as FLIR or MMWR.
